In one of the last terrible battles of the war, that of Five Forks, he was wounded and put into the hospital ward where I was an attendant. For awhile he was delirious, and in his delirium he would sometimes think that I was his mother and at other times his sister. I humored his fancies, would often sing to him when he was restless, and my voice almost invariably soothed him to sleep. One day I sang to him that old hymn we used to sing on the plantation:﻿—

> “Drooping souls no longer grieve,
 

 Heaven is propitious;
 

 If on Christ you do believe,
 

 You will find Him precious.”

===Chinese===
“记得，”玛丽答道，语气里带着探询。

“是这样，”伊奥拉接着说，眼里盛满欢喜，“我想，我已经找到他们了。”

“这怎么可能！”玛丽惊呼道，满脸惊讶。“我们离散已经三十多年了。我怕是你弄错了。”

“没有弄错，妈妈；我是凭着扎实的旁证下的结论。自从被人从你身边夺走以后，我经受了一场如围城般可怕的苦难，说给你听，只会撕痛你的心。如今回想起来，我还常常不寒而栗。最后把我攥在手心里的那个人，卑劣、粗暴，又残忍。联邦军开进 C—— 城的时候——我那时就住在那里——我还落在他手里。一批有色人种男子蜂拥投奔了联邦军的行列，领头的是位先生，我想那就是你的弟弟。他的一位朋友把我的事报告了驻地指挥官，指挥官当即下令放我自由。医院里给了我一个护士的职位。那位朋友临终的病榻，就是我看护的。可怜的人！在我颠沛流离的这些年月里，他是待我最好的朋友。我想是你弟弟的那位先生，似乎十分挂念他朋友的伤势，对他的死也哀恸不已。在战争末了那几场惨烈的大战之一——五叉口战役——中，他负了伤，被送进我做看护的病房。有一阵子他神志昏乱，昏乱中他有时把我当成他的母亲，有时又当成他的姐姐。我顺着他的痴想将就他，常在他躁动不宁时唱歌给他听，而我的嗓音几乎回回都能哄他入睡。有一天，我给他唱了那首我们从前在种植园常唱的老圣诗：

> “忧伤低垂的灵魂啊，不要再悲叹，
 

 上苍已然施恩；
 

 你若信靠基督，
 

 必觉祂何等宝贵。”

===Original===
“I remember,” said Marie, with a sigh, as memories of the past swept over her.

“After I had finished the hymn,” continued Iola, “he looked earnestly and inquiringly into my face, and asked, ‘Where did you learn that hymn? I have heard my mother sing it when I was a boy, but I have never heard it since.’ I think, mamma, the words, ‘I was lost but now I’m found; glory! glory! glory!’ had imprinted themselves on his memory, and that his mind was assuming a higher state of intellectuality. He asked me to sing it again, which I did, until he fell asleep. Then I noticed a marked resemblance between him and Harry, and I thought, ‘Suppose he should prove to be your long-lost brother?’ During his convalescence we found that we had a common ground of sympathy. We were anxious to be reunited to our severed relations. We had both been separated from our mothers. He told me of his little sister, with whom he used to play. She had a mole on her cheek which he called her beauty spot. He had the red spot on his forehead which you told me of.”

===Chinese===
“我记得，”玛丽说着叹了一口气，往昔的记忆涌过了她的心头。

“那首圣诗唱完之后，”伊奥拉接着说，“他恳切而探询地凝视着我的脸，问道：‘你从哪儿学来这首圣歌？我做孩子的时候，听我母亲唱过它，可打那以后，就再没有听人唱过。’妈妈，我想，那句‘我曾迷失，如今已被寻回；荣耀！荣耀！荣耀！’已经深印在他的记忆里，他的心智也正在向更高的境界升华。他请我再唱一遍，我便唱了又唱，直到他睡去。这时我才注意到，他和哈里长得明显相像，我心里想：‘万一他真是你那位失散多年的弟弟呢？’在他康复期间，我们发现彼此之间有了一片息息相通的同情之地。我们都渴望与离散的骨肉重新团聚。我们都是与母亲分开过的人。他跟我讲起他的姐姐，姐弟俩幼时常常一起玩耍。她脸颊上有一颗痣，他管它叫她的美人痣。他额头上那块红记，正是你从前对我说起过的那块。”

## XIV Harry Leroy / 第十四章 哈里·勒罗伊

===Original===
It was several weeks after Iola had written to her brother that her letter reached him. The trusty servant to whom she delivered it watched his opportunity to mail it. At last he succeeded in slipping it into Lorraine’s mail and dropping them all into the post office together. Harry was studying at a boys’ academy in Maine. His father had given that State the preference because, while on a visit there, he had been favorably impressed with the kindness and hospitality of the people. He had sent his son a large sum of money, and given him permission to spend awhile with some school-chums till he was ready to bring the family North, where they could all spend the summer together. Harry had returned from his visit, and was looking for letters and remittances from home, when a letter, all crumpled, was handed him by the principal of the academy. He recognized his sister’s handwriting and eagerly opened the letter. As he read, he turned very pale; then a deep flush overspread his face and an angry light flashed from his eyes. As he read on, his face became still paler; he gasped for breath and fell into a swoon. Appalled at the sudden change which had swept over him like a deadly sirocco, the principal rushed to the fallen boy, picked up the missive that lay beside him, and immediately rang for help and dispatched for the doctor. The doctor came at once and was greatly puzzled. Less than an hour before, he had seen him with a crowd of merry, laughter-loving boys, apparently as lighthearted and joyous as any of them; now he lay with features drawn and pinched, his face deadly pale, as if some terrible suffering had sent all the blood in his veins to stagnate around his heart. Harry opened his eyes, shuddered, and relapsed into silence. The doctor, all at sea in regard to the cause of the sudden attack, did all that he could to restore him to consciousness and quiet the perturbation of his spirit. He succeeded, but found he was strangely silent. A terrible shock had sent a tremor through every nerve, and the doctor watched with painful apprehension its effect upon his reason. Giving him an opiate and enjoining that he should be kept perfectly quiet, the doctor left the room, sought the principal, and said:﻿—

“Mr. Bascom, here is a case that baffles my skill. I saw that boy pass by my window not more than half an hour ago, full of animation, and now he lies hovering between life and death. I have great apprehension for his reason. Can you throw any light on the subject?”

Mr. Bascom hesitated.

“I am not asking you as a matter of idle curiosity, but as a physician. I must have all the light I can get in making my diagnosis of the case.”

The principal arose, went to his desk, took out the letter which he had picked up from the floor, and laid it in the physician’s hand. As the doctor read, a look of indignant horror swept over his face. Then he said: “Can it be possible! I never suspected such a thing. It must be a cruel, senseless hoax.”

===Chinese===
伊奥拉给她弟弟写好信之后，又过了好几个星期，那封信才送到他手里。她把信托付给一个可靠的老仆，那仆人一直在瞅机会把它寄出去，最后终于把信悄悄夹进洛兰要寄的邮件里，同它们一并投进了邮局。哈里当时正在缅因州一所男子中学读书。他父亲特意挑中了那个州，是因为当年到那里游历时，当地人的和善好客给他留下了极好的印象。他给儿子汇来一大笔钱，又准许他先到几位同窗好友家盘桓些时日，等他把家眷接来北方，一家人好一起消夏。哈里访友归来，正盼着家里的来信和汇款，校长却递给他一封皱作一团的信。他认出了姐姐的笔迹，急急地拆开来读。读着读着，他的脸刷地变得惨白；随即一片深红涌上脸来，眼中闪出愤怒的光。再往下读，他的脸色愈发惨白，一口气喘不上来，便昏厥过去。这突如其来的变故像一股致命的西罗科热风（sirocco）从他身上扫过，校长惊骇万分，抢步奔到倒下的少年跟前，拾起落在他身边的信，立即打铃唤人，又打发人去请医生。医生即刻赶到，却大惑不解。不到一个钟头之前，他还看见这孩子夹在一群快活爱笑的男孩中间，看那模样，同他们哪一个一样无忧无虑、欢天喜地；如今他却躺在那里，面容扭曲紧缩，脸色死灰，仿佛一场骇人的痛楚已把他全身的血液都驱回心脏四周，凝滞不动了。哈里睁开眼，打了个寒噤，又归于沉默。医生对这场突发病症的缘由全然摸不着头脑，只得竭尽所能唤他恢复知觉，平复他心头的纷乱。他做到了，却发现这孩子沉默得出奇。一场可怕的打击震颤了他的每一根神经；医生忧心忡忡地注视着这震荡会对他的神志产生什么影响。医生给他服下一剂安眠的鸦片药，叮嘱务必让他绝对静养，随后走出房去，找到校长，说道：——

“巴斯康姆先生（Mr. Bascom），眼下这桩病症，实在叫我的医术束手无策。不到半个钟头之前，我还看见那孩子从我的窗前走过，浑身是劲；如今他却躺在那儿，在生死之间徘徊。我很为他的神志担忧。您能不能给我指点一点端倪？”

巴斯康姆先生迟疑着。

“我不是出于无聊的猎奇来问您，而是以医生的身份来问。要对这病症下诊断，我必须把能弄明白的都弄明白。”

校长站起身，走到自己的书桌前，取出那封从地板上拾起的信，放到医生手里。医生读着读着，一脸又愤慨又惊骇的神色掠过他的面孔。随后他说道：“这怎么可能！我万万没有疑心到这样的事。这必定是一场残忍而愚蠢的恶作剧。”

===Original===
“Doctor,” said Mr. Bascom, “I have been a lifelong Abolitionist and have often read of the cruelties and crimes of American slavery, but never before did I realize the low moral tone of the social life under which such shameless cruelties could be practiced on a defenseless widow and her orphaned children. Let me read the letter again. Just look at it, all tear-blotted and written with a trembling hand:﻿—

> ‘**Dear Brother**:﻿—I have dreadful news for you and I hardly know how to tell it. Papa and Gracie are both dead. He died of yellow fever. Mamma is almost distracted. Papa’s cousin has taken possession of our property, and instead of heirs we are chattels. Mamma has explained the whole situation to me. She was papa’s slave before she married. He loved her, manumitted, educated, and married her. When he died Mr. Lorraine entered suit for his property and Judge Starkins has decided in his favor. The decree of the court has made their marriage invalid, robbed us of our inheritance, and remanded us all to slavery. Mamma is too wretched to attempt to write herself, but told me to entreat you not to attempt to come home. You can do us no good, and that mean, cruel Lorraine may do you much harm. Don’t attempt, I beseech you, to come home. Show this letter to Mr. Bascom and let him advise you what to do. But don’t, for our sake, attempt to come home.
> ‘Your heartbroken sister,
> ‘Iola Leroy.’ ”

“This,” said the doctor, “is a very awkward affair. The boy is too ill to be removed. It is doubtful if the nerves which have trembled with such fearful excitement will ever recover their normal condition. It is simply a work of mercy to watch over him with the tenderest care.”

===Chinese===
“医生，”巴斯康姆先生说，“我做废奴主义者做了一辈子，时常读到美国奴隶制种种残虐与罪孽的记载，可直到今天我才真正认清，容许对一个无依无靠的寡妇和她的一群孤儿孤女下这种无耻毒手的社会，世道人心已败坏到了何等低下的地步。让我把信再读一遍。您且看看它，泪痕斑斑，字字都出自一只颤抖的手：——

> ‘**亲爱的弟弟**：——我有可怕的消息要告诉你，简直不知从何说起。爸爸和格雷茜（Gracie）都不在人世了。爸爸是害黄热病死的，妈妈悲痛得几乎失了神。爸爸的表兄霸占了我们的家产，我们非但做不成继承人，反倒成了任人支配的动产。妈妈已把事情的原委原原本本讲给我听了。出嫁之前，她原是爸爸的奴隶。爸爸爱她，为她解除奴籍，供她读书，然后娶了她。爸爸一死，洛兰先生便上告争夺家产，斯塔金斯法官（Judge Starkins）判了他胜诉。法院的判决宣判他们的婚姻无效，夺走了我们的继承权，把我们全都发还为奴。妈妈悲苦得提不动笔，只嘱咐我恳求你，千万不要设法回家。你回来帮不上我们什么忙，那个卑鄙狠毒的洛兰倒可能害你不浅。我求求你，千万别想回家。把这封信拿给巴斯康姆先生看，请他替你拿主意。可是千万看在我们的份上，不要设法回家。
> ‘你心碎的姐姐，
> ‘伊奥拉·勒罗伊。’”

“这桩事，”医生说，“真是棘手得很。这孩子病得经不起挪动。受了这么大惊骇的神经还能不能恢复常态，实在难说。眼下以最温柔的看护照料他，纯粹是一桩仁慈的善举。”

===Original===
Fortunately for Harry he had fallen into good hands, and the most tender care and nursing were bestowed upon him. For awhile Harry was strangely silent, never referring to the terrible misfortune which had so suddenly overshadowed his life. It seemed as if the past were suddenly blotted out of his memory. But he was young and of an excellent constitution, and in a few months he was slowly recovering.

“Doctor,” said he one day, as the physician sat at his bedside, “I seem to have had a dreadful dream, and to have dreamt that my father was dead, and my mother and sister were in terrible trouble, but I could not help them. Doctor, was it a dream, or was it a reality? It could not have been a dream, for when I fell asleep the grass was green and the birds were singing, but now the winds are howling and the frost is on the ground. Doctor, tell me how it is? How long have I been here?”

Sitting by his bedside, and taking his emaciated hand in his, the doctor said, in a kind, fatherly tone: “My dear boy, you have been very ill, and everything depends on your keeping quiet, very quiet.”

As soon as he was strong enough the principal gave him his letter to read.

===Chinese===
哈里总算万幸，落进了好心人手里，得到最细心的照料和护养。有一阵子，哈里沉默得出奇，从不提起那场突然笼罩了他一生的可怕灾祸，仿佛过去的一切忽然从他的记忆里被抹去了。可他年纪轻，底子又好，不出几个月，便慢慢康复起来。

一天，医生坐在他床边，他说道：“医生，我像是做了一场可怕的梦，梦里爸爸死了，妈妈和姐姐陷在可怕的苦难里，我却是帮不了她们。医生，那是一场梦呢，还是真的？那不会是梦，因为我睡去的时候，草还青着，鸟儿还在歌唱；可如今风在呼啸，地上结了霜。医生，告诉我这是怎么回事？我在这儿躺多久了？”

医生坐在他床边，把他那只消瘦的手握在自己手里，用慈父般的口吻说道：“亲爱的孩子，你病得很重，一切都全靠你静养，要非常安静。”

等他身子撑得住了，校长才把那封信拿给他亲自读。

===Original===
“But, Mr. Bascom,” Harry said, “I do not understand this. It says my mother and father were legally married. How could her marriage be set aside and her children robbed of their inheritance? This is not a heathen country. I hardly think barbarians would have done any worse; yet this is called a Christian country.”

“Christian in name,” answered the principal. “When your father left you in my care, knowing that I was an Abolitionist, he confided his secret to me. He said that life was full of vicissitudes, and he wished you to have a good education. He wanted you and your sister to be prepared for any emergency. He did not wish you to know that you had negro blood in your veins. He knew that the spirit of caste pervaded the nation, North and South, and he was very anxious to have his children freed from its depressing influences. He did not intend to stay South after you had finished your education.”

“But,” said Harry, “I cannot understand. If my mother was lawfully married, how could they deprive her of her marital rights?”

“When Lorraine,” continued Mr. Bascom, “knew your father was dead, all he had to do was to find a flaw in her manumission, and, of course, the marriage became illegal. She could not then inherit property nor maintain her freedom; and her children followed her condition.”

===Chinese===
“可是，巴斯康姆先生，”哈里说，“这个我弄不明白。信上说，我母亲和父亲是合法结的婚。她的婚姻怎么能被一笔勾销，她的孩子怎么能被夺走继承权？这里又不是异教的蛮荒之地。我想就是野蛮人做事，也不会比这更狠；可这里偏偏还称作基督教国家。”

“不过是挂名的基督教罢了，”校长答道。“你父亲把你托付给我的时候，知道我是个废奴主义者，便把他的秘密告诉了我。他说人生充满祸福变迁，愿意你受一份良好的教育。他希望你和你姐姐为一切不测之变做好准备。他不愿让你知道自己血管里流着黑人的血。他知道等级偏见的风气弥漫全国，北方南方都一样，一心想让他的孩子们摆脱它那压得人抬不起头的影响。他原打算等你学业一成，就不再在南方待下去。”

“可是，”哈里说，“我还是不懂。我母亲既是明媒正娶，他们怎么能剥夺她婚姻赋予的权利？”

“洛兰知道你父亲死了，”巴斯康姆先生接着说，“只消在她解除奴籍的文书里寻出一处漏洞，那桩婚姻自然就成了非法。这么一来，她既不能继承财产，也保不住自己的自由身；儿女呢，也就随着母亲的身分，一并沦为奴隶。”

===Original===
Harry listened attentively. Things which had puzzled him once now became perfectly clear. He sighed heavily, and, turning to the principal, said: “I see things in a new light. Now I remember that none of the planters’ wives ever visited my mother; and we never went to church except when my father took us to the Cathedral in New Orleans. My father was a Catholic, but I don’t think mamma is.”

“Now, Harry,” said the principal, “life is before you. If you wish to stay North, I will interest friends in your behalf, and try to get you a situation. Going South is out of the question. It is probable that by this time your mother and sister are removed from their home. You are powerless to fight against the law that enslaved them. Should you fall into the clutches of Lorraine, he might give you a great deal of trouble. You would be pressed into the Confederate service to help them throw up barricades, dig trenches, and add to the strength of those who enslaved your mother and sister.”

“Never! never!” cried Harry. “I would rather die than do it! I should despise myself forever if I did.”

“Numbers of our young men,” said Mr. Bascom, “have gone to the war which is now raging between North and South. You have been sick for several months, and much has taken place of which you are unaware. Would you like to enlist?”

===Chinese===
哈里凝神听着。先前叫他百思不解的事，如今全变得清清楚楚。他沉沉地叹了口气，转向校长说道：“如今我看事情，眼睛里换了一层光。我想起来了，种植园主们的太太，从没有一位登门看过我母亲；我们也从不上教堂，除非父亲带我们去新奥尔良的天主大教堂。父亲是天主教徒，我想妈妈不是。”

“好了，哈里，”校长说，“人生就摆在你眼前。你若愿意留在北方，我会请几位朋友为你出力，想法替你谋个职位。回南方是万万谈不上的。到这会儿，你母亲和姐姐多半已经被人从家里撵走了。你无力对抗那部奴役她们的法律。你若落进洛兰的魔爪，他会给你大苦头吃。他们会把你强征进叛军里当差，帮他们垒壁垒、挖壕沟，给你母亲和姐姐套枷锁的那些人添气力。”

“绝不！绝不！”哈里喊道。“我宁可死也不干！要是干了，我会一辈子瞧不起自己！”

“我们有好多年轻人，”巴斯康姆先生说，“都已投身到眼下南北双方打得正凶的那场战争里去了。你病了几个月，外界发生了许多大事，你全都不知道。你愿意入伍吗？”

===Original===
“I certainly would; not so much for the sake of fighting for the Government, as with the hope of finding my mother and sister, and avenging their wrongs. I should like to meet Lorraine on the battlefield.”

“What kind of a regiment would you prefer, white or colored?”

Harry winced when the question was asked. He felt the reality of his situation as he had not done before. It was as if two paths had suddenly opened before him, and he was forced to choose between them. On one side were strength, courage, enterprise, power of achievement, and memories of a wonderful past. On the other side were weakness, ignorance, poverty, and the proud world’s social scorn. He knew nothing of colored people except as slaves, and his whole soul shrank from equalizing himself with them. He was fair enough to pass unchallenged among the fairest in the land, and yet a Christless prejudice had decreed that he should be a social pariah. He sat, thoughtful and undecided, as if a great struggle were going on in his mind. Finally the principal said, “I do not think that you should be assigned to a colored regiment because of the blood in your veins, but you will have, in such a regiment, better facilities for finding your mother and sister.”

“You are right, Mr. Bascom. To find my mother and sister I call no task too heavy, no sacrifice too great.”

===Chinese===
“我当然愿意；倒不那么多是为了替政府打仗，多半是盼着能找到我的母亲和姐姐，为她们蒙受的冤屈报仇。我倒愿意跟洛兰在战场上会一会。”

“你愿意进哪一种团队，白人的，还是有色人种的？”

这个问题一出口，哈里浑身一缩。他从来没有像此刻这样，真切地感到自己处境的现实。仿佛眼前忽然岔出两条路，逼着他从中择一。一边是力量、勇气、闯劲、成就大业的本领，还有一段锦绣往昔的记忆；另一边是孱弱、愚昧、穷困，和傲慢世人的白眼。除了身为奴隶的有色人种，他对这个族群一无所知，他整个灵魂都在退缩，不肯把自己同他们摆成平起平坐。他的肤色白净，走在全国最白皙的人们中间也不会受人盘问；然而一种没有基督精神的偏见，却注定了他要做社会的弃儿。他坐在那里，沉思不决，仿佛心里正翻腾着一场大交战。末了，校长说：“我并不认为单凭你血管里的血，就该把你编进有色人种团；不过在这样一支团队里，你要寻你的母亲和姐姐，反倒有更多便利。”

“您说得对，巴斯康姆先生。只要能找到我的母亲和姐姐，再重的担子我也不嫌重，再大的牺牲我也不嫌大。”

===Original===
Since Harry had come North he had learned to feel profound pity for the slave. But there is a difference between looking on a man as an object of pity and protecting him as such, and being identified with him and forced to share his lot. To take his place with them on the arena of life was the test of his life, but love was stronger than pride.

His father was dead. His mother and sister were enslaved by a mockery of justice. It was more than a matter of choice where he should stand on the racial question. He felt that he must stand where he could strike the most effective blow for their freedom. With that thought strong in his mind, and as soon as he recovered, he went westward to find a colored regiment. He told the recruiting officer that he wished to be assigned to a colored regiment.

“Why do you wish that,” said the officer, looking at Harry with an air of astonishment.

“Because I am a colored man.”

===Chinese===
自从来到北方，哈里已学会对奴隶深怀怜悯。可是，把一个人当作怜悯和护佑的对象，同与他认作一体、被迫与他共命运，这中间大有分别。要在人生的竞技场上同他们站到一处，是对他一生的考验；可是，爱比骄傲更强。

他的父亲死了。他的母亲和姐姐被一场对正义的嘲弄夺去自由，沦为奴隶。在种族问题上他该站在哪里，已不只是个人取舍的事。他觉得，自己必须站在能为她们的自由打出最有效一击的地方。这个念头在他心里牢牢扎了根；身子一康复，他便向西去寻一支有色人种团。他对募兵的军官说，希望被编进有色人种团。

“你为什么愿意这样？”军官说，一脸惊讶地打量着哈里。

“因为我是个有色人。”

===Original===
The officer look puzzled. It was a new experience. He had seen colored men with fair complexions anxious to lose their identity with the colored race and pose as white men, but here was a man in the flush of his early manhood, to whom could come dreams of promotion from a simple private to a successful general, deliberately turning his back upon every gilded hope and dazzling opportunity, to cast his lot with the despised and hated negro.

“I do not understand you,” said the officer. “Surely you are a white man, and, as such, I will enlist you in a white regiment.”

“No,” said Harry, firmly, “I am a colored man, and unless I can be assigned to a colored regiment I am not willing to enter the army.”

“Well,” said the officer, “you are the d﻿—﻿—﻿d’st fool I ever saw﻿—a man as white as you are turning his back upon his chances of promotion! But you can take your choice.”

===Chinese===
军官面露困惑。这可是桩新鲜事。他见过肤色白净的有色人，一个个巴不得抹掉自己同有色人种的关联，冒充白人；可眼前这个人，正当青春盛年，从一名普通列兵一路升到功成名就的将军的美梦都可能落到他头上，他却存心背弃每一线镀金的希望、每一个耀眼的机会，把命运同备受鄙视和憎恨的黑人连在一起。

“我搞不懂你，”军官说。“你分明是个白人；既是白人，我就把你编进白人团。”

“不，”哈里坚定地说，“我是个有色人。除非把我编进有色人种团，否则我不愿参军。”

“好吧，”军官说，“你真是我这辈子见过的最该——死的傻瓜——一个长你这样白净的人，居然掉头背弃自己的升迁机会！不过，悉听尊便。”

===Original===
So Harry was permitted to enter the army. By his promptness and valor he soon won the hearts of his superior officers, and was made drill sergeant. Having nearly all of his life been used to colored people, and being taught by his mother to be kind and respectful to them, he was soon able to gain their esteem. He continued in the regiment until Grant began the task of opening the Mississippi. After weeks of fruitless effort, Grant marched his army down the west side of the river, while the gunboats undertook the perilous task of running the batteries. Men were found for the hour. The volunteers offered themselves in such numbers that lots were cast to determine who should have the opportunity to enlist in an enterprise so fraught with danger. Harry was one on whom the lot fell.

Grant crossed the river below, coiled his forces around Vicksburg like a boa-constrictor, and held it in his grasp. After forty-seven days of endurance the city surrendered to him. Port Hudson, after the surrender of Vicksburg, gave up the unequal contest, and the Mississippi was open to the Gulf.

===Chinese===
就这样，哈里获准参军。他办事利落、作战骁勇，很快赢得了上级军官的心，被任命为操练军士。他这一辈子几乎同有色人种处惯了，母亲又从小教他待他们和善、恭敬，他很快便赢得了众人的敬重。他在这支团队里一直待到格兰特（Grant）着手打通密西西比河。经过数周劳而无功的努力，格兰特率大军沿河西岸南下，炮艇则担起冲越炮台火线的危险任务。危难时刻，自有豪杰挺身而出。自愿请命的人多到只好抽签，来决定谁有机会加入这项凶险备尝的壮举。哈里正是抽中签的人之一。

格兰特在下游渡过河去，像一条巨蟒把大军盘绕在维克斯堡（Vicksburg）四周，将它牢牢攥在掌中。苦撑了四十七天之后，这座城向他投降了。维克斯堡既下，哈德逊港（Port Hudson）也放弃了这场众寡悬殊的较量，密西西比河直通墨西哥湾的水路就此洞开。

## Introduction / 导言

===Original===
I confess when I first learned that Mrs. Harper was about to write “a story” on some features of the Anglo-African race, growing out of what was once popularly known as the “peculiar institution,” I had my doubts about the matter. Indeed it was far from being easy for me to think that she was as fortunate as she might have been in selecting a subject which would afford her the best opportunity for bringing out a work of merit and lasting worth to the race﻿—such a work as some of her personal friends have long desired to see from her graphic pen. However, after hearing a good portion of the manuscript read, and a general statement with regard to the object in view, I admit frankly that my partial indifference was soon swept away; at least I was willing to wait for further developments.

Being very desirous that one of the race, so long distinguished in the cause of freedom for her intellectual worth as Mrs. Harper has had the honor of being, should not at this late date in life make a blunder which might detract from her own good name, I naturally proposed to await developments before deciding too quickly in favor of giving encouragement to her contemplated effort.

However, I was perfectly aware of the fact that she had much material in her possession for a most interesting book on the subject of the condition of the colored people in the South. I know of no other woman, white or colored, anywhere, who has come so intimately in contact with the colored people in the South as Mrs. Harper. Since emancipation she has labored in every Southern State in the Union, save two, Arkansas and Texas; in the colleges, schools, churches, and the cabins not excepted, she has found a vast field and open doors to teach and speak on the themes of education, temperance, and good home building, industry, morality, and the like, and never lacked for evidences of hearty appreciation and gratitude.

===Chinese===
我承认，当初听说哈珀太太（Mrs. Harper）打算就盎格鲁-非洲人种（Anglo-African）的某些面貌写“一部小说”，而题材又生发于昔日世人惯称的“特殊制度”，我心里是存有疑虑的。说实话，要我相信她选题时如愿地走运——恰好选中了一个最能让她有机会拿出一部于这个种族有真价值、能传世的作品的题目，而这样一部作品，正是她的一些挚友长久以来渴望从她那支生花妙笔之下读到的——实在不是件容易的事。然而，在听人朗读了手稿的相当一部分、又听她大体说明了写作的旨归之后，我坦白承认，我先前那几分冷淡很快便烟消云散；至少，我愿意静候进一步的进展了。

她身为本种族的一员，以其才学在自由事业中久享殊荣；正因为极不愿看到这样一位人物在人生迟暮之年行差踏错、有损自己素来的令名，我自然主张静观其变，不忙于过早表态、为她筹划中的这番努力鼓劲助威。

不过我完全清楚，就南方有色人种状况这个题目而言，她手头掌握着大量材料，尽够写出一本极为引人入胜的书。天下女子，无论白人还是有色人种，我还不知道有谁能像哈珀太太这样，与南方的有色人种有过如此亲密的接触。自奴隶解放以来，联邦所属的南方各州她全都辛苦奔走过，只除了阿肯色和得克萨斯两州；从学院、学校到教堂，连黑人住的小木屋也不例外，她处处找到广阔的园地和敞开的大门，就教育、戒酒、营建美好家庭、勤勉、道德等题目施教演讲，而由衷的赏识与感激之情，她从来不曾缺少过。

===Original===
Everywhere help was needed, and her heart being deeply absorbed in the cause she willingly allowed her sympathies to impel her to perform most heroic services.

With her it was no uncommon occurrence, in visiting cities or towns, to speak at two, three, and four meetings a day; sometimes to promiscuous audiences composed of everybody who would care to come.

But the kind of meetings she took greatest interest in were meetings called exclusively for women. In this attitude she could pour out her sympathies to them as she could not do before a mixed audience; and indeed she felt their needs were far more pressing than any other class.

===Chinese===
处处都需要有人援手；她的心又深深系在这项事业上，便甘愿听从同情心的驱策，去做种种堪称英勇的服务。

对她来说，走访城镇时一天连赶两场、三场乃至四场集会登台演说，本是司空见惯的事；有时台下的听众形形色色，凡是愿意来的都在其中。

但她最热心的一类集会，是专为妇女召开的集会。在这样的场合，她可以向她们尽情倾吐自己的同情，这是在男女杂处的听众面前办不到的；而且她也确实觉得，她们的需要远比任何别的阶层都更为迫切。

===Original===
And now I am prepared to most fully endorse her story. I doubt whether she could, if she had tried ever so much, have hit upon a subject so well adapted to reach a large number of her friends and the public with both entertaining and instructive matter as successfully as she has done in this volume.

The grand and ennobling sentiments which have characterized all her utterances in laboring for the elevation of the oppressed will not be found missing in this book.

The previous books from her pen, which have been so very widely circulated and admired, North and South﻿—*Forest Leaves*, *Miscellaneous Poems*, *Moses, a Story of the Nile*, *Poems*, and *Sketches of Southern Life* (five in number)﻿—these, I predict, will be by far eclipsed by this last effort, which will, in all probability, be the crowning effort of her long and valuable services in the cause of humanity.

===Chinese===
而如今，我已经能够最充分地推许她这部小说了。我看，纵使她再怎么煞费苦心，恐怕也难物色到一个题目，能像她在这一卷里做得这样成功——把既饶有趣味又富有教益的内容，送到她众多的朋友和公众面前。

她历来为受压迫者的提升奔走效力，言语之间素来特有的那种崇高而令人向上的情怀，在这本书里也不会缺席。

她此前出自笔端、在南北两地流传极广、备受赞赏的那几种书——《森林之叶》《杂诗集》《摩西：尼罗河的故事》《诗集》和《南方生活速写》（共五种）——我预言，都将被她这最新的一番心力远远盖过；而这一部，十之八九将成为她漫长而可贵的人道事业生涯的冠冕之作。

===Original===
While, as indicated, Mrs. Harper has done a large amount of work in the South, she has at the same time done much active service in the temperance cause in the North, as thousands of this class can testify.

Before the war she was engaged as a speaker by anti-slavery associations; since then, by appointment of the Women’s Christian Temperance Union, she has held the office of “Superintendent of Colored Work” for years. She has also held the office of one of the Directors of the Women’s Congress of the United States.

Under the auspices of these influential, earnest, and intelligent associations, she has been seen often on their platforms with the leading lady orators of the nation.

===Chinese===
如前所述，哈珀太太固然在南方做了大量工作，与此同时，她在北方的戒酒事业中同样出力甚多、身体力行，成千上万这一界的人士都可以作证。

战前，她受各废奴协会之聘担任演说人；此后，她受基督教妇女戒酒联合会（Women’s Christian Temperance Union）委派，连年出任“有色人种事务督导”一职。她还担任过美国妇女代表大会的理事。

在这些有影响、有热忱、有见识的团体的提挈之下，她时常与全国一流的女演说家们一同出现在她们的讲坛之上。

===Original===
Hence, being widely known not only amongst her own race but likewise by the reformers, laboring for the salvation of the intemperate and others equally unfortunate, there is little room to doubt that the book will be in great demand and will meet with warm congratulations from a goodly number outside of the author’s social connections.

Doubtless the thousands of colored Sunday-schools in the South, in casting about for an interesting, moral storybook, full of practical lessons, will not be content to be without *Iola Leroy, or Shadows Uplifted*.

William Still.

===Chinese===
因此，她不仅在本种族中广有令名，也为那班致力于拯救纵酒之徒及其他同样不幸之人的改革人士所熟知；这本书必将广受欢迎、需求旺盛，也必将博得作者社交圈子之外许许多多人的热烈祝贺——这一点几乎毋庸置疑。

毫无疑问，南方成千上万的有色人种主日学校，在四处物色一本有趣、有益道德、满载切实教益的故事书时，是不会甘心缺少一部《伊奥拉·勒罗伊：或，阴影消散》的。

威廉·斯蒂尔（William Still）。

## I Mystery of Market Speech and Prayer-Meeting / 第一章 集市行话之谜与祈祷会

===Original===
“Good mornin’, Bob; how’s butter dis mornin’?”

“Fresh; just as fresh, as fresh can be.”

“Oh, glory!” said the questioner, whom we shall call Thomas Anderson, although he was known among his acquaintances as Marster Anderson’s Tom.

His informant regarding the condition of the market was Robert Johnson, who had been separated from his mother in his childhood and reared by his mistress as a favorite slave. She had fondled him as a pet animal, and even taught him to read. Notwithstanding their relation as mistress and slave, they had strong personal likings for each other.

===Chinese===
“早晨好啊，鲍勃；今儿早上的黄油咋样？”

“新鲜；要多新鲜有多新鲜。”

“哦，荣耀啊！”发问的人叹道。我们姑且称他托马斯·安德森（Thomas Anderson），虽然相熟的人们都只叫他安德森主人的汤姆。

向他报告市场行情的是罗伯特·约翰逊（Robert Johnson）。他幼年便与母亲离散，被女主人当作得宠的奴隶抚养长大。女主人抚爱他，如同抚爱一只宠物，甚至还教他读书识字。尽管两人之间是主奴的名分，彼此却存着挺深的情分。


===Original===
Tom Anderson was the servant of a wealthy planter, who lived in the city of C﻿—﻿—, North Carolina. This planter was quite advanced in life, but in his earlier days he had spent much of his time in talking politics in his State and National capitals in winter, and in visiting pleasure resorts and watering places in summer. His plantations were left to the care of overseers who, in their turn, employed negro drivers to aid them in the work of cultivation and discipline. But as the infirmities of age were pressing upon him he had withdrawn from active life, and given the management of his affairs into the hands of his sons. As Robert Johnson and Thomas Anderson passed homeward from the market, having bought provisions for their respective homes, they seemed to be very lighthearted and careless, chatting and joking with each other; but every now and then, after looking furtively around, one would drop into the ears of the other some news of the battle then raging between the North and South which, like two great millstones, were grinding slavery to powder.

As they passed along, they were met by another servant, who said in hurried tones, but with a glad accent in his voice:﻿—

===Chinese===
汤姆·安德森是北卡罗来纳 C—— 城一位富有种植园主家的仆人。这位种植园主已是桑榆暮景；早年间的他，冬天大半光阴都耗在州首府和首都里谈政治，夏天便到各处游乐胜地和温泉疗养地消磨岁月。他的种植园都交由监工照管，监工们又雇下黑人领班，帮着他们督耕田亩、管束人手。无奈老年体衰的种种病痛步步相逼，他便退出了操劳的生活，把产业的管理交到儿子们手里。罗伯特·约翰逊和托马斯·安德森替各自的主家在集市上置办好了吃食，一路往回走。两人看上去轻快得很、无忧无虑，一路说说笑笑、互相打趣；可时不时地，其中一个先偷偷四下一望，便凑到另一个耳边，悄悄递上几句战场的消息——那时南北双方正打得难解难分，像两扇巨大的磨盘，正把奴隶制碾作齑粉。

走着走着，他们又迎面碰上一个仆人。那人语声匆匆，嗓音里却透着欢喜：——


===Original===
“Did you see de fish in de market dis mornin’? Oh, but dey war splendid, jis’ as fresh, as fresh kin be.”

“That’s the ticket,” said Robert, as a broad smile overspread his face. “I’ll see you later.”

“Good mornin’, boys,” said another servant on his way to market. “How’s eggs dis mornin’?”

“Fust rate, fust rate,” said Tom Anderson. “Bob’s got it down fine.”

“I thought so; mighty long faces at de pos’-office dis mornin’; but I’d better move ’long,” and with a bright smile lighting up his face he passed on with a quickened tread.

===Chinese===
“今儿早上在集上瞧见那些鱼了吗？哦，那叫一个呱呱叫，要多新鲜有多新鲜。”

“这就对喽。”罗伯特说，一脸绽开大大的笑容。“回头见。”

“早晨好啊，小伙子们。”另一个正往集市去的仆人招呼道。“今儿早上鸡蛋咋样？”

“好得很，好得很。”汤姆·安德森说。“鲍勃报得准着呢。”

“俺就料到是这么回事；今儿早上邮局里一张张脸都拉得老长；不过俺还是赶紧赶路吧。”他脸上绽起一抹亮堂堂的笑，脚下加快，自顾走过去了。


===Original===
There seemed to be an unusual interest manifested by these men in the state of the produce market, and a unanimous report of its good condition. Surely there was nothing in the primeness of the butter or the freshness of the eggs to change careless looking faces into such expressions of gratification, or to light dull eyes with such gladness. What did it mean?

During the dark days of the Rebellion, when the bondman was turning his eyes to the American flag, and learning to hail it as an ensign of deliverance, some of the shrewder slaves, coming in contact with their masters and overhearing their conversations, invented a phraseology to convey in the most unsuspected manner news to each other from the battlefield. Fragile women and helpless children were left on the plantations while their natural protectors were at the front, and yet these bondmen refrained from violence. Freedom was coming in the wake of the Union army, and while numbers deserted to join their forces, others remained at home, slept in their cabins by night and attended to their work by day; but under this apparently careless exterior there was an undercurrent of thought which escaped the cognizance of their masters. In conveying tidings of the war, if they wished to announce a victory of the Union army, they said the butter was fresh, or that the fish and eggs were in good condition. If defeat befell them, then the butter and other produce were rancid or stale.

===Chinese===
这几个人对市场行情表现出的关切异乎寻常，而且异口同声，都说行情好得很。当然，单凭黄油的上等、鸡蛋的新鲜，断然不至于把一张张原本散淡的脸变作这副喜形于色的模样，也不至于把一双双黯淡的眼睛点得这般欢亮。这是什么缘故呢？

在叛乱的那些黑暗岁月里，被奴役的人们正把目光转向美国的星旗，学着把它当作解救的旌旗来欢呼。一些更机灵的黑奴，平日与主人朝夕相处，偷听他们的言谈，便发明出一套行话，用最不惹人疑心的法子，把战场的消息彼此传递。男人们上了前线，种植园里只剩下柔弱的妇女和无助的孩子，然而这些被奴役的人们没有动用暴力。自由正随着联邦军的脚步而来；大批人逃离庄园去投他们的队伍，其余的留在家里，夜里睡在自家小木屋中，白天照旧照管活计；可在这副看似漫不经心的外表底下，涌动着一股主人无从察觉的思潮。传递战况时，他们若要通报联邦军打了胜仗，便说黄油新鲜，或者说鱼和鸡蛋成色好；若是己方吃了败仗，那黄油和别的出产便是哈喇的、陈败的。


===Original===
Entering his home, Robert set his basket down. In one arm he held a bundle of papers which he had obtained from the train to sell to the boarders, who were all anxious to hear from the seat of battle. He slipped one copy out and, looking cautiously around, said to Linda, the cook, in a low voice:﻿—

“Splendid news in the papers. Secesh routed. Yankees whipped ’em out of their boots. Papers full of it. I tell you the eggs and the butter’s mighty fresh this morning.”

“Oh, sho, chile,” said Linda, “I can’t read de newspapers, but ole Missus’ face is newspaper nuff for me. I looks at her ebery mornin’ wen she comes inter dis kitchen. Ef her face is long an’ she walks kine o’ droopy den I thinks things is gwine wrong for dem. But ef she comes out yere looking mighty pleased, an’ larffin all ober her face, an’ steppin’ so frisky, den I knows de Secesh is gittin’ de bes’ ob de Yankees. Robby, honey, does you really b’lieve for good and righty dat dem Yankees is got horns?”

===Chinese===
回到家中，罗伯特放下手里的篮子。他一只胳膊底下还夹着一沓报纸，是从火车上取来预备卖给寄膳寄宿客人的——他们都急着想听到战场来的消息。他抽出其中一份，小心地四下望了望，压低声音对厨娘琳达（Linda）说：——

“报纸上来了顶好的消息。分裂派被打得落花流水。北佬把他们打得连靴子都跑丢了。报上登得满满的。我告诉你，今儿早上这鸡蛋和黄油，新鲜得很呐。”

“嗐，孩子，”琳达说，“俺不识字，看不了报纸，可老女主人的那张脸，对俺来说就是顶够用的报纸。她每天早上进这厨房，俺都瞧她的脸色。她要是脸拉得老长、走道儿蔫头耷脑的，俺就知道他们那边要倒霉。可她要是出来的时候一脸高兴，笑得满脸开花，脚步踩得欢欢实实的，俺就知道是分裂派占了北佬的上风。罗比，乖孩子，你当真真儿地信，那些北佬头上长着犄角吗？”


===Original===
“Of course not.”

“Well, I yered so.”

“Well, you heard a mighty big whopper.”

“Anyhow, Bobby, things goes mighty contrary in dis house. Ole Miss is in de parlor prayin’ for de Secesh to gain de day, and we’s prayin’ in de cabins and kitchens for de Yankees to get de bes’ ob it. But wasn’t Miss Nancy glad wen dem Yankees run’d away at Bull’s Run. It was nuffin but Bull’s Run an’ run away Yankees. How she did larff and skip ’bout de house. An’ den me thinks to myself you’d better not holler till you gits out ob de woods. I specs ’fore dem Yankees gits froo you’ll be larffin tother side ob your mouf. While you was gone to market ole Miss com’d out yere, her face looking as long as my arm, tellin’ us all ’bout de war and saying dem Yankees whipped our folks all to pieces. And she was ’fraid dey’d all be down yere soon. I thought they couldn’t come too soon for we. But I didn’t tell her so.”

===Chinese===
“当然不信。”

“嗐，俺可是听人这么说的来着。”

“那你可是听了句弥天大谎喽。”

“还有哇，博比，这个家里的日子，净是拧着来的事儿。老女主人坐在客厅里，祷告分裂派打赢这一仗；俺们呢，在小木屋里、厨房里祷告，求北佬得胜。可布尔溪（Bull’s Run）那一仗，北佬撒腿溃逃的时候，南希小姐（Miss Nancy）那个高兴哟！那阵子满耳朵不是布尔溪，就是逃跑的北佬。她又是笑又是蹦，满屋子转悠。俺心里就琢磨：你呀，还没走出林子，最好先别嚷嚷。俺估摸着，等那些北佬打完了这仗，你就要笑不成、改哭喽。你上集市去的那会儿，老女主人出来到这儿，脸拉得跟俺的胳膊一样长，跟俺们大讲这场仗，说北佬把俺们这边的人打得七零八落。她还生怕他们不多日子就要打到这里来。俺倒寻思，他们来得越快才越好呢。可这话俺没说给她听。”

===Original===
“No, I don’t expect you did.”

“No, I didn’t; ef you buys me for a fool you loses your money shore. She said when dey com’d down yere she wanted all de men to hide, for dey’d kill all de men, but dey wouldn’t tech de women.”

“It’s no such thing. She’s put it all wrong. Why them Yankees are our best friends.”

“Dat’s jis’ what I thinks. Ole Miss was jis’ tryin to skeer a body. An’ when she war done she jis’ set down and sniffled an’ cried, an’ I war so glad I didn’t know what to do. But I had to hole in. An’ I made out I war orful sorry. An’ Jinny said, ‘O Miss Nancy, I hope dey won’t come yere.’ An’ she said, ‘I’se jis’ ’fraid dey will come down yere and gobble up eberything dey can lay dere hands on.’ An’ she jis’ looked as ef her heart war mos’ broke, an’ den she went inter de house. An’ when she war gone, we jis’ broke loose. Jake turned somersets, and said he warnt ’fraid ob dem Yankees; he know’d which side his brad was buttered on. Dat Jake is a cuter. When he goes down ter git de letters he cuts up all kines ob shines and capers. An’ to look at him skylarking dere while de folks is waitin’ for dere letters, an’ talkin’ bout de war, yer wouldn’t think dat boy had a thimbleful of sense. But Jake’s listenin’ all de time wid his eyes and his mouf wide open, an’ ketchin’ eberything he kin, an’ a heap ob news he gits dat way. As to Jinny, she jis’ capered and danced all ober de flore. An’ I jis’ had to put my han’ ober her mouf to keep ole Miss from yereing her. Oh, but we did hab a good time. Boy, yer oughter been yere.”

===Chinese===
“是啊，我想你也没说。”

“是啊，没说；你要是花冤枉钱把俺当傻子买下，那你这钱可是白瞎定了。她说，他们打下来的时候，她要家里所有的男人都躲起来，因为他们见男人就杀；可女人家，他们不会碰。”

“根本没那回事。她全都说拧了。那些北佬可是我们顶好的朋友。”

“俺就是这么想的。老女主人不过是要吓唬吓唬人罢了。她说完了，就坐下来抽抽搭搭地抹眼泪；俺那个高兴啊，都不知道该咋好了。可俺还只好憋着，装出一副难过得不得了的模样。金妮（Jinny）就说：‘哦，南希小姐，俺盼着他们可别上这儿来。’她答：‘我就是怕他们打将下来，把能抓到手的样样东西都吞个精光。’她那副模样，瞧着心都快碎啦，说完就进屋去了。她一走，俺们可就撒开了欢。杰克（Jake）一个劲儿翻跟头，还说他才不怕那些北佬呢；他晓得自个儿的面包哪一面抹着黄油。那个杰克呀，是个顶机灵的活宝。他下楼去取信的时候，什么花活儿、什么把戏都耍得出来。瞧他在那儿疯玩疯闹——人们都在等各自的信件，议论着打仗的事——你准以为那孩子连一顶针尖的脑子都没有。可杰克呢，眼睛睁得溜圆、耳朵支棱着，时时刻刻都在听，能捞到的每一样都捞到手，他的好些消息就是这么到手的。至于金妮，她在地板上蹦啊跳啊，满屋子疯转。俺只好用手捂住她的嘴，别让老女主人听见她。哦，俺们那一顿快活，真没法说。孩子，你真该在场瞧瞧的。”


===Original===
“And, Aunt Linda, what did you do?”

“Oh, honey, I war jis’ ready to crack my sides larffin, jis’ to see what a long face Jinny puts on wen ole Miss is talkin’, an’ den to see dat face wen missus’ back is turned, why it’s good as a circus. It’s nuff to make a horse larff.”

“Why, Aunt Linda, you never saw a circus?”

“No, but I’se hearn tell ob dem, and I thinks dey mus’ be mighty funny. An’ I know it’s orful funny to see how straight Jinny’s face looks wen she’s almos’ ready to bust, while ole Miss is frettin’ and fumin’ ’bout dem Yankees an’ de war. But, somehow, Robby, I ralely b’lieves dat we cullud folks is mixed up in dis fight. I seed it all in a vision. An’ soon as dey fired on dat fort, Uncle Dan’el says to me: ‘Linda, we’s gwine to git our freedom.’ An’ I says: ‘Wat makes you think so?’ An’ he says: ‘Dey’ve fired on Fort Sumter, an’ de Norf is boun’ to whip.’ ”

===Chinese===
“那么，琳达大婶，您又做了什么呢？”

“哦，乖孩子，俺简直要笑破肚皮喽——光是瞧金妮在老女主人说话时装出的那副长脸，再瞧女主人一转过身去她那副模样，嗐，比马戏还好看。连马见了都要发笑。”

“咦，琳达大婶，您从来没看过马戏？”

“没看过，可俺听人说起过，俺琢磨着，那一定乐得很。俺就知道一样顶乐的：瞧金妮把脸绷得直挺挺的——她憋得都快撑不住啦，老女主人还在那儿为北佬呀、打仗呀又急又躁。不过说也奇怪，罗比，俺真心觉得，俺们有色人种也牵连在这场仗里头。俺在一幅异象里全看见啦。他们朝那座炮台一开炮，丹尼尔大叔（Uncle Dan’el）就对俺说：‘琳达，俺们就要得自由啦。’俺问：‘你咋这样想？’他说：‘他们炮打了萨姆特堡（Fort Sumter），北方是一定要打赢的。’”


===Original===
“I hope so,” said Robert. “I think that we have a heap of friends up there.”

“Well, I’m jis’ gwine to keep on prayin’ an’ b’lievin’.”

Just then the bell rang, and Robert, answering, found Mrs. Johnson suffering from a severe headache, which he thought was occasioned by her worrying over the late defeat of the Confederates. She sent him on an errand, which he executed with his usual dispatch, and returned to some work which he had to do in the kitchen. Robert was quite a favorite with Aunt Linda, and they often had confidential chats together.

===Chinese===
“我希望如此，”罗伯特说。“我想，我们在北方有一大帮朋友呢。”

“那俺就只管接着祷告、接着信。”

正在这时，铃响了。罗伯特应铃过去，见约翰逊太太（Mrs. Johnson）正犯着剧烈的头痛；他心想，这头痛多半是她近日为叛军的战败忧心忡忡，才勾起来的。她差他去办一桩事，他以一贯的利落办妥了，便回到厨房，去干他手头还有的活计。罗伯特很得琳达大婶的欢心，两人常常凑在一处说体己话。


===Original===
“Bobby,” she said, when he returned, “I thinks we ort ter hab a prayer-meetin’ putty soon.”

“I am in for that. Where will you have it?”

“Lem me see. Las’ Sunday we had it in Gibson’s woods; Sunday ’fore las’, in de old cypress swamp; an’ nex’ Sunday we’el hab one in McCullough’s woods. Las’ Sunday we had a good time. I war jis’ chock full an’ runnin’ ober. Aunt Milly’s daughter’s bin monin all summer, an’ she’s jis’ come throo. We had a powerful time. Eberythin’ on dat groun’ was jis’ alive. I tell yer, dere was a shout in de camp.”

===Chinese===
“博比，”他回来的时候，她说道，“俺琢磨着，俺们该趁早办一场祈祷会了。”

“我赞成。打算在哪儿办呢？”

“让俺想想。上个礼拜天，俺们是在吉布森树林（Gibson’s woods）开的；再上个礼拜天，是在老柏树沼泽；下个礼拜天，俺们要在麦卡洛克树林（McCullough’s woods）开一场。上个礼拜天那一场可真好。俺只觉得心里满满当当，直往外溢。米莉大婶（Aunt Milly）的闺女哀恸认罪了一整个夏天，如今总算渡过来啦。那一场大有能力，那块地上样样都是活的。俺告诉你们，营盘里响起了一片欢呼。”


===Original===
“Well, you had better look out, and not shout too much, and pray and sing too loud, because, ’fore you know, the patrollers will be on your track and break up your meetin’ in a mighty big hurry, before you can say ‘Jack Robinson.’ ”

“Oh, we looks out for dat. We’s got a nice big pot, dat got cracked las’ winter, but it will hole a lot o’ water, an’ we puts it whar we can tell it eberything. We has our own good times. An’ I want you to come Sunday night an’ tell all ’bout the good eggs, fish, and butter. Mark my words, Bobby, we’s all gwine to git free. I seed it all in a vision, as plain as de nose on yer face.”

“Well, I hope your vision will come out all right, and that the eggs will keep and the butter be fresh till we have our next meetin’.”

“Now, Bob, you sen’ word to Uncle Dan’el, Tom Anderson, an’ de rest ob dem, to come to McCullough’s woods nex’ Sunday night. I want to hab a sin-killin’ an’ debil-dribin’ time. But, boy, you’d better git out er yere. Ole Miss’ll be down on yer like a scratch cat.”

===Chinese===
“唉，你们可得留点儿神，欢呼别太欢，祷告唱诗也别太响；不然说不上什么时候，巡逻队就循声追来，还没等你喊出一声‘杰克·罗宾逊’，就把你们的聚会冲个七零八落。”

“哦，那个俺们自有防备。俺们有一口好好儿的大锅，去年冬天裂了道缝，可还盛得住好些水呢；俺们把它搁在一个地方，在那儿能跟它说上体己话。俺们自有俺们的快活日子。俺还要你礼拜天夜里来，把那些好鸡蛋、好鱼、好黄油的事儿，全都说给俺们听听。记住俺的话，博比，俺们大伙儿全都要得自由啦。俺在一幅异象里看得真真儿的，就像看见你脸上长的鼻子一样分明。”

“好啊，我但愿你的异象有个圆满的结果，也盼着这些鸡蛋搁得住、黄油保得新鲜，一直新鲜到我们下次聚会。”

“还有，鲍勃，你捎信给丹尼尔大叔、汤姆·安德森，还有其余那几位，叫他们下个礼拜天夜里到麦卡洛克树林来。俺要办一场消罪孽、赶魔鬼的大会。不过，孩子，你顶好快些离开这儿。老女主人发起威来，可要像只抓人的野猫一样朝你扑下来啦。”


===Original===
Although the slaves were denied unrestricted travel, and the holding of meetings without the surveillance of a white man, yet they contrived to meet by stealth and hold gatherings where they could mingle their prayers and tears, and lay plans for escaping to the Union army. Outwitting the vigilance of the patrollers and home guards, they established these meetings miles apart, extending into several States.

Sometimes their hope of deliverance was cruelly blighted by hearing of some adventurous soul who, having escaped to the Union army, had been pursued and returned again to bondage. Yet hope survived all these disasters which gathered around the fate of their unfortunate brethren, who were remanded to slavery through the undiscerning folly of those who were strengthening the hands which were dealing their deadliest blows at the heart of the Nation. But slavery had cast such a glamour over the Nation, and so warped the consciences of men, that they failed to read aright the legible transcript of Divine retribution which was written upon the shuddering earth, where the blood of God’s poor children had been as water freely spilled.

===Chinese===
奴隶们既不被准许随意走动，也不被准许在没有一个白人监视的情况下聚会，但他们还是想方设法偷偷相会，聚起一场一场的集会——在那里，他们可以把祷告和眼泪汇在一处，商定投奔联邦军的计策。他们瞒过巡逻队和地方民团的耳目，把这些聚会一处一处办了起来，彼此相隔好几英里，一直延展到好几个州。

有时，他们得救的希望也会被残酷地摧折——听说某个胆大的魂灵逃到了联邦军那边，却又被人追回，重新套上奴役的枷锁。然而，希望还是挺过了这一切灾殃。这些灾殃，团团围住他们不幸同胞的命运——那些同胞被发还为奴，只因一些人识不真切的愚妄；而正是这些愚妄之人，还在给那双正对着国家心脏连连施以致命重击的手增添气力。可是，奴隶制给整个国家罩下了一重迷障，把世人的良心都扭歪了，以致他们读不真切那篇写在这战栗大地上的文告——一篇字迹分明的文告，出自上帝报应之手；在那块土地上，上帝贫苦儿女的血，曾被当作清水一般随意泼洒。

## XXIV Northern Experience / 第二十四章 北方的经历

===Original===
“Uncle Robert,” said Iola, after she had been North several weeks, “I have a theory that every woman ought to know how to earn her own living. I believe that a great amount of sin and misery springs from the weakness and inefficiency of women.”

“Perhaps that’s so, but what are you going to do about it?”

“I am going to join the great rank of breadwinners. Mr. Waterman has advertised for a number of saleswomen, and I intend to make application.”

“When he advertises for help he means white women,” said Robert.

“He said nothing about color,” responded Iola.

“I don’t suppose he did. He doesn’t expect any colored girl to apply.”

===Chinese===
“罗伯特舅舅，”伊奥拉来到北方几个星期后说，“我有一个看法：凡是女人，都应当学会自己谋生。我相信，世间许许多多的罪孽与苦难，都是从女人的软弱和无能里生出来的。”

“这话兴许不假，可你打算怎么办呢？”

“我打算加入浩浩荡荡的谋生者行列。沃特曼先生（Mr. Waterman）登了广告，要招几名女售货员，我准备去应征。”

“他登广告招人，招的是白人女子，”罗伯特说。

“他可没提肤色，”伊奥拉答道。

“我想他是没提。他压根儿就没料到会有有色人种姑娘去应征。”

===Original===
“Well, I think I could fill the place. At least I should like to try. And I do not think when I apply that I am in duty bound to tell him my great-grandmother was a negro.”

“Well, child, there is no necessity for you to go out to work. You are perfectly welcome here, and I hope that you feel so.”

“Oh, I certainly do. But still I would rather earn my own living.”

That morning Iola applied for the situation, and, being prepossessing in her appearance, she obtained it.

===Chinese===
“可我想，这个位子我胜任得了。至少我愿意试一试。而且我去应征的时候，并不觉得自己有义务告诉他，我的曾外祖母是个黑人。”

“唉，孩子，你用不着出去做工。你在这儿住着，再受欢迎不过了，但愿你自己也有这个感觉。”

“哦，我当然有。可我还是宁愿自己挣钱过活。”

当天上午，伊奥拉就去应征这份差事；她仪态惹人好感，当场便被录用了。

===Original===
For awhile everything went as pleasantly as a marriage bell. But one day a young colored lady, well-dressed and well-bred in her manner, entered the store. It was an acquaintance which Iola had formed in the colored church which she attended. Iola gave her a few words of cordial greeting, and spent a few moments chatting with her. The attention of the girls who sold at the same counter was attracted, and their suspicion awakened. Iola was a stranger in that city. Who was she, and who were her people? At last it was decided that one of the girls should act as a spy, and bring what information she could concerning Iola.

The spy was successful. She found out that Iola was living in a good neighborhood, but that none of the neighbors knew her. The man of the house was very fair, but there was an old woman whom Iola called “Grandma,” and she was unmistakably colored. The story was sufficient. If that were true, Iola must be colored, and she should be treated accordingly.

Without knowing the cause, Iola noticed a chill in the social atmosphere of the store, which communicated itself to the cash-boys, and they treated her so insolently that her situation became very uncomfortable. She saw the proprietor, resigned her position, and asked for and obtained a letter of recommendation to another merchant who had advertised for a saleswoman.

In applying for the place, she took the precaution to inform her employer that she was colored. It made no difference to him; but he said:﻿—

===Chinese===
起初一段时间，事事都顺遂得像婚礼上的钟声一样悦耳。可是有一天，一位年轻的有色人种女士走进店来，她衣着讲究，举止娴雅。这是伊奥拉在自己常去做礼拜的那家有色人种教堂里结识的熟人。伊奥拉向她热诚地问了好，又同她攀谈了片刻。同一柜台卖货的姑娘们注意到了这一幕，疑心也随之被勾了起来。伊奥拉在这座城里是个陌生面孔。她是谁？她家里又是什么人？末了，姑娘们商定推出一个人来充当暗探，去搜罗她所能打听到的一切有关伊奥拉的消息。

暗探大功告成。她打听到伊奥拉住在一处体面的街区，可左邻右舍没有一个认识她。那家的男主人肤色很白净，家里却有位老太太，伊奥拉唤她“外祖母”，一望便知是有色人种。有这样的底细就够了。倘若属实，伊奥拉必定是有色人种，就该按有色人种的份儿来对待她。

不知就里的伊奥拉，只觉得店里的社交气氛透出一股寒意，这寒意还传给了那些跑账的小伙计，他们对她的态度放肆无礼，弄得她的处境十分难堪。她求见店主，辞去了职位，又讨得一封推荐信，转荐给另一位登广告招女售货员的商人。

去应征那份差事时，她先存了个心眼，向雇主言明自己是有色人种。雇主倒不以为意，只是说道：

===Original===
“Don’t say anything about it to the girls. They might not be willing to work with you.”

Iola smiled, did not promise, and accepted the situation. She entered upon her duties, and proved quite acceptable as a saleswoman.

One day, during an interval in business, the girls began to talk of their respective churches, and the question was put to Iola:﻿—

===Chinese===
“这事跟姑娘们一个字也别提。她们兴许不愿意和你共事。”

伊奥拉微微一笑，没有应承，把这份差事接受了下来。她走马上任，做起售货来颇为称职。

一天，趁生意清闲的当口，姑娘们谈起各自的教堂来，有人便问伊奥拉：

===Original===
“Where do you go to church?”

“I go,” she replied, “to Rev. River’s church, corner of Eighth and L Streets.”

“Oh, no; you must be mistaken. There is no church there except a colored one.”

“That is where I go.”

“Why do you go there?”

===Chinese===
“你上哪家教堂做礼拜？”

“我上的是，”她答道，“里弗牧师（Rev. River）的教堂，在第八街和 L 街的街角。”

“哦，不对；你准是弄错了。那儿除了有色人种教堂，再没有别的教堂。”

“我上的正是那一家。”

“你为什么要上那儿去？”

===Original===
“Because I liked it when I came here, and joined it.”

“A member of a colored church? What under heaven possessed you to do such a thing?”

“Because I wished to be with my own people.”

Here the interrogator stopped, and looked surprised and pained, and almost instinctively moved a little farther from her. After the store was closed, the girls had an animated discussion, which resulted in the information being sent to Mr. Cohen that Iola was a colored girl, and that they protested against her being continued in his employ. Mr. Cohen yielded to the pressure, and informed Iola that her services were no longer needed.

===Chinese===
“因为我初来这儿的时候就喜欢它，便入了会。”

“有色人种教堂的教友？你到底是着了什么魔，竟做出这样的事来？”

“因为我要同自己的同胞待在一起。”

问话的人到此便收住了口，脸上又是惊愕又是难过，几乎出于本能地往旁边挪了挪，离她远了些。店铺打烊之后，姑娘们展开了一场激烈的议论，结果是给科恩先生（Mr. Cohen）递了话，说伊奥拉是个有色人种姑娘，她们抗议店主人继续雇用她。科恩先生顶不住这股压力，便通知伊奥拉，店里不再需要她了。

===Original===
When Robert came home in the evening, he found that Iola had lost her situation, and was looking somewhat discouraged.

“Well, uncle,” she said, “I feel out of heart. It seems as if the prejudice pursues us through every avenue of life, and assigns us the lowest places.”

“That is so,” replied Robert, thoughtfully.

===Chinese===
傍晚罗伯特回到家，见伊奥拉丢了差事，神色有些沮丧。

“唉，舅舅，”她说，“我心里怪灰心的。这偏见仿佛追逼着我们穿过人生的每一条通路，偏把最低贱的位置分派给我们。”

“的确是这样，”罗伯特若有所思地答道。

===Original===
“And yet I am determined,” said Iola, “to win for myself a place in the fields of labor. I have heard of a place in New England, and I mean to try for it, even if I only stay a few months.”

“Well, if you *will* go, say nothing about your color.”

“Uncle Robert, I see no necessity for proclaiming that fact on the housetop. Yet I am resolved that nothing shall tempt me to deny it. The best blood in my veins is African blood, and I am not ashamed of it.”

“Hurrah for you!” exclaimed Robert, laughing heartily.

===Chinese===
“可我还是拿定了主意，”伊奥拉说，“要在劳动的天地里为自己挣得一席之地。我听说新英格兰有个空缺，我打算去试试，哪怕只待上几个月也好。”

“唉，你既然非去不可，就对你的肤色只字不提。”

“罗伯特舅舅，我看没有必要把这件事嚷嚷到房顶上去。可我也拿定主意，无论什么都休想诱我否认它。我血管里最高贵的血，就是非洲血，我并不以此为耻。”

“好哇，为你叫好！”罗伯特开怀大笑，欢声嚷道。

===Original===
As Iola wished to try the world for herself, and so be prepared for any emergency, her uncle and grandmother were content to have her go to New England. The town to which she journeyed was only a few hours’ ride from the city of P﻿—﻿—, and Robert, knowing that there is no teacher like experience, was willing that Iola should have the benefit of her teaching.

Iola, on arriving in H﻿—﻿—, sought the firm, and was informed that her services were needed. She found it a pleasant and lucrative position. There was only one drawback﻿—her boarding place was too far from her work. There was an institution conducted by professed Christian women, which was for the special use of respectable young working girls. This was in such a desirable location that she called at the house to engage board.

The matron conducted her over the house, and grew so friendly in the interview that she put her arm around her, and seemed to look upon Iola as a desirable accession to the home. But, just as Iola was leaving, she said to the matron: “I must be honest with you; I am a colored woman.”

Swift as light a change passed over the face of the matron. She withdrew her arm from Iola, and said: “I must see the board of managers about it.”

===Chinese===
伊奥拉既然想亲自到世面上闯一闯，好为种种不测做准备，舅舅和外祖母也就乐意让她去新英格兰。她此去的那座小镇，离 P—— 城不过几个钟头的车程；罗伯特深知天下没有比阅历更好的老师，乐得让伊奥拉去受一受它的教益。

伊奥拉到了 H—— 镇，寻到那家商行，得到的答复是正需要她这样的人才。她发觉这份差事既称心又进项可观，只有一样美中不足——她的寄宿处离干活的地方太远。镇上有一家机构，由几位以基督徒自命的妇女经办，专供体面的年轻职业女子寄宿。那地方地段再理想不过，她便登门去接洽膳宿。

女舍监领着她把整座房子看了一遍；接谈之间愈发亲热，竟伸手搂住了她，看那样子，简直把伊奥拉当作这家寓所求之不得的新住客。可就在伊奥拉告辞之际，她对女舍监说：“我得对您说实话；我是个有色人种女子。”

快得像光一样，女舍监的脸色倏地变了。她把胳膊从伊奥拉身上抽了回来，说道：“这件事，我得去见一见管理委员会。”

===Original===
When the board met, Iola’s case was put before them, but they decided not to receive her. And these women, professors of a religion which taught, “If ye have respect to persons ye commit sin,” virtually shut the door in her face because of the outcast blood in her veins.

Considerable feeling was aroused by the action of these women, who, to say the least, had not put their religion in the most favorable light.

Iola continued to work for the firm until she received letters from her mother and uncle, which informed her that her mother, having arranged her affairs in the South, was ready to come North. She then resolved to return, to the city of P﻿—﻿—, to be ready to welcome her mother on her arrival.

Iola arrived in time to see that everything was in order for her mother’s reception. Her room was furnished neatly, but with those touches of beauty that womanly hands are such adepts in giving. A few charming pictures adorned the walls, and an easy chair stood waiting to receive the travel-worn mother. Robert and Iola met her at the depot; and grandma was on her feet at the first sound of the bell, opened the door, clasped Marie to her heart, and nearly fainted for joy.

===Chinese===
管理委员会开会时，伊奥拉的申请被提到了她们面前，可她们决议不予接纳。这些妇女，信奉的明明是一门教导人“若按外貌待人，便是犯罪”的宗教，却因为她血管里流着那种遭人摈斥的血，实际上当面把门砰地关上了。

这几位妇女的行径激起了不小的义愤——往轻里说，她们这番作为，并没有让她们所信的宗教显得体面。

伊奥拉继续在那家商行做事，直到接到母亲和舅舅的来信，信上说母亲已把南方的事务料理停当，随时可以北来。她于是决意动身返回 P—— 城，好赶在母亲到埠时迎候她。

伊奥拉及时赶回，见迎接母亲的一切都安排得妥妥帖帖。母亲的房间陈设得洁净雅致，处处透着女子巧手最擅长添置的那几分秀气：墙上挂着几帧赏心悦目的图画，一把安乐椅立在一旁，专候那位旅途劳顿的母亲落座。罗伯特和伊奥拉到车站去接她；外祖母一听见铃声便霍地站起，抢步开了门，把玛丽紧紧搂在心口，欢喜得几乎晕了过去。

===Original===
“Can it be possible dat dis is my little Marie?” she exclaimed.

It did seem almost impossible to realize that this faded woman, with pale cheeks and prematurely whitened hair, was the rosy-cheeked child from whom she had been parted more than thirty years.

“Well,” said Robert, after the first joyous greeting was over, “love is a very good thing, but Marie has had a long journey and needs something that will stick by the ribs. How about dinner, mother?”

“It’s all ready,” said Mrs. Johnson.

===Chinese===
“真会是俺的小玛丽吗？”她叫了起来。

眼前这形容黯淡的妇人，两颊苍白，头发也过早地斑白；要说她就是三十多年前被生生拆散的那个双颊红润的孩子，实在叫人难以相信。

“唉，”头一阵欢喜的寒暄过后，罗伯特说道，“爱情固然是样好东西，可玛丽赶了这么远的路，得来点贴得住肋巴骨的东西才成。妈妈，晚饭弄得怎么样了？”

“都弄妥当啦，”约翰逊太太说。

===Original===
After Marie had gone to her room and changed her dress, she came down and partook of the delicious repast which her mother and Iola had prepared for her.

In a few days Marie was settled in the home, and was well pleased with the change. The only drawback to her happiness was the absence of her son, and she expected him to come North after the closing of his school.

“Uncle Robert,” said Iola, after her mother had been with them several weeks, “I am tired of being idle.”

===Chinese===
玛丽上楼回房换过衣裳，走下楼来，享用了母亲和伊奥拉为她备下的一席可口饭菜。

没过几天，玛丽便在新家里安顿下来，对这番变动十分称心。她美中不足的只有一桩：儿子不在身边。她盼着他那边的学校放假之后北上。

“罗伯特舅舅，”母亲同他们一起住了几个星期之后，伊奥拉说，“我闲得发慌了。”

===Original===
“What’s the matter now?” asked Robert. “You are surely not going East again, and leave your mother?”

“Oh, I hope not,” said Marie, anxiously. “I have been so long without you.”

“No, mamma, I am not going East. I can get suitable employment here in the city of P﻿—﻿—.”

===Chinese===
“这又是怎么了？”罗伯特问道，“你总不至于又要到东部去，撇下你母亲吧？”

“哦，但愿不会，”玛丽焦急地说，“没有你在身边，我已经熬得太久了。”

“不会的，妈妈，我不去东部。在这 P—— 城里，我就能找到合适的活计。”

===Original===
“But, Iola,” said Robert, “you have tried, and been defeated. Why subject yourself to the same experience again?”

“Uncle Robert, I think that every woman should have some skill or art which would insure her at least a comfortable support. I believe there would be less unhappy marriages if labor were more honored among women.”

“Well, Iola,” said her mother, “what is your skill?”

“Nursing. I was very young when I went into the hospital, but I succeeded so well that the doctor said I must have been a born nurse. Now, I see by the papers, that a gentleman who has an invalid daughter wants someone who can be a nurse and companion for her, and I mean to apply for the situation. I do not think, if I do my part well in that position, that the blood in my veins will be any bar to my success.”

===Chinese===
“可是，伊奥拉，”罗伯特说，“你已经试过一回，也败过一回了。何苦再让自己受一遍同样的经历呢？”

“罗伯特舅舅，我认为凡是女人，都该有一样技能或手艺，好保自己至少衣食无虞。我相信，倘若劳作在女人中间更受敬重，不美满的婚姻准会少得多。”

“那么，伊奥拉，”她母亲说，“你的一技之长是什么？”

“看护。我进医院的时候年纪还很轻，可我干得实在出色，连医生都说我准是天生的看护。如今我在报上看到，有位先生的女儿体弱多病，想寻一位既能充当看护、又能做伴的人，我打算去应征这份差事。我想，只要我在那个位置上尽好自己的本分，我血管里的血便不会成为我成功的障碍。”

===Original===
A troubled look stole over Marie’s face. She sighed faintly, but made no remonstrance. And so it was decided that Iola should apply for the situation.

Iola made application, and was readily accepted. Her patient was a frail girl of fifteen summers, who was ill with a low fever. Iola nursed her carefully, and soon had the satisfaction of seeing her restored to health. During her stay, Mr. Cloten, the father of the invalid, had learned some of the particulars of Iola’s Northern experience as a breadwinner, and he resolved to give her employment in his store when her services were no longer needed in the house. As soon as a vacancy occurred he gave Iola a place in his store.

The morning she entered on her work he called his employees together, and told them that Miss Iola had colored blood in her veins, but that he was going to employ her and give her a desk. If anyone objected to working with her, he or she could step to the cashier’s desk and receive what was due. Not a man remonstrated, not a woman demurred; and Iola at last found a place in the great army of breadwinners, which the traditions of her blood could not affect.

“How did you succeed?” asked Mrs. Cloten of her husband, when he returned to dinner.

===Chinese===
一丝忧色悄悄爬上玛丽的脸。她轻轻叹了口气，却没有出言劝阻。事情就这样定了：伊奥拉去应征那份差事。

伊奥拉递了申请，马上就被接受了。她的病人是个孱弱的姑娘，年方十五，正害着一场缠绵的低热。伊奥拉悉心看护，不久便欣慰地看到她康复了。寄住期间，病人父亲克洛滕先生听到了伊奥拉在北方谋生经历的一些细节，便打定主意，等家里不再需要她伺候时，就让她到自己店里做事。店里一有空缺，他便给伊奥拉安插了一个位置。

她上工那天早晨，他把雇员们召集到一起，告诉他们：伊奥拉小姐的血管里流着有色人种的血，但他打算雇用她，给她一个位子。谁若不愿意同她共事，不论男女，都可以到出纳台去结清应得的工钱。没有一个男子表示异议，没有一个女子支吾推托；伊奥拉终于在浩浩荡荡的谋生大军里得到了一个位置——一个血统的旧例再也撼动不了的位置。

“事情办得怎么样？”丈夫回家来吃午饭时，克洛滕太太（Mrs. Cloten）问道。

===Original===
“Admirably! ‘Everything is lovely and the goose hangs high.’ I gave my employees to understand that they could leave if they did not wish to work with Miss Leroy. Not one of them left, or showed any disposition to rebel.”

“I am very glad,” said Mrs. Cloten. “I am ashamed of the way she has been treated in our city, when seeking to do her share in the world’s work. I am glad that you were brave enough to face this cruel prejudice, and give her a situation.”

“Well, my dear, do not make me a hero for a single act. I am grateful for the care Miss Leroy gave our Daisy. Money can buy services, but it cannot purchase tender, loving sympathy. I was also determined to let my employees know that I, not they, commanded my business. So, do not crown me a hero until I have won a niche in the temple of fame. In dealing with Southern prejudice against the negro, we Northerners could do it with better grace if we divested ourselves of our own. We irritate the South by our criticisms, and, while I confess that there is much that is reprehensible in their treatment of colored people, yet if our Northern civilization is higher than theirs we should ‘criticise by creation.’ We should stamp ourselves on the South, and not let the South stamp itself on us. When we have learned to treat men according to the complexion of their souls, and not the color of their skins, we will have given our best contribution towards the solution of the negro problem.”

“I feel, my dear,” said Mrs. Cloten, “that what you have done is a right step in the right direction, and I hope that other merchants will do the same. We have numbers of business men, rich enough to afford themselves the luxury of a good conscience.”

===Chinese===
“好极了！‘样样都称心，连鹅都挂得老高。’我向雇员们言明，谁不愿意同勒罗伊小姐共事，尽可以走人。结果没有一个人走，也没有一个人露出半点想作对的意思。”

“我真高兴，”克洛滕太太说，“她想在这世上的劳作里尽自己的一份力，却在咱们城里受了那样的对待，我想起来就害臊。你有胆量直面这残酷的偏见，给她一个位置，我很欣慰。”

“唉，亲爱的，可别凭一桩事就把我捧成英雄。勒罗伊小姐看护过我们的黛西（Daisy），我感激她。金钱买得到差役服侍，却买不来那份温柔的、怀着爱意的体恤。我还存心要让雇员们明白：我的生意由我做主，不由他们做主。所以，等我哪天在名声的殿堂里赢得一席之地，你再给我戴上英雄的桂冠不迟。同南方人对黑人的偏见打交道，我们北方人倘若能先脱去自己身上那一份，办起来才会更光彩。我们的批评惹得南方人恼火；我承认，他们对待有色人种确有许多可诟病之处，可倘若我们北方的文明当真比他们的高，我们就该‘以创作来批评’。我们应当把我们自己的印记打在南方身上，而不是听任南方把它的印记打在我们身上。等到我们学会了按各人灵魂的色泽、而不是皮肤的颜色来待人，我们才算为黑人问题的解决献出了自己最好的一份力量。”

“我觉得，亲爱的，”克洛滕太太说，“你这件事是朝着正确的方向迈出的正确一步，但愿别的商家也照样去办。我们有的是商人，阔绰得足以让自己享受得起一副好良心这份奢侈品。”

## Note / 作者注

===Original===
From threads of fact and fiction I have woven a story whose mission will not be in vain if it awaken in the hearts of our countrymen a stronger sense of justice and a more Christlike humanity in behalf of those whom the fortunes of war threw, homeless, ignorant and poor, upon the threshold of a new era. Nor will it be in vain if it inspire the children of those upon whose brows God has poured the chrism of that new era to determine that they will embrace every opportunity, develop every faculty, and use every power God has given them to rise in the scale of character and condition, and to add their quota of good citizenship to the best welfare of the nation. There are scattered among us materials for mournful tragedies and mirth-provoking comedies, which some hand may yet bring into the literature of the country, glowing with the fervor of the tropics and enriched by the luxuriance of the Orient, and thus add to the solution of our unsolved American problem.

===Chinese===
我从事实与虚构的丝线之中，织成了这样一个故事。倘若它能在我们同胞的心中，唤起一份更强烈的正义感，和一种更近基督精神的仁爱，去关怀那些被战争的际遇抛到新时代门槛上的人们——他们无家可归、目不识丁、一贫如洗——那么，这个故事的使命便不算落空。倘若它能激励那些孩子们立定心志——他们的父辈，额上曾受上帝倾注新时代的圣膏——决意抓住每一个机会，发展每一种才能，运用上帝所赋予的每一分力量，在品格与境遇上步步上升，并把自己作为良好公民的一份力量，献于国家最大的福祉，那么，它也同样不曾白写。在我们中间，还散落着多少凄恻的悲剧与逗人开怀的喜剧的素材，或许将来有一天，某一只手会把它们写进这个国家的文学，让它们燃着热带的炽烈、染着东方的繁茂，从而为解决我们这个悬而未决的美国问题，添上一分力。

===Original===
The race has not had very long to straighten its hands from the hoe, to grasp the pen and wield it as a power for good, and to erect above the ruined auction-block and slave-pen institutions of learning, but

> There is light beyond the darkness,
 

 Joy beyond the present pain;
 

 There is hope in God’s great justice
 

 And the negro’s rising brain.
 

 Though the morning seems to linger
 

 O’er the hilltops far away,
 

 Yet the shadows bear the promise
 

 Of a brighter coming day.

===Chinese===
这个种族还不曾有太多的时光，把双手从锄柄上直起来，去握住笔杆、把它挥成一股向善的力量，并在坍毁的拍卖台和奴隶围栏之上，建起一座座学府；然而——

> 黑暗之外，自有光明，
 

 眼前的苦痛之外，自有欢欣；
 

 上帝伟大的公义中自有盼望，
 

 还有黑人那正在崛起的心智。
 

 纵然晨光仿佛迟迟
 

 才肯翻过远方的山巅，
 

 阴影里却已载着应许：
 

 一个更光明的日子就要到来。

## XXVI Open Questions / 第二十六章 悬而未决的问题

===Original===
In the evening Robert and Rev. Carmicle called on Dr. Gresham, and found Dr. Latrobe, the Southerner, and a young doctor by the name of Latimer, already there. Dr. Gresham introduced Dr. Latrobe, but it was a new experience to receive colored men socially. His wits, however, did not forsake him, and he received the introduction and survived it.

“Permit me, now,” said Dr. Gresham, “to introduce you to my friend, Dr. Latimer, who is attending our convention. He expects to go South and labor among the colored people. Don’t you think that there is a large field of usefulness before him?”

“Yes,” replied Dr. Latrobe, “if he will let politics alone.”

“And why let politics alone?” asked Dr. Gresham.

“Because,” replied Dr. Latrobe, “we Southerners will never submit to negro supremacy. We will never abandon our Caucasian civilization to an inferior race.”

“Have you any reason,” inquired Rev. Carmicle, “to dread that a race which has behind it the heathenism of Africa and the slavery of America, with its inheritance of ignorance and poverty, will be able, in less than one generation, to domineer over a race which has behind it ages of dominion, freedom, education, and Christianity?”

===Chinese===
当天晚上，罗伯特（Robert）和卡迈克牧师（Rev. Carmicle）到格雷沙姆医生（Dr. Gresham）处登门拜访，发现南方人拉特罗布医生（Dr. Latrobe）和一位名叫拉蒂默（Latimer）的年轻医生已经先在座中了。格雷沙姆医生为他们引见了拉特罗布医生；不过，在社交场合接待有色人种，这对拉特罗布医生来说还是头一遭。好在他机敏未失，受了这番引见，也安然撑了过去。

“现在请容我，”格雷沙姆医生说，“把我的朋友拉蒂默医生介绍给两位，他也来出席我们的大会。他打算到南方去，在有色人种中间效力。两位不觉得，他面前摆着一片大可有为的广阔天地吗？”

“是啊，”拉特罗布医生答道，“只要他不沾政治。”

“为什么不该沾政治呢？”格雷沙姆医生问。

“因为，”拉特罗布医生答道，“我们南方人决不会屈从于黑人至上，也决不会把我们高加索人（Caucasian）的文明，拱手丢弃给一个劣等种族。”

“您可有什么理由，”卡迈克牧师问道，“担心一个身后背着非洲的异教蒙昧与美国的奴隶制度、承袭着愚昧与贫穷的遗产的种族，竟能在不到一代人的时间里，凌驾于一个身后有着千百年的统治、自由、教育与基督教的种族之上？”

===Original===
A slight shade of vexation and astonishment passed over the face of Dr. Latrobe. He hesitated a moment, then replied:﻿—

“I am not afraid of the negro as he stands alone, but what I dread is that in some closely-contested election ambitious men will use him to hold the balance of power and make him an element of danger. He is ignorant, poor, and clannish, and they may impact him as their policy would direct.”

“Any more,” asked Robert, “than the leaders of the Rebellion did the ignorant, poor whites during our late conflict?”

“Ignorance, poverty, and clannishness,” said Dr. Gresham, “are more social than racial conditions, which may be outgrown.”

“And I think,” said Rev. Carmicle, “that we are outgrowing them as fast as any other people would have done under the same conditions.”

“The negro,” replied Dr. Latrobe, “always has been and always will be an element of discord in our country.”

===Chinese===
一丝恼怒和讶异的神色掠过拉特罗布医生的脸。他迟疑了片刻，答道：

“黑人单枪匹马，我倒不怕；我怕的是，在某场势均力敌的选举里，野心勃勃之徒会利用他来左右政局的均势，把他变成一种危险的因素。他无知、贫穷、又爱抱团，他们会照着自家政策的指点去驱使他。”

“难道这能比，”罗伯特问道，“叛乱的首领们在上次那场战祸里利用无知而贫穷的白人，利用得更甚吗？”

“无知、贫穷和爱抱团，”格雷沙姆医生说，“与其说是种族的境况，不如说是社会的境况，都是可以随着时代摆脱掉的。”

“而且我认为，”卡迈克牧师说，“我们摆脱这些境况的速度，不亚于任何别的民族处在同样境地时所能做到的。”

“黑人，”拉特罗布医生答道，“过去一直是、将来也永远是我国的一个不和的因素。”

===Original===
“What, then, is your remedy?” asked Dr. Gresham.

“I would eliminate him from the politics of the country.”

“As disfranchisement is a punishment for crime, is it just to punish a man before he transgresses the law?” asked Dr. Gresham.

“If,” said Dr. Latimer, “the negro is ignorant, poor, and clannish, let us remember that in part of our land it was once a crime to teach him to read. If he is poor, for ages he was forced to bend to unrequited toil. If he is clannish, society has segregated him to himself.”

“And even,” said Robert, “has given him a negro pew in your churches and a negro seat at your communion table.”

“Wisely, or unwisely,” said Dr. Gresham, “the Government has put the ballot in his hands. It is better to teach him to use that ballot aright than to intimidate him by violence or vitiate his vote by fraud.”

===Chinese===
“那么，你的对策是什么？”格雷沙姆医生问。

“我要把他从国家的政治中清除出去。”

“既然剥夺选举权（disfranchisement）是对罪行的惩罚，那么一个人尚未触犯法律就先惩办他，这公正吗？”格雷沙姆医生问。

“如果，”拉蒂默医生说，“黑人无知、贫穷、爱抱团，那就请记住：在我国的一部分土地上，教他读书识字曾经是一种罪行。如果他贫穷——多少个世代，他被迫俯身于无偿的苦役。如果他爱抱团——那是社会把他隔离，把他抛回了他自己的族类中间。”

“而且社会甚至还，”罗伯特说，“在你们的教堂里给他划出黑人专席，在你们的圣餐桌旁给他摆了黑人坐席。”

“明智也罢，不明智也罢，”格雷沙姆医生说，“联邦政府已经把选票放到了他手里。与其用暴力恫吓他、用欺诈作废他的选票，不如教会他正当地使用那张选票。”

===Original===
“Today,” said Dr. Latimer, “the negro is not plotting in beer-saloons against the peace and order of society. His fingers are not dripping with dynamite, neither is he spitting upon your flag, nor flaunting the red banner of anarchy in your face.”

“Power,” said Dr. Gresham, “naturally gravitates into the strongest hands. The class who have the best brain and most wealth can strike with the heaviest hand. I have too much faith in the inherent power of the white race to dread the competition of any other people under heaven.”

“I think you Northerners fail to do us justice,” said Dr. Latrobe. “The men into whose hands you put the ballot were our slaves, and we would rather die than submit to them. Look at the carpetbag governments the wicked policy of the Government inflicted upon us. It was only done to humiliate us.”

“Oh, no!” said Dr. Gresham, flushing, and rising to his feet. “We had no other alternative than putting the ballot in their hands.”

“I will not deny,” said Rev. Carmicle, “that we have made woeful mistakes, but with our antecedents it would have been miraculous if we had never committed any mistakes or made any blunders.”

“They were allies in war,” continued Dr. Gresham, “and I am sorry that we have not done more to protect them in peace.”

===Chinese===
“今天，”拉蒂默医生说，“黑人并没有在啤酒馆里密谋危害社会的安宁与秩序。他的手指上没有滴着炸药，他没有朝你们的国旗吐唾沫，也没有把无政府主义的红旗挥舞到你们脸上。”

“权力，”格雷沙姆医生说，“自然而然要落进最强有力的手里。头脑最好、财富最多的阶级，出手才能够最重。我对白人种族的内在力量有足够的信心，普天之下无论哪个民族同它竞争，我都不害怕。”

“我认为你们北方人没有公平对待我们，”拉特罗布医生说。“你们把选票交到手里的那些人，从前是我们的奴隶，我们宁死也不肯屈服于他们。看看联邦政府那套恶毒政策强加给我们的提包客政府（carpetbag governments，指战后携提包南下、投机把持南方州政权的北方政客主导的政府）吧。那么做，无非是要羞辱我们。”

“哦，不！”格雷沙姆医生涨红了脸，站起身来。“我们当时别无选择，只有把选票放到他们手里。”

“我不否认，”卡迈克牧师说，“我们犯过可悲的错误；可是以我们以往所处的境遇，倘若从未犯过任何过错、出过任何纰漏，那才真是奇迹呢。”

“他们是战争中的盟友，”格雷沙姆医生接着说，“我很遗憾，在和平岁月里，我们没有为保护他们多做一些。”

===Original===
“Protect them in peace!” said Robert, bitterly. “What protection does the colored man receive from the hands of the Government? I know of no civilized country outside of America where men are still burned for real or supposed crimes.”

“Johnson,” said Dr. Gresham, compassionately, “it is impossible to have a policeman at the back of each colored man’s chair, and a squad of soldiers at each crossroad, to detect with certainty, and punish with celerity, each invasion of his rights. We tried provisional governments and found them a failure. It seemed like leaving our former allies to be mocked with the name of freedom and tortured with the essence of slavery. The ballot is our weapon of defense, and we gave it to them for theirs.”

“And there,” said Dr. Latrobe, emphatically, “is where you signally failed. We are numerically stronger in Congress today than when we went out. You made the law, but the administration of it is in our hands, and we are a unit.”

“But, Doctor,” said Rev. Carmicle, “you cannot willfully deprive the negro of a single right as a citizen without sending demoralization through your own ranks.”

“I think,” said Dr. Latrobe, “that we are right in suppressing the negro’s vote. This is a white man’s government, and a white man’s country. We own nineteen-twentieths of the land, and have about the same ratio of intelligence. I am a white man, and, right or wrong, I go with my race.”

===Chinese===
“在和平岁月里保护他们！”罗伯特辛酸地说。“有色人种从政府手里得到过什么保护？除了美国，我想不出还有哪一个文明国家，人们依然会为了真实的或莫须有的罪名被活活烧死。”

“约翰逊，”格雷沙姆医生满怀怜惜地说，“总不可能在每个有色人种的座椅背后都站上一名警察，在每个十字路口都驻上一小队士兵，好把每一次侵犯他权利的行为都查个确实、惩个迅速。我们试行过临时政府，发现那是失败的。那情形就像是撇下我们从前的盟友，任他们顶着自由的名分受人嘲弄，被奴隶制的精髓所煎熬。选票是我们自卫的武器，我们也把它给了他们，作为他们自卫的武器。”

“而正是在这一点上，”拉特罗布医生加重了语气说，“你们一败涂地。今天我们在国会里的人数，比我们退出联邦的时候还要多。法律是你们立的，法律的施行却握在我们手里，而且我们上下一心。”

“可是，医生，”卡迈克牧师说，“你们若存心剥夺黑人作为公民的哪怕一项权利，就不能不在你们自己的队伍里散下道德败坏的种子。”

“我认为，”拉特罗布医生说，“我们压制黑人的选票是对的。这是一个白人的政府，一个白人的国家。我们拥有这片土地的十分之十九，智力所占的比例也大致相仿。我是个白人，不管对错，我都跟着我的种族走。”

===Original===
“But, Doctor,” said Rev. Carmicle, “there are rights more sacred than the rights of property and superior intelligence.”

“What are they?” asked Dr. Latrobe.

“The rights of life and liberty,” replied Rev. Carmicle.

“That is true,” said Dr. Gresham; “and your Southern civilization will be inferior until you shall have placed protection to those rights at its base, not in theory but in fact.”

“But, Dr. Gresham, we have to live with these people, and the North is constantly irritating us by its criticisms.”

“The world,” said Dr. Gresham, “is fast becoming a vast whispering gallery, and lips once sealed can now state their own grievances and appeal to the conscience of the nation, and, as long as a sense of justice and mercy retains a hold upon the heart of our nation, you cannot practice violence and injustice without rousing a spirit of remonstrance. And if it were not so I would be ashamed of my country and of my race.”

===Chinese===
“可是，医生，”卡迈克牧师说，“还有一些权利，比财产的权利和智识优越的权利更为神圣。”

“是什么权利？”拉特罗布医生问。

“生命与自由的权利，”卡迈克牧师答道。

“这话不错，”格雷沙姆医生说，“你们的南方文明，一天不把对这些权利的保护置于它的根基——不是在理论上，而是在事实上——就一天还是低人一等。”

“可是，格雷沙姆医生，我们总得同这些人一起过日子，而北方又不住口地指摘，时时激怒我们。”

“这个世界，”格雷沙姆医生说，“正在迅速变成一座浩大的回音长廊（whispering gallery）；从前被封缄的嘴唇，如今尽可以陈说自己的冤屈，向国民的良知发出呼吁。只要正义感和仁慈之心还握得住我国国民的心头，你们就不能施行暴力与不义，而不激起一片抗议的精神。倘若不是这样，我就要为我的国家、为我的种族感到羞耻了。”

===Original===
“You speak,” said Dr. Latrobe, “as if we had wronged the negro by enslaving him and being unwilling to share citizenship with him. I think that slavery has been of incalculable value to the negro. It has lifted him out of barbarism and fetish worship, given him a language of civilization, and introduced him to the world’s best religion. Think what he was in Africa and what he is in America!”

“The negro,” said Dr. Gresham, thoughtfully, “is not the only branch of the human race which has been low down in the scale of civilization and freedom, and which has outgrown the measure of his chains. Slavery, polygamy, and human sacrifices have been practiced among Europeans in bygone days; and when Tyndall tells us that out of savages unable to count to the number of their fingers and speaking only a language of nouns and verbs, arise at length our Newtons and Shakespeares, I do not see that the negro could not have learned our language and received our religion without the intervention of ages of slavery.”

“If,” said Rev. Carmicle, “Mohammedanism, with its imperfect creed, is successful in gathering large numbers of negroes beneath the Crescent, could not a legitimate commerce and the teachings of a pure Christianity have done as much to plant the standard of the Cross over the ramparts of sin and idolatry in Africa? Surely we cannot concede that the light of the Crescent is greater than the glory of the Cross, that there is less constraining power in the Christ of Calvary than in the Prophet of Arabia? I do not think that I underrate the difficulties in your way when I say that you young men are holding in your hands golden opportunities which it would be madness and folly to throw away. It is your grand opportunity to help build up a new South, not on the shifting sands of policy and expediency, but on the broad basis of equal justice and universal freedom. Do this and you will be blessed, and will make your life a blessing.”

===Chinese===
“你说话的口气，”拉特罗布医生说，“倒好像我们蓄他为奴、又不愿同他共享公民身份，是亏待了黑人似的。依我看，奴隶制对黑人有着不可估量的价值。它把他从野蛮状态和拜物崇拜里提升出来，给了他一种文明的语言，还把他引荐给世界上最好的宗教。想想他在非洲是什么样，在美国又是什么样吧！”

“黑人，”格雷沙姆医生若有所思地说，“并不是人类当中唯一一支曾在文明与自由的阶梯上居于低位、而又挣脱了自身枷锁的族裔。在往昔的岁月里，欧洲人也曾实行奴隶制、多妻制和人祭；当廷德尔（Tyndall）告诉我们，从那些数不清自己手指头的数目、只会说一种仅由名词和动词构成的语言的野人当中，最终崛起了我们的牛顿和莎士比亚，我看不出有什么理由认为，黑人非要经过多少世纪的奴隶制居间干预，才能学会我们的语言、接受我们的宗教。”

“如果，”卡迈克牧师说，“伊斯兰教（Mohammedanism）凭着它残缺的信条，尚且能成功地把大批黑人收归到新月旗下（新月系伊斯兰教的象征），那么正当的商贸和纯正基督教的教诲，难道不能同样把十字架的旌旗，插上非洲的罪恶与偶像崇拜的堡垒吗？我们当然不能承认，新月的亮光大过十字架的荣耀，各各他（Calvary）的基督感召之力，反不如阿拉伯的先知。当我说你们这些年轻人手里正握着黄金般的机遇、抛开它们便是疯狂与愚蠢时，我想我并没有低估你们道路上的重重困难。帮助建设一个新南方——不建立在政策与权宜的流沙之上，而建立在平等正义与普遍自由的广阔根基之上——这正是你们宏大的机遇。这样做了，你们必蒙福佑，也必使你们的人生成为他人的福佑。”

===Original===
After Robert and Rev. Carmicle had left the hotel, Drs. Latimer, Gresham, and Latrobe sat silent and thoughtful awhile, when Dr. Gresham broke the silence by asking Dr. Latrobe how he had enjoyed the evening.

“Very pleasantly,” he replied. “I was quite interested in that parson. Where was he educated?”

“In Oxford, I believe. I was pleased to hear him say that he had no white blood in his veins.”

“I should think not,” replied Dr. Latrobe, “from his looks. But one swallow does not make a summer. It is the exceptions which prove the rule.”

“Don’t you think,” asked Dr. Gresham, “that we have been too hasty in our judgment of the negro? He has come handicapped into life, and is now on trial before the world. But it is not fair to subject him to the same tests that you would a white man. I believe that there are possibilities of growth in the race which we have never comprehended.”

===Chinese===
罗伯特和卡迈克牧师离开旅馆之后，拉蒂默、格雷沙姆、拉特罗布三位医生默默坐了片刻，各自出神；随后格雷沙姆医生打破了沉默，问拉特罗布医生今晚过得可尽兴。

“非常愉快，”他答道。“我对那位牧师很感兴趣。他是在哪儿受的教育？”

“我想是在牛津（Oxford）吧。听他说自己血管里没有白人的血，我很高兴。”

“看他那副模样，我也这么想，”拉特罗布医生答道。“不过，一燕不成夏。恰恰是例外证实了规律。”

“你不觉得，”格雷沙姆医生问道，“我们对黑人的评判太过性急了吗？他负着重累走进人生，如今正在世人面前受审。可是，拿你考验一个白人的那些测试去考验他，是不公平的。我相信，这个种族身上有着我们从来不曾领会过的成长潜力。”

===Original===
“The negro,” said Dr. Latrobe, “is perfectly comprehensible to me. The only way to get along with him is to let him know his place, and make him keep it.”

“I think,” replied Dr. Gresham, “every man’s place is the one he is best fitted for.”

“Why,” asked Dr. Latimer, “should any place be assigned to the negro more than to the French, Irish, or German?”

“Oh,” replied Dr. Latrobe, “they are all Caucasians.”

“Well,” said Dr. Gresham, “is all excellence summed up in that branch of the human race?”

===Chinese===
“黑人嘛，”拉特罗布医生说，“我看得一清二楚。同他相处的唯一法子，就是让他知道自己的位置，并且逼他守稳那个位置。”

“我看，”格雷沙姆医生答道，“每个人应有的位置，就是他最胜任的位置。”

“为什么，”拉蒂默医生问道，“偏要给黑人指定一个位置，却不给法国人、爱尔兰人或德国人指定呢？”

“哦，”拉特罗布医生答道，“他们都是高加索人。”

“那么，”格雷沙姆医生说，“人类的一切优异，就都汇总在人类的那一个支系身上了吗？”

===Original===
“I think,” said Dr. Latrobe, proudly, “that we belong to the highest race on earth and the negro to the lowest.”

“And yet,” said Dr. Latimer, “you have consorted with them till you have bleached their faces to the whiteness of your own. Your children nestle in their bosoms; they are around you as body servants, and yet if one of them should attempt to associate with you your bitterest scorn and indignation would be visited upon them.”

“I think,” said Dr. Latrobe, “that feeling grows out of our Anglo-Saxon regard for the marriage relation. These white negroes are of illegitimate origin, and we would scorn to share our social life with them. Their blood is tainted.”

“Who tainted it?” asked Dr. Latimer, bitterly. “You give absolution to the fathers, and visit the misfortunes of the mothers upon the children.”

“But, Doctor, what kind of society would we have if we put down the bars and admitted everybody to social equality?”

===Chinese===
“我认为，”拉特罗布医生傲然说，“我们属于世上最高等的种族，黑人则属于最低等的。”

“然而，”拉蒂默医生说，“你们同他们厮混得那样久，把他们的脸蛋都厮混得同你们自己的一样白了。你们的儿女偎在他们怀里；他们作为贴身仆役围绕在你们身边；可他们当中若有人试图同你们交往，你们最刻毒的鄙夷和愤慨就会劈头盖脑落到他们身上。”

“我认为，”拉特罗布医生说，“那种情绪源出于我们盎格鲁-撒克逊人对婚姻关系的尊重。这些白皮肤的黑人出身私生，我们耻于同他们共享社交生活。他们的血统是脏的。”

“是谁把它弄脏的？”拉蒂默医生辛酸地问道。“你们把赦免给了父亲们，却把母亲们的不幸报应在孩子们头上。”

“可是，医生，要是我们撤下横杆、放人人进来共享社会平等，我们的社交圈还成个什么样子？”

===Original===
“This idea of social equality,” said Dr. Latimer, “is only a bugbear which frightens well-meaning people from dealing justly with the negro. I know of no place on earth where there is perfect social equality, and I doubt if there is such a thing in heaven. The sinner who repents on his deathbed cannot be the equal of St. Paul or the Beloved Disciple.”

“Doctor,” said Dr. Gresham, “I sometimes think that the final solution of this question will be the absorption of the negro into our race.”

“Never! never!” exclaimed Dr. Latrobe, vehemently. “It would be a death blow to American civilization.”

“Why, Doctor,” said Dr. Latimer, “you Southerners began this absorption before the war. I understand that in one decade the mixed bloods rose from one-ninth to one-eighth of the population, and that as early as 1663 a law was passed in Maryland to prevent English women from intermarrying with slaves; and, even now, your laws against miscegenation presuppose that you apprehend danger from that source.”

“Doctor, it is no use talking,” replied Dr. Latrobe, wearily. “There are niggers who are as white as I am, but the taint of blood is there and we always exclude it.”

===Chinese===
“这种社会平等之说，”拉蒂默医生说，“不过是一个吓人的鬼怪，专把好心人吓得不敢公正对待黑人。据我所知，普天之下没有一个地方存在十全十美的社会平等，我甚至怀疑天上有没有这样的东西。一个在死榻上才悔改的罪人，总不能同圣保罗（St. Paul）或耶稣所爱的门徒平起平坐吧。”

“医生，”格雷沙姆医生说，“我有时想，这个问题最终的解决，将是把黑人融合进我们的种族里去。”

“决不！决不！”拉特罗布医生激烈地喊道。“那将是给美国文明的一记致命打击。”

“咦，医生，”拉蒂默医生说，“你们南方人早在战前就开始这种融合了。据我所知，仅仅十年之间，混血人口就从总人口的九分之一升到了八分之一；早在 1663 年，马里兰（Maryland）就通过了一项法律，禁止英国妇女同奴隶通婚；即便是现在，你们那些禁止种族通婚的法律，也恰恰预设了你们心畏这一重隐患。”

“医生，说这些没有用，”拉特罗布医生疲惫地答道。“有的黑鬼白得跟我一样，可血里的脏东西总归在那里，我们永远要把他们排除在外。”

===Original===
“How do you know it is there?” asked Dr. Gresham.

“Oh, there are tricks of blood which always betray them. My eyes are more practiced than yours. I can always tell them. Now, that Johnson is as white as any man; but I knew he was a nigger the moment I saw him. I saw it in his eye.”

Dr. Latimer smiled at Dr. Latrobe’s assertion, but did not attempt to refute it; and bade him good night.

“I think,” said Dr. Latrobe, “that our war was the great mistake of the nineteenth century. It has left us very serious complications. We cannot amalgamate with the negroes. We cannot expatriate them. Now, what are we to do with them?”

===Chinese===
“你怎么知道它在那里？”格雷沙姆医生问。

“哦，血液自有种种把戏，总会把他们出卖。我的眼睛比你们的老练。我总能认出他们来。就说那个约翰逊吧，他白得跟随便哪个男人都不差；可我一见到他，就知道他是个黑鬼。我就是从他眼睛里看出来的。”

拉蒂默医生听了拉特罗布医生这番断言，微微一笑，却不打算反驳，便向他道了晚安。

“我认为，”拉特罗布医生说，“我们打的那场战争，是十九世纪铸成的大错。它给我们留下了极其棘手的难题。我们不能同黑人融合，又不能把他们送出国门。那么，我们拿他们怎么办呢？”

===Original===
“Deal justly with them,” said Dr. Gresham, “and let them alone. Try to create a moral sentiment in the nation, which will consider a wrong done to the weakest of them as a wrong done to the whole community. Whenever you find ministers too righteous to be faithless, cowardly, and time serving; women too Christly to be scornful; and public men too noble to be tricky and too honest to pander to the prejudices of the people, stand by them and give them your moral support.”

“Doctor,” said Latrobe, “with your views you ought to be a preacher striving to usher in the millennium.”

“It can’t come too soon,” replied Dr. Gresham.

===Chinese===
“公正地对待他们，”格雷沙姆医生说，“别去搅扰他们。要努力在国民中间养成一种道德的公论，把加在他们当中最弱者身上的不义，看作加在整个社会身上的不义。什么时候你们见到有牧师，正直得不肯背信弃义、不肯怯懦怕事、不肯趋时媚世；有妇人，怀有基督的精神，不屑出言轻蔑；有公众人物，高尚得不弄权术、诚实得不迎合民众的偏见——你们就要站在他们一边，给他们道义上的支持。”

“医生，”拉特罗布说，“抱着你这套见解，你本当去做个传道人，努力迎来千禧之年才是。”

“它来得越早越好，”格雷沙姆医生答道。

## XV Robert and His Company / 第十五章 罗伯特与他的连队

===Original===
“Good morning, gentlemen,” said Robert Johnson, as he approached Colonel Robinson, the commander of the post, who was standing at the door of his tent, talking with Captain Sybil.
“Good morning,” responded Colonel Robinson, “I am glad you have come. I was just about to send for you. How is your company getting on?”
“First rate, sir,” replied Robert.
“In good health?”
