“Excellent. They are all in good health and spirits. Our boys are used to hardship and exposure, and the hope of getting their freedom puts new snap into them.”

===Chinese===
“早上好，二位先生。”罗伯特·约翰逊（Robert Johnson）说着，朝驻地指挥官罗宾逊上校（Colonel Robinson）走去。上校正站在帐篷门口，同西比尔上尉（Captain Sybil）谈话。
“早上好，”罗宾逊上校应道，“你来得正好，我很高兴。我正要派人去请你。你的连队近来带得怎么样？”
“好极了，长官。”罗伯特答道。
“弟兄们身体都好？”
“好得很。他们个个身体康健、精神抖擞。我们的弟兄们吃惯了苦、受惯了风霜，而获得自由的盼头，又给他们添了一股新的劲头。”

===Original===
“I am glad of it,” said Colonel Robinson. “They make good fighters and very useful allies. Last night we received very valuable intelligence from some fugitives who had escaped through the Rebel lines. I do not think many of the Northern people realize the service they have been to us in bringing information and helping our boys when escaping from Rebel prisons. I never knew a full-blooded negro to betray us. A month ago, when we were encamped near the Rebel lines, a colored woman managed admirably to keep us posted as to the intended movements of the enemy. She was engaged in laundry work, and by means of hanging her sheets in different ways gave us the right signals.”
“I hope,” said Captain Sybil, “that the time will come when some faithful historian will chronicle all the deeds of daring and service these people have performed during this struggle, and give them due credit therefor.”
“Our great mistake,” said Colonel Robinson, “was our long delay in granting them their freedom, and even what we have done is only partial. The border States still retain their slaves. We ought to have made a clean sweep of the whole affair. Slavery is a serpent which we nourished in its weakness, and now it is stinging us in its strength.”
“I think so, too,” said Captain Sybil. “But in making his proclamation of freedom, perhaps Mr. Lincoln went as far as he thought public opinion would let him.”

===Chinese===
“这我很高兴，”罗宾逊上校说。“他们都是能打的好手，也是极有用的盟友。昨夜我们收到一些逃奴送来的情报，十分宝贵——他们是从叛军防线那边逃出来的。我看，北方人有许许多多并不明白，他们在给我们递送消息、帮助我们的小伙子们逃出叛军监狱这两件事上，出了多大的力。我从没见过哪个纯血统的黑人出卖过我们。一个月前，我们在叛军防线附近扎营，有一位有色人种妇女想方设法让我们随时得知敌军的动向，办得漂亮极了。她是做洗衣活计的，把床单晾成各种不同的样式，就给我们送出了准确的信号。”
“我盼望，”西比尔上尉说，“将来总有一天，某位忠实可靠的历史家，会把这些人在这次斗争中建立的全部英勇功勋与辛劳贡献一一载入史册，并给他们应得的赞誉。”
“我们最大的失策，”罗宾逊上校说，“就是在还他们自由这件事上拖延了那么久；就连我们已经做了的，也不过是做了一半。边界各州仍旧蓄着他们的奴隶。我们本该把整个事情来个连根的大扫除。奴隶制是一条蛇，它弱小的时候，是我们把它喂养大的；如今它强大了，回过头来噬咬我们。”
“我也这样想，”西比尔上尉说。“不过，林肯先生颁布那份解放宣言的时候，多半已经走到他估量着舆论所能容许的极限了。”

===Original===
“It is remarkable,” said Colonel Robinson, “how these Secesh hold out. It surprises me to see how poor white men, who, like the negroes, are victims of slavery, rally around the Stripes and Bars. These men, I believe, have been looked down on by the aristocratic slaveholders, and despised by the well-fed and comfortable slaves, yet they follow their leaders into the very jaws of death; face hunger, cold, disease, and danger; and all for what? What, under heaven, are they fighting for? Now, the negro, ignorant as he is, has learned to regard our flag as a banner of freedom, and to look forward to his deliverance as a consequence of the overthrow of the Rebellion.”
“I think,” said Captain Sybil, “that these ignorant white men have been awfully deceived. They have had presented to their imaginations utterly false ideas of the results of Secession, and have been taught that its success would bring them advantages which they had never enjoyed in the Union.”
“And I think,” said Colonel Robinson, “that the women and ministers have largely fed and fanned the fires of this Rebellion, and have helped to create a public opinion which has swept numbers of benighted men into the conflict. Well might one of their own men say, ‘This is a rich man’s war and a poor man’s fight.’ They were led into it through their ignorance, and held in it by their fears.”
“I think,” said Captain Sybil, “that if the public school had been common through the South this war would never have occurred. Now things have reached such a pass that able-bodied men must report at headquarters, or be treated as deserters. Their leaders are desperate men, of whom it has been said: ‘They have robbed the cradle and the grave.’ ”
“They are fighting against fearful odds,” said Colonel Robinson, “and their defeat is only a question of time.”

===Chinese===
“真叫人称奇，”罗宾逊上校说，“这些分裂派居然撑持到了今天。让我吃惊的是，那些穷苦的白人——他们同黑人一样，也是奴隶制的牺牲品——竟然还聚拢在星杠旗（the Stripes and Bars，邦联旗）的周围。据我看，这些人一向受着趾高气扬的奴隶主们的轻贱，又遭那些吃得饱饱、住得安安逸逸的奴隶们的白眼；可他们偏偏跟着自己的头目，一直钻进死亡的血盆大口；挨饿、受冻、直面疾病与危险；这一切到底图的是什么？天底下，他们究竟是在为什么打仗？再看黑人吧，他虽然无知，却已经学会把我们的旗帜看作一面自由的旗帜，已经懂得指望自己的解救，将随着这场叛乱的覆灭一同到来。”
“依我看，”西比尔上尉说，“这些无知的白人上了天大的当。有人往他们的想象里灌输了一套关于分裂后果的彻头彻尾的谬见，又教他们相信：分裂一旦得逞，就会给他们带来在联邦里从未享受过的好处。”
“而我认为，”罗宾逊上校说，“这场叛乱的火，是那些妇女和牧师们添柴扇风烧旺的，出了大力的；他们还助长出一种舆论，把成群成群的蒙昧之人卷进了这场冲突。难怪他们自己人当中就有一个人说过：‘这是富人兴的战，穷人打的仗。’他们是因无知而被引进去的，又因恐惧而被扣在里面。”
“我认为，”西比尔上尉说，“倘若公立学校早就在南方各地普及，这场战争根本就不会发生。如今事势已经到了这般地步：凡年富力强的男子都必须到司令部报到，否则就按逃兵处置。他们的头目都是些铤而走险的亡命之徒，说起他们，有人已经说过：‘他们连摇篮和坟墓都抢劫一空了。’”
“他们顶着悬殊得可怕的劣势在打，”罗宾逊上校说，“他们的败亡只是个时间问题。”

===Original===
“As soon,” said Robert, “as they fired on Fort Sumter, Uncle Daniel, a dear old father who had been praying and hoping for freedom, said to me: ‘Dey’s fired on Fort Sumter, an’ mark my words, Bob, de Norf’s boun’ ter whip.’ ”
“Had we freed the slaves at the outset,” said Captain Sybil, “we wouldn’t have given the Rebels so much opportunity to strengthen themselves by means of slave labor in raising their crops, throwing up their entrenchments, and building their fortifications. Slavery was a deadly cancer eating into the life of the nation; but, somehow, it had cast such a glamour over us that we have acted somewhat as if our national safety were better preserved by sparing the cancer than by cutting it out.”
“Political and racial questions have sadly complicated this matter,” said Colonel Robinson. “The North is not wholly made up of anti-slavery people. At the beginning of this war we were not permeated with justice, and so were not ripe for victory. The battle of Bull Run inaugurated the war by a failure. Instead of glory we gathered shame, and defeat in place of victory.”
“We have been slow,” said Captain Sybil, “to see our danger and to do our duty. Our delay has cost us thousands of lives and millions of dollars. Yet it may be it is all for the best. Our national wound was too deep to be lightly healed. When the President issued his Emancipation Proclamation my heart overflowed with joy, and I said: ‘This is the first bright rift in the war cloud.’ ”

===Chinese===
“他们朝萨姆特堡（Fort Sumter）一开炮，”罗伯特说，“丹尼尔大叔（Uncle Daniel）——一位一直为自由祷告、为自由盼望的亲爱的老长辈——就对我说：‘他们炮打了萨姆特堡啦；鲍勃，你记住俺这句话，北方是一定要打赢的。’”
“倘若我们当初一开战就放了奴隶，”西比尔上尉说，“就不会给叛军这么多机会，让他们借着奴隶的劳力去种庄稼、筑壕垒、修工事，以此壮大他们自己。奴隶制是一颗致命的癌瘤，正啃噬着国家的生命；可是不知怎么，它给我们罩下了一重魔障，害我们行事竟有些像是：留着这颗毒瘤不割，倒比剜掉它更能保全国家的安全。”
“政治问题与种族问题，把这件事搅得糟透了，”罗宾逊上校说。“北方并非全是反奴隶制的人。这场战争开头的时候，我们心中还未浸透正义，因此也还未熟到能赢得胜利的地步。布尔溪（Bull Run）一役，以一场失败为这场战争揭了幕。我们收获的不是光荣，而是耻辱；不是胜利，而是溃败。”
“在看清自己的危险、尽自己的本分这两件事上，我们都迟了，”西比尔上尉说。“我们的拖延，已经使我们付出了千万条性命、千百万钱财的代价。然而这也许反倒是最妙的安排。我们国家的创口太深，本就不能轻轻巧巧地痊愈。总统颁布《解放宣言》的那天，我满心的喜悦直往外溢，我说：‘这是战云上裂开的第一道亮缝。’”

===Original===
“And did you really think that they would accept the terms of freedom and lay down their arms?” asked Robert.
“I hardly thought they would,” continued Captain Sybil. “I did not think that their leaders would permit it. I believe the rank and file of their army are largely composed of a mass of ignorance, led, manipulated, and moulded by educated and ambitious wickedness. In attempting to overthrow the Union, a despotism and reign of terror were created which encompassed them as fetters of iron, and they will not accept the conditions until they have reached the last extremity. I hardly think they are yet willing to confess that such extremity has been reached.”
“Captain,” said Robert, as they left Colonel Robinson’s tent, “I have lived all my life where I have had a chance to hear the ‘Secesh’ talk, and when they left their papers around I used to read everything I could lay my hands on. It seemed to me that the big white men not only ruled over the poor whites and made laws for them, but over the whole nation.”
“That was so,” replied Captain Sybil. “The North was strong but forbearing. It was busy in trade and commerce, and permitted them to make the Northern States hunting-grounds for their slaves. When we sent back Simms and Burns from beneath the shadow of Bunker Hill Monument and Faneuil Hall, they mistook us; looked upon us as a lot of money-grabbers, who would be willing to purchase peace at any price. I do not believe when they fired on the ‘Star of the West’ that they had the least apprehension of the fearful results which were to follow their madness and folly.”

===Chinese===
“那么，您当时当真想过，他们会接受自由的条件、放下武器吗？”罗伯特问道。
“我本来就不大信他们会，”西比尔上尉接着说。“我不认为他们的头目们会容许这样。我相信，他们军队的兵员，大部分是一大团愚昧之众，被一些受过教育、野心勃勃的恶徒牵引着、操纵着、揉捏着。他们在图谋倾覆联邦的时候，造出了一种专制与恐怖统治，像铁镣似的把他们自己箍住了；不到山穷水尽的最后关头，他们是不会接受这些条件的。至于这样的绝境是否已然临头，我看他们至今还不肯认账。”
“上尉，”他们走出罗宾逊上校的帐篷后，罗伯特说，“我这一辈子住的地方，一直有机会听那些‘分裂派’高谈阔论；他们把报纸文件丢得四处都是的时候，凡是能弄到手的，我都拿来读。依我看，那些白人大人物不光是统治穷苦的白人、替他们立法，而且是统治着整个国家。”
“确实是这样，”西比尔上尉答道。“北方强大，却一味忍让。它忙着经商贸易，听任他们把北方各州变成追捕奴隶的猎场。当我们把西姆斯（Simms）和彭斯（Burns）从邦克山纪念碑（Bunker Hill Monument）和法纳尔大厅（Faneuil Hall）的影子底下送还回去的时候，他们看错了我们；把我们看成了一伙见钱眼开的财迷，以为我们肯出任何价钱买个太平。他们朝‘西部之星’号（the Star of the West）开炮的那会儿，我决不相信，他们对自己的疯狂与愚蠢将要招来的种种可怕后果，有过一丝一毫的预感。”

===Original===
“Well, Captain,” asked Robert, “if the free North would submit to be called on to help them catch their slaves, what could be expected of us, who all our lives had known no other condition than that of slavery? How much braver would you have been, if your first recollections had been those of seeing your mother maltreated, your father cruelly beaten, or your fellow-servants brutally murdered? I wonder why they never enslaved the Indians!”
“You are mistaken, Robert, if you think the Indians were never enslaved. I have read that the Spaniards who visited the coasts of America kidnapped thousands of Indians, whom they sent to Europe and the West Indies as slaves. Columbus himself, we are informed, captured five hundred natives, and sent them to Spain. The Indian had the lesser power of endurance, and Las Cassas suggested the enslavement of the negro, because he seemed to possess greater breadth of physical organization and stronger power of endurance. Slavery was an old world’s crime which, I have heard, the Indians never practiced among themselves. Perhaps it would have been harder to reduce them to slavery and hold them in bondage when they had a vast continent before them, where they could hide in the vastnesses of its mountains or the seclusion of its forests, than it was for white men to visit the coasts of Africa and, with their superior knowledge, obtain cargoes of slaves, bring them across the ocean, hem them in on the plantations, and surround them with a pall of dense ignorance.”
“I remember,” said Robert, “in reading a history I once came across at our house, that when the Africans first came to this country they did not all speak one language. Some had only met as mutual enemies. They were not all one color, their complexions ranging from tawny yellow to deep black.”
“Yes,” said Captain Sybil, “and in dealing with the negro we wanted his labor; in dealing with the Indian we wanted his lands. For one we had weapons of war; for the other we had real and invisible chains, the coercion of force, and the terror of the unseen world.”

===Chinese===
“可是，上尉，”罗伯特问道，“连自由的北方都甘愿应召去帮他们捉拿奴隶，还能指望我们这些人怎样呢？我们一辈子只知为奴，不知道世上还有别的处境。倘或您最早的记忆，就是眼看着自己的母亲受尽糟践、父亲被狠命毒打、同为奴仆的伙伴被人凶残地杀害，您还能比这勇敢多少呢？我倒一直纳闷，他们怎么从来没把印第安人蓄成奴隶！”
“你要是以为印第安人从来没被蓄为奴隶，那就想岔了，罗伯特。我读到过，到美洲海岸来的那些西班牙人，拐走了成千上万的印第安人，当作奴隶送到欧洲和西印度群岛去。据我们所知，哥伦布（Columbus）本人就掳了五百名土人，送到西班牙去。印第安人的耐受力差些，拉斯·卡萨斯（Las Cassas）便出主意改蓄黑人，因为黑人似乎体格更壮阔、耐受力也更强。奴隶制本是旧世界的一桩罪恶，而据我所闻，印第安人自己人中间，是从不来这一套的。把印第安人压进奴隶的枷锁、再把他们牢牢拘住，恐怕本来就更难——他们面前横着一片广袤无垠的大陆，可以躲进莽莽群山的深处，也可以藏入层层密林的幽僻；这比起白人跑到非洲海岸，凭着一副更胜一筹的知识，弄到一船船的奴隶，漂洋过海运回来，圈禁在种植园里，再裹上一层浓黑的愚昧，总要难得多。”
“我还记得，”罗伯特说，“从前在家里偶然翻到过一部史书，上面说，非洲人初到这个国家的时候，并不是人人说同一种话。有些人先前相遇，只是互为仇敌。他们的肤色也不是清一色，从黄褐色到深黑色，深深浅浅，各不相同。”
“是的，”西比尔上尉说，“对付黑人，我们要的是他们的劳力；对付印第安人，我们要的是他们的土地。对这一方，我们手里是战争的兵器；对那一方，我们手里是有形与无形的锁链、暴力的强制，再加上对那个看不见的世界的恐怖。”

===Original===
“That’s exactly so, Captain! When I was a boy I used to hear the old folks tell what would happen to bad people in another world; about the devil pouring hot lead down people’s throats and stirring them up with a pitchfork; and I used to get so scared that I would be afraid to go to bed at night. I don’t suppose the Indians ever heard of such things, or, if they had, I never heard of them being willing to give away all their lands on earth, and quietly wait for a home in heaven.”
“But, surely, Robert, you do not think religion has degraded the negro?”
“Oh, I wouldn’t say that. But a man is in a tight fix when he takes his part, like Nat Turner or Denmark Veasy, and is made to fear that he will be hanged in this world and be burned in the next. And, since I come to think of it, we colored folks used to get mightily mixed up about our religion. Mr. Gundover had on his plantation a real smart man. He was religious, but he would steal.”
“Oh, Robert,” queried Sybil, “how could he be religious and steal?”

===Chinese===
“一点儿也不差，上尉！我小时候常听老人们讲，坏人到了另一个世界会落个什么下场；讲魔鬼把滚烫的铅水灌进人的喉咙，还拿草叉把他们搅动；我常吓得夜里连床都不敢上。我想印第安人从来没听说过这些事；就算听见过，我也从没听说他们甘心让出地上全部的土地，安安静静地等着到天上领一个家。”
“可是，罗伯特，你总不至于认为，是宗教把黑人给败坏了的吧？”
“哦，那话我可不说。可一个人要是像纳特·特纳（Nat Turner）或丹马克·维西（Denmark Veasy）那样挺身起来拼一回，处境就险了：人家叫他又怕今世被绞死，又怕来世被火烧。再说，我如今想起来，我们有色人种从前在宗教上头，也着实容易搅得稀里糊涂。冈多弗先生（Mr. Gundover）的种植园上有一个顶精明的人。他信教，可他就是要偷。”
“哦，罗伯特，”西比尔问道，“他又信教、又偷东西，这怎么成呢？”

===Original===
“He didn’t think,” retorted Robert, “it was any harm to steal from his master. I guess he thought it was right to get from his master all he could. He would have thought it wrong to steal from his fellow-servants. He thought that downright mean, but I wouldn’t have insured the lives of Gundover’s pigs and chickens, if Uncle Jack got them in a tight place. One day there was a minister stopping with Mr. Gundover. As a matter of course, in speaking of his servants, he gave Jack’s sins an airing. He would much rather confess Jack’s sins than his own. Now Gundover wanted to do two things, save his pigs and poultry, and save Jack’s soul. He told the minister that Jack was a liar and a thief, and gave the minister a chance to talk with Uncle Jack about the state of his soul. Uncle Jack listened very quietly, and when taxed with stealing his master’s wheat he was ready with an answer. ‘Now Massa Parker,’ said Jack, ‘lem’me tell yer jis’ how it war ’bout dat wheat. Wen ole Jack com’d down yere, dis place war all growed up in woods. He go ter work, clared up de groun’ an’ plowed, an’ planted, an’ riz a crap, an’ den wen it war all done, he hadn’t a dollar to buy his ole woman a gown; an’ he jis’ took a bag ob wheat.’ ”
“What did Mr. Parker say?” asked Sybil.
“I don’t know, though I reckon he didn’t think it was a bad steal after all, but I don’t suppose he told Jack so. When he came to the next point, about Jack’s lying, I suppose he thought he had a clear case; but Jack was equal to the occasion.”
“How did he clear up that charge?” interrogated Captain Sybil.

===Chinese===
“他可不觉得，”罗伯特反驳道，“偷主人家的东西有什么罪过。我猜他是认定了：能从主人手里弄到多少，就弄多少，这才叫对。要是偷同为奴仆的伙伴，他就会觉得那是作孽。那样的事，他认为未免太下作了。不过，要是冈多弗家的猪和鸡落到杰克大叔（Uncle Jack）手里，我可不敢替它们的性命打保票。有一天，一位牧师在冈多弗先生家做客。说起自家的仆人，他自然要把杰克的罪过拿出来晾一晾。他宁可替杰克认罪，也不肯认自己的罪。冈多弗想办成两桩事：一桩是保住他的猪和鸡，一桩是救下杰克的灵魂。他告诉牧师，杰克又撒谎、又偷东西，还给了牧师一个机会，去同杰克大叔谈谈他灵魂的光景。杰克大叔安安静静地听着；等说他偷了主人的麦子，他张口就有对答。‘帕克老爷（Massa Parker），’杰克说，‘让俺跟你说说，那麦子到底是怎么档子事。老杰克刚到这地界的时候，这地方满处都长着树林子。他去干活，清了地，又犁又种，收出一茬庄稼；可等全都忙完了，他连一个子儿也没落下，给他老婆子买件衣裳的钱都拿不出；他这才拿了那么一袋麦子。’”
“帕克先生怎么说呢？”西比尔问。
“这我不知道；不过我估摸，他到底也没觉得那一偷真有什么了不得，只是我料想，他没有把这个话说给杰克听。等他讲到下一桩，讲到杰克撒谎，我猜他以为自己证据干净、稳稳占理了；可杰克应付这个场面，照样绰绰有余。”
“那他是怎样把那桩指控洗刷清楚的呢？”西比尔上尉追问道。

===Original===
“Finely. I think if he had been educated he would have made a first-rate lawyer. He said, ‘Marse Parker, dere’s old Joe. His wife don’t lib on dis plantation. Old Joe go ober ter see her, but he stayed too long, an’ didn’t git back in time fer his work. Massa’s oberseer kotched him an’ cut him all up. When de oberseer went inter de house, pore old Joe war all tired an’ beat up, an’ so he lay down by de fence corner and go ter sleep. Bimeby Massa oberseer com’d an’ axed, “all bin a workin’ libely?” I say “Yes, Massa.” ’ Then said Mr. Parker, ‘You were lying, Joe had been sleeping, not working.’ ‘I know’s dat, but ef I tole on Joe, Massa oberseer cut him all up again, and Massa Jesus says, “Blessed am de Peacemaker.” ’ I heard, continued Robert, that Mr. Parker said to Gundover, ‘You seem to me like a man standing in a stream where the blood of Jesus can reach you, but you are standing between it and your slaves. How will you answer that in the Day of Judgment?’ ”
“What did Gundover say?” asked Captain Sybil.
“He turned pale, and said, ‘For God’s sake don’t speak of the Day of Judgment in connection with slavery.’ ”

===Chinese===
“洗得漂漂亮亮。我想，他要是受过教育，准能当一名头等的律师。他说：‘帕克老爷，就说老乔吧。他老婆不在这个园子上住。老乔过去瞧她，可他待得太久，没赶回来做他的活儿。主人的监工逮住了他，把他抽得遍体鳞伤。监工进屋去了，可怜的老乔又累又挨了打，就靠着栅栏拐角躺下睡了。过不多时，主人的监工走过来问：“活儿都干得欢实吧？”我说：“欢实，老爷。”’帕克先生便说：‘你这是撒谎；乔在睡觉，并没有干活。’‘俺知道是这么回事；可俺要是告发了老乔，主人的监工又要把他抽个稀烂；耶稣主人说了：“使人和睦的人有福了。”’我还听说——罗伯特接着往下讲——帕克先生对冈多弗说过：‘我看你就像一个人，站在一道溪流里，耶稣的宝血本可以流到你身上；可你偏偏挡在那血与你的奴隶中间。到了审判的日子，这一条你怎样交代？’”
“冈多弗怎么说呢？”西比尔上尉问。
“他脸色发白，说：‘看在上帝的份上，别把审判的日子跟奴隶制扯到一块儿说。’”

===Original===
Just then a messenger brought a communication to Captain Sybil. He read it attentively, and, turning to Robert, said, “Here are orders for an engagement at Five Forks tomorrow. Oh, this wasting of life and scattering of treasure might have been saved had we only been wiser. But the time is passing. Look after your company, and see that everything is in readiness as soon as possible.”
Carefully Robert superintended the arrangements for the coming battle of a strife which for years had thrown its crimson shadows over the land. The Rebels fought with a valor worthy of a better cause. The disaster of Bull Run had been retrieved. Sherman had made his famous march to the sea. Fighting Joe Hooker had scaled the stronghold of the storm king and won a victory in the palace chamber of the clouds; the Union soldiers had captured Columbia, replanted the Stars and Stripes in Charleston, and changed that old sepulchre of slavery into the cradle of a newborn freedom. Farragut had been as triumphant on water as the other generals had been victorious on land, and New Orleans had been wrenched from the hands of the Confederacy. The Rebel leaders were obstinate. Misguided hordes had followed them to defeat and death. Grant was firm and determined to fight it out if it took all summer. The closing battles were fought with desperate courage and firm resistance, but at last the South was forced to succumb. On the ninth day of April, 1865, General Lee surrendered to General Grant. The lost cause went down in blood and tears, and on the brows of a ransomed people God poured the chrism of a new era, and they stood a race newly anointed with freedom.

===Chinese===
正在这时，一名信差给西比尔上尉送来一件公文。他仔细读了，转过身来对罗伯特说：“这里有命令，明天在五叉口（Five Forks）打一仗。唉，只要我们当初更明智一些，这生命的虚掷、财货的抛散，原本都可以省下的。可是时间正在溜走。照管好你的连队，务必尽快把一切准备停当。”
罗伯特悉心督理着迎战来日的各项部署——这场战事，多年来已把它血红的阴影投在这片国土上。叛军打得英勇，那份英勇原配得上一桩更好的事业。布尔溪惨败之辱已经洗雪。谢尔曼（Sherman）完成了他那次向大海进军的著名行军。战将乔·胡克（Fighting Joe Hooker）攻上了风暴之王的堡寨，在云霄的宫室之中赢了一场胜利；联邦军的士兵们攻下了哥伦比亚（Columbia）城，把星条旗重新插上查尔斯顿（Charleston），把那座奴隶制的老坟茔，改成了新生的自由的摇篮。法拉格特（Farragut）在水上的凯旋，不亚于众位将军在陆上的奏捷，新奥尔良（New Orleans）已经从邦联（the Confederacy）的手里硬生生夺了回来。叛军的头目们顽固不化。被引入歧途的乌合之众，跟着他们走向溃败与死亡。格兰特（Grant）坚定不移，哪怕打上整整一个夏天，也决心打到底。最后几仗，是在拼命的勇气和顽强的抵抗之中打完的；但南方终究被迫屈服了。1865 年 4 月 9 日，李将军（General Lee）向格兰特将军投降。那失败的事业在血与泪中沉没了；上帝把新时代的圣膏，倾注在一个被赎回的民族的额头上，他们挺立着，成了一个新受自由膏立的种族。

## VI Robert Johnson’s Promotion and Religion / 第六章 罗伯特·约翰逊的晋升与信仰

===Original===
Robert Johnson, being able to meet the army requirements, was enlisted as a substitute to help fill out the quota of a Northern regiment. With his intelligence, courage, and prompt obedience, he rose from the ranks and became lieutenant of a colored company. He was daring, without being rash; prompt, but not thoughtless; firm, without being harsh. Kind and devoted to the company he drilled, he soon won the respect of his superior officers and the love of his comrades.
“Johnson,” said a young officer, Captain Sybil, of Maine, who had become attached to Robert, “what is the use of your saying you’re a colored man, when you are as white as I am, and as brave a man as there is among us. Why not quit this company, and take your place in the army just the same as a white man? I know your chances for promotion would be better.”
“Captain, you may doubt my word, but today I would rather be a lieutenant in my company than a captain in yours.”
“I don’t understand you.”
“Well, Captain, when a man’s been colored all his life it comes a little hard for him to get white all at once. Were I to try it, I would feel like a cat in a strange garret. Captain, I think my place is where I am most needed. You do not need me in your ranks, and my company does. They are excellent fighters, but they need a leader. To silence a battery, to capture a flag, to take a fortification, they will rush into the jaws of death.”

===Chinese===
罗伯特·约翰逊（Robert Johnson）体格合格，达到了军队的入伍要求，便作为替身兵应征，补足了一个北方团的兵额。凭着他的聪明、勇气和雷厉风行的服从，他从行伍间擢升起来，当上了有色人种连的一名中尉。他胆大而不鲁莽，敏捷而不轻率，坚定而不严苛。他对自己操练的那支连队又和善又尽心，很快就赢得了上级军官的敬重与战友们的爱戴。
“约翰逊，”缅因州（Maine）来的年轻军官西比尔上尉（Captain Sybil）说——他已经同罗伯特结下了情谊——“你明明跟我一样白，人也同我们中间任何一个人一样勇敢，一口咬定自己是有色人种，这有什么用？干吗不离开这个连，像白人一样到军队里去占你自己的位置？我知道，那样你晋升的机会会好得多。”
“上尉，您也许不肯信我的话，可是今天，我宁可在自己的连里当中尉，也不愿到您的连里当上尉。”
“我不明白你的意思。”
“唉，上尉，一个人一辈子都是有色人种，要他一下子变白，可有点难。我要真去试一试，准会觉得像一只闯进陌生阁楼的猫。上尉，我想，我的位置应该在最需要我的地方。您那边用不着我，我的连队却用得着。他们都是顶出色的战士，可他们需要一位领头的人。要压哑一门炮，要夺取一面军旗，要拿下一座工事，他们都会一头冲进死亡的虎口。”

===Original===
“Yes, I have often wondered at their bravery.”
“Captain, these battles put them on their mettle. They have been so long taught that they are nothing and nobody, that they seem glad to prove they are something and somebody.”
“But, Johnson, you do not look like them, you do not talk like them. It is a burning shame to have held such a man as you in slavery.”
“I don’t think it was any worse to have held me in slavery than the blackest man in the South.”
“You are right, Johnson. The color of a man’s skin has nothing to do with the possession of his rights.”
“Now, there is Tom Anderson,” said Robert, “he is just as black as black can be. He has been bought and sold like a beast, and yet there is not a braver man in all the company. I know him well. He is a noble-hearted fellow. True as steel. I love him like a brother. And I believe Tom would risk his life for me any day. He don’t know anything about his father or mother. He was sold from them before he could remember. He can read a little. He used to take lessons from a white gardener in Virginia. He would go between the hours of 9 p.m. and 4 a.m. He got a book of his own, tore it up, greased the pages, and hid them in his hat. Then if his master had ever knocked his hat off he would have thought them greasy papers, and not that Tom was carrying his library on his head. I had another friend who lived near us. When he was nineteen years old he did not know how many letters there were in the A.B.C.s. One night, when his work was done, his boss came into his cabin and saw him with a book in his hand. He threatened to give him five hundred lashes if he caught him again with a book, and said he hadn’t work enough to do. He was getting out logs, and his task was ten logs a day. His employer threatened to increase it to twelve. He said it just harassed him; it set him on fire. He thought there must be something good in that book if the white man didn’t want him to learn. One day he had an errand in the kitchen, and he heard one of the colored girls going over the A.B.C.s. Here was the key to the forbidden knowledge. She had heard the white children saying them, and picked them up by heart, but did not know them by sight. He was not content with that, but sold his cap for a book and wore a cloth on his head instead. He got the sounds of the letters by heart, then cut off the bark of a tree, carved the letters on the smooth inside, and learned them. He wanted to learn how to write. He had charge of a warehouse where he had a chance to see the size and form of letters. He made the beach of the river his copybook, and thus he learned to write. Tom never got very far with his learning, but I used to get the papers and tell him all I knew about the war.”

===Chinese===
“是啊，他们的勇敢，我常常觉得惊奇。”
“上尉，这些战斗激起了他们的斗志。这么长久以来，一直有人教他们，说他们什么也不是、谁也不是；如今他们像是巴不得证明，他们是有点什么的、是个人物。”
“可是，约翰逊，你长得不像他们，说话也不像他们。把像你这样的一个人按在奴隶制里，真是莫大的耻辱。”
“我不觉得把我在奴隶制里扣着，就比把南方皮肤最黑的人扣着更糟。”
“你说得对，约翰逊。一个人皮肤的颜色，同他权利的归属毫不相干。”
“就说汤姆·安德森（Tom Anderson）吧，”罗伯特说，“他黑得不能再黑了。他像牲口一样被人买进卖出，可是全连再找不出一个比他更勇敢的人。我跟他熟得很。他是个心地高贵的人，像钢铁一样可靠。我爱他，就像爱自己的兄弟。而且我相信，汤姆随便哪一天都肯为我舍出性命。他一点也不知道自己的父亲母亲是什么人。还没到记事的年纪，他就被人从他们身边卖掉了。他识得几个字。他从前在弗吉尼亚（Virginia）跟一个白人园丁上课，总在晚上九点到凌晨四点之间去。他自己弄到一本书，把书撕开，给书页抹上油，藏进帽子里。这样一来，主人就算把他的帽子打落在地，也只会当那是几张油乎乎的纸，决想不到汤姆是把他的图书馆顶在脑袋上。我还有一个朋友，住在我们家附近。他十九岁了，还不知道字母表（A.B.C.s）里一共有多少个字母。一天夜里，他活儿干完了，老板走进他的小木屋，看见他手里拿着一本书。老板威胁说，再要逮着他拿书，就抽他五百鞭子，还说他的活儿不够多。他是往外拖运木头的，每天的定额是十根原木，雇主还威胁要加到十二根。他说，这简直是折磨他；这话把他心里点着了火。他想，那本书里必定有什么好东西，不然白人怎么不肯让他学。有一天，他到厨房去办事，听见一个有色人种姑娘在背字母表。这可是通往禁书知识的钥匙。她是听白人孩子们念熟的，靠耳朵记在了心里，见了字形却认不出来。他还不以此为足，把便帽卖掉换了一本书，头上改裹一块布。他把字母的读音背熟，然后剥下一块树皮，在光滑的树皮里子上刻出字母，一个个认会了。他还想学写字。他管着一座货栈，有机会看到字母的大小和形体。他把河边的沙滩当作习字本，就这样学会了写字。汤姆的学问从没学多深，可我常弄到报纸，把我所知道的战事都讲给他听。”

===Original===
“How did you get the papers?”
“I used to have very good privileges for a slave. All of our owners were not alike. Some of them were quite clever, and others were worse than git out. I used to get the morning papers to sell to the boarders and others, and when I got them I would contrive to hide a paper, and let some of the fellow-servants know how things were going on. And our owners thought we cared nothing about what was going on.”
“How was that? I thought you were not allowed to hold meetings unless a white man were present.”
“That was so. But we contrived to hold secret meetings in spite of their caution. We knew whom we could trust. My ole Miss wasn’t mean like some of them. She never wanted the patrollers around prowling in our cabins, and poking their noses into our business. Her husband was an awful drunkard. He ran through every cent he could lay his hands on, and she was forced to do something to keep the wolf from the door, so she set up a boardinghouse. But she didn’t take in Tom, Dick, and Harry. Nobody but the big bugs stopped with her. She taught me to read and write, and to cast up accounts. It was so handy for her to have someone who could figure up her accounts, and read or write a note, if she were from home and wanted the like done. She once told her cousin how I could write and figure up. And what do you think her cousin said?”
“ ‘Pleased,’ I suppose, ‘to hear it.’ ”
“Not a bit of it. She said, if I belonged to her, she would cut off my thumbs; her husband said, ‘Oh, then he couldn’t pick cotton.’ As to my poor thumbs, it did not seem to be taken into account what it would cost me to lose them. My ole Miss used to have a lot of books. She would let me read any one of them except a novel. She wanted to take care of my soul, but she wasn’t taking care of her own.”

===Chinese===
“你是怎么弄到报纸的？”
“作为一个奴隶，我从前享有的待遇算是很好的。我们的主人各家并不一样。有的相当不赖，有的可就坏得没边儿了。我常领了晨报来，卖给寄宿的客人和别的人；领到报纸，我就想法子藏下一份，让几个一同当差的伙伴知道局势怎么样了。我们的主人还当我们对这些事毫不放在心上呢。”
“那是怎么回事？我原以为你们不许集会，除非有个白人在场。”
“确是那样。可他们尽管百般提防，我们还是想法子开秘密集会。我们信得过谁，心里有数。我的老女主人不像有些人家那样刻薄。她从来不愿意让巡逻队在我们的小木屋里转来转去，把鼻子伸进我们的事务里来。她丈夫是个不折不扣的酒鬼，凡是他抓得到手的钱，一个子儿不剩全挥霍光了；她不得不想个法子把饿狼挡在门外，于是就开了一处供膳寄宿屋。可她收留的不是张三李四随便什么人。在她那儿落脚的，尽是有头有脸的大人物。她教我读书、写字，还教我算账。有个能替她把账目一笔笔算清、她人不在家时需要写个条子读封信也有人能办的人，实在方便。她有一回跟她表亲夸说我会写字会算账。您猜她表亲说了什么？”
“我猜呀，她准是‘听了很高兴’。”
“一点儿也不。她说，我若是归了她，她就要砍掉我的两个大拇指；她丈夫说：‘哦，那他就摘不成棉花啦。’至于我那对可怜的大拇指——没了它们我得付出什么代价，倒像是没有人往心里去。我的老女主人从前存着好多书。随便哪本她都肯让我读，只除了小说。她要照管我的灵魂，她自己的灵魂却不去照管。”

===Original===
“Wasn’t she religious?”
“She went for it. I suppose she was as good as most of them. She said her prayers and went to church, but I don’t know that that made her any better. I never did take much stock in white folks’ religion.”
“Why, Robert, I’m afraid you are something of an infidel.”
“No, Captain, I believe in the real, genuine religion. I ain’t got much myself, but I respect them that have. We had on our place a dear, old saint, named Aunt Kizzy. She was a happy soul. She had seen hard times, but was what I call a living epistle. I’ve heard her tell how her only child had been sold from her, when the man who bought herself did not want to buy her child. Poor little fellow! he was only two years old. I asked her one day how she felt when her child was taken away. ‘I felt,’ she said, ‘as if I was going to my grave. But I knew if I couldn’t get justice here, I could get it in another world.’ ”
“That was faith,” said Captain Sybil, as if speaking to himself, “a patient waiting for death to redress the wrongs of life.”
“Many a time,” continued Robert, “have I heard her humming to herself in the kitchen and saying, ‘I has my trials, ups and downs, but it won’t allers be so. I specs one day to wing and wing wid de angels, Hallelujah! Den I specs to hear a voice sayin’, “Poor ole Kizzy, she’s done de bes’ she kin. Go down, Gabriel, an’ tote her in.” Den I specs to put on my golden slippers, my long white robe, an’ my starry crown, an’ walk dem golden streets, Hallelujah!’ I’ve known that dear, old soul to travel going on two miles, after her work was done, to have someone read to her. Her favorite chapter began with, ‘Let not your heart be troubled, ye believe in God, believe also in Me.’ ”

===Chinese===
“那她难道不虔诚信教吗？”
“她对那一套可卖力呢。我看她跟他们大多数人一样，也算不坏了。她念祷告，上教堂，可我不知道这些让她变好了多少。白人的宗教，我从来就不怎么当真。”
“哎呀，罗伯特，我看你多少有点像个不信教的人了。”
“不，上尉，我信的是又真又正的宗教。我自己拥有的不多，可我敬重那些有信仰的人。我们那儿有一位亲爱的老圣徒，名叫基齐大婶（Aunt Kizzy）。她是个快活的灵魂。她吃过苦日子，可她是我所说的一封活生生的书信。我听她讲过，她唯一的孩子怎样被人从她身边卖掉，因为买下她本人的那个男人不肯买她的孩子。可怜的小家伙！他才两岁。有一天我问她，孩子被夺走的时候，她心里是什么滋味。‘我当时觉得，’她说，‘就像自己正走向坟墓。可我知道，若是这世上讨不到公道，我还能在另一个世界讨到。’”
“那就是信心，”西比尔上尉说，像是自言自语，“耐心等候死亡来昭雪生前所受的冤屈。”
“多少回，”罗伯特接着说，“我听见她在厨房里独自哼着，自言自语：‘俺也有俺的磨难，起起落落，可不会老是这样。俺盼着有一天，跟天使们比翼齐飞，哈利路亚！到那时俺盼着听见一个声音说：“可怜的老基齐，她尽了她的力。加百列，下去把她领进来。”到那时俺盼着穿上俺的金拖鞋、俺雪白的长袍、俺缀星的冠冕，去把那些黄金的街道走个遍，哈利路亚！’这位亲爱的老灵魂，干完了活儿，还常常走上将近两英里路，去请人念书给她听。她顶爱的一章，开头是：‘你们心里不要忧愁，你们信神，也当信我。’”

===Original===
“I have been deeply impressed,” said Captain Sybil, “with the childlike faith of some of these people. I do not mean to say that they are consistent Christians, but I do think that this faith has in a measure underlain the life of the race. It has been a golden thread woven amid the sombre tissues of their lives. A ray of light shimmering amid the gloom of their condition. And what would they have been without it?”
“I don’t know. But I know what she was with it. And I believe if there are any saints in glory, Aunt Kizzy is one of them.”
“She is dead, then?”
“Yes, went all right, singing and rejoicing until the last, ‘Troubles over, troubles over, and den my troubles will be over. We’ll walk de golden streets all ’roun’ in de New Jerusalem.’ Now, Captain, that’s the kind of religion that I want. Not that kind which could ride to church on Sundays, and talk so solemn with the minister about heaven and good things, then come home and light down on the servants like a thousand of bricks. I have no use for it. I don’t believe in it. I never did and I never will. If any man wants to save my soul he ain’t got to beat my body. That ain’t the kind of religion I’m looking for. I ain’t got a bit of use for it. Now, Captain, ain’t I right?”

===Chinese===
“这些人身上那种孩子般的信心，给我留下了极深的印象，”西比尔上尉说，“我并不是说他们个个都是表里如一的基督徒，可我确实认为，这种信心在某种程度上托住了这个种族的生活。它是一条金线，织在他们晦暗的生命织物中间；是一缕光线，在他们境况的阴郁里闪烁。要是没有它，他们又会变成什么样子呢？”
“我不知道。可我知道，有了它，她成了什么样的人。而且我相信，荣耀里若真有圣徒，基齐大婶必是其中一位。”
“那么，她已经过世了？”
“是的，她走得很安好，一路唱着、欢欣着直到最后：‘苦难过去了，苦难过去了，俺的苦难就要过去了。俺们要把新耶路撒冷的黄金街道走个遍。’上尉，我要的就是这一种宗教。不是那种礼拜天坐着车子上教堂、同牧师一脸庄严地谈论天国和美善之事、回到家却像千块砖头劈头盖脸砸下来一样落在仆人头上的宗教。那种宗教我派不上用场。我不信它。我从来不信，也永远不信。谁要想拯救我的灵魂，就用不着先毒打我的身子。那不是我要找的宗教。它对我一点儿用处也没有。上尉，难道我说得不对吗？”

===Original===
“Well, yes, Robert, I think you are more than half right. You ought to know my dear, old mother who lives in Maine. We have had colored company at our house, and I never saw her show the least difference between her colored and white guests. She is a Quaker preacher, and don’t believe in war, but when the rest of the young men went to the front, I wanted to go also. So I thought it all over, and there seemed to be no way out of slavery except through the war. I had been taught to hate war and detest slavery. Now the time had come when I could not help the war, but I could strike a blow for freedom. So I told my mother I was going to the front, that I expected to be killed, but I went to free the slave. It went hard with her. But I thought that I ought to come, and I believe my mother’s prayers are following me.”
“Captain,” said Robert, rising, “I am glad that I have heard your story. I think that some of these Northern soldiers do two things﻿—hate slavery and hate niggers.”
“I am afraid that is so with some of them. They would rather be whipped by Rebels than conquer with negroes. Oh, I heard a soldier,” said Captain Sybil, “say, when the colored men were being enlisted, that he would break his sword and resign. But he didn’t do either. After Colonel Shaw led his charge at Fort Wagner, and died in the conflict, he got bravely over his prejudices. The conduct of the colored troops there and elsewhere has done much to turn public opinion in their favor. I suppose any white soldier would rather have his black substitute receive the bullets than himself.”

===Chinese===
“嗯，是啊，罗伯特，我看你说对的可不止一半。你真该认识一下我那住在缅因州的亲爱的老母亲。我们家接待过有色人种的客人，我从没见她对有色人种客人和白人客人显出过一丝一毫的差别。她是一位贵格会（Quaker）的传道人，不信战争这一套；可是别的年轻人都上了前线，我也想去。我把前前后后想了一遍：要走出奴隶制，除了打这场战争，好像再没有别的路。我从小受的教导，是憎恨战争、痛恨奴隶制。如今时候到了——这场战争我拦不住，可我能为自由挥出一击。于是我告诉母亲，我要上前线去，多半会战死，可我是去解放奴隶的。她心里难受极了。可我觉得我理应来，而且我相信，我母亲的祷告一直跟着我呢。”
“上尉，”罗伯特站起身来说，“听了您的故事，我很高兴。我看，这些北方兵做的是两件事——恨奴隶制，也恨黑鬼。”
“恐怕有些人确实是这样。他们宁可挨叛军的揍，也不愿跟黑人一道打胜仗。哦，当初征募有色人种入伍的时候，我听一个士兵说过，”西比尔上尉说，“他要折断佩刀、辞官不干。可他两样都没有做。肖上校（Colonel Shaw）率队冲锋瓦格纳堡（Fort Wagner）、战死在那场恶战里之后，他就勇敢地跨过了自己的偏见。有色人种部队在那儿和在别处的表现，让舆论大大转而对他们有利了。我想，随便哪个白人士兵，都宁可叫自己的黑人替身兵去挨子弹，而不是自己挨。”

## XII Schoolgirl Notions / 第十二章 女学生的念头

===Original===

During Iola’s stay in the North she found a strong tide of opposition against slavery. Arguments against the institution had entered the Church and made legislative halls the arenas of fierce debate. The subject had become part of the social converse of the fireside, and had enlisted the best brain and heart of the country. Anti-slavery discussions were pervading the strongest literature and claiming, a place on the most popular platforms.

Iola, being a Southern girl and a slaveholder’s daughter, always defended slavery when it was under discussion.

“Slavery can’t be wrong,” she would say, “for my father is a slaveholder, and my mother is as good to our servants as she can be. My father often tells her that she spoils them, and lets them run over her. I never saw my father strike one of them. I love my mammy as much as I do my own mother, and I believe she loves us just as if we were her own children. When we are sick I am sure that she could not do anything more for us than she does.”

===Chinese===

伊奥拉在北方居留期间，发现一股反对奴隶制的强大浪潮。反对这一制度的论战已经打进了教会，把立法议事厅变成了激烈争辩的竞技场。这个题目已经成为炉边社交谈资的一部分，并且唤起了这个国家最优秀的头脑与心灵。反对奴隶制的讨论正弥漫于最有力度的文学作品之中，并在最热门的讲坛上争得一席之地。

伊奥拉身为南方姑娘、奴隶主的女儿，每逢奴隶制被人议论，总是挺身为其辩护。

“奴隶制不可能是错的，”她总爱这么说，“因为我父亲就是个奴隶主，我母亲待我们的仆人好得不能再好。父亲常对她说，她把那些人都惯坏了，随他们骑到她头上去。我从没见过父亲动手打他们中的哪一个。我爱我的嬷嬷，就像爱我自己的母亲一样；我相信她也爱我们，就像我们是她亲生的孩子似的。我们生病的时候，我敢说，她为我们做的，再多一分也做不出来了。”

===Original===

“But, Iola,” responded one of her school friends, “after all, they are not free. Would you be satisfied to have the most beautiful home, the costliest jewels, or the most elegant wardrobe if you were a slave?”

“Oh, the cases are not parallel. Our slaves do not want their freedom. They would not take it if we gave it to them.”

“That is not the case with them all. My father has seen men who have encountered almost incredible hardships to get their freedom. Iola, did you ever attend an anti-slavery meeting?”

===Chinese===

“可是，伊奥拉，”她的一位同窗好友应道，“说到底，他们并不自由。倘若你自己是个奴隶，就算住着最华美的宅邸，戴着最贵重的珠宝，穿着最讲究的衣裳，你会心满意足吗？”

“哦，这两种情形不能相提并论。我们的奴隶并不想要自由。就算我们把自由给他们，他们也未必肯要。”

“他们可不见得个个如此。我父亲就见过一些人，为了争得自由，吃了几乎令人难以置信的苦头。伊奥拉，你去听过反对奴隶制的集会吗？”

===Original===

“No; I don’t think these Abolitionists have any right to meddle in our affairs. I believe they are prejudiced against us, and want to get our property. I read about them in the papers when I was at home. I don’t want to hear my part of the country run down. My father says the slaves would be very well contented if no one put wrong notions in their heads.”

“I don’t know,” was the response of her friend, “but I do not think that that slave mother who took her four children, crossed the Ohio River on the ice, killed one of the children and attempted the lives of the other two, was a contented slave. And that other one, who, running away and finding herself pursued, threw herself over the Long Bridge into the Potomac, was evidently not satisfied. I do not think the numbers who are coming North on the Underground Railroad can be very contented. It is not natural for people to run away from happiness, and if they are so happy and contented, why did Congress pass the Fugitive Slave Bill?”

“Well, I don’t think,” answered Iola, “any of our slaves would run away. I know mamma don’t like slavery very much. I have often heard her say that she hoped the time would come when there would not be a slave in the land. My father does not think as she does. He thinks slavery is not wrong if you treat them well and don’t sell them from their families. I intend, after I have graduated, to persuade pa to buy a house in New Orleans, and spend the winter there. You know this will be my first season out, and I hope that you will come and spend the winter with me. We will have such gay times, and you will so fall in love with our sunny South that you will never want to come back to shiver amid the snows and cold of the North. I think one winter in the South would cure you of your Abolitionism.”

===Chinese===

“没有；我看这些废奴主义者毫无权利来插手我们的事务。我相信他们对咱们心存偏见，还想抢我们的财产。我在家的时候，常在报纸上读到他们的种种行径。我不爱听人把我家乡说得一钱不值。我父亲说，只要没有人往奴隶们脑袋里灌输歪念头，他们个个都心满意足。”

“这我可不知道，”她的朋友回答说，“不过我想，那位带着四个孩子、踏着冰面渡过俄亥俄河（Ohio River）、杀死其中一个、又试图结果另外两个孩子性命的奴隶母亲，总不能算是个心满意足的奴隶。还有另一位，逃亡途中发觉自己被人追捕，便从长桥（Long Bridge）上纵身跳进了波托马克河（the Potomac），她显然也并不满意。我想，那些正沿着地下铁路（Underground Railroad）源源北上的人们，也心满意足不到哪儿去。人天生不会逃离幸福；他们既然那样快活知足，国会为什么还要通过《逃奴法案》（Fugitive Slave Bill）呢？”

“唉，我想，”伊奥拉答道，“我们家的奴隶谁也不会逃跑。我知道妈妈不太喜欢奴隶制。我常听她说，但愿有朝一日，这片土地上再没有一个奴隶。父亲的看法跟她不一样。他认为，只要你好好待他们、不把他们从家人身边卖走，奴隶制就没有什么错。我打算毕业以后，劝爸爸在新奥尔良买下一所房子，到那边去过冬。你知道，这将是我头一回出门过社交季，我盼望你来陪我一起过冬。我们会过得快活极了，你准会深深爱上我们阳光和煦的南方，再也不想回来缩在北方的冰雪严寒里打哆嗦。我想，在南方过一个冬天，保管治好你的废奴主义。”

===Original===

“Have you seen her yet?”

This question was asked by Louis Bastine, an attorney who had come North in the interests of Lorraine. The scene was the New England village where Mr. Galen’s academy was located, and which Iola was attending. This question was addressed to Camille Lecroix, Bastine’s intimate friend, who had lately come North. He was the son of a planter who lived near Leroy’s plantation, and was familiar with Iola’s family history. Since his arrival North, Bastine had met him and communicated to him his intentions.

“Yes; just caught a glimpse of her this morning as she was going down the street,” was Camille’s reply.

===Chinese===

“你见到她了吗？”

这话是路易斯·巴斯廷（Louis Bastine）问的。他是一位律师，此番为洛兰的利益北上。说话的场景在新英格兰的一座村庄，盖伦先生（Mr. Galen）的学校就设在那里，伊奥拉正在那所学校就读。这个问题是问卡米尔·勒克罗瓦（Camille Lecroix）的——他是巴斯廷的挚友，新近来到北方。他是一位种植园主的儿子，那种植园就在勒罗伊家产业的附近，因此他对伊奥拉的家世知之甚详。巴斯廷到北方后遇见了他，把自己的来意告诉了他。

“见到了；今天早晨她上街去，我刚好瞥见了她一眼。”卡米尔回答说。
===Original===

“She is a most beautiful creature,” said Louis Bastine. “She has the proud poise of Leroy, the most splendid eyes I ever saw in a woman’s head, lovely complexion, and a glorious wealth of hair. She would bring $2,000 any day in a New Orleans market.”

“I always feel sorry,” said Camille, “when I see one of those Creole girls brought to the auction block. I have known fathers who were deeply devoted to their daughters, but who through some reverse of fortune were forced to part with them, and I always think the blow has been equally terrible on both sides. I had a friend who had two beautiful daughters whom he had educated in the North. They were cultured, and really belles in society. They were entirely ignorant of their lineage, but when their father died it was discovered that their mother had been a slave. It was a fearful blow. They would have faced poverty, but the knowledge of their tainted blood was more than they could bear.”

“What became of them?”

===Chinese===

“她真是个绝色的美人儿，”路易斯·巴斯廷说。“她有勒罗伊家那份高傲的仪态，那双眼睛是我在任何女人脸上见过的最华美的眼睛，肤色娇美，还有一头丰茂的秀发，光彩照人。在新奥尔良的市场上，随便哪一天她都能卖到两千美元（$2,000）。”

“每当我看见那些克里奥尔姑娘被带上拍卖台，”卡米尔说，“心里总觉得难过。我认识一些做父亲的，对女儿疼爱至深，却因家道变故，被迫与她们离散；我总觉得，这打击对双方同样是惨烈的。我有一位朋友，有两个美丽的女儿，都在北方受过教育。她们颇有教养，实在是社交界的一对名媛。她们对自己的血统一无所知，可是父亲一死，人们才发现她们的母亲原来当过奴隶。这是一个可怕的打击。贫穷，她们本还可以面对；可是得知自己的血统带着污点，这就叫她们承受不起了。”

“她们后来怎么样了？”

===Original===

“They both died, poor girls. I believe they were as much killed by the blow as if they had been shot. To tell you the truth, Bastine, I feel sorry for this girl. I don’t believe she has the least idea of her negro blood.”

“No, Leroy has been careful to conceal it from her,” replied Bastine.

“Is that so?” queried Camille. “Then he has made a great mistake.”

“I can’t help that,” said Bastine; “business is business.”

===Chinese===

“她们俩都死了，可怜的姑娘们。我相信，那个打击要了她们的命，就跟一颗子弹打中了她们一样。跟你说实话吧，巴斯廷，我替这个姑娘难过。我相信，她对自己身上的黑人血统连一丁点儿概念都没有。”

“是啊，勒罗伊一直小心提防，不让她知道。”巴斯廷答道。

“当真如此？”卡米尔问道。“那他可犯了一个大错。”

“那我可管不着，”巴斯廷说；“公事就是公事。”

===Original===

“How can you get her away?” asked Camille. “You will have to be very cautious, because if these pesky Abolitionists get an inkling of what you’re doing they will balk your game double quick. And when you come to look at it, isn’t it a shame to attempt to reduce that girl to slavery? She is just as white as we are, as good as any girl in the land, and better educated than thousands of white girls. A girl with her apparent refinement and magnificent beauty, were it not for the cross in her blood, I would be proud to introduce to our set. She would be the sensation of the season. I believe today it would be easier for me to go to the slums and take a young girl from there, and have her introduced as my wife, than to have society condone the offense if I married that lovely girl. There is not a social circle in the South that would not take it as a gross insult to have her introduced into it.”

“Well,” said Bastine, “my plan is settled. Leroy has never allowed her to spend her vacations at home. I understand she is now very anxious to get home, and, as Lorraine’s attorney, I have come on his account to take her home.”

===Chinese===

“你要怎么把她弄走呢？”卡米尔问。“你得格外小心才成，因为一旦让那些讨人嫌的废奴主义者嗅出一点儿风声，他们立刻就会搅了你的局。再说，细想起来，硬要把那位姑娘贬为奴隶，这不是无耻之尤吗？她的皮肤跟我们一样白净，人品跟这片国土上任何一个姑娘一样好，受的教育还胜过成千上万的白人姑娘。像她这样仪态显然文雅、容貌又那样华美的姑娘，若不是血里背着那道十字架，我会十分自豪地把她引荐进我们的圈子。她准会成为整个社交季最轰动的人物。我相信，时至今日，我宁可到贫民窟里领一个年轻姑娘出来，把她说成是我的妻子引荐给众人，也强似让我娶了那位可爱姑娘之后，指望上流社会宽宥这桩罪过。南方没有一个社交圈子，不把让她进门视作奇耻大辱。”

“这个嘛，”巴斯廷说，“我的计划已经定了。勒罗伊从来不许她回家过假期。我了解到，她如今正急着回家；我身为洛兰的律师，就是奉他之命来带她回家的。”

===Original===

“How will you do it?”

“I shall tell her her father is dangerously ill, and desires her to come as quickly as possible.”

“And what then?”

“Have her inventoried with the rest of the property.”

===Chinese===

“你打算怎么做？”

“我就告诉她，她父亲病危，盼她尽快回去。”

“然后再怎么样？”

“把她跟其余的财产一道登记造册。”

===Original===

“Don’t she know that her father is dead?”

“I think not,” said Bastine. “She is not in mourning, but appeared very lighthearted this morning, laughing and talking with two other girls. I was struck with her great beauty, and asked a gentleman who she was. He said, ‘Miss Leroy, of Mississippi.’ I think Lorraine has managed the affair so as to keep her in perfect ignorance of her father’s death. I don’t like the job, but I never let sentiment interfere with my work.”

Poor Iola! When she said slavery was not a bad thing, little did she think that she was destined to drink to its bitter dregs the cup she was so ready to press to the lips of others.

===Chinese===

“她不知道她父亲已经死了吗？”

“我想她还不知道，”巴斯廷说。“她没有戴孝，今天早晨还显得非常轻快，正跟另外两个姑娘又说又笑。她的美貌让我吃了一惊，便问一位先生她是谁。他说：‘密西西比的勒罗伊小姐。’我想，洛兰把这件事布置得天衣无缝，好让她对她父亲的死一无所知。我不喜欢这桩差事，可我从不让感情妨碍我的工作。”

可怜的伊奥拉！她说奴隶制不是什么坏事的时候，何曾想到，那只她如此乐于端到别人唇边的杯盏，她自己注定要饮尽它最苦的残渣。
===Original===

“How do you think she will take to her situation?” asked Camille.

“O, I guess,” said Bastine, “she will sulk and take it pretty hard at first; but if she is managed right she will soon get over it. Give her plenty of jewelry, fine clothes, and an easy time.”

“All this business must be conducted with the utmost secrecy and speed. Her mother could not have written to her, for she has been suffering with brain fever and nervous prostration since Leroy’s death. Lorraine knows her market value too well, and is too shrewd to let so much property pass out of his hands without making an effort to retain it.”

===Chinese===

“你看她受得了自己落到头上的处境吗？”卡米尔问。

“哦，我估摸着，”巴斯廷说，“她起先会怄气闹性子，一时很过不去；可只要调教得法，她很快就会想开的。多给她些珠宝首饰、漂亮衣裳，让她过舒坦日子就是了。”

“这桩事必须办得极为隐秘、极为迅速。她母亲不可能写过信给她，因为自勒罗伊死后，她一直害着脑热病和神经衰弱。洛兰太清楚她的市价了；他那样精明，绝不会不设法把这么大一笔财产攥在手里，任凭它从自己手中溜走。”

===Original===

“Has she any brothers or sisters?”

“Yes, a brother,” replied Bastine; “but he is at another school, and I have no orders from Lorraine in reference to him. If I can get the girl I am willing to let well enough alone. I dread the interview with the principal more than anything else. I am afraid he will hem and haw, and have his doubts. Perhaps, when he sees my letters and hears my story, I can pull the wool over his eyes.”

“But, Louis, this is a pitiful piece of business. I should hate to be engaged in it.”

===Chinese===

“她有兄弟姐妹吗？”

“有，一个兄弟，”巴斯廷答道；“不过他在另一所学校，洛兰没有给我关于他的任何指示。只要我能把姑娘弄到手，其余的事我愿意见好就收。我最犯怵的，是跟校长面谈那一关。我怕他会吞吞吐吐、满腹疑虑。不过，等他看了我的信、听了我的一套说辞，我或许能把他的眼睛蒙混过去。”

“可是，路易斯，这是桩可鄙的营生。要我沾手这种事，我可不干。”

===Original===

A deep flush of shame overspread for a moment the face of Lorraine’s attorney, as he replied: “I don’t like the job, but I have undertaken it, and must go through with it.”

“I see no ‘*must*’ about it. Were I in your place I would wash my hands of the whole business.”

“I can’t afford it,” was Bastine’s hard, businesslike reply. On the next morning after this conversation between these two young men, Louis Bastine presented himself to the principal of the academy, with the request that Iola be permitted to leave immediately to attend the sickbed of her father, who was dangerously ill. The principal hesitated, but while he was deliberating, a telegram, purporting to come from Iola’s mother, summoned Iola to her father’s bedside without delay. The principal, set at rest in regard to the truthfulness of the dispatch, not only permitted but expedited her departure.

===Chinese===

一时间，一阵深深的羞惭红潮漫上了这位洛兰律师的面庞，他答道：“我不喜欢这桩差事，可既然应承了下来，就得办到底。”

“我可看不出这里面有什么‘非做不可’。我要是处在你的地位，早就跟这整桩买卖洗手不干了。”

“这亏我可吃不起，”巴斯廷答道，语气冷硬，一副公事公办的腔调。这两个年轻人此番交谈之后的第二天早晨，路易斯·巴斯廷便去见学校校长，请求准许伊奥拉即刻离校，去侍奉她病危父亲的病榻。校长犹豫了；可正在他踌躇不决的当口，一封自称是伊奥拉母亲拍来的电报到了，召她毫不耽搁地赶到父亲床前。校长对电报的真实性放了心，不仅准她离校，还催她快些动身。

===Original===

Iola and Bastine took the earliest train, and traveled without pausing until they reached a large hotel in a Southern city. There they were obliged to wait a few hours until they could resume their journey, the train having failed to make connection. Iola sat in a large, lonely parlor, waiting for the servant to show her to a private room. She had never known a great sorrow. Never before had the shadows of death mingled with the sunshine of her life.

Anxious, travel-worn, and heavyhearted, she sat in an easy chair, with nothing to divert her from the grief and anxiety which rendered every delay a source of painful anxiety.

“Oh, I hope that he will be alive and growing better!” was the thought which kept constantly revolving in her mind, until she fell asleep. In her dreams she was at home, encircled in the warm clasp of her father’s arms, feeling her mother’s kisses lingering on her lips, and hearing the joyous greetings of the servants and Mammy Liza’s glad welcome as she folded her to her heart. From this dream of bliss she was awakened by a burning kiss pressed on her lips, and a strong arm encircling her. Gazing around and taking in the whole situation, she sprang from her seat, her eyes flashing with rage and scorn, her face flushed to the roots of her hair, her voice shaken with excitement, and every nerve trembling with angry emotion.

===Chinese===

伊奥拉和巴斯廷搭上了最早的一班火车，一路不曾停歇，直到南方一座城市里的一家大旅馆。因为火车误了接续的班次，他们不得不在那里等上几个钟头再继续赶路。伊奥拉坐在一间空荡荡的大客厅里，等着仆人来领她去单独的房间。她从来不曾经历过巨大的悲伤。死亡的阴影，从来不曾渗入过她生命的阳光。

她忧心忡忡、旅途劳顿、心头沉重，坐在一把安乐椅上；那份悲伤与焦灼，把每一次耽搁都变成一番痛苦的煎熬，身边又没有任何东西能替她排遣分毫。

“哦，但愿他还活着，还在一天天好转！”这个念头在她心里翻来覆去，直到她沉沉睡去。梦里，她回到了家，被父亲温暖的手臂搂在怀中，感觉母亲的亲吻还停留在唇上，听见仆人们欢欣的问候，听见丽扎嬷嬷（Mammy Liza）把她搂进怀里时那声欢喜的招呼。一场极乐的美梦，却被唇上印下的一个火烫的吻、箍上她身子的一条有力胳膊惊破了。她环顾四周，看清了眼前的全部情形，顿时从座位上跳了起来——双眼迸射着愤怒与轻蔑，脸一直红涨到发根，声音激动得发颤，每一根神经都挟着怒火发抖。

===Original===

“How dare you do such a thing! Don’t you know if my father were here he would crush you to the earth?”

“Not so fast, my lovely tigress,” said Bastine, “your father knew what he was doing when he placed you in my charge.”

“My father made a great mistake, if he thought he had put me in charge of a gentleman.”

“I am your guardian for the present,” replied Bastine. “I am to see you safe home, and then my commission ends.”

===Chinese===

“你怎么敢做出这种事！你可知道，要是我父亲在这里，他会把你碾进土里！”

“别这么性急，我可爱的小雌虎，”巴斯廷说，“你父亲把你交给我照管的时候，是一清二楚自己在做什么的。”

“我父亲若是以为把我托付给了一位绅士，那他可是犯了一个大错。”

“眼下我是你的监护人，”巴斯廷答道。“我要把你平平安安送到家，到那时，我的差事就算了结了。”
===Original===

“I wish it were ended now,” she exclaimed, trembling with anger and mortification. Her voice was choked by emotion, and broken by smothered sobs. Louis Bastine thought to himself, “she is a real spitfire, but beautiful even in her wrath.”

During the rest of her journey Iola preserved a most freezing reserve towards Bastine. At length the journey was ended. Pale and anxious she rode up the avenue which led to her home.

A strange silence pervaded the place. The servants moved sadly from place to place, and spoke in subdued tones. The windows were heavily draped with crape, and a funeral air pervaded the house.

===Chinese===

“我巴不得它现在就了结，”她喊道，因愤怒和羞辱而浑身发抖。她的嗓音被激动哽住，被强自压下的呜咽弄得断断续续。路易斯·巴斯廷心想：“她真是个火爆性子，可发起怒来也照样是美的。”

在余下的旅途中，伊奥拉对巴斯廷始终保持着冷若冰霜的疏远。旅程终于到了尽头。她面色苍白、忧心忡忡，乘车驶上了通往家门的那条林荫道。

一种异样的寂静笼罩着整座宅子。仆役们神色悲戚地在各处走动，说话都压着嗓子。窗户上层层叠叠挂满了黑纱，整座房子弥漫着一股丧亡的气氛。

===Original===

Mammy Liza met her at the door, and, with streaming eyes and convulsive sobs, folded her to her heart, as Iola exclaimed, in tones of hopeless anguish:﻿—

“Oh, papa’s dead!”

“Oh, my pore baby!” said mammy, “ain’t you hearn tell ’bout it? Yore par’s dead, an’ your mar’s bin drefful sick. She’s better now.”

Mam Liza stepped lightly into Mrs. Leroy’s room, and gently apprised her of Iola’s arrival. In a darkened room lay the stricken mother, almost distracted by her late bereavement.

===Chinese===

丽扎嬷嬷在门口迎住她，热泪滚滚、抽抽噎噎地把她搂进怀里；伊奥拉用绝望哀恸的声调喊道：

“哦，爸爸死了！”

“哦，俺的苦命宝贝儿！”嬷嬷说，“你没听说这事儿吗？你爹没了，你娘害了一场大病，病得可凶哪。她这会儿好些了。”

丽扎嬷嬷轻步走进勒罗伊太太的房间，柔声禀报伊奥拉到了。昏暗的房间里，躺着那位惨遭打击的母亲，新近的丧亲之痛几乎把她折磨得失了常态。

===Original===

“Oh, Iola,” she exclaimed, as her daughter entered, “is this you? I am so sorry you came.”

Then, burying her head in Iola’s bosom, she wept convulsively. “Much as I love you,” she continued, between her sobs, “and much as I longed to see you, I am sorry you came.”

“Why, mother,” replied Iola, astonished, “I received your telegram last Wednesday, and I took the earliest train I could get.”

“My dear child, I never sent you a telegram. It was a trick to bring you down South and reduce you to slavery.”

===Chinese===

“哦，伊奥拉，”女儿进屋时她失声喊道，“真的是你吗？你来了，我真难过啊。”

随后她把头埋进伊奥拉的怀里，抽搐般地痛哭起来。“我是那样爱你，”她呜咽着继续说，“又是那样盼望见你，可你来了，我真难过啊。”

“咦，妈妈，”伊奥拉吃惊地答道，“我上星期三收到了你的电报，便搭上了我能赶上的最早一班车。”

“我亲爱的孩子，我从来没有给你拍过电报。那是个圈套，为的是把你诱回南方，把你贬为奴隶。”

===Original===

Iola eyed her mother curiously. What did she mean? Had grief dethroned her reason? Yet her eye was clear, her manner perfectly rational.

Marie saw the astounded look on Iola’s face, and nerving herself to the task, said: “Iola, I must tell you what your father always enjoined me to be silent about. I did not think it was the wisest thing, but I yielded to his desires. I have negro blood in my veins. I was your father’s slave before I married him. His relatives have set aside his will. The courts have declared our marriage null and void and my manumission illegal, and we are all to be remanded to slavery.”

An expression of horror and anguish swept over Iola’s face, and, turning deathly pale, she exclaimed, “Oh, mother, it can’t be so! you must be dreaming!”

“No, my child; it is a terrible reality.”

===Chinese===

伊奥拉诧异地注视着母亲。她这话是什么意思？莫非悲伤把她的理智摧垮了？可她目光清明，举止又全然清醒。

玛丽看出伊奥拉脸上惊愕的神情，便强打起精神，说道：“伊奥拉，有一件事，你父亲总要我守口如瓶，如今我必须告诉你了。我从来不认为那样做最明智，可我依从了他的心意。我的血管里流着黑人的血。嫁给你父亲以前，我是他的奴隶。他的亲属已经把他的遗嘱撇在一旁。法庭已经宣布我们的婚姻无效、我解除奴籍的文书非法，我们全都要被发还为奴。”

恐怖与哀痛的神色掠过伊奥拉的脸；她变得死一般地苍白，喊道：“哦，妈妈，不可能是这样的！你准是在做梦！”

“不，我的孩子；这是可怕的现实。”
===Original===

Almost wild with agony, Iola paced the floor, as the fearful truth broke in crushing anguish upon her mind. Then bursting into a paroxysm of tears succeeded by peals of hysterical laughter, said:﻿—

“I used to say that slavery is right. I didn’t know what I was talking about.” Then growing calmer, she said, “Mother, who is at the bottom of this downright robbery?”

“Alfred Lorraine; I have always dreaded that man, and what I feared has come to pass. Your father had faith in him; I never had.”

===Chinese===

那可怕的真相挟着摧心裂肝的痛楚向她压来，伊奥拉几乎狂乱了，在地板上来回踱着步。接着，她迸发出一阵剧烈的痛哭，哭声之后又是一阵阵歇斯底里的大笑，她说道：

“我从前总说奴隶制是对的。那时候，我不知道自己说的是什么。”随后她平静了一些，又说道：“妈妈，这场彻头彻尾的抢劫，谁是幕后主使？”

“阿尔弗雷德·洛兰；我一直怕那个人怕得厉害，而我所惧怕的，终于临头了。你父亲信得过他；我从来不曾。”

===Original===

“But, mother, could we not contest his claim. You have your marriage certificate and papa’s will.”

“Yes, my dear child, but Judge Starkins has decided that we have no standing in the court, and no testimony according to law.”

“Oh, mother, what can I do?”

===Chinese===

“可是，妈妈，我们难道不能对他的权利提出抗辩吗？你有你的结婚证书，还有爸爸的遗嘱。”

“有的，我亲爱的孩子，可是斯塔金斯法官已经裁定，我们在法庭上没有立足之地，按法律也没有作证的资格。”

“哦，妈妈，我能做些什么呢？”

===Original===

“Nothing, my child, unless you can escape to the North.”

“And leave you?”

“Yes.”

“Mother, I will never desert you in your hour of trial. But can nothing be done? Had father no friends who would assist us?”

===Chinese===

“什么也做不了，我的孩子，除非你能逃到北方去。”

“撇下你吗？”

“是的。”

“妈妈，在你受难的时辰，我永远不会弃你而去。可难道真的无计可施了吗？父亲就没有一位肯帮我们的朋友吗？”

===Original===

“None that I know of. I do not think he had an acquaintance who approved of our marriage. The neighboring planters have stood so aloof from me that I do not know where to turn for either help or sympathy. I believe it was Lorraine who sent the telegram. I wrote to you as soon as I could after your father’s death, but fainted just as I finished directing the letter. I do not think he knows where your brother is, and, if possible, he must not know. If you can by any means, *do* send a letter to Harry and warn him not to attempt to come home. I don’t know how you will succeed, for Lorraine has us all under surveillance. But it is according to law.”

“What law, mother?”

“The law of the strong against the weak.”

===Chinese===

“据我所知，一个也没有。我想，他不曾有过一个赞成我们婚姻的相识。邻近的种植园主们待我那样疏远，我简直不知道该到哪里去求取帮助或同情。我相信，电报就是洛兰拍去的。你父亲去世之后，我一能提笔就给你写了信，可是刚写完信封上的地址，就晕了过去。我想他并不知道你兄弟在哪里，而且千万不能让他知道。你若能想出什么法子，务必给哈里捎一封信去，警告他切莫试图回家。我不知道你能有什么办法，因为洛兰把我们所有人都置于监视之下。可这是合乎法律的。”

“什么法律，妈妈？”

“强者对付弱者的法律。”
===Original===

“Oh, mother, it seems like a dreadful dream, a fearful nightmare! But I cannot shake it off. Where is Gracie?”

“The dear child has been running down ever since her papa’s death. She clung to me night and day while I had the brain fever, and could not be persuaded to leave me. She hardly ate anything for more than a week. She has been dangerously ill for several days, and the doctor says she cannot live. The fever has exhausted all her rallying power, and yet, dear as she is to me, I would rather consign her to the deepest grave than see her forced to be a slave.”

“So would I. I wish I could die myself.”

===Chinese===

“哦，妈妈，这就像一场可怕的梦，一场骇人的噩梦！可我怎么也甩不脱它。格雷茜在哪儿？”

“自打她爸爸去世，这亲爱的孩子就一天不如一天。我害脑热病的那阵子，她日夜寸步不离地守着我，怎么劝也不肯离开。有一个多星期，她几乎什么都没吃。这几天她病得十分危险，医生说她活不成了。热病耗尽了她全部的元气；可是，尽管她于我是那样亲爱，我宁可亲手把她送进最深的坟墓，也不愿眼看她被迫沦为奴隶。”

“我也是。但愿死去的倒是我。”

===Original===

“Oh, Iola, do not talk so. Strive to be a Christian, to have faith in the darkest hour. Were it not for my hope of heaven I couldn’t stand all this trouble.”

“Mother, are these people Christians who made these laws which are robbing us of our inheritance and reducing us to slavery? If this is Christianity I hate and despise it. Would the most cruel heathen do worse?”

“My dear child, I have not learned my Christianity from them. I have learned it at the foot of the cross, and from this book,” she said, placing a New Testament in Iola’s hands. “Some of the most beautiful lessons of faith and trust I have ever learned were from among our lowly people in their humble cabins.”

===Chinese===

“哦，伊奥拉，不要这样说话。要奋力做一个基督徒，在最黑暗的时刻也要持守信心。若不是我还有升入天堂的盼望，这一切苦难我怎么也撑不住。”

“妈妈，那些制定出这些法律、夺走我们的继承权、把我们贬为奴隶的人，是基督徒吗？如果这就是基督教，我憎恨它、鄙视它。最残忍的异教徒还能干出更坏的事来吗？”

“我亲爱的孩子，我的基督教不是从他们那里学来的。我是在十字架的脚下、从这本书里学来的。”说着，她把一部《新约全书》放进伊奥拉手中。“我此生学过的一些最美好的信心与信靠的功课，恰恰是从我们卑贱的族人、在他们简陋的小木屋里学来的。”

===Original===

“Mamma!” called a faint voice from the adjoining room. Marie immediately arose and went to the bedside of her sick child, where Mammy Liza was holding her faithful vigils. The child had just awakened from a fitful sleep.

“I thought,” she said, “that I heard Iola’s voice. Has she come?”

“Yes, darling; do you want to see her?”

===Chinese===

“妈妈！”隔壁房间里传来一声微弱的呼唤。玛丽立即起身，走到病孩子的床边，丽扎嬷嬷正在那里忠实地守夜。孩子刚从一阵时断时续的睡眠中醒来。

“我方才，”她说，“好像听见伊奥拉的声音了。她来了吗？”

“来了，宝贝儿；你想见她吗？”

===Original===

“Oh, yes,” she said, as a bright smile broke over her dying features.

Iola passed quickly into the room. Gracie reached out her thin, bloodless hand, clasped Iola’s palm in hers, and said: “I am so glad you have come. Dear Iola, stand by mother. You and Harry are all she has. It is not hard to die. You and mother and Harry must meet me in heaven.”

Swiftly the tidings went through the house that Gracie was dying. The servants gathered around her with tearful eyes, as she bade them all goodbye. When she had finished, and Mammy had lowered the pillow, an unwonted radiance lit up her eye, and an expression of ineffable gladness overspread her face, as she murmured: “It is beautiful, so beautiful!” Fainter and fainter grew her voice, until, without a struggle or sigh, she passed away beyond the power of oppression and prejudice.

===Chinese===

“哦，想见，”她说，垂死的面容上绽开了一抹明朗的微笑。

伊奥拉快步走进房间。格雷茜伸出瘦削无血色的手，把伊奥拉的手掌攥进自己手心，说道：“你来了，我真高兴。亲爱的伊奥拉，要站在妈妈身边。你和哈里就是她仅有的一切了。死并不难受。你和妈妈、还有哈里，一定要在天上与我相会。”

格雷茜命在旦夕的消息，顷刻传遍了整座宅子。仆人们含着泪围拢到她身旁，听她一一向大家道别。她道别完毕，嬷嬷把枕头放低了些，一种异样的光彩顿时点亮了她的眼睛，一种难以言喻的欢欣漫上了她的脸庞，她喃喃说道：“真美啊，真美啊！”她的声音越来越微弱，越来越微弱，终于，没有一声挣扎、没有一声叹息，她去了——去了那压迫与偏见的威力再也够不着的地方。

## XVIII Searching for Lost Ones / 第十八章 寻访失散的亲人

===Original===
To bind anew the ties which slavery had broken and gather together the remnants of his scattered family became the earnest purpose of Robert’s life. Iola, hopeful that in Robert she had found her mother’s brother, was glad to know she was not alone in *her* search. Having sent out lines of inquiry in different directions, she was led to hope, from some of the replies she had received, that her mother was living somewhere in Georgia.

Hearing that a Methodist conference was to convene in that State, and being acquainted with the bishop of that district, she made arrangements to accompany him thither. She hoped to gather some tidings of her mother through the ministers gathered from different parts of that State.

From her brother she had heard nothing since her father’s death. On his way to the conference, the bishop had an engagement to dedicate a church, near the city of C﻿—﻿—, in North Carolina. Iola was quite willing to stop there a few days, hoping to hear something of Robert Johnson’s mother. Soon after she had seated herself in the cars she was approached by a gentleman, who reached out his hand to her, and greeted her with great cordiality. Iola looked up, and recognized him immediately as one of her last patients at the hospital. It was none other than Robert Johnson.
===Chinese===
把奴隶制扯断的骨肉纽带重新接续起来，把四散飘零的家人残枝聚拢到一处，成了罗伯特热切的人生志向。伊奥拉满心期盼罗伯特就是自己母亲的弟弟；得知在“她”自己的寻访路上并非孤身一人，她很是欢喜。她已向四面八方发出了寻访的信函，从收到的几封回信里，她渐渐生出一丝指望：母亲或许就活在佐治亚的某个地方。

她听说卫理公会年会要在那个州召开，又与那个教区的主教相熟，便安排好随他同行。她盼望能借那些从全州各地聚来的牧师之口，打听到母亲的一点音讯。

自父亲去世后，她再没有听到弟弟的音讯。主教在赴年会的途中，还约好要到北卡罗来纳 C—— 城附近，为一座教堂主持献堂礼。伊奥拉很愿意在那儿盘桓几日，指望能听到些罗伯特·约翰逊母亲的消息。她刚在火车上坐定，便有一位先生朝她走来，向她伸出手，热忱地招呼她。伊奥拉抬起头，一眼就认出他来——他正是她在这所医院里照护过的最后一批病人之一。此人不是别人，正是罗伯特·约翰逊。

===Original===
“I am so glad to meet you,” he said. “I am on my way to C﻿—﻿— in search of my mother. I want to see the person who sold her last, and, if possible, get some clue to the direction in which she went.”

“And I,” said Iola, “am in search of *my* mother. I am convinced that when we find those for whom we are searching they will prove to be very nearly related. Mamma said, before we were parted, that her brother had a red spot on his temple. If I could see that spot I should rest assured that my mother is your sister.”

“Then,” said Robert, “I can give you that assurance,” and smilingly he lifted his hair from his temple, on which was a large, red spot.

“I am satisfied,” exclaimed Iola, fixing her eyes, beaming with hope and confidence, on Robert. “Oh, I am so glad that I can, without the least hesitation, accept your services to join with me in the further search. What are your plans?”

“To stop for awhile in C﻿—﻿—,” said Robert, “and gather all the information possible from those who sold and bought my mother. I intend to leave no stone unturned in searching for her.”
===Chinese===
“遇见您真是太好了，”他说。“我正要上 C—— 城去寻我的母亲。我想去见一见最后卖掉她的那个人，要是可能的话，再打听出她被带走的去向。”

“而我，”伊奥拉说，“寻找的却是‘我’的母亲。我深信，等我们找到了各自寻找的人，就会发觉彼此原是至亲。妈妈在我们离散之前说过，她的弟弟额角上有一块红记。我若能亲眼见到那块红记，便可以确信，我的母亲就是您的姐姐。”

“那么，”罗伯特说，“我就能给您这份确据。”他笑吟吟地掀起额角上的头发——那儿有一大块红记。

“这下我确信了！”伊奥拉喊道，一双满含希望与自信的眼睛定定地望着罗伯特。“啊，我真高兴，可以毫不迟疑地请您与我携手，一同继续寻访。您打算怎么做？”

“先在 C—— 城盘桓一阵，”罗伯特说，“从买卖过我母亲的那些人嘴里，把能问到的消息统统搜集起来。寻她的事，我打算千方百计，不把她寻到决不罢休。”
===Original===
“Oh, I *do* hope that you will succeed. I expect to stop over there a few days, and I shall be so glad if, before I leave, I hear your search has been crowned with success, or, a least, that you have been put on the right track. Although I was born and raised in the midst of slavery, I had not the least idea of its barbarous selfishness till I was forced to pass through it. But we lived so much alone I had no opportunity to study it, except on our own plantation. My father and mother were very kind to their slaves. But it was slavery, all the same, and I hate it, root and branch.”

Just then the conductor called out the station.

“We stop here,” said Robert. “I am going to see Mrs. Johnson, and hunt up some of my old acquaintances. Where do you stop?”

“I don’t know,” replied Iola. “I expect that friends will be here to meet us. Bishop B﻿—﻿—, permit me to introduce you to Mr. Robert Johnson, whom I have every reason to believe is my mother’s brother. Like myself, he is engaged in hunting up his lost relatives.”
===Chinese===
“啊，我真心盼望您马到成功。我打算在那儿停留几天，要是在我动身之前，能听到您的寻访大功告成，或者至少已经寻对了路子，那我就太高兴了。我虽是生在奴隶制之中、长在奴隶制之中，可直到被迫亲身经历它之前，我对它那种野蛮的自私毫无概念。只是我们家的日子过得太孤单了，除了自家的种植园，我没有机会见识它的别的面目。我的父母待他们的奴隶都很仁厚。可那终究还是奴隶制，我对它连根带枝地恨透了。”

正在这时，列车员报出了站名。

“我们到站了，”罗伯特说。“我要去看看约翰逊太太，再寻访几位旧日的相识。您在哪儿落脚？”

“我还不知道，”伊奥拉答道。“我想会有朋友来接我们。B—— 主教，请允许我向您介绍罗伯特·约翰逊先生——我有充分的理由相信，他就是我母亲的弟弟。他和我一样，也在寻访自己失散的亲人。”

===Original===
“And I,” said Robert, “am very much pleased to know that we are not without favorable clues.”

“Bishop,” said Iola, “Mr. Johnson wishes to know where I am to stop. He is going on an exploring expedition, and wishes to let me know the result.”

“We stop at Mrs. Allston’s, 313 New Street,” said the bishop. “If I can be of any use to you, I am at your service.”

“Thank you,” said Robert, lifting his hat, as he left them to pursue his inquiries about his long-lost mother.
===Chinese===
“我也很高兴，”罗伯特说，“知道我们并非没有可喜的线索。”

“主教，”伊奥拉说，“约翰逊先生想知道我在哪里落脚。他要去进行一番探访，也想把结果告诉我。”

“我们住在新街 313 号奥尔斯顿太太（Mrs. Allston）家，”主教说。“若有效劳之处，随时听凭吩咐。”

“谢谢您，”罗伯特说着，举起帽子告辞，离开他们，继续寻访他那失踪多年的母亲去了。
===Original===
Quickly he trod the old familiar streets which led to his former home. He found Mrs. Johnson, but she had aged very fast since the war. She was no longer the lithe, active woman, with her proud manner and resolute bearing. Her eye had lost its brightness, her step its elasticity, and her whole appearance indicated that she was slowly sinking beneath a weight of sorrow which was heavier far than her weight of years. When she heard that Robert had called to see her she was going to receive him in the hall, as she would have done any of her former slaves, but her mind immediately changed when she saw him. He was not the lighthearted, careless, mischief-loving Robby of former days, but a handsome man, with heavy moustache, dark, earnest eyes, and proud military bearing. He smiled, and reached out his hand to her. She hardly knew how to address him. To her colored people were either boys and girls, or “aunties and uncles.” She had never in her life addressed a colored person as “Mr. or Mrs.” To do so now was to violate the social customs of the place. It would be like learning a new language in her old age. Robert immediately set her at ease by addressing her under the old familiar name of “Miss Nancy.” This immediately relieved her of all embarrassment. She invited him into the sitting-room, and gave him a warm welcome.
===Chinese===
他快步踏上那些通往旧日宅邸的熟稔街道。他找到了约翰逊太太，可自开战以来，她老得极快。她已经不是当年那个身段轻捷、精力充沛的女人了——那股傲然的气派、决断的仪态都不见了。她的眼睛失去了光彩，脚步失去了弹性，整个人的模样都在表明：她正被一副哀伤的重担慢慢压垮，而那副担子，远比岁月加在她身上的分量沉重。听说罗伯特来看她，她本打算就在门厅里见他——从前她哪个旧日的奴隶来见，都是这样打发——可一见到他，她的主意立刻变了。眼前不再是当年那个乐呵呵、没心没肺、专爱淘气的罗比，而是一个俊美的男子，蓄着浓髭，一双黑眼睛诚恳动人，带着军人挺拔的气概。他微笑着向她伸出手来。她一时竟不知该怎么称呼他。在她的观念里，有色人种不是“男孩”“姑娘”，便是“大婶”“大叔”。她这辈子从没用“先生”或“太太”称呼过一个有色人种。如今要这样称呼，就是违背此地的社会习俗，就像要在迟暮之年再学一门新的语言。罗伯特立刻用“南希小姐”这个旧日熟稔的称呼向她问安，一下子就让她自在起来，所有的局促霎时烟消云散。她请他进了客厅，热诚地欢迎他。

===Original===
“Well, Robby,” she said, “I once thought that you would have been the last one to leave me. You know I never ill-treated you, and I gave you everything you needed. People said that I was spoiling you. I thought you were as happy as the days were long. When I heard of other people’s servants leaving them I used to say to myself, ‘I can trust my Bobby; he will stick to me to the last.’ But I fooled myself that time. Soon as the Yankee soldiers got in sight you left me without saying a word. That morning I came down into the kitchen and asked Linda, ‘Where’s Robert? Why hasn’t he set the table?’ She said ‘she hadn’t seen you since the night before.’ I thought maybe you were sick, and I went to see, but you were not in your room. I couldn’t believe at first that you were gone. Wasn’t I always good to you?”

“Oh, Miss Nancy,” replied Robert; “you were good, but freedom was better.”

“Yes,” she said, musingly, “I suppose I would have done the same. But, Robby, it did go hard with me at first. However, I soon found out that my neighbors had been going through the same thing. But its all over now. Let bygones be bygones. What are you doing now, and where are you living?”

“I am living in the city of P﻿—﻿—. I have opened a hardware store there. But just now I am in search of my mother and sister.”

“I hope that you may find them.”
===Chinese===
“唉，罗比，”她说，“我从前还以为，你准是最后一个才会离开我的人呢。你知道，我从没亏待过你，你要什么，我就给什么。人家都说我把你惯坏了。我还当你的日子有多长，你就多快活。每回听说别人家的仆人跑了，我总对自己说：‘我的博比信得过；他会陪着我到最后一刻。’可那一回，我是自欺欺人了。北佬的兵刚一露面，你一声不吭就撇下我走了。那天早晨我下厨房问琳达：‘罗伯特呢？他怎么还不摆桌子？’她说，‘打头天晚上起就没见着你’。我想你兴许是病了，就去瞧，可你不在房里。起初我简直不能相信你走了。我待你不是一向很好吗？”

“唉，南希小姐，”罗伯特答道；“您是好，可自由更好。”

“是啊，”她若有所思地说，“换了是我，怕也会这么办。不过罗比，起初那阵子我心里实在不好受。好在没多久我就明白，左邻右舍谁家不曾经历这一遭呢。可如今都过去了。过去的事，就让它过去吧。你现在做什么营生？住在哪儿？”

“我住在 P—— 城，在那儿开了一家五金行。只是眼下，我正在寻访我的母亲和姐姐。”

“盼你能找到他们。”
===Original===
“How long,” asked Robert, “do you think it has been since they left here?”

“Let me see; it must have been nearly thirty years. You got my letter?”

“Yes, ma’am; thank you.”

“There have been great changes since you left here,” Mrs. Johnson said. “Gundover died, and a number of colored men have banded together, bought his plantation, and divided it among themselves. And I hear they have a very nice settlement out there. I hope, since the Government has set them free, that they will succeed.”
===Chinese===
“你看，”罗伯特问，“他们离开这儿有多久了？”

“让我想想；总有三十年了吧。你收到我的信了？”

“收到了，太太；谢谢您。”

“自你走后，这地方变了许多，”约翰逊太太说。“冈多弗死了，一批有色人男子凑在一起，买下了他的种植园，自己分了。我听说他们在那边有一片顶好的聚居区。联邦政府既然给了他们自由，我盼着他们能兴旺起来。”

===Original===
After Robert’s interview with Mrs. Johnson he thought he would visit the settlement and hunt up his old friends. He easily found the place. It was on a clearing in Gundover’s woods, where Robert and Uncle Daniel had held their last prayer-meeting. Now the gloomy silence of those woods was broken by the hum of industry, the murmur of cheerful voices, and the merry laughter of happy children. Where they had trodden with fear and misgiving, freedmen walked with light and bounding hearts. The schoolhouse had taken the place of the slave-pen and auction-block. “How is yer, ole boy?” asked one laborer of another.

“Everything is lobly,” replied the other. The blue sky arching overhead and the beauty of the scenery justified the expression.
===Chinese===
同约翰逊太太叙过话，罗伯特想着该去聚居区走一趟，寻访寻访老朋友们。他很容易就找到了那地方。那是在冈多弗家林子里的一片空场上——当初，罗伯特和丹尼尔大叔就是在那儿开了他们最后一次祈祷会。如今，林中那阴沉的寂静被打破了：处处是劳作的嗡嗡声、欢快的人语，还有幸福孩子们的笑声。从前他们怀着恐惧与忐忑踏过的地方，如今获得自由的人们怀着轻快跳跃的心走来走去。校舍取代了奴隶围栏和拍卖台。“你好哇，老伙计？”一个做工的向另一个打招呼。

“样样都美得很呐。”另一个答道。头顶拱起的蓝天与四下的景致之美，足证这话并非虚言。
===Original===
Gundover had died soon after the surrender. Frank Anderson had grown reckless and drank himself to death. His brother Tom had been killed in battle. Their mother, who was Gundover’s daughter, had died insane. Their father had also passed away. The defeat of the Confederates, the loss of his sons, and the emancipation of his slaves, were blows from which he never recovered. As Robert passed leisurely along, delighted with the evidences of thrift and industry which constantly met his eye, he stopped to admire a garden filled with beautiful flowers, clambering vines, and rustic adornments.
===Chinese===
冈多弗在投降之后不久便死了。弗兰克·安德森（Frank Anderson）变得放浪形骸，把自己喝死了；他的弟弟汤姆阵亡在战场上；他们的母亲——冈多弗家的女儿——疯了，也死了；他们的父亲也故去了。邦联军的战败、儿子的丧亡、奴隶的解放，对他都是接连的重击，他再也没能缓过来。罗伯特一路缓缓行去，眼前不断见到勤俭兴家的气象，心中欢喜；走到一处，他停下脚步，欣赏起一座花园来——园中开满美丽的花朵，藤蔓四处攀缘，还点缀着村野风味的装饰。

===Original===
On the porch sat an elderly woman, darning stockings, the very embodiment of content and good humor. Robert looked inquiringly at her. On seeing him, she almost immediately exclaimed, “Shore as I’se born, dat’s Robert! Look yere, honey, whar did yer come from? I’ll gib my head fer a choppin’ block ef dat ain’t Miss Nancy’s Bob. Ain’t yer our Bobby? Shore yer is.”

“Of course I am,” responded Robert. “It isn’t anybody else. How did you know me?”

“How did I know yer? By dem mischeebous eyes, ob course. I’d a knowed yer if I had seed yer in Europe.”

“In Europe, Aunt Linda? Where’s that?”

“I don’t know. I specs its some big city, somewhar. But yer looks jis’ splendid. Yer looks good ’nuff ter kiss.”
===Chinese===
门廊上坐着一位上了年纪的妇人，正在织补长袜，活脱脱是知足与和气的化身。罗伯特探询地望着她。她一见他，几乎立刻叫了起来：“俺敢拿命赌，那是罗伯特！你瞧，宝贝，你打哪儿来的呀？要说那不是南希小姐家的鲍勃，俺就把脑袋砍下来给你当砧板！你不就是俺们的博比吗？保准没错。”

“当然是我，”罗伯特应道。“不是我又能是谁。您是怎么认出我来的？”

“俺咋认出你的？还不是凭那双淘气眼睛。你就是到了欧洲，俺也能把你一眼认出来。”

“在欧洲？琳达大婶，那是什么地方？”

“俺不晓得。俺琢磨着，那是个顶大的城，在哪块儿就不知道了。可你瞧着真体面。瞧着就叫人想亲一口。”
===Original===
“Oh, Aunt Linda, don’t say that. You make me blush.”

“Oh you go ’long wid yer. I specs yer’s got a nice little wife up dar whar yer comes from, dat kisses yer ebery day, an’ Sunday, too.”

“Is that the way your old man does you?”

“Oh, no, not a bit. He isn’t one ob de kissin’ kine. But sit down,” she said, handing Robert a chair. “Won’t yer hab a glass ob milk? Boy, I’se a libin’ in clover. Neber ’spected ter see sich good times in all my born days.”

“Well, Aunt Linda,” said Robert, seating himself near her, and drinking the glass of milk which she had handed him, “how goes the battle? How have you been getting on since freedom?”
===Chinese===
“哦，琳达大婶，快别这么说。您都要说得我脸红了。”

“哦，去你的吧。俺琢磨着，你在老家那边准娶了个俊俏的小媳妇，天天亲你，礼拜天也不落。”

“您当家的也是这样亲您的吗？”

“哦，不，他才不呢。他可不是那号爱亲嘴的人。可你坐下呀，”她说着，递给罗伯特一把椅子。“喝杯牛奶好不好？乖乖，俺如今可是掉进福窝里啦。俺活了一辈子，做梦也没想到能过上这样的好日子。”

“唉，琳达大婶，”罗伯特说着，在她身旁坐下，喝着她递来的那杯牛奶，“日子过得怎么样？自由以后，你们一向可好？”

===Original===
“Oh, fust rate, fust rate! Wen freedom com’d I jist lit out ob Miss Johnson’s kitchen soon as I could. I wanted ter re’lize I war free, an’ I couldn’t, tell I got out er de sight and soun’ ob ole Miss. When de war war ober an’ de sogers war still stopping’ yere, I made pies an’ cakes, sole em to de sogers, an’ jist made money han’ ober fist. An’ I kep’ on a workin’ an’ a savin’ till my ole man got back from de war wid his wages and his bounty money. I felt right set up an’ mighty big wen we counted all dat money. We had neber seen so much money in our lives befo’, let alone hab it fer ourselbs. An’ I sez, ‘John, you take dis money an’ git a nice place wid it.’ An’ he sez, ‘Dere’s no use tryin’, kase dey don’t want ter sell us any lan’.’ Ole Gundover said, ’fore he died, dat he would let de lan’ grow up in trees ’fore he’d sell it to us. An’ dere war Mr. Brayton; he buyed some lan’ and sole it to some cullud folks, an’ his ole frien’s got so mad wid him dat dey wouldn’t speak ter him, an’ he war borned down yere. I tole ole Miss Anderson’s daughter dat we wanted ter git some homes ob our ownselbs. She sez, ‘Den you won’t want ter work for us?’ Jis’ de same as ef we could eat an’ drink our houses. I tell yer, Robby, dese white folks don’t know eberything.”

“That’s a fact, Aunt Linda.”

“Den I sez ter John, ‘wen one door shuts anoder opens.’ An’ shore ’nough, ole Gundover died, an’ his place war all in debt, an’ had to be sole. Some Jews bought it, but dey didn’t want to farm it, so dey gib us a chance to buy it. Dem Jews hez been right helpful to cullud people wen dey hab lan’ to sell. I reckon dey don’t keer who buys it so long as dey gits de money. Well, John didn’t gib in at fust; didn’t want to let on his wife knowed more dan he did, an’ dat he war ruled ober by a woman. Yer know he is an’ ole Firginian, an’ some ob dem ole Firginians do so lub to rule a woman. But I kep’ naggin at him, till I specs he got tired of my tongue, an’ he went and buyed dis piece ob lan’. Dis house war on it, an’ war all gwine to wrack. It used to belong to John’s ole marster. His wife died right in dis house, an’ arter dat her husband went right to de dorgs; an’ now he’s in de pore-house. My! but ain’t dem tables turned. When we knowed it war our own, warn’t my ole man proud! I seed it in him, but he wouldn’t let on. Ain’t you men powerful ’ceitful?”

“Oh, Aunt Linda, don’t put me in with the rest!”
===Chinese===
“哦，好得很，好得很！自由一来，俺撒腿就逃出了约翰逊太太的厨房。俺要想真切觉得自个儿是自由了，就非得走出老女主人的眼皮和耳朵不可。仗打完、大兵们还驻在这儿那阵子，俺做馅饼、烤蛋糕，卖给他们，钱挣得一把一把地往兜里流。俺就这么又干又攒，直攒到俺们老头子带着他的军饷和赏金从部队回来。数那一堆钱的时候，俺们心里那份得意、那份神气哟。俺们活到那阵子，还从没见过那么些钱，更别说归自个儿所有了。俺就说：‘约翰，你拿这钱去置个好地方吧。’他说：‘置也是白搭，他们不愿意卖地给俺们。’老冈多弗死前说过，他宁可让地长满树木，也不卖给俺们。还有布雷顿先生（Mr. Brayton）；他买了些地转卖给有色人，他的老朋友们气得从此不跟他说话，而他还是本地土生土长的。俺跟老安德森家的小姐说，俺们想置几处自个儿的家业。她说：‘那你们往后就不给俺们干活啦？’——就跟俺们能把房子当饭吃、当水喝似的。俺告诉你，罗比，这些白人可不是什么都懂。”

“可不是嘛，琳达大婶。”

“后来俺就跟约翰说：‘一扇门关上了，就有一扇门开。’可不是嘛，老冈多弗死了，他那产业欠了一屁股债，只好变卖。几个犹太人把它买了下来，可他们不想自己种，就给了俺们一个买的机会。那些犹太人手头有地要卖的时候，对有色人可真帮了大忙。俺琢磨，只要钱能到手，他们才不管谁来买呢。唉，约翰起初不肯依；他不想露出老婆比他懂得多、自个儿叫个女人管着的样子。你知道，他是个老弗吉尼亚人，有些老弗吉尼亚人就是那样爱管着女人。可俺一个劲儿地念叨他，俺琢磨他是叫俺这张嘴念烦了，就去把这块地买了下来。这房子就在地上，本来都快塌完了。这宅子从前是约翰的老主人的。他的太太就是在这座房子里咽的气；打那以后，她丈夫就一路烂了下去；如今进了济贫院。天哪！这世道可真是翻了个个儿。俺们得知这地方是自个儿的了，俺们老头子那份得意哟！俺瞧得出来，他偏不肯露在脸上。你们这些男人，是不是都这么会装？”

“哦，琳达大婶，可别把我也算在他们里头！”
===Original===
“I don’t know ’bout dat. Put you all in de bag for ’ceitfulness, an’ I don’t know which would git out fust.”

“Well, Aunt Linda, I suppose by this time you know how to read and write?”

“No, chile, sence freedom’s com’d I’se bin scratchin’ too hard to get a libin’ to put my head down to de book.”

“But, Aunt Linda, it would be such company when your husband is away, to take a book. Do you never get lonesome?”

“Chile, I ain’t got no time ter get lonesome. Ef you had eber so many chickens to feed, an’ pigs squealin’ fer somethin’ ter eat, an’ yore ducks an’ geese squakin’ ’roun’ yer, yer wouldn’t hab time ter git lonesome.”
===Chinese===
“那可说不准。把你们统统装进一条‘会装’的袋子里，俺也闹不清谁会头一个钻出来。”

“那么，琳达大婶，我琢磨着，到这会儿您总学会读书写字了吧？”

“没有，孩子，自打自由以来，俺光顾着刨食谋生计了，脑袋就没顾得上埋到书本子里去。”

“可是，琳达大婶，您当家的不在家的时候，有本书作伴多好。您就从不觉得冷清吗？”

“孩子，俺哪有工夫冷清。你要是养着那么一大群鸡等着喂，猪嗷嗷叫着讨食，鸭子鹅子围着你嘎嘎乱叫，你也不会有工夫冷清的。”

===Original===
“But, Aunt Linda, you might be sick for months, and think what a comfort it would be if you could read your Bible.”

“Oh, I could hab prayin’ and singin’. Dese people is mighty good ’bout prayin’ by de sick. Why, Robby, I think it would gib me de hysterics ef I war to try to git book larnin’ froo my pore ole head. How long is yer gwine to stay? An’ whar is yer stoppin?”

“I got here today,” said Robert, “but I expect to stay several days.”
===Chinese===
“可是，琳达大婶，您万一病上几个月呢——想想吧，要是那时候您能自己读《圣经》，会是多大的安慰。”

“哦，俺有祷告，还有唱诗。这些人在病人床边祷告，可热心啦。嗐，罗比，俺寻思，要把书本子学问硬塞进俺这可怜的老脑壳，非把俺逼出癔症来不可。你这回打算待多久？眼下住在哪儿？”

“我今天刚到，”罗伯特说，“不过打算待上几天。”
===Original===
“Well, I wants yer to meet my ole man, an’ talk ’bout ole times. Couldn’t yer come an’ stop wid me, or isn’t my house sniptious ’nuff?”

“Yes, thank you; but there is a young lady in town whom I think is my niece, my sister’s daughter, and I want to be with her all I can.”

“Your niece! Whar did you git any niece from?”

“Don’t you remember,” asked Robert, “that my mother had a little daughter, when Mrs. Johnson sold her? Well, I believe this young lady is that daughter’s child.”

“Laws a marcy!” exclaimed Aunt Linda, “yer don’t tell me so! Whar did yer ketch up wid her?”

“I met her first,” said Robert, “at the hospital here, when our poor Tom was dying; and when I was wounded at Five Forks she attended me in the field hospital there. She was just as good as gold.”
===Chinese===
“唉，俺想叫你见见俺们老头子，聊聊老年间的事。你就不能上俺家来住？还是嫌俺这房子不够气派？”

“好的，谢谢您；只是城里有一位年轻小姐，我想是我的外甥女——我姐姐的女儿——我想尽量陪着她。”

“你的外甥女！你打哪儿冒出个外甥女来？”

“您不记得了吗，”罗伯特问，“约翰逊太太卖掉我母亲的时候，我母亲还带着一个年幼的女儿？我如今相信，这位年轻小姐就是那个女儿的孩子。”

“我的老天爷！”琳达大婶叫了起来，“你竟说这个！你是在哪儿碰上她的？”

“我头一回遇见她，”罗伯特说，“是在本城的医院里，我们可怜的汤姆临终的时候；后来我在五叉口负了伤，又是她在那边的野战医院看护我。她真是百里挑一的好心肠。”

===Original===
“Well, did I eber! You jis’ fotch dat chile to see me, ef she ain’t too fine. I’se pore, but I’se clean, an’ I ain’t forgot how ter git up good dinners. Now, I wants ter hab a good talk ’bout our feller-sarvants.”

“Yes, and I,” said Robert, “want to hear all about Uncle Daniel, and Jennie, and Uncle Ben Tunnel.”

“Well, I’se got lots an’ gobs ter tell yer. I’se kep’ track ob dem all. Aunt Katie died an’ went ter hebben in a blaze ob glory. Uncle Dan’el stayed on de place till Marse Robert com’d back. When de war war ober he war smashed all ter pieces. I did pity him from de bottom ob my heart. When he went ter de war he looked so brave an’ han’some; an’ wen he com’d back he looked orful. ’Fore he went he gib Uncle Dan’el a bag full ob money ter take kere ob. ’An wen he com’d back Uncle Dan’el gibed him ebery cent ob it. It warn’t ebery white pusson he could hab trusted wid it. ’Cause yer know, Bobby, money’s a mighty temptin’ thing. Dey tells me dat Marster Robert los’ a heap ob property by de war; but Marse Robert war always mighty good ter Uncle Dan’el and Aunt Katie. He war wid her wen she war dyin’ an’ she got holt his han’ an’ made him promise dat he would meet her in glory. I neber seed anybody so happy in my life. She singed an’ prayed ter de last. I tell you dis ole time religion is good ’nuff fer me. Mr. Robert didn’t stay yere long arter her, but I beliebs he went all right. But ’fore he went he looked out fer Uncle Dan’el. Did you see dat nice little cabin down dere wid de green shutters an’ nice little garden in front? Well, ’fore Marse Robert died he gib Uncle Dan’el dat place, an’ Miss Mary and de chillen looks arter him yet; an’ he libs jis’ as snug as a bug in a rug.
