I’se gwine ter axe him ter take supper wid you. He’ll be powerful glad ter see you.”
===Chinese===
“嗬，可不是嘛！只要那孩子不嫌俺们寒碜，你就带她来见见俺。俺家是穷，可俺们干净，俺也没忘了怎么整治出一桌好饭来。俺还想痛痛快快聊聊俺们那些一块儿当差的老伙计呢。”

“是啊，我也想，”罗伯特说，“听听丹尼尔大叔、金妮，还有本·滕纳尔大叔（Uncle Ben Tunnel）的近况。”

“唉，俺有一大筐话要跟你讲。他们的事儿，俺个个都记着呢。凯蒂大婶（Aunt Katie）死了，裹着一团荣耀的光焰升了天。丹尼尔大叔在庄园上一直守到罗伯特少爷回来。仗打完他回来的时候，整个人都垮了。俺打心眼里可怜他。他出征的时候多威武、多体面；回来的时候那模样，惨得没法瞧。走之前，他交给丹尼尔大叔一袋子钱，托他保管。等他回来，丹尼尔大叔一个子儿不少地都还给了他。这钱可不是随便哪个白人都信得过的。你知道的，博比，钱这东西，顶能撩动人的心。听人说，罗伯特少爷打这场仗里折了好些家产；可罗伯特少爷待丹尼尔大叔和凯蒂大婶一向好得很。她咽气的时候他守在跟前，她攥住他的手，要他应许日后在荣耀里与她相见。俺这辈子再没见过那么喜乐的人。她唱着、祷告着，直到最后一口气。俺跟你说，这种老派的信仰，对俺就尽够好了。罗伯特先生在她之后没在这儿待多久，可俺信他走得不差。临走之前，他把丹尼尔大叔安顿好了。你瞧见下边那座绿百叶窗、门前带个小花园的漂亮小木屋没有？喏，罗伯特少爷临死之前，把那地方给了丹尼尔大叔，如今玛丽小姐和孩子们还照看着他呢；他过得呀，就像钻在绒毯里的小虫子一样舒坦。俺打算去请他来陪你吃晚饭。他见了你，保管乐坏了。”
===Original===
“Do you ever go to see old Miss?” asked Robert.

“Oh, yes; I goes ebery now and den. But she’s jis’ fell froo. Ole Johnson jis’ drunk hisself to death. He war de biggest guzzler I eber seed in my life. Why, dat man he drunk up ebery thing he could lay his han’s on. Sometimes he would go ’roun’ tryin’ to borrer money from pore cullud folks. ’Twas rale drefful de way dat pore feller did frow hisself away. But drink did it all. I tell you, Bobby, dat drink’s a drefful thing wen it gits de upper han’ ob you. You’d better steer clar ob it.”

“That’s so,” assented Robert.
===Chinese===
“您还常去看老女主人吗？”罗伯特问。

“哦，去；俺隔三差五就去一趟。可她是彻底败落啦。老约翰逊把自个儿给灌死了。俺活了这辈子，就没见过比他更能灌的酒缸。嗐，那个人哪，凡是手能够得着的东西，他都喝了个精光。有时候他还挨家挨户，想跟穷苦的有色人借钱呢。那可怜虫那样糟践自个儿，真是惨呐。可全是酒闹的。俺告诉你，博比，酒这东西一旦骑到你头上，可了不得。你顶好离它远远的。”

“说的是，”罗伯特附和道。

===Original===
“I know’d Miss Nancy’s fadder and mudder. Dey war mighty rich. Some ob de real big bugs. Marse Jim used to know dem, an’ come ober ter de plantation, an’ eat an’ drink wen he got ready, an’ stay as long as he choose. Ole Cousins used to have wine at dere table ebery day, an’ Marse Jim war mighty fon’ ob dat wine, an’ sometimes he would drink till he got quite boozy. Ole Cousins liked him bery well, till he foun’ out he wanted his darter, an’ den he didn’t want him fer rags nor patches. But Miss Nancy war mighty headstrong, an’ allers liked to hab her own way; an’ dis time she got it. But didn’t she step her foot inter it? Ole Johnson war mighty han’some, but when dat war said all war said. She run’d off an’ got married, but wen she got down she war too spunkey to axe her pa for anything. Wen you war wid her, yer know she only took big bugs. But wen de war com’d ’roun’ it tore her all ter pieces, an’ now she’s as pore as Job’s turkey. I feel’s right sorry fer her. Well, Robby, things is turned ’roun’ mighty quare. Ole Mistus war up den, an’ I war down; now, she’s down, an’ I’se up. But I pities her, ’cause she warn’t so bad arter all. De wuss thing she eber did war ta sell your mudder, an’ she wouldn’t hab done dat but she snatched de whip out ob her han’ an gib her a lickin’. Now I belieb in my heart she war ’fraid ob your mudder arter dat. But we women had ter keep ’em from whippin’ us, er dey’d all de time been libin’ on our bones. She had no man ter whip us ’cept dat ole drunken husband ob hern, an’ he war allers too drunk ter whip hisself. He jis’ wandered off, an’ I reckon he died in somebody’s pore-house. He warn’t no ’count nohow you fix it. Weneber I goes to town I carries her some garden sass, er a little milk an’ butter. An’ she’s mighty glad ter git it. I ain’t got nothin’ agin her. She neber struck me a lick in her life, an’ I belieb in praising de bridge dat carries me ober. Dem Yankees set me free, an’ I thinks a powerful heap ob dem. But it does rile me ter see dese mean white men comin’ down yere an’ settin’ up dere grog-shops, tryin’ to fedder dere nests sellin’ licker to pore culled people. Deys de bery kine ob men dat used ter keep dorgs to ketch de runaways. I’d be chokin’ fer a drink ’fore I’d eber spen’ a cent wid dem, a spreadin’ dere traps to git de black folks’ money. You jis’ go down town ’fore sun up to-morrer mornin’ an’ you see ef dey don’t hab dem bars open to sell dere drams to dem hard workin’ culled people ’fore dey goes ter work. I thinks some niggers is mighty big fools.”
===Chinese===
“俺认得南希小姐的爹娘。他们阔得很，都是有头有脸的大人物。吉姆少爷从前跟他们相熟，常到种植园来，想什么时候吃就什么时候吃，想什么时候喝就什么时候喝，爱待多久待多久。老卡辛斯（Cousins）家天天饭桌上摆着葡萄酒，吉姆少爷顶贪那口酒，有时候喝得醉醺醺的。老卡辛斯起先挺喜欢他，等发觉他打自家女儿的主意，就连块破布烂片也不肯给他了。可南希小姐犟得很，从来都要由着自个儿的性子；这一回她还真由成了。可她这不是一脚踩进泥坑里去了吗？老约翰逊当年俊是俊，可除了俊也就说不出别的了。她私奔着嫁了，可等日子落下来，她又死要面子，不肯朝她爹开口要一个钱。想当年你在她手下的时候，你知道的，她只跟有头有脸的大人物往来。可战争一转到这儿，就把她整个儿扯碎了，如今她穷得活像约伯家那只火鸡。俺心里着实替她难过。唉，罗比，世道真是怪得很。从前老女主人在上头，俺在底下；如今她下来了，俺上去了。可俺还是可怜她，她到底不算坏。她这辈子干的最坏的一件事，就是把你的妈妈卖了；要不是你的妈妈从她手里夺过鞭子、反倒抽了她一顿，她也不至于那么干。俺打心眼里相信，打那以后她就怕你的妈妈了。可俺们女人家总得不叫他们鞭打，不然他们就成天骑在俺们骨头上过活了。她家里没有一个男人可以替她鞭打俺们，除了她那个喝醉酒的老丈夫，可他又成天醉得连自个儿都抽不动。他就是那么游荡出去的，俺琢磨他死在哪家的济贫院里了。横竖他就是个不中用的货。俺每回进城，总给她捎点儿自家园子的菜蔬，要么一点儿牛奶黄油。她得了这些东西，高兴得很。俺跟她没仇没怨。她这辈子没抽过俺一鞭子，俺信“驮俺过河的桥，俺就得夸它”这个理儿。是北佬们给了俺自由，俺心里顶顶感念他们。可一瞧见这些下作的白人男人跑到俺们这地方来，开起一个个烧酒店，卖烈酒给穷苦的有色人、往自个儿窝里搂羽毛，俺这气就不打一处来。他们就是从前养狗抓逃奴的那号人。俺宁可渴得嗓子冒烟，也绝不掏一个子儿给他们，去上他们那些圈套的当，叫他们把黑人的钱捞走。你明儿天亮前下城里去瞧瞧，看他们是不是不等卖力气的有色人上工，就先把柜台支起来、一杯杯烧酒卖给他们。俺看有些黑鬼就是天大的傻瓜。”
===Original===
“Oh, Aunt Linda, don’t run down your race. Leave that for the white people.”

“I ain’t runnin’ down my people. But a fool’s a fool, wether he’s white or black. An’ I think de nigger who will spen’ his hard-earned money in dese yere new grog-shops is de biggest kine ob a fool, an’ I sticks ter dat. You know we didn’t hab all dese low places in slave times. An’ what is dey fer, but to get the people’s money. An’ its a shame how dey do sling de licker ’bout ’lection times.”

“But don’t the temperance people want the colored people to vote the temperance ticket?”

“Yes, but some ob de culled people gits mighty skittish ef dey tries to git em to vote dare ticket ’lection time, an’ keeps dem at a proper distance wen de ’lection’s ober. Some ob dem say dere’s a trick behine it, an’ don’t want to tech it. Dese white folks could do a heap wid de culled folks ef dey’d only treat em right.”
===Chinese===
“哦，琳达大婶，别作践自个儿的种族。把那留给白人去干吧。”

“俺可不是作践俺的族人。可傻子就是傻子，管他皮肤是白是黑。俺看，哪个黑鬼肯把血汗钱花进这些新开的烧酒店，他就是傻瓜里头号等的傻瓜，这话俺认死了不改。你知道，做奴隶那阵子俺们可没有这些下等去处。它们生来图什么？不就是为了捞人们口袋里的钱。一到选举的时候，他们那样把酒泼来泼去，真是造孽。”

“可戒酒会的人不是想让有色人投戒酒党的票吗？”

“是想啊，可有些有色人一见他们选举的时候来拉票，就警惕得不得了；等人投了票，选举一过，他们又把人客客气气地晾在一边。有些人心说这背后有猫腻，就不肯沾它。这些白人只要真心好好待有色人，本来能帮上大忙的。”

===Original===
“When our people say there is a trick behind it,” said Robert, “I only wish they could see the trick before it﻿—the trick of worse than wasting their money, and of keeping themselves and families poorer and more ignorant than there is any need for them to be.”

“Well, Bobby, I beliebs we might be a people ef it warn’t for dat mizzable drink. An’ Robby, I jis’ tells yer what I wants; I wants some libe man to come down yere an’ splain things ter dese people. I don’t mean a politic man, but a man who’ll larn dese people how to bring up dere chillen, to keep our gals straight, an’ our boys from runnin’ in de saloons an’ gamblin’ dens.”

“Don’t your preachers do that?” asked Robert.
===Chinese===
“我们的人说这背后有猫腻，”罗伯特说，“我只盼他们能看清摆在眼前的猫腻——那猫腻可不止是白糟蹋钱，还叫他们自己和一家老小，穷得没有必要地穷，无知得没有必要地无知。”

“唉，博比，俺琢磨，要不是那害人的酒，俺们没准真能撑起一个民族来。罗比，俺索性告诉你俺盼什么吧：俺盼有个精明强干的活人上俺们这地方来，给这些人把事理讲讲明白。俺说的不是政客，是一个能教这些人怎么拉扯儿女的人——教俺们的闺女守住清白，教俺们的儿子别往酒馆里、赌窟里钻。”

“你们的传道人不做这些吗？”罗伯特问。
===Original===
“Well, some ob dem does, an’ some ob dem doesn’t. An’ wen dey preaches, I want dem to practice wat dey preach. Some ob dem says dey’s called, but I jis’ thinks laziness called some ob dem. An’ I thinks since freedom come deres some mighty pore sticks set up for preachers. Now dere’s John Anderson, Tom’s brudder; you ’member Tom.”

“Yes; as brave a fellow and as honest as ever stepped in shoe leather.”

“Well, his brudder war mighty diffrent. He war down in de lower kentry wen de war war ober. He war mighty smart, an’ had a good headpiece, an’ a orful glib tongue. He set up store an’ sole whisky, an’ made a lot ob money. Den he wanted ter go to de legislatur. Now what should he do but make out he’d got ’ligion, an’ war called to preach. He had no more ’ligion dan my ole dorg. But he had money an’ built a meetin’ house, whar he could hole meeting, an’ hab funerals; an’ you know cullud folks is mighty great on funerals. Well dat jis’ tuck wid de people, an’ he got ’lected to de legislatur. Den he got a fine house, an’ his ole wife warn’t good ’nuff for him. Den dere war a young schoolteacher, an’ he begun cuttin’ his eyes at her. But she war as deep in de mud as he war in de mire, an’ he jis’ gib up his ole wife and married her, a fusty thing. He war a mean ole hypocrit, an’ I wouldn’t sen’ fer him to bury my cat. Robby, I’se down on dese kine ob preachers like a thousand bricks.”
===Chinese===
“唉，有的做，有的不做。他们既然站出来讲道，俺就要他们把讲的那些行出来。有的说自己蒙了召唤，俺看呐，是懒骨头召唤了他们里头的一些人。俺还琢磨着，自打自由以来，有些糊不上墙的烂木头也立起来充传道人了。就说约翰·安德森（John Anderson）吧，汤姆的兄弟；你还记得汤姆吧。”

“记得；论勇敢、论老实，天底下穿鞋走路的人里也挑不出几个比他强的。”

“唉，他这个兄弟可大不一样。战争结束那阵子，他在下边的乡里。他人精明得很，脑瓜好使，还有一张滑溜得吓人的嘴。他开了铺子卖威士忌，挣下一大笔钱。跟着他就想进州议会。你猜他干了什么？他装作得了教，蒙了传道的召唤。他肚子里的信仰还赶不上俺家的老狗多。可他有钱，盖了座会堂，好在里头主持聚会、操办丧事；你知道，有色人办丧事最讲究排场。喏，这一手正搔着人们的痒处，他就选进了州议会。跟着他又置了一处好宅子，原配太太就配不上他了。后来又来了个年轻的女教师，他就开始朝人家飞眼儿。可她也早陷在泥里，跟他一个坑一个洼；他索性甩了原配，一转眼娶了她。他是个地道的老伪君子，俺家猫死了，俺都不打发人去请他来埋。罗比，对这号传道人，俺就是要像千块砖头劈头盖脸地砸下去。”

===Original===
“Well, Aunt Linda, all the preachers are not like him.”

“No; I knows dat; not by a jug full. We’s got some mighty good men down yere, an’ we’s glad when dey comes, an’ orful sorry when dey goes ’way. De las preacher we had war a mighty good man. He didn’t like too much hollerin’.”

“Perhaps,” said Robert, “he thought it were best for only one to speak at a time.”
===Chinese===
“唉，琳达大婶，传道人也不都是他那样的。”

“不；这俺知道；可不是一回事，差得远呢。俺们这地方也有顶好的人，他们来了俺们欢喜，走了俺们难过得不行。俺们上一任传道人就是个顶好的人。他不喜欢太吵太嚷。”

“也许，”罗伯特说，“他觉得一次只让一个人开口才好。”
===Original===
“I specs so. His wife war de nicest and sweetest lady dat eber I did see. None ob yer airish, stuck up folks, like a tarrapin carryin’ eberything on its back. She used ter hab meetins fer de mudders, an’ larn us how to raise our chillen, an’ talk so putty to de chillen. I sartinly did lub dat woman.”

“Where is she now?” asked Robert.

“De Conference moved dem ’bout thirty miles from yere. Deys gwine to hab a big meetin’ ober dere next Sunday. Don’t you ’member dem meetins we used to hab in de woods? We don’t hab to hide like we did den. But it don’t seem as ef de people had de same good ’ligion we had den. ’Pears like folks is took up wid makin’ money an’ politics.”
===Chinese===
“俺琢磨是这么回事。他的太太是俺这辈子见过的顶娴雅、顶和气的太太。不是那种娇气、眼高于顶的人——那种人就像乌龟，什么都驮在自个儿背上。她从前常给做母亲的们开母亲会，教俺们怎么拉扯孩子，跟孩子们说起话来也好听得很。俺是真心疼爱那位太太。”

“她如今在哪儿？”罗伯特问。

“年会把他们调到离这儿三十英里的地方去了。下个礼拜天，他们那边要开一场大会。你还记得俺们从前在林子里开的那些会不？如今俺们不用像那时候那样躲躲藏藏了。可人们好像再没有那时候的那种好信仰了。如今的人，好像一门心思都在挣钱和政治上头。”

===Original===
“Well, Aunt Linda, don’t you wish those good old days would come back?”

“No, chile; neber! neber! Wat fer you take me? I’d ruther lib in a corncrib. Freedom needn’t keep me outer heben; an’ ef I’se sich a fool as ter lose my ’ligion cause I’se free, I oughtn’ ter git dere.”

“But, Aunt Linda, if old Miss were able to take care of you, wouldn’t you just as leave be back again?”

There was a faint quiver of indignation in Aunt Linda’s voice, as she replied:﻿—
===Chinese===
“那么，琳达大婶，您就不盼着那些美好的老日子回来吗？”

“不，孩子；绝不！绝不！你把俺当成什么人啦？俺宁可去住玉米仓。自由挡不住俺进天国；要是俺傻到因为得了自由就把信仰丢了，那俺也就不配进去了。”

“可是，琳达大婶，要是老女主人还有能力照管您，您不就乐得回去了吗？”

琳达大婶的声音里颤动着一丝义愤，她答道：
===Original===
“Don’t yer want yer freedom? Well I wants ter pat my free foot. Halleluyah! But, Robby, I wants yer ter go ter dat big meetin’ de wuss kine.”

“How will I get there?” asked Robert.

“Oh, dat’s all right. My ole man’s got two ob de nicest mules you eber set yer eyes on. It’ll jis’ do yer good ter look at dem. I ’spect you’ll see some ob yer ole frens dere. Dere’s a nice settlemen’ of cullud folks ober dere, an’ I wants yer to come an’ bring dat young lady. I wants dem folks to see wat nice folks I kin bring to de meetin’. I hope’s yer didn’t lose all your ’ligion in de army.”

“Oh, I hope not,” replied Robert.

“Oh, chile, yer mus’ be shore ’bout dat. I don’t want yer to ride hope’s hoss down to torment. Now be shore an’ come to-morrer an’ bring dat young lady, an’ take supper wid me. I’se all on nettles to see dat chile.”
===Chinese===
“你不想要你的自由吗？俺可想牢牢踩住俺这双自由的脚哩。哈利路亚！不过罗比，那个大会你死活也得去。”

“我怎么去呢？”罗伯特问。

“哦，那不愁。俺们老头子有两头骡子，好得你这辈子都没见过。光瞧瞧它们就够你受用的。俺想你会在那边碰见几个老朋友。那边有一片有色人住的好聚居区，俺要你去，还要把那位年轻小姐一并带去。俺要让那边的人瞧瞧，俺能带多体面的人物去赴会。但愿你在队伍里没把信仰丢光了。”

“啊，但愿没有，”罗伯特答道。

“哦，孩子，这件事你可千万要拿得稳。俺可不想叫你骑着“指望”这匹马，一路骑到地狱里去。明儿千万来，把那位年轻小姐带来，在俺家吃晚饭。俺这心里跟坐在针毡上似的，就等着见那孩子。”

## X Shadows in the Home / 第十章 家中的阴影

===Original===
On the next morning after this conversation Leroy left for the North, to attend the commencement and witness the graduation of his ward. Arriving in Ohio, he immediately repaired to the academy and inquired for the principal. He was shown into the reception-room, and in a few moments the principal entered.

“Good morning,” said Leroy, rising and advancing towards him; “how is my ward this morning?”

“She is well, and has been expecting you. I am glad you came in time for the commencement. She stands among the foremost in her class.”

“I am glad to hear it. Will you send her this?” said Leroy, handing the principal a card. The principal took the card and immediately left the room.
===Chinese===
这场谈话之后的第二天早晨，勒罗伊动身北上，去出席毕业典礼，亲眼看着他监护的姑娘毕业。到了俄亥俄，他径直赶往那所学校，求见校长。他被引进接待室，不一会儿，校长走了进来。

“早上好，”勒罗伊起身迎上前去，说道，“我监护的姑娘今早可好？”

“她很好，正盼着您来呢。您来得正赶上毕业典礼，我很高兴。她在班里名列前茅。”

“听你这么说，我很高兴。劳驾把这个交给她，可以吗？”勒罗伊说着，递给校长一张卡片。校长接过卡片，随即走出了房间。

===Original===
Very soon Leroy heard a light step, and looking up he saw a radiantly beautiful woman approaching him.

“Good morning, Marie,” he said, greeting her cordially, and gazing upon her with unfeigned admiration. “You are looking very handsome this morning.”

“Do you think so?” she asked, smiling and blushing. “I am glad you are not disappointed; that you do not feel your money has been spent in vain.”

“Oh, no, what I have spent on your education has been the best investment I ever made.”
===Chinese===
很快，勒罗伊听见一阵轻快的脚步声；抬头一看，一位光彩照人的美丽女子正向他走来。

“早上好，玛丽，”他热诚地招呼她，带着毫不掩饰的赞叹打量着她，“你今天早上真好看。”

“您真觉得好看？”她微笑着，脸红了。“您没有失望，我很高兴；也不觉得您这笔钱白花了。”

“哦，不会，我在你教育上花的钱，是我这辈子做过的最好的一笔投资。”

===Original===
“I hope,” said Marie, “you may always find it so. But Mas﻿—”

“Hush!” said Leroy, laying his hand playfully on her lips; “you are free. I don’t want the dialect of slavery to linger on your lips. You must not call me that name again.”

“Why not?”

“Because I have a nearer and dearer one by which I wish to be called.”
===Chinese===
“我盼着，”玛丽说，“您永远都能这么想。可是主——”

“嘘！”勒罗伊说着，顽皮地把手按在她的唇上，“你自由了。我不愿奴隶制的腔调还留在你的唇边。往后不许再那样称呼我了。”

“为什么不能？”

“因为我有一个更亲近、更亲爱的称呼，盼着你能这样叫我。”

===Original===
Leroy drew her nearer, and whispered in her ear a single word. She started, trembled with emotion, grew pale, and blushed painfully. An awkward silence ensued, when Leroy, pressing her hand, exclaimed: “This is the hand that plucked me from the grave, and I am going to retain it as mine; mine to guard with my care until death us do part.”

Leroy looked earnestly into her eyes, which fell beneath his ardent gaze. With admirable self-control, while a great joy was thrilling her heart, she bowed her beautiful head and softly repeated, “Until death us do part.”

Leroy knew Southern society too well to expect it to condone his offense against its social customs, or give the least recognition to his wife, however cultured, refined, and charming she might be, if it were known that she had the least infusion of negro blood in her veins. But he was brave enough to face the consequences of his alliance, and marry the woman who was the choice of his heart, and on whom his affections were centred.

After Leroy had left the room, Marie sat awhile thinking of the wonderful change that had come over her. Instead of being a lonely slave girl, with the fatal dower of beauty, liable to be bought and sold, exchanged, and bartered, she was to be the wife of a wealthy planter; a man in whose honor she could confide, and on whose love she could lean.
===Chinese===
勒罗伊把她拉近身旁，在她耳边低低说了一个字。她猛地一怔，激动得颤抖起来，脸色发白，又窘得满脸通红。接着是一阵难堪的沉默；后来，勒罗伊紧握着她的手，说道：“正是这只手，把我从坟墓里拉了回来；我要把它留作我的手——归我所有，由我悉心守护，直到死亡将我们分开。”

勒罗伊恳切地凝视着她的眼睛；在他热切的注视下，她的目光垂了下去。巨大的喜悦激荡着她的心，她却以令人赞叹的自持，低下美丽的头，轻声复诵道：“直到死亡将我们分开。”

勒罗伊深知南方社会的脾性，绝不指望它宽宥自己冒犯其社会习俗的过犯，也不指望它对他的妻子给予最起码的承认——无论她多么有教养、多么娴雅、多么迷人，只要让人知道她血管里掺着哪怕一丁点黑人的血。但他有足够的勇气面对这桩结合带来的一切后果，娶这个他心中的选择、他一腔深情所系的女子为妻。

勒罗伊离开房间后，玛丽坐了一会儿，想着降临到自己身上的奇妙变化。她本是一个孤苦的女奴，带着美色这份致命的嫁妆，随时可能被人买卖、交换、易物；如今却要做一位富有种植园主的妻子——一个其品行可以信赖、其爱情可以倚靠的男人。


===Original===
Very interesting and pleasant were the commencement exercises in which Marie bore an important part. To enlist sympathy for her enslaved race, and appear to advantage before Leroy, had aroused all of her energies. The stimulus of hope, the manly love which was environing her life, brightened her eye and lit up the wonderful beauty of her countenance. During her stay in the North she had constantly been brought in contact with anti-slavery people. She was not aware that there was so much kindness among the white people of the country until she had tested it in the North. From the anti-slavery people in private life she had learned some of the noblest lessons of freedom and justice, and had become imbued with their sentiments. Her theme was “American Civilization, its Lights and Shadows.”

Graphically she portrayed the lights, faithfully she showed the shadows of our American civilization. Earnestly and feelingly she spoke of the blind Sampson in our land, who might yet shake the pillars of our great Commonwealth. Leroy listened attentively. At times a shadow of annoyance would overspread his face, but it was soon lost in the admiration her earnestness and zeal inspired. Like Esther pleading for the lives of her people in the Oriental courts of a despotic king, she stood before the audience, pleading for those whose lips were sealed, but whose condition appealed to the mercy and justice of the Nation. Strong men wiped the moisture from their eyes, and women’s hearts throbbed in unison with the strong, brave words that were uttered in behalf of freedom for all and chains for none. Generous applause was freely bestowed, and beautiful bouquets were showered upon her. When it was known that she was to be the wife of her guardian, warm congratulations were given, and earnest hopes expressed for the welfare of the lonely girl, who, nearly all her life, had been deprived of a parent’s love and care. On the eve of starting South Leroy procured a license, and united his destiny with the young lady whose devotion in the darkest hour had won his love and gratitude.
===Chinese===
毕业典礼上的种种仪式饶有趣味、令人愉快，玛丽在其中担任了重要的角色。为她受奴役的种族赢得同情，并在勒罗伊面前显露光彩，这激发了她全部的精力。希望的激励，加上环绕着她生活的男子汉的爱情，使她的双眼明亮起来，点亮了她容颜上惊人的美丽。在北方逗留期间，她不断接触到反蓄奴的人士。直到在北方亲身领受，她才知道这个国家的白人中间竟有这么多的善意。从私下生活里的反蓄奴人士那里，她学到了一些关于自由与正义的最崇高的教诲，也被他们的情操浸染了。她的讲题是“美国文明：它的光明与阴影”。

她把我国文明的光明描绘得有声有色，也把它的阴影揭示得忠实无隐。她恳切而动情地谈到我们这片土地上那位瞎眼的参孙——说不定有朝一日，他会把我们这个伟大共和国的柱子摇塌。勒罗伊听得专注。时而有一丝愠色浮上他的脸，但很快就消融在她那份恳切与热诚所激起的赞赏里。她像在专制君王的东方宫廷里为族人性命哀求的以斯帖，站在听众面前，为那些嘴唇被封住的人恳求——他们的境遇正向着这个国家的怜悯与正义发出呼吁。刚强的男子汉抹去了眼角的湿意，女人们的心也随着那一声声坚强勇敢的话语一同激荡——那些话，是为了人人得自由、无人戴锁链而发的。人们慷慨地报以掌声，美丽的花束雨点般落到她的手中。待到大家得知她将要做自己监护人的妻子，纷纷送上热诚的祝贺，又为这个孤苦姑娘的福祉殷切祝愿——她几乎终其一生，都被剥夺了父母的爱护。南下启程的前夜，勒罗伊领了结婚证书，把自己的命运同那位年轻小姐结合在一起——正是她在最黑暗的时刻里的忠贞，赢得了他的爱情与感激。

===Original===
In a few days Marie returned as mistress to the plantation from which she had gone as a slave. But as unholy alliances were common in those days between masters and slaves, no one took especial notice that Marie shared Leroy’s life as mistress of his home, and that the family silver and jewelry were in her possession. But Leroy, happy in his choice, attended to the interests of his plantation, and found companionship in his books and in the society of his wife. A few male companions visited him occasionally, admired the magnificent beauty of his wife, shook their heads, and spoke of him as being very eccentric, but thought his marriage the great mistake of his life. But none of his female friends ever entered his doors, when it became known that Marie held the position of mistress of his mansion, and presided at his table. But she, sheltered in the warm clasp of loving arms, found her life like a joyous dream.

Into that quiet and beautiful home three children were born, unconscious of the doom suspended over their heads.

“Oh, how glad I am,” Marie would often say, “that these children are free. I could never understand how a cultured white man could have his own children enslaved. I can understand how savages, fighting with each other, could doom their vanquished foes to slavery, but it has always been a puzzle to me how a civilized man could drag his own children, bone of his bone, flesh of his flesh, down to the position of social outcasts, abject slaves, and political pariahs.”
===Chinese===
几天之后，玛丽以女主人的身份，回到了那座她以奴隶身份离开的种植园。不过，在那个年月，主奴之间非圣洁的结合本是寻常事，没有人特别留意玛丽作为一家主妇与勒罗伊共同生活，也没有人留意家中的银器和首饰都归她掌管。勒罗伊对自己的选择深感幸福，专心料理种植园的产业，在书籍和妻子的陪伴中自得其乐。偶尔有几位男性友人前来探访，艳羡他妻子雍容华贵的美貌，摇着头说他为人古怪透顶，心想这桩婚姻是他一生的大错。然而，待到大家知道玛丽坐上了宅邸主妇的位置、主持起他的餐桌，他的女性朋友便再没有一个跨进过他的家门。而她，栖身在那双爱的臂膀温暖的环抱里，只觉得自己的生活像一场欢愉的梦。

三个孩子相继在这座宁静而美丽的宅邸里出生，全然不知厄运正悬在他们头顶。

“哦，想到这些孩子生来是自由身，我多高兴啊，”玛丽常常这样说，“我怎么也不能理解，一个有教养的白人，怎么忍心让自己亲生的孩子沦为奴隶。野蛮人互相争斗，把战败的仇敌贬为奴隶，这个我能理解；可一个文明人，怎么能把自己亲生的孩子——骨中之骨、肉中之肉——拖到社会弃儿、卑贱奴隶、政治贱民的地步，这在我始终是个解不开的谜。”

===Original===
“But, Marie,” said Eugene, “all men do not treat their illegitimate children in the manner you describe. The last time I was in New Orleans I met Henri Augustine at the depot, with two beautiful young girls. At first I thought that they were his own children, they resembled him so closely. But afterwards I noticed that they addressed him as ‘Mister.’ Before we parted he told me that his wife had taken such a dislike to their mother that she could not bear to see them on the place. At last, weary of her dissatisfaction, he had promised to bring them to New Orleans and sell them. Instead, he was going to Ohio to give them their freedom, and make provision for their future.”

“What a wrong!” said Marie.

“Who was wronged?” said Leroy, in astonishment.

“Everyone in the whole transaction,” answered Marie. “Your friend wronged himself by sinning against his own soul. He wronged his wife by arousing her hatred and jealousy through his unfaithfulness. He wronged those children by giving them the *status* of slaves and outcasts. He wronged their mother by imposing upon her the burdens and cares of maternity without the rights and privileges of a wife. He made her crown of motherhood a circlet of shame. Under other circumstances she might have been an honored wife and happy mother. And I do think such men wrong their own legitimate children by transmitting to them a weakened moral fibre.”
===Chinese===
“可是，玛丽，”尤金说，“并不是人人都像你说的那样对待自己的私生子女。我上回到新奥尔良去，在车站遇见亨利·奥古斯丁（Henri Augustine），带着两个年轻貌美的姑娘。起初我以为她们是他亲生的孩子——她们长得跟他太像了。可后来我注意到，她们称他‘先生’。临别之前他告诉我，他的妻子对她们的母亲厌恶到了极点，见不得她们待在庄园上。末了，他不堪她的不满，答应把她们带到新奥尔良去卖掉。实际上，他却打算上俄亥俄去，恢复她们的自由身，并为她们的日后做好安排。”

“这是多大的罪过啊！”玛丽说。

“谁受了亏负？”勒罗伊愕然问道。

“这桩事情里的每一个人，”玛丽答道。“你的朋友亏负了他自己，因为他得罪了自己的灵魂。他亏负了他的妻子，用他的不忠激起了她的仇恨与嫉妒。他亏负了那两个孩子，把奴隶和弃儿的*身份*加在她们头上。他亏负了她们的母亲，把为人母的重担与辛劳压在她身上，却不给她为人妻的权利与名分。他把她的母亲冠冕变成了一道羞辱的箍环。换一种境遇，她本可以是一位受敬重的妻子、幸福的母亲。而且我确实认为，这样的人也亏负了他们自己正当的婚生子女，因为他们传给子女的，是一根被削弱了的道德纤维。”

===Original===
“Oh, Marie, you have such an uncomfortable way of putting things. You make me feel that we have done those things which we ought not to have done, and have left undone those things which we ought to have done.”

“If it annoys you,” said Marie, “I will stop talking.”

“Oh, no, go on,” said Leroy, carelessly; and then he continued more thoughtfully, “I know a number of men who have sent such children North, and manumitted, educated, and left them valuable legacies. We are all liable to err, and, having done wrong, all we can do is to make reparation.”

“My dear husband, this is a wrong where reparation is impossible. Neither wealth nor education can repair the wrong of a dishonored birth. There are a number of slaves in this section who are servants to their own brothers and sisters; whose fathers have robbed them not simply of liberty but of the right of being well born. Do you think these things will last forever?”
===Chinese===
“哦，玛丽，你讲事情的方式真叫人不好受。你让我觉得，我们做了那些不该做的事，又把那些该做的事搁着没做。”

“若是惹你不快，”玛丽说，“我就不说下去了。”

“哦，不，你说下去，”勒罗伊漫不经心地说；随后又接着说下去，语气沉思了许多，“我认识好些人，把这样的孩子送到北方，解除了他们的奴籍，让他们受了教育，还给他们留下可观的遗产。人人都难免犯错；既然做错了，我们能做的，唯有补救。”

“我亲爱的丈夫，这是一种无从补救的罪过。财富也好，教育也好，都修复不了一个生而不光彩的出身所受的亏负。这一带就有不少奴隶，正在给自己的亲兄弟姐妹当仆人；他们的父亲剥夺的不光是他们的自由，还有他们生而清白的名分。你以为这些事情会永远持续下去吗？”

===Original===
“I suppose not. There are some prophets of evil who tell us that the Union is going to dissolve. But I know it would puzzle their brains to tell where the crack will begin. I reckon we’ll continue to jog along as usual. ‘Cotton fights, and cotton conquers for American slavery.’ ”

Even while Leroy dreamed of safety the earthquake was cradling its fire; the ground was growing hollow beneath his tread; but his ear was too dull to catch the sound; his vision too blurred to read the signs of the times.

“Marie,” said Leroy, taking up the thread of the discourse, “slavery is a sword that cuts both ways. If it wrongs the negro, it also curses the white man. But we are in it, and what can we do?”
===Chinese===
“我想不会。有几个专报凶信的先知告诉我们，联邦就要解体了。可我知道，要说清裂缝会从哪里先裂开，准得叫他们绞尽脑汁。我估摸着，咱们还会照常这么一路颠簸着走下去。‘棉花打仗，棉花为美国的奴隶制打赢这场仗。’”

就在勒罗伊梦想着平安无事的时候，地震正在孕育它的地火；他脚下的大地正日渐被掏空；可他的耳朵太迟钝，听不见那声响；他的目光太昏花，读不出时代的征兆。

“玛丽，”勒罗伊重新拾起话头，说道，“奴隶制是一把两面开刃的剑。它若是亏负了黑人，它也同样诅咒着白人。可我们身在其中，又能怎么样呢？”

===Original===
“Get out of it as quickly as possible.”

“That is easier said than done. I would willingly free every slave on my plantation if I could do so without expatriating them. Some of them have wives and children on other plantations, and to free them is to separate them from their kith and kin. To let them remain here as a free people is out of the question. My hands are tied by law and custom.”

“Who tied them?” asked Marie.

“A public opinion, whose meshes I cannot break. If the negro is the thrall of his master, we are just as much the thralls of public opinion.”

“Why do you not battle against public opinion, if you think it is wrong?”

“Because I have neither the courage of a martyr, nor the faith of a saint; and so I drift along, trying to make the condition of our slaves as comfortable as I possibly can. I believe there are slaves on this plantation whom the most flattering offers of freedom would not entice away.”
===Chinese===
“越快脱身越好。”

“说来容易，做来难。要是能不必让他们离乡去国，我心甘情愿解放我种植园上的每一个奴隶。他们当中有些人，在别的种植园上有妻儿；解放他们，就是把他们和亲眷骨肉生生拆散。要让他们作为自由人留在此地，又绝无可能。我的手脚被法律和习俗捆住了。”

“是谁捆的呢？”玛丽问。

“一种舆论，它的罗网我挣不破。黑人若是主人的奴仆，我们也同样是舆论的奴仆。”

“既然你认为它错了，为什么不同舆论斗一斗呢？”

“因为我既没有殉道者的勇气，也没有圣徒的信心；于是我就这么随波逐流地漂着，尽力让我们奴隶的境遇过得舒服一点。我相信这座种植园上有些奴隶，就是开出再优厚的自由条件，也勾引不动他们。”

===Original===
“I do not think,” said Marie, “that some of you planters understand your own slaves. Lying is said to be the vice of slaves. The more intelligent of them have so learned to veil their feelings that you do not see the undercurrent of discontent beneath their apparent good humor and jollity. The more discontented they are, the more I respect them. To me a contented slave is an abject creature. I hope that I shall see the day when there will not be a slave in the land. I hate the whole thing from the bottom of my heart.”

“Marie, your Northern education has unfitted you for Southern life. You are free, yourself, and so are our children. Why not let well enough alone?”

“Because I love liberty, not only for myself but for every human being. Think how dear these children are to me; and then for the thought to be forever haunting me, that if you were dead they could be turned out of doors and divided among your relatives. I sometimes lie awake at night thinking of how there might be a screw loose somewhere, and, after all, the children and I might be reduced to slavery.”
===Chinese===
“我看，”玛丽说，“你们这些种植园主当中，有些人并不了解自己的奴隶。人们常说，撒谎是奴隶的恶习。他们当中那些更有头脑的，早已学会把真实的感情遮掩起来，你们根本看不出他们表面好性儿、乐呵呵底下涌动着的不满暗流。他们越是不满，我越敬重他们。在我看来，一个心满意足的奴隶，是一种丧尽志气的东西。我盼望能亲眼见到那一天：这片土地上再没有一个奴隶。我从心底里憎恨这整个的制度。”

“玛丽，你在北方受的教育，让你过不惯南方的生活了。你自己是自由人，我们的孩子也是。如今的日子已经够好了，何必多事呢？”

“因为我爱自由，不光是为我自己，也是为每一个人。想想这些孩子在我心上有多亲；再想想，有一个念头永远缠绕着我：倘若你死了，他们就会被扫地出门，被你的亲戚们瓜分。有时我夜里躺着睡不着，琢磨着会不会哪里松了一颗螺丝，到头来我和孩子们还是会被贬为奴隶。”

===Original===
“Marie, what in the world is the matter with you? Have you had a presentiment of my death, or, as Uncle Jack says, ‘hab you seed it in a vision?’ ”

“No, but I have had such sad forebodings that they almost set me wild. One night I dreamt that you were dead; that the lawyers entered the house, seized our property, and remanded us to slavery. I never can be satisfied in the South with such a possibility hanging over my head.”

“Marie, dear, you are growing nervous. Your imagination is too active. You are left too much alone on this plantation. I hope that for your own and the children’s sake I will be enabled to arrange our affairs so as to find a home for you where you will not be doomed to the social isolation and ostracism that surround you here.”

“I don’t mind the isolation for myself, but the children. You have enjoined silence on me with respect to their connection with the negro race, but I do not think we can conceal it from them very long. It will not be long before Iola will notice the offishness of girls of her own age, and the scornful glances which, even now, I think, are leveled at her. Yesterday Harry came crying to me, and told me that one of the neighbor’s boys had called him ‘nigger.’ ”
===Chinese===
“玛丽，你到底是怎么了？你是得了关于我死亡的预感呢，还是像杰克大叔说的，‘你可在异象里瞧见它啦？’”

“不是，可那些悲伤的预感几乎把我逼得发疯。有天夜里，我梦见你死了；律师们闯进家里，查封了我们的家产，把我们发还为奴。头顶悬着这样的可能，我在南方永远安不下心来。”

“亲爱的玛丽，你变得神经衰弱了。你的想象力太活跃了。你在这座种植园上，独自一人待的时候太多。为了你自己，也为了孩子们，我希望能把咱们的事务安排妥当，为你寻一个家，让你不必再遭受此地围困着你的那种社会隔绝与社会放逐。”

“隔绝冷落，我自己倒不介意，我担心的是孩子们。你嘱我对他们与黑人种族的牵连保持缄默，可我看，我们瞒不了他们多久。要不了多久，伊奥拉就会察觉同龄姑娘们对她的疏远，还有那些轻蔑的眼色——我想，就是眼下，也已经有人朝她投来了。昨天哈里哭着来找我，说邻居家的一个男孩骂他‘黑鬼’。”

===Original===
A shadow flitted over Leroy’s face, as he answered, somewhat soberly, “Oh, Marie, do not meet trouble halfway. I have manumitted you, and the children will follow your condition. I have made you all legatees of my will. Except my cousin, Alfred Lorraine, I have only distant relatives, whom I scarcely know and who hardly know me.”

“Your cousin Lorraine? Are you sure our interests would be safe in his hands?”

“I think so; I don’t think Alfred would do anything dishonorable.”

“He might not with his equals. But how many men would be bound by a sense of honor where the rights of a colored woman are in question? Your cousin was bitterly opposed to our marriage, and I would not trust any important interests in his hands. I do hope that in providing for our future you will make assurance doubly sure.”
===Chinese===
一丝阴影掠过勒罗伊的脸。他答话时，神色郑重了几分：“哦，玛丽，别去半路上迎那个烦恼。我已经解除了你的奴籍，孩子们随你的身分，生来就是自由人。我已经立下遗嘱，让你们母子都做我的继承人。除了表兄阿尔弗雷德·洛兰，我剩下的只有些远亲，我几乎不认识他们，他们也几乎不认识我。”

“你的表兄洛兰？你能肯定，我们的权益到了他手里就稳当了吗？”

“我想是稳当的；我不认为阿尔弗雷德会做出什么不光彩的事来。”

“对自己的同等人，他或许不会。可是，一旦事关一个有色人种女性的权利，还有多少男人会被荣誉感约束住呢？你这位表兄当年极力反对我们的婚事，我不会把任何重要的权益托付到他手里。我衷心盼望，在为我们日后作安排时，你要把稳当的事再弄得加倍的稳当。”

===Original===
“I certainly will, and all that human foresight can do shall be done for you and our children.”

“Oh,” said Marie, pressing to her heart a beautiful child of six summers, “I think it would almost make me turn over in my grave to know that every grace and charm which this child possesses would only be so much added to her value as an article of merchandise.”

As Marie released the child from her arms she looked wonderingly into her mother’s face and clung closely to her, as if to find refuge from some unseen evil. Leroy noticed this, and sighed unconsciously, as an expression of pain flitted over his face.
===Chinese===
“我一定照办。凡属人力远虑所能做到的，都一定为你和我们的孩子们做到。”

“哦，”玛丽说着，把一个六岁模样的漂亮孩子紧紧搂在心口，“一想到这孩子身上的每一分优雅、每一分妩媚，到头来只会给她这件商品的价钱再添上几分，我想我在坟墓里都要翻身了。”

玛丽把孩子从怀中放开时，她诧异地望着母亲的脸，紧紧偎着她，仿佛要躲避某种看不见的凶邪。勒罗伊注意到了这一幕，不觉叹了一口气，一丝痛楚掠过他的脸。

===Original===
“Now, Marie,” he continued, “stop tormenting yourself with useless fears. Although, with all her faults, I still love the South, I will make arrangements either to live North or go to France. There life will be brighter for us all. Now, Marie, seat yourself at the piano and sing:﻿—

> ‘Sing me the songs that to me were so dear,
 

 Long, long ago.
 

 Sing me the songs I delighted to hear,
 

 Long, long ago.’ ”
===Chinese===
“好了，玛丽，”他接着说，“别再用那些无谓的忧虑折磨自己了。南方纵有千般不是，我依然爱它；不过我会作出安排，要么迁到北方去住，要么去法国。在那边，我们大家的日子都会更明亮。来，玛丽，坐到钢琴前，唱一唱：

> ‘给我唱一唱那些歌吧，那些曾叫我倍感亲切的歌，
 

 很久，很久以前。
 

 给我唱一唱那些我曾爱听的歌曲吧，
 

 很久，很久以前。’”

===Original===
As Marie sang the anxiety faded from her face, a sense of security stole over her, and she sat among her loved ones a happy wife and mother. What if no one recognized her on that lonely plantation! Her world was, nevertheless, there. The love and devotion of her husband brightened every avenue of her life, while her children filled her home with music, mirth, and sunshine.

Marie had undertaken their education, but she could not give them the culture which comes from the attrition of thought, and from contact with the ideas of others. Since her schooldays she had read extensively and thought much, and in solitude her thoughts had ripened. But for her children there were no companions except the young slaves of the plantation, and she dreaded the effect of such intercourse upon their lives and characters.

Leroy had always been especially careful to conceal from his children the knowledge of their connection with the negro race. To Marie this silence was oppressive.
===Chinese===
玛丽唱着唱着，焦虑从她脸上褪去了，一种安然之感悄悄爬上心头；她坐在亲人们中间，做了一个幸福的妻子和母亲。哪怕在那座冷清的种植园上没有一个人认她，又有什么要紧！她的天地，反正就在那里。丈夫的爱与深情，照亮了她生命的每一条途径；而她的孩子们，用音乐、欢笑和阳光，盛满了她的家。

孩子们的学业由玛丽亲自承担起来，可她给不了他们那种教养——从思想的砥砺中、从与他人观念的切磋中得来的那种教养。自求学时代起，她就博览群书、勤于思考；在孤独中，她的思想成熟了。可对她的孩子们来说，除了种植园上的小奴隶，再没有别的同伴；她深怕这样的交往会给他们的生命和品性带来影响。

勒罗伊一向格外小心，不让孩子们知道他们与黑人种族的牵连。对玛丽来说，这份沉默压得人透不过气来。

===Original===
One day she said to him, “I see no other way of finishing the education of these children than by sending them to some Northern school.”

“I have come,” said Leroy, “to the same conclusion. We had better take Iola and Harry North and make arrangements for them to spend several years in being educated. Riches take wings to themselves and fly away, but a good education is an investment on which the law can place no attachment. As there is a possibility of their origin being discovered, I will find a teacher to whom I can confide our story, and upon whom I can enjoin secrecy. I want them well fitted for any emergency in life. When I discover for what they have the most aptitude I will give them especial training in that direction.”

A troubled look passed over the face of Marie, as she hesitatingly said: “I am so afraid that you will regret our marriage when you fully realize the complications it brings.”
===Chinese===
一天，她对他说：“要完成这几个孩子的教育，我看除了送他们去北方上学，再没有别的法子了。”

“我得出的，”勒罗伊说，“也是同样的结论。我们最好把伊奥拉和哈里带到北方去，安排他们花上几年工夫受教育。财富会长出翅膀飞去，良好的教育却是一笔法律也无法查封的投资。既然他们的出身有被人发现的可能，我就去找一位教师——一位我能吐露我们的故事、能嘱他守口如瓶的教师。我要他们受到良好的装备，能应付人生中的任何变故。等我发现了他们最擅长的天分是什么，就朝那个方向给他们特别的培养。”

一丝忧色掠过玛丽的脸，她迟疑地说：“我真害怕，等你完全看清这桩婚事带来的种种纠葛时，你会后悔的。”

===Original===
“No, no,” said Leroy, tenderly, “it is not that I regret our marriage, or feel the least disdain for our children on account of the blood in their veins; but I do not wish them to grow up under the contracting influence of this race prejudice. I do not wish them to feel that they have been born under a proscription from which no valor can redeem them, nor that any social advancement or individual development can wipe off the ban which clings to them. No, Marie, let them go North, learn all they can, aspire all they may. The painful knowledge will come all too soon. Do not forestall it. I want them simply to grow up as other children; not being patronized by friends nor disdained by foes.”

“My dear husband, you may be perfectly right, but are you not preparing our children for a fearful awakening? Are you not acting on the plan, ‘After me the deluge?’ ”
===Chinese===
“不，不，”勒罗伊温柔地说，“我后悔的决不是我们的婚姻，也决不是因为孩子们血管里流的血，就对他们有半分轻贱；我只是不愿意他们在这种种族偏见的束缩性影响下长大。我不愿他们觉得自己生来就罩着一纸禁令，任凭什么英勇也无法赎回；也不愿他们觉得，任凭怎样的社会上进、怎样的个人发展，都拭不去紧贴在他们身上的那一道禁痕。不，玛丽，让他们去北方吧，能学多少学多少，能怀多高的志向就怀多高的志向。那痛苦的知识来得已经够快的了，不要抢到它前头去。我只要他们像别的孩子一样长大——不受朋友居高临下的照拂，也不受仇人的白眼。”

“我亲爱的丈夫，你或许完全有道理，可你这不是在让我们的孩子为一场可怕的惊醒做准备吗？你行事的方针，莫不正是那一条‘我死后，哪管它洪水滔天’？”

===Original===
“Not at all, Marie. I want our children to grow up without having their self-respect crushed in the bud. You know that the North is not free from racial prejudice.”

“I know it,” said Marie, sadly, “and I think one of the great mistakes of our civilization is that which makes color, and not character, a social test.”

“I think so, too,” said Leroy. “The strongest men and women of a downtrodden race may bare their bosoms to an adverse fate and develop courage in the midst of opposition, but we have no right to subject our children to such crucial tests before their characters are formed. For years, when I lived abroad, I had an opportunity to see and hear of men of African descent who had distinguished themselves and obtained a recognition in European circles, which they never could have gained in this country. I now recall the name of Ira Aldridge, a colored man from New York City, who was covered with princely honors as a successful tragedian. Alexander Dumas was not forced to conceal his origin to succeed as a novelist. When I was in St. Petersburg I was shown the works of Alexander Sergevitch, a Russian poet, who was spoken of as the Byron of Russian literature, and reckoned one of the finest poets that Russia has produced in this century. He was also a prominent figure in fashionable society, and yet he was of African lineage. One of his paternal ancestors was a negro who had been ennobled by Peter the Great. I can’t help contrasting the recognition which these men had received with the treatment which has been given to Frederick Douglass and other intelligent colored men in this country. With me the wonder is not that they have achieved so little, but that they have accomplished so much. No, Marie, we will have our children educated without being subjected to the depressing influences of caste feeling. Perhaps by the time their education is finished I will be ready to wind up my affairs and take them abroad, where merit and ability will give them entrance into the best circles of art, literature, and science.”
===Chinese===
“根本不是，玛丽。我要我们的孩子在长大成人的过程中，自尊心不至于还在萌芽里就被碾碎。你知道，北方也不是没有种族偏见。”

“这我知道，”玛丽黯然道，“而且我认为，我们文明的一大错处，就在于拿肤色、而不是品格，当作社会的试金石。”

“我也这么想，”勒罗伊说。“一个被踩在脚下的种族里，最强健的男男女女，或许可以袒露胸膛迎向厄逆的命运，在重重反对之中磨砺出勇气来；可我们没有权利在孩子品格尚未成形之前，就叫他们去经受这样严酷的考验。我在国外旅居多年，有机会亲见、也有机会听闻一些非洲血统的男子扬名立身，在欧洲人的圈子里赢得了承认——那样的承认，他们在这个国家是永远得不到的。我如今还记得艾拉·奥尔德里奇（Ira Aldridge）的名字，他是纽约城的有色人种，作为一位成就斐然的悲剧演员，浑身披满了亲王一般的荣宠。亚历山大·仲马（Alexander Dumas）作为小说家功成名就，从不必隐瞒自己的出身。我在圣彼得堡的时候，有人指给我看亚历山大·谢尔盖维奇（Alexander Sergevitch，即俄国诗人普希金）的作品——一位俄国诗人，人们称他为俄国文学界的拜伦，公认是本世纪俄国所产出的最优秀的诗人之一。他也是上流社会中的一位显赫人物，而他偏偏有着非洲的血统。他的一位父系先祖是个黑人，曾被彼得大帝册封为贵族。我禁不住拿这些人所获得的承认，与弗雷德里克·道格拉斯（Frederick Douglass）以及这个国家里其他有识的有色人种男子所受的待遇两相对照。在我看来，可奇的倒不是他们成就如此之少，而是他们竟成就了如此之多。不，玛丽，我们要让我们的孩子受教育，而不受等级偏见那使人消沉的影响的浸染。也许等他们学业完成之时，我也准备好收束我的事务，带他们出国；在那里，凭德行与才干，他们自能走进艺术、文学与科学的最高圈子。”

===Original===
After this conversation Leroy and his wife went North, and succeeded in finding a good school for their children. In a private interview he confided to the principal the story of the cross in their blood, and, finding him apparently free from racial prejudice, he gladly left the children in his care. Gracie, the youngest child, remained at home, and her mother spared no pains to fit her for the seminary against the time her sister should have finished her education.
===Chinese===
这次谈话之后，勒罗伊夫妇去了北方，顺利地为孩子们寻到了一所好学校。在一次单独的密谈中，他把他们血脉中背负十字架的身世，向校长和盘托出；见他显然没有种族偏见，便欣然把孩子们留在他的照管之下。幼女格雷茜留在家里，母亲不辞辛劳地栽培她，只等她姐姐一完成学业，就送她进那所女子学校。

## XIX Striking Contrasts / 第十九章 鲜明的对照

===Original===
The next day, Robert, accompanied by Iola, went to the settlement to take supper with Aunt Linda, and a very luscious affair it was. Her fingers had not lost their skill since she had tasted the sweets of freedom. Her biscuits were just as light and flaky as ever. Her jelly was as bright as amber, and her preserves were perfectly delicious. After she had set the table she stood looking in silent admiration, chuckling to herself: “Ole Mistus can’t set sich a table as dat. She ought’er be yere to see it. Specs ’twould make her mouf water. Well, I mus’ let bygones be bygones. But dis yere freedom’s mighty good.”

Aunt Linda had invited Uncle Daniel, and, wishing to give him a pleasant surprise, she had refrained from telling him that Robert Johnson was the one she wished him to meet.

===Chinese===
第二天，罗伯特由伊奥拉陪同，前往聚居区到琳达大婶家吃晚饭；那真是一顿极其丰美的家宴。自打尝到了自由的甜头，她那一双手也没荒了手艺。她做的烤饼还同往常一样松软起酥；她熬的果子冻亮得如同琥珀，蜜渍的果子也甜美极了。摆好饭桌之后，她站在一旁默默欣赏，独自暗笑：“老女主人可摆不出这样一桌席面。她真该上这儿来瞧瞧，俺看准得馋得她流口水。唉，过去的就让它过去吧。可这自由的日子呀，真是美得很。”

琳达大婶请了丹尼尔大叔来；她有心让他惊喜一回，便没有告诉他，她想让他见的人正是罗伯特·约翰逊。

===Original===
“Do you know dis gemmen?” said Aunt Linda to Uncle Daniel, when the latter arrived.

“Well, I can’t say’s I do. My eyes is gittin dim, an I disremembers him.”

“Now jis’ you look right good at him. Don’t yer ’member him?”

Uncle Daniel looked puzzled and, slowly scanning Robert’s features, said: “He do look like somebody I used ter know, but I can’t make him out ter save my life. I don’t know whar to place him. Who is de gemmen, ennyhow?”

“Why, Uncle Dan’el,” replied Aunt Linda, “dis is Robby; Miss Nancy’s bad, mischeebous Robby, dat war allers playin’ tricks on me.”

“Well, shore’s I’se born, ef dis ain’t our ole Bobby!” exclaimed Uncle Daniel, delightedly. “Why, chile, whar did yer come from? Thought you war dead an’ buried long ’go.”

===Chinese===
“你认得这位先生不？”丹尼尔大叔到了以后，琳达大婶问他。

“唔，俺可说不上认得。俺这眼睛越来越花，把他给忘啦。”

“那你这就好好儿瞅瞅他。你不记得他啦？”

丹尼尔大叔一脸纳闷，把罗伯特的相貌慢慢端详了一遍，说：“他瞧着是像俺从前认得的一个人，可俺打死也认不出来。俺不知道该把他往哪儿搁。这位先生到底是谁呀？”

“哎呀，丹尼尔大叔，”琳达大婶答道，“这就是罗比呀；南希小姐家那个淘气包罗比，老是变着法儿捉弄俺的那个。”

“哎哟，千真万确，这不就是俺们的老博比嘛！”丹尼尔大叔喜出望外地喊道。“哎呀，孩子，你是打哪儿来的呀？俺还当你早就死了埋了呢。”

===Original===
“Why, Uncle Daniel, did you send anybody to kill me?” asked Robert, laughingly.

“Oh, no’n ’deed, chile! but I yeard dat you war killed in de battle, an’ I never ’spected ter see you agin.”

“Well, here I am,” replied Robert, “large as life, and just as natural. And this young lady, Uncle Daniel, I believe is my niece.” As he spoke he turned to Iola. “Do you remember my mother?”

“Oh, yes,” said Uncle Daniel, looking intently at Iola as she stepped forward and cordially gave him her hand.

“Well, I firmly believe,” continued Robert, “that this is the daughter of the little girl whom Miss Nancy sold away with my mother.”

“Well, I’se rale glad ter see her. She puts me mighty much in mine ob dem days wen we war all young togedder; wen Miss Nancy sed, ‘Harriet war too high fer her.’ It jis’ seems like yisterday wen I yeard Miss Nancy say, ‘No house could flourish whar dere war two mistresses.’ Well, Mr. Robert﻿—”

===Chinese===
“怎么，丹尼尔大叔，难道是您派人来杀我的吗？”罗伯特笑着问。

“哦，真没有，孩子！俺是听说你在打仗的时候被打死了，压根儿没指望还能见着你。”

“瞧，我这不是好好儿来了嘛，”罗伯特答道，“活生生的一个人，一点儿没走样。丹尼尔大叔，我相信这位年轻小姐就是我的外甥女。”说着，他转向伊奥拉。“您还记得我母亲吗？”

“哦，记得，”丹尼尔大叔说；伊奥拉走上前来，亲亲热热地把手伸给他，他目不转睛地望着她。

“我深信不疑，”罗伯特接着说，“当年南希小姐把我母亲连同那个小姑娘一并卖掉，这位就是那小姑娘的女儿。”

“哎，俺见了她可真高兴。她叫俺想起了那些日子——那时候俺们大家都还年轻，都在一处；南希小姐说：‘哈丽特心气太高，她降不住。’俺听见南希小姐说‘一家容不下两个女主人，这家就兴不起来’，就跟昨天的事一样。这个嘛，罗伯特先生——”

===Original===
“Oh, no, no, Uncle Daniel,” interrupted Robert, “don’t say that! Call me Robby or Bob, just as you used to.”

“Well, Bobby, I’se glad klar from de bottom of my heart ter see yer.”

“Even if you wouldn’t go with us when we left?”

“Oh, Bobby, dem war mighty tryin’ times. You boys didn’t know it, but Marster Robert hab giben me a bag ob money ter take keer ob, an’ I promised him I’d do it an’ I had ter be ez good ez my word.”

“Oh, Uncle Daniel, why didn’t you tell us boys all about it? We could have helped you take care of it.”

“Now, wouldn’t dat hab bin smart ter let on ter you chaps, an’ hab you huntin’ fer it from Dan ter Barsheba? I specs some ob you would bin a rootin’ fer it yit!”

===Chinese===
“哦，别介，别介，丹尼尔大叔，”罗伯特打断他，“可别这么叫！照从前那样，叫我罗比或者鲍勃就成。”

“好嘞，博比，俺打心坎儿里亮亮堂堂地高兴见着你。”

“哪怕当年我们动身走的时候，您不肯跟我们一块儿走？”

“哦，博比，那些日子可真是难熬啊。你们这帮孩子不知道，当年罗伯特少爷交给俺一袋子钱，叫俺替他保管，俺应承了他，就非得说话算话不可。”

“哎呀，丹尼尔大叔，您怎么不把这事儿跟我们这些孩子说个明白呢？我们也好帮您一起照看那袋钱呀。”

“嘿，要是透露给你们这帮小家伙，叫你们从但一直找到别是巴，那才叫傻呢！俺看你们里头保不齐有人到这会儿还在地里刨呢！”

===Original===
“Well, Uncle Daniel, we were young then; I can’t tell what we would have done if we had found it. But we are older now.”

“Yes, yer older, but I wouldn’t put it pas’ yer eben now, ef yer foun’ out whar it war.”

“Yes,” said Iola, laughing, “they say ‘caution is the parent of safety.’ ”

“Money’s a mighty tempting thing,” said Robert, smiling.

“But, Robby, dere’s nothin’ like a klar conscience; a klar conscience, Robby!”

Just then Aunt Linda, who had been completing the preparations for her supper, entered the room with her husband, and said, “Salters, let me interdoos you ter my fren’, Mr. Robert Johnson, an’ his niece, Miss Leroy.”

===Chinese===
“话虽这么说，丹尼尔大叔，我们那时还年轻；真找着了会干出什么来，我也说不好。可如今我们都长大啦。”

“是呀，你们是大了，可要是叫你们探出那钱搁在哪儿，俺就是对如今的你们也信不过。”

“是呀，”伊奥拉笑着说，“常言道，‘谨慎是安全之本。’”

“钱这东西，可迷人心窍着呢，”罗伯特微笑着说。

“可是罗比呀，啥也比不上一颗干干净净的良心；干干净净的良心哪，罗比！”

正在这时，一直在拾掇晚饭的琳达大婶同丈夫一道走进屋来，说道：“索尔特斯，让俺把你介绍给俺的朋友罗伯特·约翰逊先生，还有他的外甥女勒罗伊小姐。”

===Original===
“Why, is it possible,” exclaimed Robert, rising, and shaking hands, “that you are Aunt Linda’s husband?”

“Dat’s what de parson sed,” replied Salters.

“I thought,” pursued Robert, “that your name was John Andrews. It was such when you were in my company.”

“All de use I’se got fer dat name is ter git my money wid it; an’ wen dat’s done, all’s done. Got ’nuff ob my ole Marster in slave times, widout wearin’ his name in freedom. Wen I got done wid him, I got done wid his name. Wen I ’listed, I war John Andrews; and wen I gits my pension, I’se John Andrews; but now Salters is my name, an’ I likes it better.”

“But how came you to be Aunt Linda’s husband? Did you get married since the war?”

“Lindy an’ me war married long ’fore de war. But my ole Marster sole me away from her an’ our little gal, an’ den sole her chile ter somebody else. Arter freedom, I hunted up our little gal, an’ foun’ her. She war a big woman den. Den I com’d right back ter dis place an’ foun’ Lindy. She hedn’t married agin, nuther hed I; so we jis’ let de parson marry us out er de book; an’ we war mighty glad ter git togedder agin, an’ feel hitched togedder fer life.”

===Chinese===
“哎呀，竟有这样的事，”罗伯特起身握手，惊呼道，“您就是琳达大婶的丈夫？”

“牧师是这么说的，”索尔特斯答道。

“我记得，”罗伯特接着说，“您名叫约翰·安德鲁斯。您在我连里的时候，用的就是这个名字。”

“那个名字如今就剩一样用处——领钱的时候用得着它；钱一领完，也就万事皆休。做奴隶那阵子，俺在老主人手里受得够够的了，得了自由，犯不着再顶他的姓。俺入伍那会儿，是约翰·安德鲁斯；领抚恤金的时候，俺是约翰·安德鲁斯；可如今俺叫索尔特斯，这名字俺更喜欢。”

“可您怎么会成了琳达大婶的丈夫呢？你们是战后成的亲吗？”

“琳迪跟俺成亲，早在打仗以前。可俺们家老主人把俺从她跟俺们的小闺女身边卖掉，跟着又把她的孩子卖给了别人。自由以后，俺四处寻俺们的小闺女，把她寻着了。她那时已经长成个大女人啦。随后俺径直回到这个地方，又寻着了琳迪。她没有再嫁，俺也没有再娶；俺们就请牧师照着规矩，给俺们正经办了婚礼；俺们能重新团聚，觉得往后一辈子都拴在一处了，心里头那个高兴哟。”

===Original===
“Well, Uncle Daniel,” said Robert, turning the conversation toward him, “you and Uncle Ben wouldn’t go with us, but you came out all right at last.”

“Yes, indeed,” said Aunt Linda, “Ben got inter a stream of luck. Arter freedom com’d, de people had a heap of fath in Ben; an’ wen dey wanted someone to go ter Congress dey jist voted for Ben ter go. An’ he went, too. An’ wen Salters went to Washin’ton to git his pension, who should he see dere wid dem big men but our Ben, lookin’ jist as big as any ob dem.”

“An’ it did my ole eyes good jist ter see it,” broke in Salters; “if I couldn’t go dere myself, I war mighty glad to see someone ob my people dat could. I felt like de boy who, wen somebody said he war gwine to slap off his face, said, ‘Yer kin slap off my face, but I’se got a big brudder, an’ you can’t slap off his face.’ I went to see him ’fore I lef, and he war jist de same as he war wen we war boys togedder. He hadn’t got de big head a bit.”

“I reckon Mirandy war mighty sorry she didn’t stay wid him. I know I should be,” said Aunt Linda.

“Uncle Daniel,” asked Robert, “are you still preaching?”

“Yes, chile, I’se still firing off de Gospel gun.”

===Chinese===
“哎，丹尼尔大叔，”罗伯特把话头转回他身上，“当年您和本大叔不肯跟我们一块儿走，可到头来你们也都好好的。”

“可不是嘛，”琳达大婶说，“本交上了一路好运。自由以后，大家伙儿对本信得很；到他们想派人进国会，就投了本的票，叫他去。他还真去了。索尔特斯上华盛顿领抚恤金那回，在那些大人物中间瞧见的是谁？正是俺们的本，瞧那气派，跟他们里头哪一个都不差分毫。”

“俺这双老眼睛光瞧瞧那个就舒坦，”索尔特斯插嘴道；“俺自个儿去不了那地方，能见着俺们自己人里有一个去得了的，心里就欢喜得很。俺就觉着自己像那个孩子：有人说要扇他的耳光，他就说：‘你扇得着我的脸，可俺有个高大的哥哥，你扇不着他的脸。’俺临走前去瞧了他，他还是跟俺们小时候在一块儿那阵子一个样，一点儿也没翘尾巴。”

“俺估摸着米兰迪准悔得慌，懊悔当初没跟着他。换了俺，俺知道准得后悔，”琳达大婶说。

“丹尼尔大叔，”罗伯特问，“您还在讲道吗？”

“是啊，孩子，俺还在放福音的炮呢。”

===Original===
“I hear some of the Northern folks are down here teaching theology, that is, teaching young men how to preach. Why don’t you study theology?”

“Look a yere, boy, I’se been a preachin’ dese thirty years, an’ you come yere a tellin’ me ’bout studying yore ologies. I larn’d my ’ology at de foot ob de cross. You bin dar?”

“Dear Uncle Daniel,” said Iola, “the moral aspect of the nation would be changed if it would learn at the same cross to subordinate the spirit of caste to the spirit of Christ.”

“Does yer ’member Miss Nancy’s Harriet,” asked Aunt Linda, “dat she sole away kase she wouldn’t let her whip her? Well, we think dis is Harriet’s gran’chile. She war sole away from her mar, an’ now she’s a lookin’ fer her.”

“Well, I hopes she may fine her,” replied Salters. “I war sole ’way from my mammy wen I war eighteen mont’s ole, an’ I wouldn’t know her now from a bunch ob turnips.”

“I,” said Iola, “am on my way South seeking for my mother, and I shall not give up until I find her.”

===Chinese===
“我听说有些北方来的人在这里教神学，就是教年轻小伙子们怎么讲道。您怎么不去学学神学呢？”

“你听着，小子，俺传这三十年的道了，你倒跑来跟俺念叨啥学不学的。俺的神学是在十字架的脚下学来的。你去过那儿吗？”

“亲爱的丹尼尔大叔，”伊奥拉说，“倘若这个国家肯在那同一座十字架的脚下，学会把等级偏见的意气降在基督的精神之下，它道德的面貌就必焕然一新了。”

“你还记得南希小姐家的哈丽特不？”琳达大婶问，“就因为她不肯让她鞭打，被她卖掉的那个？俺们琢磨着，这就是哈丽特的外孙女儿。她当年让人从她娘身边卖掉，如今正寻她娘呢。”

“哎，俺盼着她能寻着，”索尔特斯答道。“俺十八个月大上就让人从俺娘身边卖掉了，如今就算把她跟一堆萝卜摆在一块儿，俺也认她不出。”

“我呢，”伊奥拉说，“正在南下寻母的路上；不找到她，我决不罢休。”

===Original===
“Come,” said Aunt Linda, “we mustn’t stan’ yer talkin’, or de grub’ll git cole. Come, frens, sit down, an’ eat some ob my pore supper.”

Aunt Linda sat at the table in such a flutter of excitement that she could hardly eat, but she gazed with intense satisfaction on her guests. Robert sat on her right hand, contrasting Aunt Linda’s pleasant situation with the old days in Mrs. Johnson’s kitchen, where he had played his pranks upon her, and told her the news of the war.

Over Iola there stole a spirit of restfulness. There was something so motherly in Aunt Linda’s manner that it seemed to recall the bright, sunshiny days when she used to nestle in Mam Liza’s arms, in her own happy home. The conversation was full of army reminiscences and recollections of the days of slavery. Uncle Daniel was much interested, and, as they rose from the table, exclaimed:﻿—

“Robby, seein’ yer an’ hearin’ yer talk, almos’ puts new springs inter me. I feel ’mos’ like I war gittin’ younger.”

===Chinese===
“来吧，”琳达大婶说，“俺们可不能光站在这儿说话，饭菜都要凉啦。来吧，朋友们，坐下，尝尝俺这顿粗茶淡饭。”

琳达大婶坐在桌旁，心里激动得扑腾扑腾的，几乎吃不下东西，只管满怀欣喜地望着她的客人。罗伯特坐在她右手边，把琳达大婶如今的美满光景，同当年她在老女主人约翰逊太太家厨房里的日子暗暗对比——想当年，他就是在那间厨房里跟她恶作剧，给她讲战争的新闻。

一种安宁的心绪悄悄袭上伊奥拉的心头。琳达大婶的举止那样慈爱，带着母亲般的气息，仿佛使她回想起那些明亮晴和的日子——那时她还在自己幸福的家里，依偎在丽扎嬷嬷的怀抱中。席间的谈话满是军旅往事和对奴隶岁月的回忆。丹尼尔大叔听得津津有味，大家起身离席时，他感叹道：——

“罗比，见了你、听了你说话，差不多等于给俺这把老骨头换了新簧。俺觉得自个儿简直越活越年轻了。”

===Original===
After the supper, Salters and his guests returned to the front room, which Aunt Linda regarded with so much pride, and on which she bestowed so much care.

“Well, Captin,” said Salters, “I neber ’spected ter see you agin. Do you know de las’ time I seed yer? Well, you war on a stretcher, an’ four ob us war carryin’ you ter de hospital. War you much hurt?

“No,” replied Robert, “it was only a flesh wound; and this young lady nursed me so carefully that I soon got over it.”

“Is dat de way you foun’ her?”

===Chinese===
晚饭之后，索尔特斯和客人们回到前屋——那间琳达大婶引以为荣、又悉心拾掇的屋子。

“唉，上尉，”索尔特斯说，“俺压根儿没指望还能见着您。您知道俺最后一回瞧见您是啥时候不？那回您躺在担架上，俺们四个人抬着您上医院。您伤得重不重？

“不重，”罗伯特答道，“只是点皮肉伤；这位年轻小姐悉心看护我，我很快就好了。”

“您就是这么寻着她的？”

===Original===
“Yes, Andrews,”﻿—

“Salters, ef you please,” interrupted Salters. “I’se only Andrews wen I gits my money.”

“Well, Salters,” continued Robert, “our freedom was a costly thing. Did you know that Captain Sybil was killed in one of the last battles of the war? These young chaps, who are taking it so easy, don’t know the hardships through which we older ones passed. But all the battles are not fought, nor all the victories won. The colored man has escaped from one slavery, and I don’t want him to fall into another. I want the young folks to keep their brains clear, and their right arms strong, to fight the battles of life manfully, and take their places alongside of every other people in this country. And I cannot see what is to hinder them if they get a chance.”

“I don’t nuther,” said Salters. “I don’t see dat dey drinks any more dan anybody else, nor dat dere is any meanness or debilment dat a black man kin do dat a white man can’t keep step wid him.”

“Yes,” assented Robert, “but while a white man is stealing a thousand dollars, a black man is getting into trouble taking a few chickens.”

“All that may be true,” said Iola, “but there are some things a white man can do that we cannot afford to do.”

===Chinese===
“是啊，安德鲁斯，”——

“劳驾叫俺索尔特斯，”索尔特斯打断道。“俺只有领钱的时候才是安德鲁斯。”

“好吧，索尔特斯，”罗伯特接着说，“我们的自由是花了大代价换来的。您可知道西比尔上尉就牺牲在这场战争快结束的一场战斗里？如今这些年轻人，日子过得这样安逸，哪里知道我们这些年长的人吃过的苦。可是战斗还没有全打完，胜利也没有全赢得。有色人种刚刚挣脱了一种奴役，我不愿他们再跌进另一种奴役里去。我要年轻人保持头脑清醒、右臂有力，堂堂正正地打人生这场仗，同这个国家里所有别的族群并肩站到一起。只要给他们机会，我看不出还有什么能拦得住他们。”

“俺也看不出，”索尔特斯说。“俺没瞧见他们喝酒比旁人多到哪儿去，也没瞧见有啥下作事儿、坏事儿是黑人干得出来、而白人跟不上步的。”

“是啊，”罗伯特表示同意，“可白人偷的是一千美元，黑人不过摸了几只鸡，就惹上麻烦了。”

“这些话也许都不假，”伊奥拉说，“可有些事情白人做得起，我们却做不起。”

===Original===
“I beliebs eberybody, Norf and Souf, is lookin’ at us; an’ some ob dem ain’t got no good blood fer us, nohow you fix it,” said Salters.

“I specs cullud folks mus’ hab done somethin’,” interposed Aunt Linda.

“O, nonsense,” said Robert. “I don’t think they are any worse than the white people. I don’t believe, if we had the power, we would do any more lynching, burning, and murdering than they do.”

“Dat’s so,” said Aunt Linda, “it’s ralely orful how our folks hab been murdered sence de war. But I don’t think dese young folks is goin’ ter take things as we’s allers done.”

“We war cowed down from the beginnin’,” said Uncle Daniel, “but dese young folks ain’t comin’ up dat way.”

“No,” said Salters, “fer one night arter some ob our pore people had been killed, an’ some ob our women had run’d away ’bout seventeen miles, my gran’son, looking me squar in de face, said: ‘Ain’t you got five fingers? Can’t you pull a trigger as well as a white man?’ I tell yer, Cap, dat jis’ got to me, an’ I made up my mine dat my boy should neber call me a coward.”

===Chinese===
“俺看北方南方，人人都在瞧着俺们；不管咋说，他们有些人对俺们可没什么好心肠，”索尔特斯说。

“俺估摸着，有色人种准是做过啥错事，”琳达大婶插嘴道。

“哦，胡说，”罗伯特说。“我不觉得他们比白人坏到哪里去。倘若我们手里有权，我也不信我们私刑、烧杀、谋害的，会干得比他们多。”

“可不，”琳达大婶说，“战后俺们的人遭杀害的那个惨法，真是没治了。可俺瞧着，这些年轻人不会再像俺们从前那样忍气吞声了。”

“俺们打一开始就被压服得大气不敢出，”丹尼尔大叔说，“可这些年轻人不是那么长大的。”

“是啊，”索尔特斯说，“有天夜里，俺们一些穷苦的人被杀害了，俺们一些女人逃出去跑了十七英里地；俺的孙子正盯着俺的脸说：‘你不是长着五根手指头吗？你扣扳机难道就扣不过一个白人？’俺跟你说，上尉，这话真戳进俺心窝子里去了，俺横下心来，俺的孩子往后绝不能叫俺一声胆小鬼。”

===Original===
“It is not to be expected,” said Robert, “that these young people are going to put up with things as we did, when we weren’t permitted to hold a meeting by ourselves, or to own a club or learn to read.”

“I tried,” said Salters, “to git a little out’er de book wen I war in de army. On Sundays I sometimes takes a book an’ tries to make out de words, but my eyes is gittin’ dim an’ de letters all run togedder, an’ I gits sleepy, an’ ef yer wants to put me to sleep jis’ put a book in my han’. But wen it comes to gittin’ out a stan’ ob cotton, an’ plantin’ corn, I’se dere all de time. But dat gran’son ob mine is smart as a steel trap. I specs he’ll be a preacher.”

Salters looked admiringly at his grandson, who sat grinning in the corner, munching a pear he had brought from the table.

“Yes,” said Aunt Linda, “his fadder war killed by the Secesh, one night, comin’ home from a politic meetin’, an’ his pore mudder died a few weeks arter, an’ we mean to make a man ob him.”

“He’s got to larn to work fust,” said Salters, “an’ den ef he’s right smart I’se gwine ter sen’ him ter college. An’ ef he can’t get a libin’ one way, he kin de oder.”

===Chinese===
“这也怪不得他们，”罗伯特说，“不能指望这些年轻人还像我们当年那样忍下去——那时候，我们连自己开个会、手里持一根棍棒、学认几个字，都不被许可。”

“俺在队伍上那阵儿，也试着从书里认过几个字。礼拜天俺有时拿本书，一个字一个字地硬认，可俺这眼睛越来越花，那些字母都挤成一团，俺就犯困；你要是想哄俺睡觉，只消往俺手里塞本书。可要说种出一茬好棉花、种好玉米，俺可是顶呱呱、什么时候都在行。俺那个孙子呀，机灵得像口利落的钢夹子。俺看他往后能当个传道人。”

索尔特斯疼爱地望着自己的孙子；那孩子坐在墙角咧着嘴笑，嘴里嚼着一只从饭桌上带来的梨。

“是啊，”琳达大婶说，“他爹一天夜里开完政治集会回家，叫分裂派杀害了；他可怜的娘过了几个礼拜也死了。俺们打定主意，要把他栽培成个顶用的男子汉。”

“他得先学会干活儿，”索尔特斯说，“往后他要真出息，俺就送他上大学。一条道儿谋不着生计，还有另一条道儿。”

===Original===
“Yes,” said Iola, “I hope he will turn out an excellent young man, for the greatest need of the race is noble, earnest men, and true women.”

“Job,” said Salters, turning to his grandson, “tell Jake ter hitch up de mules, an’ you stay dere an’ help him. We’s all gwine ter de big meetin’. Yore grandma hab set her heart on goin’, an’ it’ll be de same as a spell ob sickness ef she don’t hab a chance to show her bes’ bib an’ tucker. That ole gal’s as proud as a peacock.”

“Now, John Salters,” exclaimed Aunt Linda, “ain’t you ’shamed ob yourself? Allers tryin’ to poke fun at yer pore wife. Never mine; wait till I’se gone, an’ you’ll miss me.”

“Ef I war single,” said Salters, “I could git a putty young gal, but it wouldn’t be so easy wid you.”

“Why not?” said Iola, smiling.

“ ’Cause young men don’t want ole hens, an’ ole men want young pullets,” was Salter’s reply.

===Chinese===
“是啊，”伊奥拉说，“我盼着他长成一个出色的年轻人；因为这个种族最需要的，就是高尚而热诚的男子，和真诚可靠的女子。”

“乔布，”索尔特斯转向孙子说，“去告诉杰克套骡子，你就在那边给他搭把手。俺们全家都要去赴大聚会。你奶奶铁了心要去，要是不让她有机会显摆显摆她那身最好的行头，那就跟害了她一场大病一个样。那老婆子呀，骄傲得像只孔雀。”

“哎哟，约翰·索尔特斯，”琳达大婶嚷了起来，“你就不害臊吗？老是想方设法拿你那可怜的老婆打趣。不跟你计较；等着吧，等俺走了那天，有你想俺的时候。”

“俺要是打光棍儿，”索尔特斯说，“俺还能娶个俊俏的年轻闺女；可换成你，就没那么容易啦。”

“为什么不容易呢？”伊奥拉含笑问。

“因为年轻汉子不要老母鸡，老汉们专要小母鸡呗。”索尔特斯答道。

===Original===
“Robby, honey,” said Aunt Linda, “when you gits a wife, don’t treat her like dat man treats me.”

“Oh, his head’s level,” answered Robert; “at least it was in the army.”

“Dat’s jis’ de way; you see dat, Miss Iola? One man takin’ up for de oder. But I’ll be eben wid you bof. I must go now an’ git ready.”

Iola laughed. The homely enjoyment of that evening was very welcome to her after the trying scenes through which she had passed. Further conversation was interrupted by the appearance of the wagon, drawn by two fine mules. John Salters stopped joking his wife to admire his mules.

“Jis’ look at dem,” he said. “Ain’t dey beauties? I bought ’em out ob my bounty-money. Arter de war war ober I had a little money, an’ I war gwine ter rent a plantation on sheers an’ git out a good stan’ ob cotton. Cotton war bringin’ orful high prices den, but Lindy said to me, ‘Now, John, you’se got a lot ob money, an’ you’d better salt it down. I’d ruther lib on a little piece ob lan’ ob my own dan a big piece ob somebody else’s. Well, I says to Lindy, I dun know nuthin’ ’bout buyin’ lan’, an’ I’se ’fraid arter I’se done buyed it an’ put all de marrer ob dese bones in it, dat somebody’s far-off cousin will come an’ say de title ain’t good, an’ I’ll lose it all.”

“You’re right thar, John,” said Uncle Daniel. “White man’s so unsartain, black man’s nebber safe.”

===Chinese===
“罗比，宝贝儿，”琳达大婶说，“往后你娶了媳妇，可别像那个人作践俺似的作践她。”

“哦，他脑子清醒得很，”罗伯特答道，“至少在队伍上那阵子是这样。”

“瞧瞧，就是这个样儿；您瞧见了没有，伊奥拉小姐？一个男人总护着另一个男人。可俺迟早要跟你们俩算账。俺这会儿得去拾掇拾掇了。”

伊奥拉笑了起来。她一路历经那些揪心的场面之后，这天晚上这份质朴的家常欢愉，对她真是莫大的慰藉。谈话被一辆马车的到来打断了——车套着两头漂亮的骡子。约翰·索尔特斯立刻收了打趣老婆的话头，欣赏起他的骡子来。

“你瞧瞧它们，”他说。“俊不俊？这是俺拿当兵的赏金买的。打完仗那会儿俺手头有点钱，本想租下种植园分股种，收上一茬好棉花。那阵子棉花的价钱高得吓人，可琳迪跟俺说：‘我说约翰，你如今揣着一大笔钱，最好把它腌存起来。俺宁肯住自家一小块地，也不去住旁人家一大块地。’俺就跟琳迪说，买地的事儿俺一窍不通，俺生怕买下来、把这把老骨头的骨髓都填进去之后，不知打哪儿冒出个远房亲戚来，说这地契不算数，俺就全赔光了。

“这话不假，约翰，”丹尼尔大叔说。“白人那么靠不住，黑人永远不得安稳。”

===Original===
“But somehow,” continued Salters, “Lindy warn’t satisfied wid rentin’, so I buyed a piece ob lan’, an’ I’se glad now I’se got it. Lindy’s got a lot ob gumption; knows most as much as a man. She ain’t got dat long head fer nuffin. She’s got lots ob sense, but I don’t like to tell her so.”

“Why not?” asked Iola. “Do you think it would make her feel too happy?”

“Well, it don’t do ter tell you women how much we thinks ob you. It sets you up too much. Ole Gundover’s overseer war my marster, an’ he used ter lib in dis bery house. I’se fixed it up sence I’se got it. Now I’se better off dan he is, ’cause he tuck to drink, an’ all his frens is gone, an’ he’s in de pore-house.”

Just then Linda came to the door with her baskets.

“Now, Lindy, ain’t you ready yet? Do hurry up.”

“Yes, I’se ready, but things wouldn’t go right ef you didn’t hurry me.”

===Chinese===
“不过也不知咋的，”索尔特斯接着说，“琳迪不甘心租地，俺就买了一块地，如今想想真买对了。琳迪那心窍灵得很；懂得的不比一个男人少。她那副长远眼光可不是白长的。她有的是见识，只是俺不爱当面夸她。”

“为什么不当面夸呢？”伊奥拉问。“您是怕她乐坏了不成？”

“唉，俺们心里多么看重你们女人家，这话可不能说出口。一说出口，你们尾巴就翘到天上去了。老冈多弗家的监工就是俺旧时的主人，他从前住的正是这所房子。俺置下它之后，把它修拾一新。如今俺的日子比他强多啦——他喝上了酒，朋友也散尽了，进了济贫院。”

正在这时，琳达挎着篮子来到门口。

“哎，琳迪，你还没拾掇利索？快着点儿。”

“利索了利索了，可没有你催着，俺这些事儿还就顺当不了。”

===Original===
“Well, put your chicken fixins an’ cake right in yere. Captin, you’ll ride wid me, an’ de young lady an’ my ole woman’ll take de back seat. Uncle Dan’el, dere’s room for you ef you’ll go.”

“No, I thank you. It’s time fer ole folks to go to bed. Good night! An’, Bobby, I hopes to see you agin’.”

===Chinese===
“那就把你的鸡肉吃食和蛋糕搁这儿。上尉，您跟俺同坐前头；这位年轻小姐和俺家老婆子坐后座。丹尼尔大叔，您要是去，车上也有您的地儿。”

“不了，谢谢。老人们到睡觉的时辰喽。晚安！还有你，博比，俺盼着还能见着你。”

## VIII The Mystified Doctor / 第八章 满腹疑团的医生

===Original===
“Colonel,” said Dr. Gresham to Col. Robinson, the commander of the post, “I am perfectly mystified by Miss Leroy.”

“What is the matter with her?” asked Col. Robinson. “Is she not faithful to her duties and obedient to your directions?”

“Faithful is not the word to express her tireless energy and devotion to her work,” responded Dr. Gresham. “She must have been a born nurse to put such enthusiasm into her work.”

“Why, Doctor, what is the matter with you? You talk like a lover.”

A faint flush rose to the cheek of Dr. Gresham as he smiled, and said, “Oh! come now, Colonel, can’t a man praise a woman without being in love with her?”

“Of course he can,” said Col. Robinson; “but I know where such admiration is apt to lead. I’ve been there myself. But, Doctor, had you not better defer your lovemaking till you’re out of the woods?”

===Chinese===
“上校，”格雷沙姆医生（Dr. Gresham）对驻地指挥官罗宾逊上校（Col. Robinson）说，“勒罗伊小姐（Miss Leroy）实在让我满腹疑团。”

“她怎么啦？”罗宾逊上校问。“她难道不是恪尽职守、事事遵从你的嘱咐吗？”

“‘恪尽职守’还不足以形容她那不知疲倦的劲头和对工作的忘我投入，”格雷沙姆医生应道。“能把这样的热忱倾注到工作里，她准是个天生的看护。”

“哎哟，医生，你这是怎么啦？说起话来倒像个谈情说爱的人。”

格雷沙姆医生的脸颊上泛起一抹淡淡的红晕，他含笑说道：“哦，得啦，上校，难道一个男人夸奖一个女人，就非得爱上她不成？”

“夸当然是可以夸的，”罗宾逊上校说；“可这样的倾慕容易把人引到什么地方去，我心里清楚。我自己就是从那条路上过来的。不过，医生，你把求爱的事推迟到出了这片林子再办，岂不更好？”

===Original===
“I assure you, Colonel, I am not thinking of love or courtship. That is the business of the drawing-room, and not of the camp. But she did mystify me last night.”

“How so?” asked Col. Robinson.

“When Tom was dying,” responded the doctor, “I saw that beautiful and refined young lady bend over and kiss him. When she found that he was dead, she just cried as if her heart was breaking. Well, that was a new thing to me. I can eat with colored people, walk, talk, and fight with them, but kissing them is something I don’t hanker after.”

“And yet you saw Miss Leroy do it?”

“Yes; and that puzzles me. She is one of the most refined and ladylike women I ever saw. I hear she is a refugee, but she does not look like the other refugees who have come to our camp. Her accent is slightly Southern, but her manner is Northern. She is self-respecting without being supercilious; quiet, without being dull. Her voice is low and sweet, yet at times there are tones of such passionate tenderness in it that you would think some great sorrow has darkened and overshadowed her life. Without being the least gloomy, her face at times is pervaded by an air of inexpressible sadness. I sometimes watch her when she is not aware that I am looking at her, and it seems as if a whole volume was depicted on her countenance. When she smiles, there is a longing in her eyes which is never satisfied. I cannot understand how a Southern lady, whose education and manners stamp her as a woman of fine culture and good breeding, could consent to occupy the position she so faithfully holds. It is a mystery I cannot solve. Can you?”

“I think I can,” answered Col. Robinson.

===Chinese===
“我向你保证，上校，我没有在想什么爱情或求婚的事。那是客厅里的事，不是军营里的事。可昨天夜里，她着实让我摸不着头脑。”

“此话怎讲？”罗宾逊上校问。

“汤姆（Tom）咽气的时候，”医生应道，“我看见那位美丽文雅的年轻小姐俯下身去吻他。她发现人已经死了，就哭起来，哭得好像心都要碎了。唉，这在我可是件新鲜事。我可以同有色人种一起吃饭、走路、说话、并肩打仗，可要说到亲吻他们，那可不是我心向往之的事。”

“可你不是亲眼看见勒罗伊小姐这么做了吗？”

“看见了；而这正是让我想不通的地方。她是我平生所见最娴雅、最有大家闺秀风范的女子之一。听说她是逃难来的，可她又不像到我们营里来的其他逃难者。她的口音略带南方腔调，举止做派却是北方的。她自尊而不倨傲，娴静而不呆板。她的嗓音低柔悦耳，可有时那声音里又透出那样炽烈的柔情，你会以为有什么莫大的哀伤遮暗了、罩住了她的一生。她的面容虽无半点阴郁，却时而弥漫着一种难以言喻的哀愁。我有时趁她不觉得我在看她的时候打量她，只觉得她那张脸上仿佛写着一整部书。她微笑的时候，眼睛里含着一种永远得不到满足的渴望。我不明白，一位南方小姐，教养与风度都表明她是位极有文化、极有教养的女子，怎么会甘心守着她如今这样尽心尽力担任的职位。这是我一猜不透的谜。你猜得透吗？”

“我想我猜得透，”罗宾逊上校答道。

===Original===
“Will you tell me?” queried the doctor.

“Yes, on one condition.”

“What is it?”

“Everlasting silence.”

“I promise,” said the doctor. “The secret between us shall be as deep as the sea.”

“She has not requested secrecy, but at present, for her sake, I do not wish the secret revealed. Miss Leroy was a slave.”

===Chinese===
“愿意讲给我听吗？”医生问道。

“愿意，不过有个条件。”

“什么条件？”

“永远守口如瓶。”

“我答应，”医生说。“咱们俩之间的这个秘密，要藏得像海一样深。”

“她本人并没有要求保密，只是眼下为了她的缘故，我不愿这个秘密泄露出去。勒罗伊小姐做过奴隶。”

===Original===
“Oh, no,” said Dr. Gresham, starting to his feet, “it can’t be so! A woman as white as she a slave?”

“Yes, it is so,” continued the Colonel. “In these States the child follows the condition of its mother. This beautiful and accomplished girl was held by one of the worst Rebels in town. Tom told me of it and I issued orders for her release.”

“Well, well! Is that so?” said Dr. Gresham, thoughtfully stroking his beard. “Wonders will never cease. Why, I was just beginning to think seriously of her.”

“What’s to hinder your continuing to think?” asked Col. Robinson.

“What you tell me changes the whole complexion of affairs,” replied the doctor.

===Chinese===
“哦，不，”格雷沙姆医生霍地站起身来，“不可能是这样！像她这样白皙的女子，会是个奴隶？”

“是的，就是这样，”上校接着说。“在这些州，孩子的身分随母亲而定。这位才貌双全的姑娘，被城里最死硬的叛党之一霸占着。这事是汤姆告诉我的，我下了释放她的命令。”

“哎呀，哎呀！当真如此？”格雷沙姆医生说着，若有所思地捋着胡须。“真是奇事迭出，没有个完。唉，我正刚开始对她认真起来呢。”

“那又有什么妨碍你继续想下去的？”罗宾逊上校问。

“你告诉我的这件事，把整桩事情的面目都改变了，”医生答道。

===Original===
“If that be so I am glad I told you before you got head over heels in love.”

“Yes,” said Dr. Gresham, absently.

Dr. Gresham was a member of a wealthy and aristocratic family, proud of its lineage, which it could trace through generations of good blood to its ancestral isle. He had become deeply interested in Iola before he had heard her story, but after it had been revealed to him he tried to banish her from his mind; but his constant observation of her only increased his interest and admiration. The deep pathos of her story, the tenderness of her ministrations, bestowed alike on black and white, and the sad loneliness of her condition, awakened within him a desire to defend and protect her all through her future life. The fierce clashing of war had not taken all the romance out of his nature. In Iola he saw realized his ideal of the woman whom he was willing to marry. A woman, tender, strong, and courageous, and rescued only by the strong arm of his Government from a fate worse than death. She was young in years, but old in sorrow; one whom a sad destiny had changed from a lighthearted girl to a heroic woman. As he observed her, he detected an undertone of sorrow in her most cheerful words, and observed a quick flushing and sudden paling of her cheek, as if she were living over scenes that were thrilling her soul with indignation or chilling her heart with horror. As nurse and physician, Iola and Dr. Gresham were constantly thrown together. His friends sent him magazines and books, which he gladly shared with her. The hospital was a sad place. Mangled forms, stricken down in the flush of their prime and energy; pale young corpses, sacrificed on the altar of slavery, constantly drained on her sympathies. Dr. Gresham was glad to have some reading matter which might divert her mind from the memories of her mournful past, and also furnish them both with interesting themes of conversation in their moments of relaxation from the harrowing scenes through which they were constantly passing. Without any effort or consciousness on her part, his friendship ripened into love. To him her presence was a pleasure, her absence a privation; and her loneliness drew deeply upon his sympathy. He would have merited his own self-contempt if, by word or deed, he had done anything to take advantage of her situation. All the manhood and chivalry of his nature rose in her behalf, and, after carefully revolving the matter, he resolved to win her for his bride, bury her secret in his Northern home, and hide from his aristocratic relations all knowledge of her mournful past. One day he said to Iola:﻿—

===Chinese===
“倘若当真如此，我很庆幸趁你还没一头栽进爱河，就把这话告诉了你。”

“是啊，”格雷沙姆医生心不在焉地说。

格雷沙姆医生出身于一个富贵的贵族门第，这个家族为自家的世系自豪——凭着世代相承的好血统，可以一直追溯到祖居的岛屿。还没有听到她的身世之前，他已对伊奥拉（Iola）深感兴趣；待身世向他揭开之后，他曾试着把她从心头排遣开去；可他日复一日地注视她，反而只让他对她的兴趣和倾慕有增无减。她身世里深沉的哀婉，她不分黑人白人一视同仁的温柔看护，以及她处境中那份凄凉孤单，都在他心里唤起了一个愿望：要在她今后的一生里守护她、卫护她。战争的激烈冲杀并没有把他天性里的浪漫气质扫荡干净。在伊奥拉身上，他看到自己愿意迎娶的那个女子的理想成了现实——一个温柔、坚强而勇敢的女子，全凭他的政府强有力的臂膀，才从比死亡更悲惨的命运中被解救出来。她年华正少，却历尽忧患；一场悲哀的命运，把这个无忧无虑的姑娘造就成了一个英勇的女子。他观察她时，听得出她最快活的话语底下也压着一缕哀愁，看得出她的脸颊忽而泛红、忽而转白，仿佛她正一幕幕重新经历着某些旧景——那些旧景或以义愤激荡她的灵魂，或以恐怖冷却她的心。身为看护和医生，伊奥拉和格雷沙姆医生时时聚在一处。他的朋友们给他寄来杂志和书籍，他都乐意与她分享。医院是个伤心之地。血肉模糊的躯体，正在血气方刚、精力旺盛的年华里被击倒；一具具苍白的年轻尸体，作为献在奴隶制祭坛上的牺牲，时时刻刻都在消耗着她的怜悯。格雷沙姆医生很高兴手头有些读物，既可引开她对伤心往事的记忆，也可在他们从一幕幕惨烈景象中抽身歇息的时刻，为两人提供饶有趣味的话题。她全不经意、也毫无所觉，他的友谊却成熟成了爱情。对他来说，她在眼前是一种快乐，不在眼前是一种缺憾；她的孤单深深地牵动他的怜惜。他若有只言片语、一举一动趁她处境之危占了她的便宜，那便辜负了他的自重，连他自己都要瞧不起自己。他天性中全部的男子气概与侠义精神都为她奋然而起；他把这件事仔细盘算了一番之后，决意娶她为妻，把她的秘密埋进他北方的家中，瞒住他那些贵族亲戚，不叫他们对她伤心的过去知道分毫。一天，他对伊奥拉说道：

===Original===
“This hospital life is telling on you. Your strength is failing, and although you possess a wonderful amount of physical endurance, you must not forget that saints have bodies and dwell in tabernacles of clay, just the same as we common mortals.”

“Compliments aside,” she said, smiling; “what are you driving at, Doctor?”

“I mean,” he replied, “that you are running down, and if you do not quit and take some rest you will be our patient instead of our nurse. You’d better take a furlough, go North, and return after the first frost.”

===Chinese===
“这种医院生活正在损耗你的身体。你的气力一天不如一天；虽然你的身体有着惊人的耐受力，可你不要忘了，圣徒也有肉体，也住在泥造的帐棚里，同我们这些寻常的凡人没有两样。”

“恭维话且收起，”她含笑说；“您到底想说什么，医生？”

“我是说，”他答道，“你的身体在走下坡路；你要是不停下来歇一歇，就要从咱们的看护变成咱们的病人了。你最好休个假，回北方去，等头场霜降之后再回来。”

===Original===
“Doctor, if that is your only remedy,” replied Iola, “I am afraid that I am destined to die at my post. I have no special friends in the North, and no home but this in the South. I am homeless and alone.”

There was something so sad, almost despairing in her tones, in the drooping of her head, and the quivering of her lip, that they stirred Dr. Gresham’s heart with sudden pity, and, drawing nearer to her, he said, “Miss Leroy, you need not be all alone. Let me claim the privilege of making your life bright and happy. Iola, I have loved you ever since I have seen your devotion to our poor, sick boys. How faithfully you, a young and gracious girl, have stood at your post and performed your duties. And now I ask, will you not permit me to clasp hands with you for life? I do not ask for a hasty reply. Give yourself time to think over what I have proposed.”

===Chinese===
“医生，要是这就是您唯一的药方，”伊奥拉答道，“我怕我是注定要死在自己的岗位上了。我在北方没有特别亲近的朋友，在南方除了这里也没有别的家。我无处安身，孤身一人。”

她的声调里、她低垂的头颅上、她颤抖的嘴唇间，有一种那样悲哀、近乎绝望的东西，霎时间激起了格雷沙姆医生满心的怜惜；他向她走近一步，说道：“勒罗伊小姐，你不必这样孤孤单单。请让我享有让你一生明亮幸福的权利吧。伊奥拉，自从看见你这样看护我们可怜的伤病员，我就爱上你了。你这样一位年轻温雅的姑娘，坚守岗位、履行职责，何等忠心。如今我问你：可否允许我与你执手相守，共度此生？我不求你立刻答复。给你自己一点时间，好好思量我的提议。”

## XI The Plague and the Law / 第十一章 瘟疫与法律

===Original===
Years passed, bringing no special change to the life of Leroy and his wife. Shut out from the busy world, its social cares and anxieties, Marie’s life flowed peacefully on. Although removed by the protecting care of Leroy from the condition of servitude, she still retained a deep sympathy for the enslaved, and was ever ready to devise plans to ameliorate their condition.

Leroy, although in the midst of slavery, did not believe in the rightfulness of the institution. He was in favor of gradual emancipation, which would prepare both master and slave for a moral adaptation to the new conditions of freedom. While he was willing to have the old rivets taken out of slavery, politicians and planters were devising plans to put in new screws. He was desirous of having it ended in the States; they were clamorous to have it established in the Territories.

But so strong was the force of habit, combined with the feebleness of his moral resistance and the nature of his environment, that instead of being an athlete, armed for a glorious strife, he had learned to drift where he should have steered, to float with the current instead of nobly breasting the tide. He conducted his plantation with as much lenity as it was possible to infuse into a system darkened with the shadow of a million crimes.

Leroy had always been especially careful not to allow his children to spend their vacations at home. He and Marie generally spent that time with them at some summer resort.

===Chinese===
岁月一年年过去，勒罗伊和妻子的生活并没有什么特别的变化。玛丽被隔绝在喧嚣忙碌的世界和它种种社交的操劳烦忧之外，日子平静地流过去。虽然靠着勒罗伊的呵护看顾，她已脱离了为奴的境遇，心里却始终对为奴的人们怀着深切的同情，总在筹谋着改善他们境遇的法子。

勒罗伊虽身处奴隶制之中，却并不信这制度有什么正当性。他主张渐进的奴隶解放，好让主人和奴隶都做好道德上的准备，去适应自由的新境况。他愿意让人把奴隶制上的旧铆钉一颗颗起下来，政客和种植园主们却在盘算着往里拧新的螺丝。他巴不得在各州把它终结，他们却鼓噪着要在领地上把它确立起来。

然而习惯的力量是那样强大，再加上他道德抵抗力的薄弱和所处环境的性质，他非但没有成为一名披甲执锐、投身光荣抗争的健将，反倒学会了在本该掌舵之处随波漂荡，在本该昂然迎浪之处顺流漂浮。他管理种植园，把能灌注进去的宽厚尽量灌注进去——只是这制度已被千百万桩罪行的阴影笼罩得黑沉沉的了。

勒罗伊向来格外小心，从不让孩子们回家消度假期。他和玛丽通常是到哪处避暑胜地去，同他们一起度过那段时光。

===Original===
“I would like,” said Marie, one day, “to have our children spend their vacations at home. Those summer resorts are pleasant, yet, after all, there is no place like home. But,” and her voice became tremulous, “our children would now notice their social isolation and inquire the cause.” A faint sigh arose to the lips of Leroy, as she added: “Man is a social being; I’ve known it to my sorrow.”

There was a tone of sadness in Leroy’s voice, as he replied: “Yes, Marie, let them stay North. We seem to be entering on a period fraught with great danger. I cannot help thinking and fearing that we are on the eve of a civil war.”

“A civil war!” exclaimed Marie, with an air of astonishment. “A civil war about what?”

“Why, Marie, the thing looks to me so wild and foolish I hardly know how to explain. But some of our leading men have come to the conclusion that North and South had better separate, and instead of having one to have two independent governments. The spirit of secession is rampant in the land. I do not know what the result will be, and I fear it will bode no good to the country. Between the fire-eating Southerners and the meddling Abolitionists we are about to be plunged into a great deal of trouble. I fear there are breakers ahead. The South is dissatisfied with the state of public opinion in the North. We are realizing that we are two peoples in the midst of one nation. William H. Seward has proclaimed that the conflict between freedom and slavery is irrepressible, and that the country cannot remain half free and half slave.”

===Chinese===
“我倒想，”一天，玛丽说，“让咱们自己的孩子回家来过假期。那些避暑胜地固然叫人欢喜，可说到底，天下没有一个地方比得上自己的家。只是——”她的声音颤抖起来，“如今咱们的孩子会察觉自己社交上的孤立，会追问个中缘由了。”勒罗伊唇边浮起一声轻叹，只听她又添了一句：“人是要活在人群里的；这个道理，我是伤透了心才领教的。”

勒罗伊答话时，声音里透着悲凉：“是啊，玛丽，就让他们留在北方吧。我们似乎正步入一个危机四伏的时期。我总是不由得想、也不由得怕：我们已处在一场内战的前夜了。”

“内战！”玛丽喊道，满脸惊愕。“为着什么要打内战？”

“唉，玛丽，这桩事在我看来实在狂悖愚蠢，我简直不知该怎么解释才好。可我们有几位头面人物已经拿定主张，认为北方和南方不如就此分开，不要一个政府，索性立两个独立的政府。分裂的气焰正在这片土地上甚嚣尘上。我不知道结局会怎样，只怕它给国家带来的绝非吉兆。一边是吞火成性的南方人，一边是多管闲事的废奴主义者，我们眼看就要被拖进重重祸患里了。我怕前头尽是碎浪暗礁。南方对北方的舆论现状心怀不满。我们正日渐看清：我们分明是一个国家当中的两个民族。威廉·H·苏厄德（William H. Seward）已经宣称，自由与奴隶制之间的冲突不可遏制，这个国家绝不可能一半自由、一半蓄奴。”

===Original===
“How will *you* go?” asked Marie.

“My heart is with the Union. I don’t believe in secession. There has been no cause sufficient to justify a rupture. The North has met us time and again in the spirit of concession and compromise. When we wanted the continuance of the African slave trade the North conceded that we should have twenty years of slave-trading for the benefit of our plantations. When we wanted more territory she conceded to our desires and gave us land enough to carve out four States, and there yet remains enough for four more. When we wanted power to recapture our slaves when they fled North for refuge, Daniel Webster told Northerners to conquer their prejudices, and they gave us the whole Northern States as a hunting ground for our slaves. The Presidential chair has been filled the greater number of years by Southerners, and the majority of offices has been shared by our men. We wanted representation in Congress on a basis which would include our slaves, and the North, whose suffrage represents only men, gave us a three-fifths representation for our slaves, whom we count as property. I think the step will be suicidal. There are extremists in both sections, but I hope, between them both, wise counsels and measures will prevail.”

Just then Alfred Lorraine was ushered into the room. Occasionally he visited Leroy, but he always came alone. His wife was the only daughter of an enterprising slave-trader, who had left her a large amount of property.

===Chinese===
“那你自己要怎么走呢？”玛丽问。

“我的心向着联邦。我不信分裂那一套。要论决裂，迄今还没有哪条理由够分量、能叫人理直气壮。北方曾一次又一次本着退让与妥协的精神迁就我们。我们要接着做非洲的奴隶贸易，北方就应允我们再做二十年的奴隶买卖，好叫我们的种植园得好处。我们要更多的疆土，它就顺从我们的心愿，给的地盘足够开辟出四个州，剩下的还够再划出四个。我们要有权在我们奴隶逃到北方避难时把他们捉拿回来，丹尼尔·韦伯斯特（Daniel Webster）便劝北方人克服自己的偏见，于是他们把整个北方各州都交给我们，当作猎捕奴隶的围场。总统的宝座，大部分年头坐的都是南方人，多数官职也由我们的人分掌。我们要求国会的代表权把奴隶也算进基数，北方——它自己的选票单只算人头——便给了我们的奴隶五分之三的代表权，尽管我们只把他们当财产看。依我看，这一步是自取灭亡。南北两边都有走极端的人，可我指望在这两派之间，明智的谋略与举措终归会占上风。”

恰在此时，有人把阿尔弗雷德·洛兰引进屋来。他偶尔来看看勒罗伊，但每次总是独自前来。他的妻子是一位买卖做得红火的奴隶贩子的独女，那贩子给她留下了一大笔财产。

===Original===
Her social training was deficient, her education limited, but she was too proud of being a pure white woman to enter the home of Leroy, with Marie as its presiding genius. Lorraine tolerated Marie’s presence as a necessary evil, while to her he always seemed like a presentiment of trouble. With his coming a shadow fell upon her home, hushing its music and darkening its sunshine. A sense of dread oppressed her. There came into her soul an intuitive feeling that somehow his coming was fraught with danger. When not peering around she would often catch his eyes bent on her with a baleful expression.

Leroy and his cousin immediately fell into a discussion on the condition of the country. Lorraine was a rank Secessionist, ready to adopt the most extreme measures of the leaders of the movement, even to the reopening of the slave trade. Leroy thought a dissolution of the Union would involve a fearful expenditure of blood and treasure for which, before the eyes of the world, there could be no justification. The debate lasted late into the night, leaving both Lorraine and Leroy just as set in their opinions as they were before they began. Marie listened attentively awhile, then excused herself and withdrew.

After Lorraine had gone Marie said: “There is something about your cousin that fills me with nameless dread. I always feel when he enters the room as if someone were walking over my grave. I do wish he would stay at home.”

“I wish so, too, since he disturbs you. But, Marie, you are growing nervous. How cold your hands are. Don’t you feel well?”

===Chinese===
她在社交的教养上有所欠缺，学识也有限，却因为自己是个血统纯正的白人女子而自傲得很，说什么也不肯踏进勒罗伊的家门——那座宅子里当家主事的，是玛丽。洛兰把玛丽的存在当作一种免不了的祸害，勉强容忍着；而在玛丽眼里，他永远像是一桩灾祸临头的预感。他一来，她家里便落下一层阴影，乐声沉寂下去，日光也黯淡下来。一种惶惧压在她心头。她灵魂里生出一种直觉，总觉得他此来冥冥中带着凶险。趁他没在四下里窥探的当口，她常常撞见他的目光盯在自己身上，带着一股恶狠狠的神气。

勒罗伊和他的表兄立刻就国家的时局争论起来。洛兰是个彻头彻尾的分裂派，随时准备采纳这场运动领袖们最极端的措施，甚至不惜重开奴隶贸易。勒罗伊则认为，联邦一旦解体，势必要付出骇人听闻的鲜血与财货的代价，这在全世界的眼前都无从辩白。辩论直到深夜才歇，结果洛兰和勒罗伊还同开场之前一样，各执一说，纹丝不移。玛丽专心听了一阵子，便告了退，回房去了。

洛兰走后，玛丽说：“你这表兄身上有一种说不清道不明的东西，叫我心里满是莫名的恐惧。每回他一进屋，我总觉得像是有人正从我的坟头上踩过去。我真巴不得他守在自己家里别出门。”

“既然他搅得你不得安宁，我也巴不得这样。可是，玛丽，你的神经越来越经不起事了。你的手多凉啊。你觉得不舒服吗？”

===Original===
