“Oh, yes; I am only a little faint. I wish he would never come. But, as he does, I must make the best of it.”

“Yes, Marie, treat him well for my sake. He is the only relative I have who ever darkens our doors.”

“I have no faith in his friendship for either myself or my children. I feel that while he makes himself agreeable to you he hates me from the bottom of his heart, and would do anything to get me out of the way. Oh, I am *so* glad I am your lawful wife, and that you married me before you brought me back to this State! I believe that if you were gone he wouldn’t have the least scruple against trying to prove our marriage invalid and remanding us to slavery.”

Leroy looked anxiously and soberly at his wife, and said: “Marie, I do not think so. Your life is too lonely here. Write your orders to New Orleans, get what you need for the journey, and let us spend the summer somewhere in the North.”

===Chinese===
“哦，没有；我只不过是有点儿发晕。我盼着他永远别登门。可他既然要来，我也只好尽量往好里应付。”

“好吧，玛丽，看在我的分上，好生待他吧。他是我亲戚里头唯一还肯登一登咱们这道门的人。”

“我信不过他对我也好、对孩子们也好会有什么情谊。我总觉得，他在你面前百般殷勤可意，心里却把我恨到了根底，为了把我挪开，什么都做得出来。啊，我是你的合法妻子，而且你是在带我回这个州之前就娶了我，我真是说不尽的庆幸！我相信，你若有个三长两短，他会毫无顾忌地设法证明我们的婚姻无效，把我们发还为奴。”

勒罗伊忧心而持重地望了妻子一眼，说道：“玛丽，我不这么想。你在这里的日子太孤清了。给新奥尔良写信置办吧，把一路要用的东西都备齐，我们到北方找个地方过这个夏天去。”

===Original===
Just then Marie’s attention was drawn to some household matters, and it was a short time before she returned.

“Tom,” continued Leroy, “has just brought the mail, and here is a letter from Iola.”

Marie noticed that he looked quite sober as he read, and that an expression of vexation was lingering on his lips.

“What is the matter?” asked Marie.

===Chinese===
恰在这时，几件家务把玛丽岔了出去，隔了好一阵她才回来。

“汤姆，”勒罗伊接着说道，“刚把邮件取回来了，这儿有一封伊奥拉的信。”

玛丽注意到，他读信时神色十分凝重，唇边还噙着一丝恼意。

“出什么事了？”玛丽问。

===Original===
“Nothing much; only a tempest in a teapot. The presence of a colored girl in Mr. Galen’s school has caused a breeze of excitement. You know Mr. Galen is quite an Abolitionist, and, being true to his principles, he could not consistently refuse when a colored woman applied for her daughter’s admission. Of course, when he took her he was compelled to treat her as any other pupil. In so doing he has given mortal offense to the mother of two Southern boys. She has threatened to take them away if the colored girl remains.”

“What will he do about it?” asked Marie, thoughtfully.

“Oh, it is a bitter pill, but I think he will have to swallow it. He is between two fires. He cannot dismiss her from the school and be true to his Abolition principles; yet if he retains her he will lose his Southern customers, and I know he cannot afford to do that.”

“What does Iola say?”

===Chinese===
“没什么大不了的事，不过是茶壶里翻了场风暴。盖伦先生的学校里收了一名有色人种姑娘，掀起了好一阵风潮。你是知道的，盖伦先生是个十足的废奴主义者，既然要忠于自己的原则，一位有色人种妇女替女儿申请入学，他便无法一口回绝。当然，他既收下了她，就不能不把她同任何别的学生一样看待。这样一来，他把两个南方男孩的母亲得罪苦了。那位太太放了话：只要那有色人种姑娘还留在学校，她就把两个儿子带走。”

“那他打算怎么办呢？”玛丽若有所思地问。

“唉，这是一剂苦药，可我想他也只好咽下去了。他被夹在两堆火中间。把那姑娘从学校里开除吧，就对他废奴主义的原则不忠；把她留下吧，又会失掉南方的那些主顾，而我知道，这份损失他可担当不起。”

“伊奥拉怎么说？”

===Original===
“He has found another boarding place for her, but she is to remain in the school. He had to throw that sop to the whale.”

“Does she take sides against the girl?”

“No, I don’t think she does. She says she feels sorry for her, and that she would hate to be colored. ‘It is so hard to be looked down on for what one can’t help.’ ”

“Poor child! I wish we could leave the country. I never would consent to her marrying anyone without first revealing to him her connection with the negro race. This is a subject on which I am not willing to run any risks.”

“My dear Marie, when you shall have read Iola’s letter you will see it is more than a figment of my imagination that has made me so loth to have our children know the paralyzing power of caste.”

===Chinese===
“他给那姑娘另觅了一处寄宿的地方，不过人还留在学校里念书。他总得丢块饵出去安抚那条鲸鱼。”

“那伊奥拉是不是也跟那姑娘作对呢？”

“不，我看没有。她说她替那姑娘难过，还说她可不愿自己是有色人种。‘一个人为自己身不由己的事受人小看，这滋味实在太苦了。’”

“可怜的孩子！我真盼望我们能离开这个国度。若不先向那人挑明她与黑人种族的牵连，我绝不会应许她嫁给他。在这个题目上，我一步风险也不愿冒。”

“我亲爱的玛丽，等你读了伊奥拉的这封信，你就会明白：叫我这样不情愿让孩子们去领教等级偏见那摧人瘫痪的威力，绝不只是我凭空捏造出来的心思。”

===Original===
Leroy, always liberal with his wife and children, spared neither pains nor expense to have them prepared for their summer outing. Iola was to graduate in a few days. Harry was attending a school in the State of Maine, and his father had written to him, apprising him of his intention to come North that season. In a few days Leroy and his wife started North, but before they reached Vicksburg they were met by the intelligence that the yellow fever was spreading in the Delta, and that pestilence was breathing its bane upon the morning air and distilling its poison upon the midnight dews.

“Let us return home,” said Marie.

“It is useless,” answered Leroy. “It is nearly two days since we left home. The fever is spreading south of us with fearful rapidity. To return home is to walk into the jaws of death. It was my intention to have stopped at Vicksburg, but now I will go on as soon as I can make the connections.”

Early next morning Leroy and his wife started again on their journey. The cars were filled with terror-stricken people who were fleeing from death, when death was everywhere. They fled from the city only to meet the dreaded apparition in the country. As they journeyed on Leroy grew restless and feverish. He tried to brace himself against the infection which was creeping slowly but insidiously into his life, dulling his brain, fevering his blood, and prostrating his strength. But vain were all his efforts. He had no armor strong enough to repel the invasion of death. They stopped at a small town on the way and obtained the best medical skill and most careful nursing, but neither skill nor art availed. On the third day death claimed Leroy as a victim, and Marie wept in hopeless agony over the grave of her devoted husband, whose sad lot it was to die from home and be buried among strangers.

===Chinese===
勒罗伊待妻子儿女向来手松，夏日出游的一应准备，他不辞劳苦、不惜花费。伊奥拉过几天就要毕业了。哈里正在缅因州一所学校里读书，父亲已给他去了信，告知自己这一季北上的打算。没过几天，勒罗伊夫妇便启程北上；可还没走到维克斯堡，消息便迎面而来：黄热病正在三角洲地区蔓延，瘟疫把毒氛呵在清晨的空气上，把毒液滴在午夜的露珠里。

“咱们回家去吧。”玛丽说。

“回去也无用了，”勒罗伊答道。“我们离家快两天了。热病正以骇人的速度朝我们南边蔓延。这时候回家，就是一头走进死亡的虎口。我原打算在维克斯堡停留，如今一赶上换车的班次，我就得赶紧往前赶。”

第二天一早，勒罗伊夫妇又上路了。火车上挤满了惊惶奔逃的人——死神明明无处不在，他们却在逃躲死神。他们逃出城市，却只在乡间迎面撞上那叫人胆寒的鬼影。一路行去，勒罗伊渐渐烦躁起来，发起热来。那疫病正缓慢而阴险地潜入他的生命，叫他脑力昏沉、血液发热、气力瘫软；他强打精神去抵挡，可一切都是枉然。他没有一副足够坚固的甲胄，能击退死神的进犯。他们途中在一座小镇停下，请来最好的医术，得着最精心的看护，然而医术也罢、本领也罢，全不中用。第三天，死神把勒罗伊认作了自己的一名祭品；玛丽伏在深情丈夫的坟上，绝望地哀哀痛哭——他命该如此：死在家门外，埋在生人中间。

===Original===
But before he died he placed his will in Marie’s hands, saying: “I have left you well provided for. Kiss the children for me and bid them goodbye.”

He tried to say a parting word to Gracie, but his voice failed, and he fainted into the stillness of death. A mortal paleness overspread his countenance, on which had already gathered the shadows that never deceive. In speechless agony Marie held his hand until it released its pressure in death, and then she stood alone beside her dead, with all the bright sunshine of her life fading into the shadows of the grave. Heartbroken and full of fearful forebodings, Marie left her cherished dead in the quiet village of H﻿—﻿— and returned to her death-darkened home.

It was a lovely day in June, birds were singing their sweetest songs, flowers were breathing their fragrance on the air, when Mam Liza, sitting at her cabin-door, talking with some of the house servants, saw a carriage approaching, and wondered who was coming.

===Chinese===
可是在咽气之前，他把遗嘱放进玛丽手里，说道：“我已经安顿好了，你的后半世衣食无缺。替我亲亲孩子们，跟他们道个别。”

他还想给格雷茜留一句告别的话，声音却出不来了，随即他幽然一沉，坠入了死亡的寂静。一片死人的苍白漫上他的面容——那上头早已聚起了绝不骗人的暗影。玛丽在无言的剧痛里握着他的手，直到那只手在死亡中松开了力道；随后她孤零零守在亡人身边，生命中全部明亮的阳光，都渐渐褪成了坟茔的阴影。玛丽心碎肠断，满怀骇人的预感，把心爱的亡人留在幽静的 H—— 村，自己回了那被死亡罩得昏暗的家。

那是六月里一个明媚的日子，鸟儿唱着最甜美的歌，花儿把芬芳吐在空气里；丽扎嬷嬷正坐在小屋门口，同几个家务仆役说话，瞥见一辆马车驶近，心里纳闷来的是谁。

===Original===
“I wonder,” she said, excitedly, “whose comin’ to de house when de folks is done gone.”

But her surprise was soon changed to painful amazement, when she saw Marie, robed in black, alighting from the carriage, and holding Gracie by the hand. She caught sight of the drooping head and grief-stricken face, and rushed to her, exclaiming:﻿—

“Whar’s Marse Eugene?”

“Dead,” said Marie, falling into Mammy Liza’s arms, sobbing out, “dead! *he died* of yellow fever.”

A wild burst of sorrow came from the lips of the servants, who had drawn near.

===Chinese===
“俺真纳闷，”她兴奋地说，“家里人都走光了，这会儿是谁上宅子来呀。”

可她一见玛丽身披黑纱、牵着格雷茜的手走下马车，惊异霎时变成了揪心的骇愕。她一眼撞见那低垂的头和悲痛欲绝的脸，抢步奔上前去，惊呼道：

“尤金老爷呢？”

“没了，”玛丽说着，扑进丽扎嬷嬷怀里，泣不成声，“没了！他就是害黄热病死的呀。”

围拢过来的仆役们，口中迸出一片恸哭。

===Original===
“Where is he?” said Mam Liza, speaking like one suddenly bewildered.

“He is buried in H﻿—﻿—. I could not bring him home,” said Marie.

“My pore baby,” said Mam Liza, with broken sobs. “I’se drefful sorry. My heart’s most broke into two.” Then, controlling herself, she dismissed the servants who stood around, weeping, and led Marie to her room.

“Come, honey, lie down an’ lem’me git yer a cup ob tea.”

===Chinese===
“他在哪儿？”丽扎嬷嬷说，那口气像个猛然发了懵的人。

“他埋在 H—— 村了。我没法把他带回家来。”玛丽说。

“俺的苦命宝贝儿，”丽扎嬷嬷说，呜咽断续。“俺心里难过死了。俺这颗心差不多裂成两半了。”随后她强自镇定，打发走围在旁边垂泪的仆役，扶着玛丽进了她的屋子。

“来，宝贝儿，躺下，俺去给你弄杯茶来。”

===Original===
“Oh, no; I don’t want anything,” said Marie, wringing her hands in bitter agony.

“Oh, honey,” said Mam Liza, “yer musn’t gib up. Yer knows whar to put yer trus’. Yer can’t lean on de arm of flesh in dis tryin’ time.” Kneeling by the side of her mistress she breathed out a prayer full of tenderness, hope, and trust.

Marie grew calmer. It seemed as if that earnest, trustful prayer had breathed into her soul a feeling of resignation.

Gracie stood wonderingly by, vainly trying to comprehend the great sorrow which was overwhelming the life of her mother.

After the first great burst of sorrow was over, Marie sat down to her desk and wrote a letter to Iola, informing her of her father’s death. By the time she had finished it she grew dizzy and faint, and fell into a swoon. Mammy Liza tenderly laid her on the bed, and helped restore her to consciousness.

===Chinese===
“哦，不；我什么都不要，”玛丽说，在锥心的苦楚里绞着自己的双手。

“哦，宝贝儿，”丽扎嬷嬷说，“你可不能就这么垮了。你知道该把你的信靠搁在哪儿。在这熬炼人的时辰，你倚不上血肉的臂膀啊。”她跪在女主人身边，吐出一篇满含温柔、盼望与信靠的祷告。

玛丽平静了些。仿佛那恳切而信靠的祷告，已往她灵魂里吹进了一缕甘于顺受的安宁。

格雷茜怔怔地站在一旁，徒然地想弄明白，那正把她母亲生命压垮的巨大悲伤究竟是什么。

第一阵大恸过后，玛丽坐到书桌前，给伊奥拉写信，告知她父亲的死讯。信才写完，她就一阵头晕目眩，昏厥过去。丽扎嬷嬷轻手轻脚把她安放床上，又帮她缓过神来。

===Original===
Lorraine, having heard of his cousin’s death, came immediately to see Marie. She was too ill to have an interview with him, but he picked up the letter she had written and obtained Iola’s address.

Lorraine made a careful investigation of the case, to ascertain whether Marie’s marriage was valid. To his delight he found there was a flaw in the marriage and an informality in the manumission. He then determined to invalidate Marie’s claim, and divide the inheritance among Leroy’s white relations. In a short time strangers, distant relatives of her husband, became frequent visitors at the plantation, and made themselves offensively familiar. At length the dreadful storm burst.

Alfred Lorraine entered suit for his cousin’s estate, and for the remanding of his wife and children to slavery. In a short time he came armed with legal authority, and said to Marie:﻿—

“I have come to take possession of these premises.”

===Chinese===
洛兰听得表兄的死讯，立刻赶来看玛丽。她病得没法同他见面，他却拾起她写好的那封信，弄到了伊奥拉的地址。

洛兰把这桩案子细细查考了一番，要弄明白玛丽的婚姻究竟有没有效力。叫他喜出望外的是，他发现婚书上有一处漏洞，解除奴籍的文书里有一处不合程式。于是他打定主意，要作废玛丽的名分，把遗产分给勒罗伊的白人亲族。不多时，一些陌生人——她丈夫的远房亲戚——成了种植园的常客，放肆得叫人反感。终于，那场可怕的风暴劈头砸了下来。

阿尔弗雷德·洛兰递上状子，一面争夺表兄的产业，一面上告要把表兄的妻儿发还为奴。不多时，他便带着法律的授权找上门来，对玛丽说道：

“我是来接管这份产业的。”

===Original===
“By what authority?” she gasped, turning deathly pale. He hesitated a moment, as if his words were arrested by a sense of shame.

“By what authority?” she again demanded.

“By the authority of the law,” answered Lorraine, “which has decided that Leroy’s legal heirs are his white blood relations, and that your marriage is null and void.”

“But,” exclaimed Marie, “I have our marriage certificate. I was Leroy’s lawful wife.”

===Chinese===
“凭什么？”她倒抽一口气，面无人色。他迟疑了片刻，仿佛一层羞恶之意把他的话拦住了。

“凭什么？”她又质问了一遍。

“凭法律的授权，”洛兰答道，“法律已经裁定：勒罗伊的合法继承人是他的白人血亲，而你的婚姻无效作废。”

“可是，”玛丽喊道，“我们是有结婚证书的。我是勒罗伊的合法妻子。”

===Original===
“Your marriage certificate is not worth the paper it is written on.”

“Oh, you must be jesting, cruelly jesting. It can’t be so.”

“Yes; it is so. Judge Starkins has decided that your manumission is unlawful; your marriage a bad precedent, and inimical to the welfare of society; and that you and your children are remanded to slavery.”

Marie stood as one petrified. She seemed a statue of fear and despair. She tried to speak, reached out her hand as if she were groping in the dark, turned pale as death as if all the blood in her veins had receded to her heart, and, with one heartrending cry of bitter agony, she fell senseless to the floor. Her servants, to whom she had been so kind in her days of prosperity, bent pityingly over her, chafed her cold hands, and did what they could to restore her to consciousness. For awhile she was stricken with brain fever, and her life seemed trembling on its frailest cord.

Gracie was like one perfectly dazed. When not watching by her mother’s bedside she wandered aimlessly about the house, growing thinner day by day. A slow fever was consuming her life. Faithfully and carefully Mammy Liza watched over her, and did all she could to bring smiles to her lips and light to her fading eyes, but all in vain. Her only interest in life was to sit where she could watch her mother as she tossed to and fro in delirium, and to wonder what had brought the change in her once happy home. Finally she, too, was stricken with brain fever, which intervened as a mercy between her and the great sorrow that was overshadowing her young life. Tears would fill the servants’ eyes as they saw the dear child drifting from them like a lovely vision, too bright for earth’s dull cares and weary, wasting pain.

===Chinese===
“你那张结婚证书，连写它的那张纸都不值。”

“啊，你一定是在说笑话，是狠心的笑话。事情不可能是这样的。”

“是的，事情就是这样。斯塔金斯法官已经裁定：你的解除奴籍不合法；你的婚姻开了恶例、有妨社会的福祉；你同你的孩子们，一并发还为奴。”

玛丽站在原地，像被人点成了石头。她俨然是一尊恐惧与绝望的雕像。她想开口，伸出手去，仿佛在黑暗中摸索；接着面如死灰，仿佛全身的血都缩回了心脏；随即她发出一声撕心裂肺的惨痛哀号，昏死在地板上。仆役们——她在顺境的日子里待他们那样仁厚——怜悯地俯下身去，搓着她冰冷的双手，想方设法救她转醒。此后有一段日子，她害了脑热病，性命仿佛悬在一缕最纤细的线上，颤颤欲断。

格雷茜整个人全然懵住了。不守在母亲床边的工夫，她就在宅子里漫无目的地荡来荡去，一天天瘦下去。一场缠绵的低热正销蚀着她的生命。丽扎嬷嬷忠心而细心地看护着她，想尽办法要引她唇边生笑、让她那双日渐黯淡的眼睛重新有光，却一概徒劳。她活在世上仅剩的牵挂，就是坐在一个望得见母亲的地方，看着母亲在谵妄里翻来覆去，心里疑惑那个曾经幸福的家究竟是遭了什么变故。到末了，她也害起了脑热病——这病倒像是一桩慈悲，横在她和那笼罩她小小生命的巨恸之间。仆役们眼望着这亲爱的孩子像一道可爱的幻影，从他们身边飘然远去——她太明媚了，这尘世沉滞的烦忧与消磨人的疼痛容她不下——个个眼里都涌满了泪水。

## V The Release of Iola Leroy / 第五章 伊奥拉·勒罗伊获释

===Original===
“Well, boys,” said Robert to his trusted friends, as they gathered together at a meeting in Gundover’s woods, almost under the shadow of the Union army, “how many of you are ready to join the army and fight for your freedom.”
“All ob us.”
“The soldiers,” continued Robert, “are camped right at the edge of the town. The General has his headquarters in the heart of the town, and one of the officers told me yesterday that the President had set us all free, and that as many as wanted to join the army could come along to the camp. So I thought, boys, that I would come and tell you. Now, you can take your bag and baggage, and get out of here as soon as you choose.”
“We’ll be ready by daylight,” said Tom. “It won’t take me long to pack up,” looking down at his seedy clothes, with a laugh. “I specs ole Marse’ll be real lonesome when I’m gone. An’ won’t he be hoppin’ mad when he finds I’m a goner? I specs he’ll hate it like pizen.”
“O, well,” said Robert, “the best of friends must part. Don’t let it grieve you.”
“I’se gwine to take my wife an’ chillen,” said one of the company.

===Chinese===
“好吧，弟兄们，”罗伯特对他那伙信得过的朋友们说道。他们正聚在冈多弗家的林子里开会，几乎就在联邦军的荫庇之下。“你们当中有多少人准备好去参军，为自己的自由打一仗。”
“俺们全都去。”
“大兵们，”罗伯特接着说，“就扎营在镇边上。将军把司令部设在镇子正中心，昨天有一位军官告诉我，总统已经让我们大伙儿全都自由了，凡是想参军的，都可以跟着到营里去。所以我想，弟兄们，我得来给你们报个信儿。如今，你们只管带上行李家当，想多早离开这儿就多早离开。”
“天一亮俺们就能动身，”汤姆说。“收拾行装用不着俺多少工夫，”他低头瞧了瞧自己那身破旧的衣裳，笑了起来。“俺估摸着，俺这一走，老主人准得冷清得慌。等他发觉俺溜了，还不得气得蹦起来？俺琢磨他准得把这事儿恨得跟毒药似的。”
“咳，没什么，”罗伯特说，“再好的朋友也有分手的时候。别为这个难过吧。”
“俺要带上俺媳妇和孩子们一块儿走，”人群里有一个人说。

===Original===
“I’se got nobody but myself,” said Tom; “but dere’s a mighty putty young gal dere at Marse Tom’s. I wish I could git her away. Dey tells me dey’s been sellin’ her all ober de kentry; but dat she’s a reg’lar spitfire; dey can’t lead nor dribe her.”
“Do you think she would go with us?” said Robert.
“I think she’s jis’ dying to go. Dey say dey can’t do nuffin wid her. Marse Tom’s got his match dis time, and I’se glad ob it. I jis’ glories in her spunk.”
“How did she come there?”
“Oh, Marse bought her ob de trader to keep house for him. But ef you seed dem putty white han’s ob hern you’d never tink she kept her own house, let ’lone anybody else’s.”

===Chinese===
“俺光杆儿一条，没有旁人牵挂，”汤姆说，“不过汤姆少爷家里有个顶俊的年轻姑娘。俺真巴望能把她弄出来。俺听人说，他们把她全国各地倒着卖；可她是个地道的火爆性子，软的硬的都使她不动。”
“你觉得她肯跟我们一块儿走吗？”罗伯特说。
“俺看她巴不得走呢。他们说她简直拿她没法子。这回汤姆少爷可算碰上对手了，俺就为这个高兴。俺就是佩服她那股子胆气。”
“她是怎么落到那儿的？”
“哦，主人从贩子手里把她买下来，让她替他操持家务。可你只要瞧瞧她那双白白净净的俊手，就绝不会信她操持过自个儿的家，更别说替旁人家操持了。”

===Original===
“Do you think you can get her away?”
“I don’t know; ’cause Marse Tom keeps her mighty close. My! but she’s putty. Beautiful long hair comes way down her back; putty blue eyes, an’ jis’ ez white ez anybody’s in dis place. I’d jis’ wish you could see her yoresef. I heerd Marse Tom talkin’ ’bout her las’ night to his brudder; tellin’ him she war mighty airish, but he meant to break her in.”
An angry curse rose to the lips of Robert, but he repressed it and muttered to himself, “Graceless scamp, he ought to have his neck stretched.” Then turning to Tom, said:﻿—
“Get her, if you possibly can, but you must be mighty mum about it.”
“Trus’ me for dat,” said Tom.

===Chinese===
“你觉得你能把她弄出来吗？”
“俺说不好；汤姆少爷把她看得可严实呢。哎呀！她可真俊。一头漂亮的长发，一直垂到腰背后；一双俊俏的蓝眼睛，皮肤跟这地方随便哪个人一样白净。俺真盼着你能亲眼去瞧瞧她。昨儿夜里俺听见汤姆少爷跟他兄弟说起她；说她傲气得不得了，可他打算把她驯服喽。”
一句怒骂涌上罗伯特的嘴边，可他把它压了下去，只暗自咕哝了一句：“无法无天的恶棍，真该把他绞死才好。”随后他转向汤姆，说道：——
“只要有一点儿可能，就把她救出来，可这件事你千万得守口如瓶。”
“这事你只管放心，”汤姆说。

===Original===
Tom was very anxious to get word to the beautiful but intractable girl who was held in durance vile by her reckless and selfish master, who had tried in vain to drag her down to his own low level of sin and shame. But all Tom’s efforts were in vain. Finally he applied to the Commander of the post, who immediately gave orders for her release. The next day Tom had the satisfaction of knowing that Iola Leroy had been taken as a trembling dove from the gory vulture’s nest and given a place of security. She was taken immediately to the General’s headquarters. The General was much impressed by her modest demeanor, and surprised to see the refinement and beauty she possessed. Could it be possible that this young and beautiful girl had been a chattel, with no power to protect herself from the highest insults that lawless brutality could inflict upon innocent and defenseless womanhood? Could he ever again glory in his American citizenship, when any white man, no matter how coarse, cruel, or brutal, could buy or sell her for the basest purposes? Was it not true that the cause of a hapless people had become entangled with the lightnings of heaven, and dragged down retribution upon the land?
The field hospital was needing gentle, womanly ministrations, and Iola Leroy, released from the hands of her tormentors, was given a place as nurse; a position to which she adapted herself with a deep sense of relief. Tom was doubly gratified at the success of his endeavors, which had resulted in the rescue of the beautiful young girl and the discomfiture of his young master who, in the words of Tom, “was mad enough to bite his head off” (a rather difficult physical feat).

===Chinese===
汤姆急切地想给那位美丽却桀骜难驯的姑娘捎个信去。她被那鲁莽而自私的主人幽禁着；那主人曾枉费心机，想把她拖落到自己那罪恶与耻辱的卑污地步。然而汤姆的一切努力都归于徒劳。最后他求到了驻地指挥官那里，指挥官当即下令放人。第二天，汤姆终于称心如意地得知：伊奥拉·勒罗伊（Iola Leroy）像一只战战兢兢的鸽子，被人从血淋淋的秃鹫巢里捧了出来，安置到一个安稳的去处。她随即被带到了将军的司令部。将军为她端庄娴静的仪态深深打动，又惊异于她身上自有的那份教养与美貌。这样一位年轻貌美的姑娘，竟曾是一件动产，面对无法无天的残暴所能加诸无辜而无助的女性的最狠毒的凌辱，竟毫无护卫自身之力——这难道可能是真的吗？任何一个白人，不论多么粗鄙、残忍、蛮横，都能为了最早鄙的目的把她买进卖出——遇着这样的事，他还怎能再为自己是个美国公民而觉得无上荣光？一个惨遭不幸的民族，其事业已同上天的雷霆缠结在一起，把报应引落到了这片国土上——这难道不是真的吗？
野战医院正需要女性温柔的看护，伊奥拉·勒罗伊脱离了折磨她的人的掌心，得了一个护士的职位；她怀着深深的如释重负之感，安顿在这个岗位上。汤姆的心血没有白费，他得到了双重的快慰：既救出了那位美丽的年轻姑娘，又叫他那年轻的主人碰了一鼻子灰——用汤姆的话说，那主子“气得恨不得把自己的脑袋咬下来”（这可是一桩难度不小的身体绝技）。

===Original===
Iola, freed from her master’s clutches, applied herself readily to her appointed tasks. The beautiful, girlish face was full of tender earnestness. The fresh, young voice was strangely sympathetic, as if some great sorrow had bound her heart in loving compassion to every sufferer who needed her gentle ministrations.
Tom Anderson was a man of herculean strength and remarkable courage. But, on account of physical defects, instead of enlisting as a soldier, he was forced to remain a servant, although he felt as if every nerve in his right arm was tingling to strike a blow for freedom. He was well versed in the lay of the country, having often driven his master’s cotton to market when he was a field hand. After he became a coachman, he had become acquainted with the different roads and localities of the country. Besides, he had often accompanied his young masters on their hunting and fishing expeditions. Although he could not fight in the army, he proved an invaluable helper. When tents were to be pitched, none were more ready to help than he. When burdens were to be borne, none were more willing to bend beneath them than Thomas Anderson. When the battlefield was to be searched for the wounded and dying, no hand was more tender in its ministrations of kindness than his. As a general factotum in the army, he was ever ready and willing to serve anywhere and at any time, and to gather information from every possible source which could be of any service to the Union army. As a Pagan might worship a distant star and wish to call it his own, so he loved Iola. And he never thought he could do too much for the soldiers who had rescued her and were bringing deliverance to his race.

===Chinese===
伊奥拉摆脱了主人的魔爪，欣然投身于分派给她的各项差事。那张少女的美丽脸庞盛满了温柔的诚挚。她那清新年轻的嗓音出奇地富于同情，仿佛某种深巨的哀伤，早已用爱怜把她的心同每一个需要她温柔照护的伤患者拴在了一起。
汤姆·安德森（Tom Anderson）有着大力士般的力气和非同寻常的胆量。可是由于身体上的缺陷，他没能应征入伍当兵，只得留在军中做仆役，虽然他只觉得自己右臂上的每一根神经都在发麻发痒，恨不得为自由狠狠挥上一拳。他对这一带的地理路径烂熟于心——当年做田里的劳力时，他常赶着主人家的棉花上市集去卖；当了车夫之后，他又把乡间各条道路、各个地方都摸熟了。此外，他还常常陪着年轻的主子们出外打猎钓鱼。虽然他不能在军中作战，他却证明了自己是个极其可贵的帮手。要搭帐篷的时候，没有人比他更抢着出力；要扛重担的时候，没有人比托马斯·安德森更甘心弯下腰去承受；要在战场上搜寻伤员和垂死者的时候，没有谁的手比他的手更温柔地施与善举。作为军中的一名总杂役，他随时随地都甘愿效力，并从一切可能的来源，搜集对联邦军能有用处的一切情报。就像一个异教徒会崇拜一颗遥远的星辰、恨不得把它唤作自个儿的一样，他就是这样爱慕着伊奥拉。对于那些解救了她、并正在给他的种族带来自由的士兵们，他从不觉得自己为他们做得再多也算多。

===Original===
“What do you think of Miss Iola?” Robert asked him one day, as they were talking together.
“I jis’ think dat she’s splendid. Las’ week I had to take some of our pore boys to de hospital, an’ she war dere, lookin’ sweet an’ putty ez an angel, a nussin’ dem pore boys, an’ ez good to one ez de oder. It looks to me ez ef dey ralely lob’d her shadder. She sits by ’em so patient, an’ writes ’em sech nice letters to der frens, an’ yit she looks so heart-broke an’ pitiful, it jis’ gits to me, an’ makes me mos’ ready to cry. I’m so glad dat Marse Tom had to gib her up. He war too mean to eat good victuals.”
“He ought,” said Robert, “to be made to live on herrings’ heads and cold potatoes. It makes my blood boil just to think that he was going to have that lovely looking young girl whipped for his devilment. He ought to be ashamed to hold up his head among respectable people.”
“I tell you, Bob, de debil will neber git his own till he gits him. When I seed how he war treating her I neber rested till I got her away. He buyed her, he said, for his housekeeper; as many gals as dere war on de plantation, why didn’t he git one ob dem to keep house, an’ not dat nice lookin’ young lady? Her han’s look ez ef she neber did a day’s work in her life. One day when he com’d down to breakfas’, he chucked her under de chin, an’ tried to put his arm roun’ her waist. But she jis’ frew it off like a chunk ob fire. She looked like a snake had bit her. Her eyes fairly spit fire. Her face got red ez blood, an’ den she turned so pale I thought she war gwine to faint, but she didn’t, an’ I yered her say, ‘I’ll die fust.’ I war mad ’nough to stan’ on my head. I could hab tore’d him all to pieces wen he said he’d hab her whipped.”

===Chinese===
“你觉得伊奥拉小姐这个人怎么样？”一天，两人正说着话，罗伯特问他。
“俺就觉着她好得没法挑。上礼拜俺送咱们几个可怜的弟兄上医院，她就在那儿，模样儿甜甜的，俊得像天使一样，正看护着那些可怜的弟兄，对这个对那个一样地好。在俺瞧着，他们真真是把她疼到心坎里去了。她坐在他们身边那么耐心，还替他们给各自的亲友写那么好的信；可她自己那模样又那么心碎、那么惹人怜，俺心里头就一阵发酸，简直要叫俺掉泪。俺真是高兴，汤姆少爷不得不把她放了出来。他那种人，坏得都不配吃一口好饭食。”
“就该罚他天天只吃鲱鱼头和冷土豆过活。一想到他竟为了自己那点下作勾当，打算叫人把那位模样可人的年轻姑娘抽一顿鞭子，我的血就往头顶上涌。他真该没脸在体面人中间抬起头来。”
“俺跟你说，鲍勃，魔鬼啊，不把这号人收了去，就永远算不得收齐了他名下的账。俺一瞧见他那么作践她，就一日也不得安生，非把她弄出来不可。他说，买她是为了给他管家；庄园上姑娘家有的是，他干吗不挑一个来操持家务，偏要那么位体面的年轻小姐？她那双手，瞧着就像这辈子没干过一天活儿。有一天他下楼来吃早饭，伸手去挑她的下巴，还想把胳膊搂上她的腰。可她一下子就甩开了，就像甩开一块烧红的火炭。她那神情，像被蛇咬了一口似的。她那双眼睛简直喷得出火来。她的脸涨得红得像血，跟着又变得煞白，俺还当她要晕过去了，可她没有，俺听见她说：‘我宁死也不。’俺当时气得都能拿大顶了。听他说要叫人抽她鞭子，俺恨不得把他撕成碎片。”

===Original===
“Did he do it?”
“I don’t know. But he’s mean ’nough to do enythin’. Why, dey say she war sole seben times in six weeks, ’cause she’s so putty, but dat she war game to de las’.”
“Well, Tom,” said Robert, “getting that girl away was one of the best things you ever did in your life.”
“I think so, too. Not dat I specs enytin’ ob it. I don’t spose she would think ob an ugly chap like me; but it does me good to know dat Marse Tom ain’t got her.”

===Chinese===
“他真抽了吗？”
“俺不知道。可他那种人，啥事儿都干得出来。哎呀，听说光六个礼拜里头，就因为她生得太俊，她被倒卖了七回，可她硬是从头到尾没服过软。”
“嗯，汤姆，”罗伯特说，“把那位姑娘救出来，是你这辈子干过的最漂亮的事情之一。”
“俺也这么想。倒不是俺指望从她身上图个啥。俺琢磨，她哪会看得上俺这么个丑汉子；可一想到汤姆少爷到底没能把她攥在手心里，俺心里就痛快。”

## VII Tom Anderson’s Death / 第七章 汤姆·安德森之死

===Original===
“Where is Tom?” asked Captain Sybil; “I have not seen him for several hours.”

“He’s gone down the sound with some of the soldiers,” replied Robert. “They wanted Tom to row them.”

“I am afraid those boys will get into trouble, and the Rebs will pick them off,” responded Sybil.

“O, I hope not,” answered Robert.

“I hope not, too; but those boys are too venturesome.”

“Tom knows the lay of the land better than any of us,” said Robert. “He is the most wide-awake and gamiest man I know. I reckon when the war is over Tom will be a preacher. Did you ever hear him pray?”

===Chinese===
“汤姆在哪儿？”西比尔上尉（Captain Sybil）问道；“我有好几个钟头没见着他了。”

“他跟几个士兵顺着海湾划下去了，”罗伯特（Robert）答道，“他们要汤姆给他们摇桨。”

“我怕那几个弟兄会惹出事来，叛军会把他们一个一个撂倒的，”西比尔应道。

“哦，但愿不会，”罗伯特答道。

“我也但愿不会；可那几个弟兄胆子实在太大了。”

“这一带的地形，汤姆比咱们谁都摸得熟，”罗伯特说，“他是我认得的人里头顶警醒、顶有胆气的一个。我寻思着，等仗打完了，汤姆能当个传道人。你听过他祷告吗？”

===Original===
“No; is he good at that?”

“First-rate,” continued Robert. “It would do you good to hear him. He don’t allow any cursing and swearing when he’s around. And what he says is law and gospel with the boys. But he’s *so* good-natured; and they *can’t* get mad at him.”

“Yes, Robert, there is not a man in our regiment I would sooner trust than Tom. Last night, when he brought in that wounded scout, he couldn’t have been more tender if he had been a woman. How gratefully the poor fellow looked in Tom’s face as he laid him down so carefully and staunched the blood which had been spurting out of him. Tom seemed to know it was an artery which had been cut, and he did just the right thing to stop the bleeding. He knew there wasn’t a moment to be lost. He wasn’t going to wait for the doctor. I have often heard that colored people are ungrateful, but I don’t think Tom’s worst enemy would say that about him.”

“Captain,” said Robert, with a tone of bitterness in his voice, “what had we to be grateful for? For ages of poverty, ignorance, and slavery? I think if anybody should be grateful, it is the people who have enslaved us and lived off our labor for generations. Captain, I used to know a poor old woman who couldn’t bear to hear anyone play on the piano.”

===Chinese===
“没有；他祷告得好吗？”

“顶呱呱，”罗伯特接着说，“听他祷告一回，对你都有好处。他在跟前的时候，谁也不许骂一句脏话、赌一句恶咒。他说的话，在弟兄们听来就是律法、就是福音。可他偏偏脾气又是那么个好；弟兄们就是想生他的气，也生不起来。”

“是啊，罗伯特，咱们团里的人，要论信得过，没有谁比得上汤姆。昨儿夜里他把那个负伤的侦察兵送进来，那份轻柔体贴，就是换成个女人也不过如此。他把那可怜人小心翼翼地放躺下，止住那正一个劲儿往外涌的血时，那可怜人望着汤姆的脸，眼里满是感激。汤姆像是看得出切断的是一条动脉，处置得恰到好处，把血止住了。他晓得一刻也耽搁不得，不肯干等着医生来。我常听人说有色人种不知感恩，可我想，就连跟汤姆最过不去的仇人，也不会拿这话来说他。”

“上尉，”罗伯特开口说，嗓音里带着一股苦涩，“我们有什么可感恩的？感恩那世世代代的贫穷、愚昧和奴役吗？依我看，真该感恩的，是那些奴役了我们、世世代代靠着我们的劳力过活的人。上尉，我从前认得一个可怜的老婆子，她听不得任何人弹钢琴。”

===Original===
“Is that so? Why, I always heard that colored people were a musical race.”

“So we are; but that poor woman’s daughter was sold, and her mistress took the money to buy a piano. Her mother could never bear to hear a sound from it.”

“Poor woman!” exclaimed Captain Sybil, sympathetically; “I suppose it seemed as if the wail of her daughter was blending with the tones of the instrument. I think, Robert, there is a great deal more in the colored people than we give them credit for. Did you know Captain Sellers?”

“The officer who escaped from prison and got back to our lines?” asked Robert.

“Yes. Well, he had quite an experience in trying to escape. He came to an aged couple, who hid him in their cabin and shared their humble food with him. They gave him some cornbread, bacon, and coffee which he thought was made of scorched bran. But he said that he never ate a meal that he relished more than the one he took with them. Just before he went they knelt down and prayed with him. It seemed as if his very hair stood on his head, their prayer was so solemn. As he was going away the man took some shingles and nailed them on his shoes to throw the bloodhounds off his track. I don’t think he will ever cease to feel kindly towards colored people. I do wonder what has become of the boys? What can keep them so long?”

===Chinese===
“是吗？咦，我倒一直听人说，有色人种是个热爱音乐的种族。”

“我们确实是；可那可怜人的女儿被卖掉了，她家女主人拿那笔钱买了一架钢琴。打那以后，她做母亲的，就连那琴上的一个音也听不得。”

“可怜的人！”西比尔上尉同情地感叹道；“我猜想，在她听来，女儿的哀哭仿佛一直混在琴声里，分不出彼此。我想，罗伯特，有色人种身上蕴藏的东西，比我们肯承认的要多得多。你认得塞勒斯上尉（Captain Sellers）吗？”

“就是那位从监狱里逃出来、回到咱们防线里的军官吗？”罗伯特问。

“认得。嗯，他逃亡那一路，可真有一番经历。他逃到一对老夫妇家里，老两口把他藏进自家的小木屋，把粗茶淡饭分给他吃。他们给了他一些玉米饼、咸肉，还有咖啡——他估摸那咖啡是拿炒焦的麸子冲的。可他说，他这辈子吃过的饭，再没有一顿比跟他们一起吃的那顿更香甜。临走之前，老两口跪下来，和他一同祷告；他们的祷告那样庄严，仿佛他头上的头发都根根竖了起来。他动身的时候，那老汉拿来几块木瓦，钉在他的鞋底上，好叫猎犬闻不出他的踪迹。我看，他对有色人种的这份善意，这辈子是断断止息不了的。我真不知道那几个弟兄怎么样了？什么事把他们耽搁这么久？”

===Original===
Just as Captain Sybil and Robert were wondering at the delay of Tom and the soldiers they heard the measured tread of men who were slowly bearing a burden. They were carrying Tom Anderson to the hospital, fearfully wounded, and nigh to death. His face was distorted, and the blood was streaming from his wounds. His respiration was faint, his pulse hurried, as if life were trembling on its frailest cords.

Robert and Captain Sybil hastened at once towards the wounded man. On Robert’s face was a look of intense anguish, as he bent pityingly over his friend.

“O, this is dreadful! How did it happen?” cried Robert.

Captain Sybil, pressing anxiously forward, repeated Robert’s question.

===Chinese===
西比尔上尉和罗伯特正为汤姆和那几个士兵迟迟不归暗自纳闷，忽听得一阵沉稳齐整的脚步声——是抬着重担的人们缓缓走来。他们抬的是汤姆·安德森（Tom Anderson），正把他送往医院：伤得怕人，命在旦夕。他的脸变了形，鲜血从伤口涔涔直淌。他呼吸微弱，脉搏急促，仿佛性命正悬在最纤细的几缕弦上，颤颤欲断。

罗伯特和西比尔上尉立刻朝伤者奔去。罗伯特满怀怜惜地俯身望着自己的朋友，脸上是一片剧烈的哀恸。

“哦，这太惨了！这是怎么出的事？”罗伯特喊道。

西比尔上尉焦灼地抢上前来，把罗伯特的问话又问了一遍。

===Original===
“Captain,” said one of the young soldiers, advancing and saluting his superior officer, “we were all in the boat when it struck against a mud bank, and there was not strength enough among us to shove her back into the water. Just then the Rebels opened fire upon us. For awhile we lay down in the boat, but still they kept firing. Tom took in the whole situation, and said: ‘Someone must die to get us out of this. I mought’s well be him as any. You are soldiers and can fight. If they kill me, it is nuthin’.’ So Tom leaped out to shove the boat into the water. Just then the Rebel bullets began to rain around him. He received seven or eight of them, and I’m afraid there is no hope for him.”

“O, Tom, I wish you hadn’t gone. O, Tom! Tom!” cried Robert, in tones of agony.

A gleam of grateful recognition passed over the drawn features of Tom, as the wail of his friend fell on his ear. He attempted to speak, but the words died upon his lips, and he became unconscious.

“Well,” said Captain Sybil, “put him in one of the best wards. Give him into Miss Leroy’s care. If good nursing can win him back to life, he shall not want for any care or pains that she can bestow. Send immediately for Dr. Gresham.”

Robert followed his friend into the hospital, tenderly and carefully helped to lay him down, and remained awhile, gazing in silent grief upon the sufferer. Then he turned to go, leaving him in the hands of Iola, but hoping against hope that his wounds would not be fatal.

===Chinese===
“上尉，”一名年轻士兵上前一步，向长官行了军礼，“我们全都在船上，船一头撞上了泥滩，我们几个人的力气加在一处，也不够把它推回水里去。就在那时，叛军朝我们开了火。有一阵子我们趴在船里，可他们还是一个劲儿地打。汤姆把整个局面看在眼里，说：‘总得有一个人死，我们才出得去。死谁不是死，我去也一样。你们是当兵的，还能打仗。他们就是打死俺，也不算个啥。’于是汤姆跳出去，把船往水里推。就在那时，叛军的子弹雨点般落在他四周。他中了七八颗，我怕是没有指望了。”

“哦，汤姆，你要是没去就好了。哦，汤姆！汤姆！”罗伯特喊道，声调里满是绞痛。

朋友的哀声一落进汤姆的耳朵，他那张扭曲的脸上便掠过一丝感激的认出了的神情。他想说话，可话到了唇边就断了，人也昏死过去。

“这样吧，”西比尔上尉说，“把他安置到最好的病房里去。交给勒罗伊小姐（Miss Leroy）照护。只要好好的看护能把他从死路上拉回来，她拿得出的看护和心力，一样也不会短了他的。马上派人去请格雷沙姆医生（Dr. Gresham）。”

罗伯特跟着朋友进了医院，温柔而小心地帮着把他安放躺下，又满怀无声的哀伤，凝望了受苦的人一阵，才转身离去，把他交到伊奥拉（Iola）手里，心里明知无望，却仍存着万一之想，盼他的伤不至于致命。

===Original===
With tender devotion Iola watched her faithful friend. He recognized her when restored to consciousness, and her presence was as balm to his wounds. He smiled faintly, took her hand in his, stroked it tenderly, looked wistfully into her face, and said, “Miss Iola, I ain’t long fer dis! I’se ’most home!”

“Oh, no,” said Iola, “I hope that you will soon get over this trouble, and live many long and happy days.”

“No, Miss Iola, it’s all ober wid me. I’se gwine to glory; gwine to glory; gwine to ring dem charmin’ bells. Tell all de boys to meet me in heben; dat dey mus’ ’list in de hebenly war.”

“O, Mr. Tom,” said Iola, tenderly, “do not talk of leaving me. You are the best friend I have had since I was torn from my mother. I should be so lonely without you.”

“Dere’s a frien’ dat sticks closer dan a brudder. He will be wid yer in de sixt’ trial, an’ in de sebbent’ he’ll not fo’sake yer.”

===Chinese===
伊奥拉怀着深挚的眷爱，守着她这位忠心的朋友。他苏醒过来时认出了她；她在跟前，便如香膏敷在他的伤口上。他淡淡一笑，把她的手握在自己手里，轻轻地摩挲着，眼巴巴地望进她的脸，说道：“伊奥拉小姐，俺在这世上待不长啦！俺这就快到家啦！”

“哦，不会的，”伊奥拉说，“我盼着你快快闯过这一关，往后长长久久，过许多快活的日子。”

“不，伊奥拉小姐，俺这一辈子算是熬到头啦。俺要上荣耀里去了；上荣耀里去了；要去敲响那好听得醉人的钟儿啦。告诉弟兄们，都到天堂来见俺；叫他们都报名投军，投到天上的战争里去。”

“哦，汤姆先生，”伊奥拉温柔地说，“别说要离开我的话。自从我被人从母亲身边夺走，你是我得到的最好的朋友。没有你，我会多么孤单啊。”

“有这么一位朋友，比亲弟兄还要亲。你遭第六回患难，他与你同在；到第七回，他也不丢弃你。”

===Original===
“Yes,” answered Iola, “I know that. He is all our dependence. But I can’t help grieving when I see you suffering so. But, dear friend, be quiet, and try to go to sleep.”

“I’ll do enythin’ fer yer, Miss Iola.”

Tom closed his eyes and lay quiet. Tenderly and anxiously Iola watched over him as the hours waned away. The doctor came, shook his head gravely, and, turning to Iola, said, “There is no hope, but do what you can to alleviate his sufferings.”

As Iola gazed upon the kind but homely features of Tom, she saw his eyes open and an unexpressed desire upon his face.

Tenderly and sadly bending over him, with tears in her dark, luminous eyes, she said, “Is there anything I can do for you?”

“Yes,” said Tom, with laboring breath; “let me hole yore han’, an’ sing ‘Ober Jordan inter glory’ an’ ‘We’ll anchor bye and bye.’ ”

===Chinese===
“是的，”伊奥拉答道，“这我知道。他正是我们全然的倚靠。可看见你受这么大的罪，我还是忍不住难过。不过，亲爱的朋友，安静些，试着睡一觉吧。”

“为了您，俺啥都肯干，伊奥拉小姐。”

汤姆合上眼睛，静静地躺着。时辰一点点过去，伊奥拉又温柔又焦心地守着他。医生来了，沉沉地摇了摇头，转身对伊奥拉说：“没有指望了，可你能减轻他多少痛苦，就尽量减轻吧。”

伊奥拉凝望着汤姆那张和善而平常的脸，看见他睁开了眼睛，脸上浮着一层没能说出口的心愿。

她温柔而哀伤地俯下身去，乌黑明亮的眼睛里噙着泪水，说道：“有什么我能为你做的吗？”

“好的，”汤姆喘着大气说，“让俺握着你的手，你给俺唱‘过了约旦河，进荣耀里去’和‘我们终要抛锚停泊’。”

===Original===
Iola laid her hand gently in the rough palm of the dying man, and, with a tremulous voice, sang the parting hymns.

Tenderly she wiped the death damps from his dusky brow, and imprinted upon it a farewell kiss. Gratitude and affection lit up the dying eye, which seemed to be gazing into the eternities. Just then Robert entered the room, and, seating himself quietly by Tom’s bedside, read the death signs in his face.

“Goodbye, Robert,” said Tom, “meet me in de kingdom.” Suddenly a look of recognition and rapture lit up his face, and he murmured, “Angels, bright angels, all’s well, all’s well!”

Slowly his hand released its pressure, a peaceful calm overspread his countenance, and without a sigh or murmur Thomas Anderson, Iola’s faithful and devoted friend, passed away, leaving the world so much poorer for her than it was before. Just then Dr. Gresham, the hospital physician, came to the bedside, felt for the pulse which would never throb again, and sat down in silence by the cot.

===Chinese===
伊奥拉把手轻轻放进垂死者粗糙的掌心，颤着嗓音，唱起那两首送别的圣歌。

她温柔地拭去他黝黑额头上的死亡冷汗，在上面印下诀别的一吻。感激与眷爱点亮了那双垂死的眼睛，那眼睛仿佛正凝望着永恒的深处。正在这时，罗伯特走进屋来，悄悄在汤姆床边坐下，从他脸上读出了死亡的征兆。

“再见了，罗伯特，”汤姆说，“咱们天国里再会。”忽然，一种认出了什么的狂喜照亮了他的脸，他喃喃地说：“天使，明亮的天使，都好了，都好了！”

他的手渐渐松开了，一片安详的平静漫上他的面容；托马斯·安德森（Thomas Anderson）——伊奥拉忠心而深爱的朋友——没有一声叹息，没有一声呻吟，就这样去了；对伊奥拉来说，这人世间从此比先前荒寒了许多。就在这时，医院的主治医官格雷沙姆医生来到床前，探了探那再也搏动不起来的脉搏，然后在床榻边默默坐了下来。

===Original===
“What do you think, Doctor,” said Iola, “has he fainted?”

“No,” said the doctor, “poor fellow! he is dead.”

Iola bowed her head in silent sorrow, and then relieved the anguish of her heart by a flood of tears. Robert rose, and sorrowfully left the room.

Iola, with tearful eyes and aching heart, clasped the cold hands over the still breast, closed the waxen lid over the eye which had once beamed with kindness or flashed with courage, and then went back, after the burial, to her daily round of duties, feeling the sad missing of something from her life.

===Chinese===
“医生，您看，”伊奥拉说，“他是晕过去了吗？”

“不，”医生说，“可怜的人！他死了。”

伊奥拉默默垂首，无声地哀伤，随后任泪水滔滔涌出，纾解心头的绞痛。罗伯特站起身，满怀哀恸地走出屋去。

伊奥拉噙着泪眼、忍着心中的酸痛，把那双冰冷的手交叠在静寂的胸前，为那只曾经闪着和善的光、或燃过勇毅之火的眼睛合上蜡白的眼睑；下葬之后，她便回到自己日复一日的职分中去，只觉得生命里从此缺了一样东西，怅然若失。

## III Uncle Daniel’s Story / 第三章 丹尼尔大叔的故事

===Original===
The Union had snapped asunder because it lacked the cohesion of justice, and the Nation was destined to pass through the crucible of disaster and defeat, till she was ready to clasp hands with the negro and march abreast with him to freedom and victory.

The Union army was encamping a few miles from C﻿—﻿—, in North Carolina. Robert, being well posted on the condition of affairs, had stealthily contrived to call a meeting in Uncle Daniel’s cabin. Uncle Daniel’s wife had gone to bed as a sick sister, and they held a prayer-meeting by her bedside. It was a little risky, but as Mr. Thurston did not encourage the visits of the patrollers, and heartily detested having them prying into his cabins, there was not much danger of molestation.

===Chinese===
联邦之所以断裂崩解，是因为它缺少正义的凝聚；而这个国家注定要穿过灾难与挫败的熔炉，直到她甘愿与黑人携手相握、并肩同行，一同迈向自由与胜利。

联邦军正在北卡罗来纳 C—— 城外几英里的地方扎营。罗伯特（Robert）消息灵通，对时局了如指掌，便悄悄张罗着在丹尼尔大叔（Uncle Daniel）的小木屋里召集了一次聚会。丹尼尔大叔的老伴已经上床躺下，权当是一位生病的姊妹，众人便在她床边举行祈祷会。这多少有些冒险，好在瑟斯顿先生（Mr. Thurston）并不欢迎巡逻队上门，又打心眼里厌恶他们到自己那些小木屋里窥探，所以遭人搅扰的危险并不大。

===Original===
“Well, Uncle Daniel, we want to hear your story, and see if you have made up your mind to go with us,” said Robert, after he had been seated a few minutes in Uncle Daniel’s cabin.

“No, chillen, I’ve no objection to finishin’ my story, but I ain’t made up my mind to leave the place till Marse Robert gits back.”

“You were telling us about Marse Robert’s mother. How did you get along after she died?”

“Arter she war gone, ole Marster’s folks come to look arter things. But eberything war lef’ to Marse Robert, an’ he wouldn’t do widout me. Dat chile war allers at my heels. I couldn’t stir widout him, an’ when he missed me, he’d fret an’ cry so I had ter stay wid him; an’ wen he went to school, I had ter carry him in de mornin’ and bring him home in de ebenin’. An’ I learned him to hunt squirrels, an’ rabbits, an’ ketch fish, an’ set traps for birds. I beliebs he lob’d me better dan any ob his kin’. An’ he showed me how to read.”

===Chinese===
“丹尼尔大叔，我们想听听您的故事，也想看看您打定了主意没有，肯不肯跟我们一道走，”罗伯特在丹尼尔大叔的小木屋里坐了几分钟之后说道。

“还没有呢，孩子们。俺的故事，讲完它没啥不可以；只是在罗伯特少爷（Marse Robert）回来之前，俺还没拿定主意离开这个地方。”

“您先前正讲到罗伯特少爷的母亲。她过世以后，您是怎么过来的？”

“她老人家去了以后，老主人家那边的亲眷过来照管家事。可家业全都留给了罗伯特少爷，而他怎么也离不得俺。那孩子整日价跟在俺脚后跟，俺走哪儿他跟哪儿，一时找不着俺，就急得哭哭啼啼，俺只得守着他；他上学以后，俺早上得送他去，傍晚再把他接回家来。俺还教会了他打松鼠、撵兔子、抓鱼、给鸟雀下套子。俺信，他疼俺，胜过疼他家哪一个亲人。他还教俺认字呢。”

===Original===
“Well,” said Tom, “ef he lob’d you so much, why didn’t he set you free?”

“Marse Robert tole me, ef he died fust he war gwine ter leave me free﻿—dat I should neber sarve anyone else.”

“Oh, sho!” said Tom, “promises, like pie crusts, is made to be broken. I don’t trust none ob dem. I’se been yere dese fifteen years, an’ I’se neber foun’ any troof in dem. An’ I’se gwine wid dem North men soon’s I gits a chance. An’ ef you knowed what’s good fer you, you’d go, too.”

“No, Tom; I can’t go. When Marster Robert went to de front, he called me to him an’ said: ‘Uncle Daniel,’ an’ he was drefful pale when he said it, ‘I are gwine to de war, an’ I want yer to take keer of my wife an’ chillen, jis’ like yer used to take keer of me wen yer called me your little boy.’ Well, dat jis’ got to me, an’ I couldn’t help cryin’, to save my life.”

===Chinese===
“这么说，”汤姆（Tom）道，“他既然这么疼你，咋不还你个自由身呢？”

“罗伯特少爷跟俺说过，他要是先走一步，就放俺自由——叫俺这辈子再也不用伺候第二家的人。”

“哦，去他的吧！”汤姆说，“诺言就跟馅饼皮一样，做出来就是等着撕破的。他们的话，俺一句也不信。俺在这儿待了十五个年头，就没见他们哪句话当过真。一有机会，俺就跟着北边来的人走。你要是识相，晓得啥对自己好，你也走吧。”

“不成，汤姆；俺不能走。罗伯特少爷开上前线的时候，把俺叫到跟前说：‘丹尼尔大叔，’他说这话的时候，脸色白得吓人，‘我要去打仗了，我要你照看我的老婆孩子，就像从前你叫我小家伙、把我照看大那样。’唉，这话一下子戳进俺心窝里，俺怎么忍也忍不住，眼泪直往下掉。”

===Original===
“I specs,” said Tom, “your tear bags must lie mighty close to your eyes. I wouldn’t cry ef dem Yankees would make ebery one ob dem go to de front, an’ stay dere foreber. Dey’d only be gittin’ back what dey’s been a doin’ to us.”

“Marster Robert war nebber bad to me. An’ I beliebs in stannin’ by dem dat stans by you. Arter Miss Anna died, I had great ’sponsibilities on my shoulders; but I war orful lonesome, an’ thought I’d like to git a wife. But dere warn’t a gal on de plantation, an’ nowhere’s roun’, dat filled de bill. So I jis’ waited, an’ ’tended to Marse Robert till he war ole ’nough to go to college. Wen he went, he allers ’membered me in de letters he used to write his grandma. Wen he war gone, I war lonesomer dan eber. But, one day, I jis’ seed de gal dat took de rag off de bush. Gundover had jis’ brought her from de upcountry. She war putty as a picture!” he exclaimed, looking fondly at his wife, who still bore traces of great beauty. “She had putty hair, putty eyes, putty mouth. She war putty all over; an’ she know’d how to put on style.”

===Chinese===
“俺瞧啊，”汤姆说，“你那泪水袋子，准是紧贴着眼窝长的。要是那些北佬能叫他们一个个都上前线去，永远待在那儿别回来，俺才不会哭呢。他们不过是把自个儿对俺们干下的事，原样收回去罢了。”

“罗伯特少爷待俺从来没有坏过。俺信一条理：谁护着俺，俺就护着谁。安娜小姐（Miss Anna）过世以后，俺肩膀上压上了天大的担子；可俺孤零零的，冷清得很，就想娶个媳妇。可是庄园上，连同四下里一带，愣是挑不出一个合意的姑娘。俺就这么等着，一心照看罗伯特少爷，直等到他长到上大学的年纪。他走了以后，往常给奶奶写信，信里回回都惦记着捎上俺几句。他不在跟前，俺比先前更冷清。可是有一天，俺偏偏就瞧见了那个把彩旗从树梢上摘下来的姑娘。冈多弗（Gundover）家刚把她从上边乡间带回来。她俊得就像画上画下来的一样！”他感叹道，疼爱地望着自己的老伴——她如今眉目间仍留着当年美貌的痕迹。“她头发俊，眼睛俊，嘴也俊，浑身上下没一处不俊；她还懂得怎么把自己打扮出个款款样儿来。”

===Original===
“O, Daniel,” said Aunt Katie, half chidingly, “how you do talk.”

“Why, it’s true. I ’member when you war de puttiest gal in dese diggins; when nobody could top your cotton.”

“I don’t,” said Aunt Katie.

“Well, I do. Now, let me go on wid my story. De fust time I seed her, I sez to myself, ‘Dat’s de gal for me, an’ I means to hab her ef I kin git her.’ So I scraped ‘quaintance wid her, and axed her ef she would hab me ef our marsters would let us. I warn’t ’fraid ’bout Marse Robert, but I warn’t quite shore ’bout Gundover. So when Marse Robert com’d home, I axed him, an’ he larf’d an’ said, ‘All right,’ an’ dat he would speak to ole Gundover ’bout it. He didn’t relish it bery much, but he didn’t like to ’fuse Marse Robert. He wouldn’t sell her, for she tended his dairy, an’ war mighty handy ’bout de house. He said, I mought marry her an’ come to see her wheneber Marse Robert would gib me a pass. I wanted him to sell her, but he wouldn’t hear to it, so I had to put up wid what I could git. Marse Robert war mighty good to me, but ole Gundover’s wife war de meanest woman dat I eber did see. She used to go out on de plantation an’ boss things like a man. Arter I war married, I had a baby. It war de dearest, cutest little thing you eber did see; but, pore thing, it got sick and died. It died ’bout three o’clock; and in de mornin’, Katie, habbin her cows to milk, lef her dead baby in de cabin. When she com’d back from milkin’ her thirty cows, an’ went to look for her pore little baby, someone had been to her cabin an’ took’d de pore chile away an’ put it in de groun’. Pore Katie, she didn’t eben hab a chance to kiss her baby ’fore it war buried. Ole Gundover’s wife has been dead thirty years, an’ she didn’t die a day too soon. An’ my little baby has gone to glory, an’ is wingin’ wid the angels an’ a lookin’ out for us. One ob de las’ things ole Gundover’s wife did ’fore she died war to order a woman whipped ’cause she com’d to de field a little late when her husband war sick, an’ she had stopped to tend him. Dat mornin’ she war taken sick wid de fever, an’ in a few days she war gone out like de snuff ob a candle. She lef’ several sons, an’ I specs she would almos’ turn ober in her grave ef she know’d she had ten culled granchillen somewhar down in de lower kentry.”

===Chinese===
“哦，丹尼尔，”凯蒂大婶（Aunt Katie）半嗔怪地说，“瞧你说的。”

“这可是真的呀。俺还记得，那时候你是这一带顶俊的姑娘，论拾棉花，谁也赛不过你。”

“我可记不得喽。”凯蒂大婶说。

“俺记得。好了，让俺接着往下讲。头一回见着她，俺心里就念叨：‘这姑娘俺要定了，只要能娶到她，俺就一定把她娶到家。’俺就这样跟她搭上了相识，问她肯不肯嫁俺——只要俺们两家的主人肯放行。罗伯特少爷这边俺不担心，可冈多弗那边，俺心里就有些没底。赶上罗伯特少爷回家，俺便求了他，他哈哈一笑，说：‘好啊，’又说他去跟老冈多弗提这件事。老冈多弗不大乐意，可又不好意思驳回罗伯特少爷。他不肯把她卖出来，因为她管着他家的奶牛棚，家里家外又实在是一把难得的好手。他说，俺可以娶她，往后只要罗伯特少爷肯发给俺通行证，俺就可以来看她。俺巴不得他把人卖出来，可他死活不松口，俺也只好能得着啥就安于啥。罗伯特少爷待俺是天大的好，可老冈多弗的婆娘，是俺这辈子见过的顶歹毒的女人。她常下到庄园里去，像条男人汉似地一手把持、发号施令。俺成了亲以后，得了一个娃娃。那真是你这辈子没见过的顶招人疼、顶逗人爱的小东西；可是苦命的小家伙，他生了病，夭折了。孩子是大约三点钟断的气；那天一早，凯蒂要挤牛奶，就把死了的娃娃留在小木屋里。等她挤完那三十头牛的奶回来，进屋去看她苦命的小宝贝，却有人进过她的屋子，把那可怜的孩子抱走了，埋进了土里。苦命的凯蒂，连亲一亲自己娃娃的机会都没捞着，孩子就下了葬。老冈多弗的婆娘死了有三十年了，她就是死得再早一天，也不嫌早。俺的小娃娃已经去了荣耀里，正张开翅膀跟天使们一道翱翔，守望着俺们。老冈多弗的婆娘临死前干的最后几档子事里，就有一件是叫人鞭打一个女人——只因她男人病着，她停下来服侍了一会儿，下地晚了一些。就在那天早晨，她自家染上了热病，没几天，就像掐灭一支蜡烛似的，一下子就没了。她留下好几个儿子；俺琢磨着，她要是知道自个儿在下边这低地乡间的什么地方，有着十个有色的孙儿孙女，怕是躺在坟里也要气得翻个身。”

===Original===
“Isn’t it funny,” said Robert, “how these white folks look down on colored people, an’ then mix up with them?”

“Marster war away when Miss ’Liza treated my Katie so mean, an’ when I tole him ’bout it, he war tearin’ mad, an’ went ober an’ saw ole Gundover, an’ foun’ out he war hard up for money, an’ he bought Katie and brought her home to lib wid me, and we’s been a libin in clover eber sence. Marster Robert has been mighty good to me. He stood by me in my troubles, an’ now his trouble’s come, I’m a gwine to stan’ by him. I used to think Gundover’s wife war jealous ob my Katie. She war so much puttier. Gundover’s wife couldn’t tech my Katie wid a ten foot pole.”

“But, Aunt Katie, you have had your trials,” said Robert, now that Daniel had finished his story; “don’t you feel bitter towards these people who are fighting to keep you in slavery?”

Aunt Katie turned her face towards the speaker. It was a thoughtful, intelligent face, saintly and calm. A face which expressed the idea of a soul which had been fearfully tempest tossed, but had passed through suffering into peace. Very touching was the look of resignation and hope which overspread her features as she replied, with the simple childlike faith which she had learned in the darkest hour, “The Lord says, we must forgive.” And with her that thought, as coming from the lips of Divine Love, was enough to settle the whole question of forgiveness of injuries and love to enemies.

===Chinese===
“说来也怪，”罗伯特说，“这些白人怎么一边瞧不起有色人种，一边又偏要跟他们混到一块儿去？”

“丽扎小姐（Miss ’Liza）那样作践俺家凯蒂的时候，罗伯特少爷正出远门；等俺把这事告诉他，他气得火冒三丈，亲自过去见了老冈多弗，打听到他手头正紧着等钱用，就把凯蒂买了下来，带回家来跟俺一起过活。打那以后，俺们俩就一直掉在福窝里过日子。罗伯特少爷待俺是天大的好。俺遭难的时候他护着俺，如今他遭了难，俺也要去护着他。俺从前就想，冈多弗家的婆娘是妒忌俺家凯蒂。凯蒂比她俊出太多了。冈多弗家的婆娘啊，就是拿十英尺的长杆子，也够不着俺家凯蒂的边儿。”

“可是，凯蒂大婶，您也受过您那份苦楚，”见丹尼尔已把故事讲完，罗伯特说道，“对那些正打着仗、要把您死死按在奴隶制里的人，您心里就不怀一点儿怨恨吗？”

凯蒂大婶把脸转向说话的人。那是一张若有所思、透着聪慧的脸，圣洁而安详。这张脸让人想到一个灵魂——它曾被惊涛骇浪可怕地抛来掷去，如今已穿过苦难，进入了平安。她作答时，顺服与盼望的神情漫过她的眉目，动人极了——那是她在最黑暗的时辰里学来的、孩童般单纯的信心。“主说，我们必须饶恕。”而在她，这一个念头既然是出自神圣之爱的唇间，便足以了结全部的疑难——怎样饶恕亏负，怎样去爱仇敌。

===Original===
“Well,” said Thomas Anderson, turning to Uncle Daniel, “we can’t count on yer to go wid us?”

“Boys,” said Uncle Daniel, and there was grief in his voice, “I’se mighty glad you hab a chance for your freedom; but, ez I tole yer, I promised Marse Robert I would stay, an’ I mus’ be as good as my word. Don’t you youngsters stay for an ole stager like me. I’m ole an’ mos’ worn out. Freedom wouldn’t do much for me, but I want you all to be as free as the birds; so, you chillen, take your freedom when you kin get it.”

“But, Uncle Dan’el, you won’t say nothin’ ’bout our going, will you?” said the youngest of the company.

===Chinese===
“这么说，”托马斯·安德森（Thomas Anderson）转过脸对丹尼尔大叔说，“俺是指望不上你跟俺们一道走喽？”

“孩子们，”丹尼尔大叔说，声音里含着悲伤，“你们能有机会得到自由，俺打心眼里高兴；可是俺跟你们说过，俺答应过罗伯特少爷要留下，俺说话就得作数。你们这些后生，可别为了俺这么个老家伙耽搁了自己。俺老了，身子骨也快熬干了。自由对俺没多大用处了，可俺盼着你们个个都像鸟儿一样自由；所以孩子们，能拿到自由的时候，就赶紧拿到手。”

“可是，丹尼尔大叔，俺们走的事儿，您不会往外说去吧？”人堆里最年轻的一个问道。

===Original===
Uncle Daniel slowly arose. There was a mournful flash in his eye, a tremor of emotion in his voice, as he said, “Look yere, boys, de boy dat axed dat question war a newcomer on dis plantation, but some ob you’s bin here all ob your lives; did you eber know ob Uncle Dan’el gittin’ any ob you inter trouble?”

“No, no,” exclaimed a chorus of voices, “but many’s de time you’ve held off de blows wen de oberseer got too mean, an’ cruelized us too much, wen Marse Robert war away. An’ wen he got back, you made him settle de oberseer’s hash.”

“Well, boys,” said Uncle Daniel, with an air of mournful dignity, “I’se de same Uncle Dan’el I eber war. Ef any ob you wants to go, I habben’t a word to say agin it. I specs dem Yankees be all right, but I knows Marse Robert, an’ I don’t know dem, an’ I ain’t a gwine ter throw away dirty water ’til I gits clean.”

===Chinese===
丹尼尔大叔缓缓站起身来。他眼里闪过一道悲凉的光，声音里带着一阵激动的颤音，说道：“你们听着，孩子们，问这话的娃是新到这庄园上来的；可你们有些人，在这儿生、在这儿长，待了一辈子；你们啥时候听说过，丹尼尔大叔把你们哪一个拖进过麻烦里去？”

“没有，没有，”众人齐声喊道，“可多少回监工太黑心、把俺们作践得太狠，都是你替俺们挡下那些鞭子——那都是罗伯特少爷不在家的时候。等他回来，你又叫他收拾了那监工。”

“好了，孩子们，”丹尼尔大叔说，带着一种哀伤的尊严，“俺还是从前那个丹尼尔大叔。你们谁想走，俺一句拦人的话也没有。俺寻思那些北佬兴许都是好人，可俺认得罗伯特少爷，不认得他们；没打来干净水之前，俺可不急着把脏水泼出去。”

===Original===
“Well, Uncle Ben,” said Robert, addressing a stalwart man whose towering form and darkly flashing eye told that slavery had failed to put the crouch in his shoulders or general abjectness into his demeanor, “you will go with us, for sure, won’t you?”

“Yes,” spoke up Tom Anderson, “ ’cause de trader’s done took your wife, an’ got her for his’n now.”

As Ben Tunnel looked at the speaker, a spasm of agony and anger darkened his face and distorted his features, as if the blood of some strong race were stirring with sudden vigor through his veins. He clutched his hands together, as if he were struggling with an invisible foe, and for a moment he remained silent. Then suddenly raising his head, he exclaimed, “Boys, there’s not one of you loves freedom more than I do, but﻿—”

===Chinese===
“那么，本大叔（Uncle Ben），”罗伯特说着，转向一个魁伟的汉子；那人身躯高耸，黑亮的眼睛灼灼闪光，一望便知奴隶制没能把弯腰俯首压进他的肩膀，也没能把一副卑躬屈膝的奴相塞进他的举止。“你准会跟我们一道走的，对不对？”

“是啊，”汤姆·安德森（Tom Anderson）提高嗓门搭腔道，“因为贩子已经把你老婆夺走了，如今她成了他的人啦。”

本·滕纳尔（Ben Tunnel）望着说话的人，一阵痛苦与愤怒的痉挛使他脸色发暗、五官扭曲，仿佛某个强健种族的血液，正突然充满力量地在他的血管里奔涌。他两手紧紧攥在一起，像是在同看不见的仇敌搏斗，一时间默然无语。随后他猛地抬起头来，喊道：“孩子们，你们当中没有谁比我更爱自由，可是——”

===Original===
“But what?” said Tom. “Do you think white folks is your bes’ friends?”

“I’ll think so when I lose my senses.”

“Well, now, I don’t belieb you’re ’fraid, not de way I yeard you talkin’ to de oberseer wen he war threatnin’ to hit your mudder. He saw you meant business, an’ he let her alone. But, what’s to hinder you from gwine wid us?”

“My mother,” he replied, in a low, firm voice. “That is the only thing that keeps me from going. If it had not been for her, I would have gone long ago. She’s all I’ve got, an’ I’m all she’s got.”

===Chinese===
“可是什么？”汤姆说。“你当白人是你们顶好的朋友吗？”

“等我神志不清了，才会那么想。”

“嗯，这俺可不信你会怕——俺可是亲耳听过你冲着监工讲话的那股子硬气，那回他扬言要动手打你娘。他瞧出你是来真的，就放过了她。可到底是啥绊住了你，不叫你跟俺们走呢？”

“我的母亲，”他答道，声音低沉而坚定。“只有这一件事拴着我，不叫我走。若不是为了她，我早就走了。她是我仅有的一切，我也是她仅有的一切。”

===Original===
It was touching to see the sorrow on the strong face, to detect the pathos and indignation in his voice, as he said, “I used to love Mirandy as I love my life. I thought the sun rose and set in her. I never saw a handsomer woman than she was. But she fooled me all over the face and eyes, and took up with that hellhound of a trader, Lukens; an’ he gave her a chance to live easy, to wear fine clothes, an’ be waited on like a lady. I thought at first I would go crazy, but my poor mammy did all she could to comfort me. She would tell me there were as good fish in the sea as were ever caught out of it. Many a time I’ve laid my poor head on her lap, when it seemed as if my brain was on fire and my heart was almost ready to burst. But in course of time I got over the worst of it; an’ Mirandy is the first an’ last woman that ever fooled me. But that dear old mammy of mine, I mean to stick by her as long as there is a piece of her. I can’t go over to the army an’ leave her behind, for if I did, an’ anything should happen, I would never forgive myself.”

“But couldn’t you take her with you,” said Robert, “the soldiers said we could bring our women.”

“It isn’t that. The Union army is several miles from here, an’ my poor mammy is so skeery that, if I were trying to get her away and any of them Secesh would overtake us, an’ begin to question us, she would get skeered almost to death, an’ break down an’ begin to cry, an’ then the fat would be in the fire. So, while I love freedom more than a child loves its mother’s milk, I’ve made up my mind to stay on the plantation. I wish, from the bottom of my heart, I could go. But I can’t take her along with me, an’ I don’t want to be free and leave her behind in slavery. I was only five years old when my master and, as I believe, father, sold us both here to this lower country, an’ we’ve been here ever since. It’s no use talking, I won’t leave her to be run over by everybody.”

===Chinese===
看着他说话时那坚毅面孔上笼罩的哀恸，听出他声音里的悲怆与愤懑，着实令人动容。“我曾经爱米兰迪（Mirandy），爱得像爱自己的性命。我原以为，太阳就是打她那儿升起，又在她那儿落下的。我从来没见过比她更好看的女人。可她把我骗了个十足十、明明白白，跟了那条地狱恶狗般的贩子卢肯斯（Lukens）；他给了她一个机会：过安逸日子，穿漂亮衣裳，像位太太似地有人伺候。起头我想我真要发疯了，可我那苦命的娘想方设法地宽慰我。她总对我说，海里的好鱼多着呢，先前捞上来的，算不得天底下独一份。多少回，我那颗可怜的脑袋枕在她的膝上，只觉得脑浆像着了火，心口几乎要炸开。可是日子久了，最熬人的那一阵总算过去了；米兰迪是头一个、也是末一个骗过我的女人。至于我那位亲爱的老娘，只要她还剩一口气在，我就要守在她身边。我不能丢下她投军去；我要是走了，万一出点什么事，我这辈子都不会原谅自己。”

“可你不能把她也带上吗？”罗伯特说，“当兵的说了，咱们可以把家眷带去。”

“不是带不带的事。联邦军离这儿还有好几英里，我那苦命的娘胆子又小，我要是带着她逃，让哪个分裂派的人追上了，盘问起来，她会吓得魂儿都要没了，撑不住劲儿，哭出声来，那可就要炸锅了。我爱自由，胜过娃娃恋着娘的奶，可我已经拿定主意，就留在庄园上。我能走的话，是打心眼里想走。可我没法把她带在身边，我也不想自己做了自由人，却把她撂在奴隶制里。我才五岁那年，我的主人——我相信，那也就是我的生身父亲——就把我们娘儿俩卖到这下边的低地来，打那以后我们就一直待在这儿。说啥都没用，我不能把她撂下，任人踩踏。”

===Original===
A few evenings after this interview, the Union soldiers entered the town of C﻿—﻿—, and established their headquarters near the home of Thomas Anderson.

Out of the little company, almost everyone deserted to the Union army, leaving Uncle Daniel faithful to his trust, and Ben Tunnel hushing his heart’s deep aspirations for freedom in a passionate devotion to his timid and affectionate mother.

===Chinese===
这次聚谈之后没几个晚上，联邦军的士兵便开进了 C—— 城，把司令部设在了托马斯·安德森家的附近。

那一小群人里，几乎个个都投奔联邦军去了；只剩丹尼尔大叔依旧信守他的托付，只剩本·滕纳尔把心底对自由的深切渴愿悄悄按捺下去，把一腔炽热的爱，全扑在他那胆小而慈爱的母亲身上。

## XXIX Visitors from the South / 第二十九章 南方来客

===Original===
“Mamma is not well,” said Iola to Robert. “I spoke to her about sending for a doctor, but she objected and I did not insist.”

“I will ask Dr. Latimer, whom I met at the Concordia, to step in. He is a splendid young fellow. I wish we had thousands like him.”

In the evening the doctor called. Without appearing to make a professional visit he engaged Marie in conversation, watched her carefully, and came to the conclusion that her failing health proceeded more from mental than physical causes.

“I am so uneasy about Harry,” said Mrs. Leroy. “He is so fearless and outspoken. I do wish the attention of the whole nation could be turned to the cruel barbarisms which are a national disgrace. I think the term ‘bloody shirt’ is one of the most heartless phrases ever invented to divert attention from cruel wrongs and dreadful outrages.”

Just then Iola came in and was introduced by her uncle to Dr. Latimer, to whom the introduction was a sudden and unexpected pleasure.

After an interchange of courtesies, Marie resumed the conversation, saying: “Harry wrote me only last week that a young friend of his had lost his situation because he refused to have his pupils strew flowers on the streets through which Jefferson Davis was to pass.”

===Chinese===
“妈妈身子不太舒服，”伊奥拉对罗伯特说。“我跟她商量要不要请位医生来，她不肯，我也就没有再坚持。”

“我去请拉蒂默医生（Dr. Latimer）顺道过来坐坐，我是在康科迪亚旅馆结识他的。他是个顶出色的年轻人。真盼着咱们能有成千上万个像他这样的人。”

傍晚时分，医生来了。他装出并非专程出诊的模样，同玛丽攀谈起来，一面细细观察她，最后得出结论：她日渐衰弱的身体，病根与其说在生理，不如说在心里。

“我为哈里悬心得慌，”勒罗伊太太说。“他那么无所畏惧，心直口快。我多么盼望举国上下的注意力都能转到那些残酷的暴戾行径上来，那实在是整个国家的耻辱。依我看，‘血衬衫’这个词，是人们为了把注意力从残忍的冤屈和骇人的暴行上引开而发明的字眼当中，最没心肝的一个。”

正在这时，伊奥拉走进屋来，舅舅把她介绍给拉蒂默医生；对医生说来，这次引见来得突然，是一份意想不到的欢喜。

寒暄了一阵之后，玛丽接回话头，说道：“哈里上礼拜还写信告诉我，他的一位年轻朋友丢了差事，只因他不肯让学生们往街上撒鲜花——杰斐逊·戴维斯（Jefferson Davis，南方邦联总统）要从那条街上经过。”


===Original===
“I think,” said Dr. Latimer, indignantly, “that the Israelites had just as much right to scatter flowers over the bodies of the Egyptians, when the waves threw back their corpses on the shores of the Red Sea, as these children had to strew the path of Jefferson Davis with flowers. We want our boys to grow up manly citizens, and not cringing sycophants. When do you expect your son, Mrs. Leroy?”

“Some time next week,” answered Marie.

“And his presence will do you more good than all the medicine in my chest.”

“I hope, Doctor,” said Mrs. Leroy, “that we will not lose sight of you, now that your professional visit is ended; for I believe your visit was the result of a conspiracy between Iola and her uncle.”

===Chinese===
“依我看，”拉蒂默医生愤然说道，“当年海浪把埃及人的尸首抛回红海岸边，以色列人若往那些尸首上撒花，其权利也恰和这些孩子往杰斐逊·戴维斯的路上撒鲜花一般无二。我们要我们的男孩子长成有男子气概的公民，而不是卑躬屈膝的谄媚之徒。勒罗伊太太，您预计令郎什么时候到呢？”

“下礼拜的哪一天吧，”玛丽答道。

“他来了，比我这药箱里全部的药加在一起，对您更管用。”

“医生，我希望，”勒罗伊太太说，“您这趟出诊虽已了结，我们往后却别因此见不着您；因为我相信，您这次来访，本是伊奥拉和她舅舅合谋的结果。”


===Original===
Dr. Latimer laughed, as he answered, “Ah, Mrs. Leroy, I see you have found us all out.”

“Oh, Doctor,” exclaimed Iola, with pleasing excitement, “there is a young lady coming here to visit me next week. Her name is Miss Lucille Delany, and she is my ideal woman. She is grand, brave, intellectual, and religious.”

“Is that so? She would make some man an excellent wife,” replied Dr. Latimer.

“Now isn’t that perfectly manlike,” answered Iola, smiling. “Mamma, what do you think of that? Did any of you gentlemen ever see a young woman of much ability that you did not look upon as a flotsam all adrift until some man had appropriated her?”

===Chinese===
拉蒂默医生笑了，答道：“呵，勒罗伊太太，我看您是把我们全都识破了。”

“哦，医生，”伊奥拉喜滋滋地嚷道，“下礼拜有位年轻小姐要来看我。她名叫露西尔·德拉尼小姐（Lucille Delany），是我心目中的理想女性。她高尚、勇敢、有学识，又虔诚。”

“是吗？她准能给哪个男人做个极好的妻子，”拉蒂默医生答道。

“瞧瞧，这十足是男人的口气，”伊奥拉含笑答道。“妈妈，您觉得这话怎么样？你们几位当先生的，可曾见过一个才干出众的年轻女子，不被你们看作一件随波漂流、无主认领的浮货——非要等到哪个男人把她据为己有，才算数？”


===Original===
“I think, Miss Leroy, that the world’s work, if shared, is better done than when it is performed alone. Don’t you think your lifework will be better done if someone shares it with you?” asked Dr. Latimer, slowly, and with a smile in his eyes.

“That would depend on the person who shared it,” said Iola, faintly blushing.

“Here,” said Robert, a few evenings after this conversation, as he handed Iola a couple of letters, “is something which will please you.”

Iola took the letters, and, after reading one of them, said: “Miss Delany and Harry will be here on Wednesday; and this one is an invitation which also adds to my enjoyment.”

===Chinese===
“勒罗伊小姐，依我想，世间的事业，若有人分担，总比独自一人操持做得更好。您不觉得，您毕生的事业若有人与您分担，也会成就得更出色吗？”拉蒂默医生慢悠悠地问道，眼里含着笑意。

“那就得看分担的人是谁了，”伊奥拉说，脸颊上泛起淡淡的红晕。

这场谈话过后没几个晚上，罗伯特把两封信递到伊奥拉手里，说道：“喏，有让你高兴的事儿。”

伊奥拉接过信，读完其中一封，说道：“德拉尼小姐和哈里礼拜三到；这一封是份请帖，也叫我多了一重欢喜。”


===Original===
“What is it?” asked Marie; “an invitation to a hop or a german?”

“No; but something which I value far more. We are all invited to Mr. Stillman’s to a conversazione.”

“What is the object?”

“His object is to gather some of the thinkers and leaders of the race to consult on subjects of vital interest to our welfare. He has invited Dr. Latimer, Professor Gradnor, of North Carolina, Mr. Forest, of New York, Hon. Dugdale, Revs. Carmicle, Cantnor, Tunster, Professor Langhorne, of Georgia, and a few ladies, Mrs. Watson, Miss Brown, and others.”

===Chinese===
“是什么请帖？”玛丽问道。“是请去参加舞会，还是去跳日耳曼舞？”

“都不是；是一样我珍视得多的东西。我们一家都受邀到斯蒂尔曼先生（Mr. Stillman）府上，参加一场交谈会（conversazione）。”

“为的是什么事呢？”

“他的用意，是把这个种族里的一些思想家和领袖人物聚到一处，就一些与我们福祉攸关的重大议题共同商讨。他请了拉蒂默医生、北卡罗来纳的格拉德诺教授（Professor Gradnor）、纽约的福雷斯特先生（Mr. Forest）、达格代尔先生（Hon. Dugdale）、卡迈克（Carmicle）、坎特诺（Cantnor）、滕斯特（Tunster）三位牧师、佐治亚的朗霍恩教授（Professor Langhorne），还有几位女士——沃森太太（Mrs. Watson）、布朗小姐（Miss Brown）等人。”


===Original===
“I am glad that it is neither a hop nor a german,” said Iola, “but something for which I have been longing.”

“Why, Iola,” asked Robert, “don’t you believe in young people having a good time?”

“Oh, yes,” answered Iola, seriously, “I believe in young people having amusements and recreations; but the times are too serious for us to attempt to make our lives a long holiday.”

“Well, Iola,” answered Robert, “this is the first holiday we have had in two hundred and fifty years, and you shouldn’t be too exacting.”

===Chinese===
“我很高兴它既不是舞会，也不是日耳曼舞，”伊奥拉说，“而是一件我盼望已久的事。”

“嗳，伊奥拉，”罗伯特问，“你难道不赞成年轻人痛痛快快地玩乐吗？”

“哦，赞成的，”伊奥拉郑重地答道，“我赞成年轻人有消遣娱乐；可眼下时局太严酷，我们没法存心把日子过成一个长长的假期。”

“得了，伊奥拉，”罗伯特答道，“这可是咱们二百五十年来头一遭的假日，你也不必太苛求啦。”


===Original===
“Yes,” replied Marie, “human beings naturally crave enjoyment, and if not furnished with good amusements they are apt to gravitate to low pleasures.”

“Someone,” said Robert, “has said that the Indian belongs to an old race and looks gloomily back to the past, and that the negro belongs to a young race and looks hopefully towards the future.”

“If that be so,” replied Marie, “our race-life corresponds more to the follies of youth than the faults of maturer years.”

On Dr. Latimer’s next visit he was much pleased to see a great change in Marie’s appearance. Her eye had grown brighter, her step more elastic, and the anxiety had faded from her face. Harry had arrived, and with him came Miss Delany.

===Chinese===
“说的是，”玛丽答道，“人天生渴求享乐；若没有正当的好消遣供给他们，他们便容易一头坠向低级的乐趣。”

“有人说过，”罗伯特说，“印第安人属于一个古老的种族，总是郁郁地回望过去；黑人属于一个年轻的种族，总是满怀希望地眺望将来。”

“倘若当真如此，”玛丽答道，“我们这个种族的生命，与其说近于成熟年纪的过失，不如说更像青春年少的痴狂。”

拉蒂默医生下一次来访时，很高兴地看到玛丽的容色大为改观：她的眼睛比先前亮了，脚步比先前轻快了，脸上的忧色也褪去了。哈里已经到家，与他同来的还有德拉尼小姐。


===Original===
“Good evening, Dr. Latimer,” said Iola, cheerily, as she entered the room with Miss Lucille Delany. “This is my friend, Miss Delany, from Georgia. Were she not present I would say she is one of the grandest women in America.”

“I am very much pleased to meet you,” said Dr. Latimer, cordially; “I have heard Miss Leroy speak of you. We were expecting you,” he added, with a smile.

Just then Harry entered the room, and Iola presented him to Dr. Latimer, saying, “This is my brother, about whom mamma was so anxious.”

“Had you a pleasant journey?” asked Dr. Latimer, after the first greetings were over.

===Chinese===
“晚上好，拉蒂默医生，”伊奥拉喜滋滋地说，一面同露西尔·德拉尼小姐走进屋来。“这位是我的朋友德拉尼小姐，从佐治亚来。她要是不在场，我就要说，她是全美国最了不起的女性之一。”

“见到您非常高兴，”拉蒂默医生热诚地说。“勒罗伊小姐同我谈起过您。我们正盼着您来呢。”他含笑又补了一句。

正在这时，哈里走进屋来，伊奥拉把他介绍给拉蒂默医生，说道：“这就是我弟弟，妈妈为他悬了好久的心。”

初见的寒暄过后，拉蒂默医生问道：“一路上都还顺当吗？”


===Original===
“Not especially,” answered Miss Delany. “Southern roads are not always very pleasant to travel. When Mr. Leroy entered the cars at A﻿—﻿—, where he was known, had he taken his seat among the white people he would have been remanded to the colored.”

“But after awhile,” said Harry, “as Miss Delany and myself were sitting together, laughing and chatting, a colored man entered the car, and, mistaking me for a white man, asked the conductor to have me removed, and I had to insist that I was colored in order to be permitted to remain. It would be ludicrous, if it were not vexatious, to be too white to be black, and too black to be white.”

“Caste plays such fantastic tricks in this country,” said Dr. Latimer.

“I tell Mr. Leroy,” said Miss Delany, “that when he returns he must put a label on himself, saying, ‘I am a colored man,’ to prevent annoyance.”

===Chinese===
“谈不上多么顺当，”德拉尼小姐答道。“南方这些路，行起旅来并不总是那么愉快。勒罗伊先生在 A—— 城上了火车，那里的人都认得他；他若是在白人堆里落了座，准会被押回有色人种的车厢去。”

“不过过了一会儿，”哈里说，“德拉尼小姐和我正坐在一起说说笑笑，一个有色人种进了车厢，把我错当成白人，便请列车员把我挪走；我只得一口咬定自己是有色人种，才获准留在原座。一个人白得做不成黑人，又黑得做不成白人——若不是惹人恼火，简直要叫人失笑了。”

“等级偏见在这个国家里耍出的花招，真是千奇百怪，”拉蒂默医生说。

“我跟勒罗伊先生说，”德拉尼小姐说，“他回去的时候，得在自己身上贴一张标签，写明‘我是有色人种’，免得招惹麻烦。”

## XXXII Wooing and Wedding / 第三十二章 求婚与婚礼

===Original===
Harry’s vacation had been very pleasant. Miss Delany, with her fine conversational powers and ready wit, had added much to his enjoyment. Robert had given his mother the pleasantest room in the house, and in the evening the family would gather around her, tell her the news of the day, read to her from the Bible, join with her in thanksgiving for mercies received and in prayer for protection through the night. Harry was very grateful to Dr. Latimer for the kindly interest he had shown in accompanying Miss Delany and himself to places of interest and amusement. He was grateful, too, that in the city of P﻿—﻿— doors were open to them which were barred against them in the South.

The bright, beautiful days of summer were gliding into autumn, with its glorious wealth of foliage, and the time was approaching for the departure of Harry and Miss Delany to their respective schools, when Dr. Latimer received several letters from North Carolina, urging him to come South, as physicians were greatly needed there. Although his practice was lucrative in the city of P﻿—﻿—, he resolved he would go where his services were most needed.

A few evenings before he started he called at the house, and made an engagement to drive Iola to the park.

At the time appointed he drove up to the door in his fine equipage. Iola stepped gracefully in and sat quietly by his side to enjoy the loveliness of the scenery and the gorgeous grandeur of the setting sun.

===Chinese===
哈里（Harry）的假期过得非常愉快。德拉尼小姐（Miss Delany）谈吐出众、妙语随时而生，为这段时光增添了许多乐趣。罗伯特（Robert）把家中最舒适的房间让给了母亲；每到晚上，一家人便围坐在她身旁，给她讲当日的新闻，读《圣经》给她听，同她一起为已蒙的恩典献上感恩，也为着一夜的平安祷告。拉蒂默医生（Dr. Latimer）殷勤关切，一路陪伴德拉尼小姐和他游览名胜、寻访游赏的去处，哈里对此十分感激。他还感激的是，在 P—— 城，对他们敞开的门扉，在南方却是向他们紧闭的。

明媚美丽的夏日正悄然滑入秋天绚烂丰盈的叶色，哈里和德拉尼小姐动身回各自学校的日子渐渐临近；就在这时，拉蒂默医生接连收到几封北卡罗来纳（North Carolina）的来信，催他南下，说那边亟需医生。尽管他在 P—— 城行医收入丰厚，他还是决意到最需要他出力的地方去。

动身前几天的一个傍晚，他登门拜访，约定驾车带伊奥拉（Iola）到公园兜风。

到了约定的时间，他驾着漂亮的马车驶到门前。伊奥拉步态优雅地登上车，安静地坐在他身旁，欣赏景色的秀美与落日辉煌壮丽的气象。

===Original===
“I expect to go South,” said Dr. Latimer, as he drove slowly along.

“Ah, indeed,” said Iola, assuming an air of interest, while a shadow flitted over her face. “Where do you expect to pitch your tent?”

“In the city of C﻿—﻿—, North Carolina,” he answered.

“Oh, I wish,” she exclaimed, “that you were going to Georgia, where you could take care of that high-spirited brother of mine.”

“I suppose if he were to hear you he would laugh, and say that he could take care of himself. But I know a better plan than that.”

“What is it?” asked Iola, innocently.

===Chinese===
“我快要动身南下了，”拉蒂默医生一边缓缓驱车前行，一边说道。

“啊，当真，”伊奥拉说着，装出一副关切的神情，一道暗影却从她脸上掠过，“您打算把帐篷扎在哪儿呢？”

“北卡罗来纳的 C—— 城，”他答道。

“哦，我真希望，”她唤道，“您去的是佐治亚（Georgia），那样您就能照管我那位血气方刚的弟弟了。”

“我想他若听见您这话，准会哈哈大笑，说自己照料得了自己。不过，我知道一个比这更好的主意。”

“什么主意？”伊奥拉天真地问。

===Original===
“That you will commit yourself, instead of your brother, to my care.”

“Oh, dear,” replied Iola, drawing a long breath. “What would mamma say?”

“That she would willingly resign you, I hope.”

“And what would grandma and Uncle Robert say?” again asked Iola.

“That they would cheerfully acquiesce. Now, what would I say if they all consent?”

“I don’t know,” modestly responded Iola.

===Chinese===
“就是请您把您自己——而不是您弟弟——托付给我照料。”

“哎呀，天哪，”伊奥拉深深吸了一口气，答道，“妈妈会怎么说呢？”

“她会心甘情愿地把您交托出来，我希望是这样。”

“那外祖母和罗伯特舅舅会怎么说呢？”伊奥拉又问。

“他们会欣然应允的。那么，要是他们全都点头了，我会说什么呢？”

“我不知道，”伊奥拉腼腆地答道。

===Original===
“Well,” replied Dr. Latimer, “I would say:﻿—

> “Could deeds my love discover,
 

 Could valor gain thy charms,
 

 To prove myself thy lover
 

 I’d face a world in arms.”

“And prove a good soldier,” added Iola, smiling, “when there is no battle to fight.”

===Chinese===
“那么，”拉蒂默医生答道，“我要说：——

> “倘若功业能揭示我的爱情，
 

 倘若勇武能赢得你的芳心，
 

 为要证明我真是你的爱人，
 

 我甘愿直面举世的兵戈。”

“还要证明自己是名好军人呢，”伊奥拉含笑添了一句，“偏偏在没仗可打的时候。”

===Original===
“Iola, I am in earnest,” said Dr. Latimer, passionately. “In the work to which I am devoted every burden will be lighter, every path smoother, if brightened and blessed with your companionship.”

A sober expression swept over Iola’s face, and, dropping her eyes, she said: “I must have time to think.”

Quietly they rode along the river bank until Dr. Latimer broke the silence by saying:﻿—

“Miss Iola, I think that you brood too much over the condition of our people.”

“Perhaps I do,” she replied, “but they never burn a man in the South that they do not kindle a fire around my soul.”

“I am afraid,” replied Dr. Latimer, “that you will grow morbid and nervous. Most of our people take life easily﻿—why shouldn’t you?”

===Chinese===
“伊奥拉，我是认真的，”拉蒂默医生满怀激情地说。“在我献身以赴的这番事业里，若有你的相伴为之生辉、为之蒙福，每一副担子都会更轻省，每一条路都会更平坦。”

一抹凝重的神情掠过伊奥拉的脸庞；她垂下眼睛，说道：“我得有时间想一想。”

两人沿着河岸静静地驱车前行，直到拉蒂默医生打破沉默，说道：——

“伊奥拉小姐，我觉得你把我们同胞的境况思虑得太重了。”

“也许是吧，”她答道，“可他们在南方每烧死一个人，就没有一次不在我的灵魂四周点起一把火。”

“我怕的是，”拉蒂默医生答道，“你会变得病态忧郁、神经衰弱。我们的同胞大多数把日子过得轻松自在——你怎么就不能呢？”

===Original===
“Because,” she answered, “I can see breakers ahead which they do not.”

“Oh, give yourself no uneasiness. They will catch the fret and fever of the nineteenth century soon enough. I have heard several of our ministers say that it is chiefly men of disreputable characters who are made the subjects of violence and lynch-law.”

“Suppose it is so,” responded Iola, feelingly. “If these men believe in eternal punishment they ought to feel a greater concern for the wretched sinner who is hurried out of time with all his sins upon his head, than for the godly man who passes through violence to endless rest.”

“That is true; and I am not counseling you to be selfish; but, Miss Iola, had you not better look out for yourself?”

“Thank you, Doctor, I am feeling quite well.”

“I know it, but your devotion to study and work is too intense,” he replied.

===Chinese===
“因为，”她答道，“我看得见前头的碎浪暗礁，他们却看不见。”

“哦，你尽管放宽心。他们很快就会染上十九世纪的焦躁与狂热，不愁染不上。我听我们好几位牧师说过，沦为暴力和私刑之对象的，多半是些声名狼藉之辈。”

“就算是这样吧，”伊奥拉动情地应道。“倘若这些人相信有永远的刑罚，那么比起那位经暴力而进入无尽安息的敬虔人，他们就更该惦念那可怜的罪人——他被匆匆赶出今生的时候，满头的罪孽还全都顶在头上。”

“这话不假；我也不是劝你自私；可是，伊奥拉小姐，你不妨多为自己操操心，岂不更好？”

“谢谢您，医生，我身子好好的呢。”

“这我知道，”他答道，“可你为学业和工作倾注的心力太过了。”

===Original===
“I am preparing to teach, and must spend my leisure time in study. Mr. Cloten is an excellent employer, and treats his employees as if they had hearts as well as hands. But to be an expert accountant is not the best use to which I can put my life.”

“As a teacher you will need strong health and calm nerves. You had better let me prescribe for you. You need,” he added, with a merry twinkle in his eyes, “change of air, change of scene, and change of name.”

“Well, Doctor,” said Iola, laughing, “that is the newest nostrum out. Had you not better apply for a patent?”

“Oh,” replied Dr. Latimer, with affected gravity, “you know you must have unlimited faith in your physician.”

“So you wish me to try the faith cure?” asked Iola, laughing.

“Yes, faith in me,” responded Dr. Latimer, seriously.

===Chinese===
“我正预备去教书，闲暇的工夫都得用在学习上。克洛滕先生（Mr. Cloten）是位极好的东家，待店里的雇员，就仿佛他们不光有一双手，还有一颗心。可是，做一个娴熟的会计，并不是我能把自己这条生命派上的最好用场。”

“做教师，你需要强健的身体和安稳的神经。你最好让我来给你开方调理。你需要，”他眼里闪着快活的光，添了一句，“换一换空气，换一换景物，再换一换姓氏。”

“哎呀，医生，”伊奥拉笑着说，“这可是时下最新出的一味万灵药。您是不是最好去申请个专利？”

“哦，”拉蒂默医生故作正经地答道，“你知道的，对于替你诊治的医生，你必须怀有无限的信任。”

“这么说，您是要我试试信仰疗法了？”伊奥拉笑着问。

“是的，要信我，”拉蒂默医生正色答道。

===Original===
“Oh, here we are at home!” exclaimed Iola. “This has been a glorious evening, Doctor. I am indebted to you for a great pleasure. I am extremely grateful.”

“You are perfectly welcome,” replied Dr. Latimer. “The pleasure has been mutual, I assure you.”

“Will you not come in?” asked Iola.

Tying his horse, he accompanied Iola into the parlor. Seating himself near her, he poured into her ears words eloquent with love and tenderness.

“Iola,” he said, “I am not an adept in courtly phrases. I am a plain man, who believes in love and truth. In asking you to share my lot, I am not inviting you to a life of ease and luxury, for year after year I may have to struggle to keep the wolf from the door, but your presence would make my home one of the brightest spots on earth, and one of the fairest types of heaven. Am I presumptuous in hoping that your love will become the crowning joy of my life?”

His words were more than a tender strain wooing her to love and happiness, they were a clarion call to a life of high and holy worth, a call which found a response in her heart. Her hand lay limp in his. She did not withdraw it, but, raising her lustrous eyes to his, she softly answered: “Frank, I love you.”

===Chinese===
“哦，我们到家啦！”伊奥拉唤道。“今晚真是美极了，医生。这份莫大的欢愉，多亏了您。我感激不尽。”

“不必客气，”拉蒂默医生答道，“我向你保证，这份欢愉是彼此的。”

“您不进来坐坐吗？”伊奥拉问。

他把马拴好，随伊奥拉走进客厅。他在她身旁坐下，向她耳边倾吐起满含爱意与柔情的款款话语。

“伊奥拉，”他说，“我不是精于典雅辞令的行家。我是个质朴的人，信的是爱情和真诚。我请求你与我共此命运，并不是邀你去过安闲奢华的日子；年复一年，我也许都得拼力把饿狼挡在门外。可只要有你在，我的家就会成为人间最明亮的角落之一、天国最美的样式之一。我盼着你的爱情能成为我此生加冕的至乐——我这样盼着，是不是太僭越了？”

他的话语，不止是一支引她走向爱情与幸福的温柔恋曲，更是一声嘹亮的号角，召唤人去过一种崇高、神圣而有价值的生活；这声号角在她心中得到了回应。她的手软软地搁在他的手里。她没有抽回手，只是抬起那双明亮的眼睛望着他的眼睛，轻声答道：“弗兰克，我爱你。”

===Original===
After he had gone, Iola sat by the window, gazing at the splendid stars, her heart quietly throbbing with a delicious sense of joy and love. She had admired Dr. Gresham and, had there been no barrier in her way, she might have learned to love him; but Dr. Latimer had grown irresistibly upon her heart. There were depths in her nature that Dr. Gresham had never fathomed; aspirations in her soul with which he had never mingled. But as the waves leap up to the strand, so her soul went out to Dr. Latimer. Between their lives were no impeding barriers, no inclination impelling one way and duty compelling another. Kindred hopes and tastes had knit their hearts; grand and noble purposes were lighting up their lives; and they esteemed it a blessed privilege to stand on the threshold of a new era and labor for those who had passed from the old oligarchy of slavery into the new commonwealth of freedom.

On the next evening, Dr. Latimer rang the bell and was answered by Harry, who ushered him into the parlor, and then came back to the sitting-room, saying, “Iola, Dr. Latimer has called to see you.”

“Has he?” answered Iola, a glad light coming into her eyes. “Come, Lucille, let us go into the parlor.”

“Oh, no,” interposed Harry, shrugging his shoulders and catching Lucille’s hand. “He didn’t ask for you. When we went to the concert we were told three’s a crowd. And I say one good turn deserves another.”

“Oh, Harry, you are so full of nonsense. Let Lucille go!” said Iola.

“Indeed I will not. I want to have a good time as well as you,” said Harry.

===Chinese===
他走后，伊奥拉坐在窗前，凝望满天璀璨的星辰，心里静静激荡着一种甜美的欢悦与爱意。她曾钦佩过格雷沙姆医生（Dr. Gresham）；倘若路上没有那道障碍，她或许也会渐渐爱上他；可拉蒂默医生却在她的心上无可抗拒地日益深植。她的天性中有一些深处，是格雷沙姆医生从未探测过的；她的灵魂中有一些向往，是他从未与之相融的。而正如波浪跃起、扑向岸边，她的灵魂也这样奔向了拉蒂默医生。他们的生命之间，没有阻碍的屏障，没有心意推向一头、义务逼向另一头的撕扯。同源的希望与相投的志趣把他们的心紧紧相连；宏大而高贵的志向正照亮他们的生命；而能够站在一个新纪元的门槛上，为那些从奴隶制的旧寡头统治跨入自由新共同体的人们效力，他们把这看作一份蒙福的特权。

第二天傍晚，拉蒂默医生按响门铃，应门的是哈里。他把医生让进客厅，自己回到起居室，说道：“伊奥拉，拉蒂默医生来看你了。”

“是吗？”伊奥拉应道，眼里亮起欢喜的光。“来，露西尔（Lucille），咱们到客厅去。”

“哦，不行，”哈里插进来，耸耸肩，一把抓住露西尔的手。“他没请你。咱们去听音乐会那回，人家不是说了嘛，两人成伴，三人嫌挤。我说呀，一报还一报。”

“哎呀，哈里，你满肚子净是浑话。让露西尔走！”伊奥拉说。

“我才不呢。你快活你的，我也想快活快活，”哈里说。

===Original===
“Oh, you’re the most nonsensical man I know,” interposed Miss Delany. Yet she stayed with Harry.

“You’re looking very bright and happy,” said Dr. Latimer to Iola, as she entered.

“My ride in the park was so refreshing! I enjoyed it so much! The day was so lovely, the air delicious, the birds sang so sweetly, and the sunset was so magnificent.”

“I am glad of it. Why, Iola, your home is so happy your heart should be as light as a schoolgirl’s.”

“Doctor,” she replied, “I must be prematurely old. I have scarcely known what it is to be lighthearted since my father’s death.”

“I know it, darling,” he answered, seating himself beside her, and drawing her to him. “You have been tried in the fire, but are you not better for the crucial test?”

===Chinese===
“哦，你真是我认得的人里头顶顶荒唐的一个，”德拉尼小姐插嘴道。可她还是陪哈里留下了。

伊奥拉走进来时，拉蒂默医生对她说：“你瞧着容光焕发、快活得很。”

“在公园里兜那一趟风，真叫人神清气爽！我太尽兴了！天色那么可爱，空气那么甘美，鸟儿唱得那么甜，落日又是那么壮丽。”

“这就对了。哎呀，伊奥拉，你的家这样幸福，你的心也该轻快得像个女学生才是。”

“医生，”她答道，“我准是未老先衰了。打父亲去世以后，我就几乎不知道心情轻快是什么滋味了。”

“这我知道，亲爱的，”他答着，在她身旁坐下，把她揽到身边。“你在火中受过试炼，可经过这场严酷的考验，你不是变得更好了吗？”

===Original===
“Doctor,” she replied, “as we rode along yesterday, mingling with the sunshine of the present came the shadows of the past. I was thinking of the bright, joyous days of my girlhood, when I defended slavery, and of how the cup that I would have pressed to the lips of others was forced to my own. Yet, in looking over the mournful past, I would not change the Iola of then for the Iola of now.”

“Yes,” responded Dr. Latimer, musingly,

> “ ‘Darkness shows us worlds of light
 

 We never saw by day.’ ”

===Chinese===
“医生，”她答道，“昨天我们一路乘车，现在的阳光里掺着过去的阴影。我想起做姑娘时那些明亮快活的日子——那时我替奴隶制辩护；又想起那杯我本要敬到别人唇边的酒，是怎样被迫灌到了我自己的唇边。然而，把这段哀伤的往事整个回望一遍，我也不愿拿从前的伊奥拉，去换如今的伊奥拉。”

“是啊，”拉蒂默医生沉吟着应道，

> “‘黑暗向我们显出诸光明的世界，
 

 那是白昼里我们从未见过的。’”

===Original===
“Oh, Doctor, you cannot conceive what it must have been to be hurled from a home of love and light into the dark abyss of slavery; to be compelled to take your place among a people you have learned to look upon as inferiors and social outcasts; to be in the power of men whose presence would fill you with horror and loathing, and to know that there is no earthly power to protect you from the highest insults which brutal cowardice could shower upon you. I am so glad that no other woman of my race will suffer as I have done.”

The flush deepened on her face, a mournful splendor beamed from her beautiful eyes, into which the tears had slowly gathered.

“Darling,” he said, his voice vibrating with mingled feelings of tenderness and resentment, “you must forget the sad past. You are like a tender lamb snatched from the jaws of a hungry wolf, but who still needs protecting, loving care. But it must have been terrible,” he added, in a painful tone.

“It was indeed! For awhile I was like one dazed. I tried to pray, but the heavens seemed brass over my head. I was wild with agony, and had I not been placed under conditions which roused all the resistance of my soul, I would have lost my reason.”

“Was it not a mistake to have kept you ignorant of your colored blood?”

===Chinese===
“哦，医生，你无法想象那是什么滋味：从一座爱与光明的家园，被猛掷进奴隶制的黑暗深渊；被迫在一群你一向学着看作低人一等、看作社会弃儿的人们中间占上一个位置；落在一班人手里——他们一露面就会让你满心恐怖与憎恶——还明知世上没有任何力量能保护你，使你免受残暴的怯懦所能泼到你身上的最大凌辱。我多么庆幸，我们种族里再没有别的女子，会受我受过的这种苦了。”

她脸上的红晕更深了，一双美丽的眼睛里已慢慢蓄起泪水，闪出一种哀婉的光彩。

“亲爱的，”他说，声音里颤动着柔情与愤懑交杂的情绪，“你必须忘掉那悲哀的过去。你就像一只从饿狼口边夺回来的柔弱羔羊，只是这羔羊仍旧需要有人保护、有人疼怜。可那必定是可怕的，”他又用一种痛苦的声调补了一句。

“何止是可怕！有一阵子，我整个人像失了魂似的。我想祷告，可头顶的天仿佛变成了铜。我痛苦得几乎发狂；倘若不是被置于激起我灵魂全部反抗的境地，我早就失掉理智了。”

“当初让你对自己身上的有色人种血液一无所知，这难道不是一个错误吗？”

===Original===
“It was the great mistake of my father’s life, but dear papa knew something of the cruel, crushing power of caste; and he tried to shield us from it.”

“Yes, yes,” replied Dr. Latimer, thoughtfully, “in trying to shield you from pain he plunged you into deeper suffering.”

“I never blame him, because I know he did it for the best. Had he lived he would have taken us to France, where I should have had a life of careless ease and pleasure. But now my life has a much grander significance than it would have had under such conditions. Fearful as the awakening was, it was better than to have slept through life.”

“Best for you and best for me,” said Dr. Latimer. “There are souls that never awaken; but if they miss the deepest pain they also lose the highest joy.”

Dr. Latimer went South, after his engagement, and through his medical skill and agreeable manners became very successful in his practice. In the following summer, he built a cosy home for the reception of his bride, and came North, where, with Harry and Miss Delany as attendants, he was married to Iola, amid a pleasant gathering of friends, by Rev. Carmicle.

===Chinese===
“那是父亲一生中最大的失策；可是亲爱的爸爸对等级偏见那残酷而足以碾碎人的力量，并非一无所知，他想把我们护在它的外面。”

“是啊，是啊，”拉蒂默医生若有所思地答道，“他本想护你避开痛苦，反倒把你抛进了更深的苦难。”

“我从不怪他，因为我知道他那样做是出于一片至善之心。他若活着，本会带我们去法国，我在那里就会过一种无忧无虑、闲适逸乐的生活。可如今，我这条生命有了比那种境况远为宏大的意义。那场觉醒纵然可怕，也总好过一生沉睡到底。”

“于你最好，于我也最好，”拉蒂默医生说。“有些灵魂永远不曾觉醒；可他们若是躲过了最深的痛，也就失掉了最高的喜乐。”

订婚之后，拉蒂默医生南下去了；凭着精湛的医术和随和可亲的风度，他的行医生涯十分兴旺。第二年夏天，他盖起一座温馨舒适的小宅，以迎娶新娘，随后北上；在哈里和德拉尼小姐的傧相陪伴下，由卡迈克牧师（Rev. Carmicle）主礼，在一群好友的欢聚之中，与伊奥拉结为夫妇。
